## Capítulo 1 — Concepts in Security Risk Management (págs. 1–16)

**Central idea:** Enterprise Security Risk Management (ESRM) is today the foundation of security management. It replaces the "program-based" approach with a risk management process. The asset owner decides about the risk; the security professional advises as a trusted partner.

**Origin and purpose**

- ESRM was born in **2010**. It was created by members of the CSO Roundtable of ASIS International, today the **CSO Center** (CSO = Chief Security Officer).
- Aim: manage security with risk principles, coordinating with the senior management whose operations generate that risk.
- Intended results: integrate the security professional into business leadership. Move from managing programs to managing risks.
- Error it combats: the "quick fix." Buying a fashionable system or service without asking which assets exist or which threats they face.
- More detail: Chapter 1 of the volume *Security Management* of Protection of Assets.

### 1.1 Taking an Enterprise Security Risk Management Approach

**Definition:** ESRM is a strategic approach to security management. It links security practice to the overall strategy of the organization. It uses globally accepted risk management principles.

- It is a **management process/system**.
- It is **not** a program or an element of an existing program. It **replaces** the security program methodology.
- It connects all the key elements of security risk to the assets. It informs the decisions of their owners.
- It is **scalable and dynamic**: it serves public or private organizations of any size or scope.

| Aspect | Traditional program-based management | ESRM |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Structure | Security program with many subprograms | No programs; addresses all security risks to assets |
| Objective | A program for each threat or topic | Effective mitigation of prioritized risks |
| Risk assignment | Risk is assigned to a program | Does not assign risks to specific programs |
| Role of security | Does what it is asked or ordered; implements processes and programs | Advises; with the owner identifies, prioritizes, and sets mitigation |
| Who decides | Security carries out what is requested | The **asset owner** owns the risk decisions |

- As partners, security and the owner identify risks, prioritize them, and set mitigation steps or methods.
- The asset owner is **ultimately responsible** for security risk decisions. Just as with any other risk to their assets.

**Two misconceptions about ESRM**

| Error | What the book says |
| --- | --- |
| 1. ESRM = convergence | False. Convergence = bringing traditional, corporate, or physical security together with information security/cybersecurity under a single leader. ESRM manages risk under any structure. There is effective ESRM with different leaders for physical and information security. |
| 2. ESRM = ERM (Enterprise Risk Management) | False. ERM covers **all** key risks: **financial, operational, and strategic**. ESRM deals with security risks. |

- Convergence: not indispensable. But, since cyber is a key risk for every organization, it can streamline management and improve resource allocation.
- Key to success: that everyone who manages security risks adopt and use ESRM.
- ESRM–ERM relationship:
  - Not all organizations have a formal ERM function, although they manage risks in various ways.
  - ESRM can be a **subcomponent** of ERM.
  - High-impact security risks can be tracked formally in ERM. Not all reach that level.
  - That is why ESRM is an **independent** system: it works with or without ERM.
  - Where there is a well-defined ERM, security is usually on the **ERM operations committee**.

#### 1.1.1 The Benefits of an ESRM Program

> **Exam note:** the title says "ESRM Program," but the chapter insists that ESRM is **not** a program, but a management process.

**Benefits for the security professional and team (10)**

| # | Benefit |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | By passing the decision to the asset owner, more resources and perspectives are added to risk management. |
| 2 | Security moves from tactical program owner to strategic partner and trusted advisor. It can oversee mitigation processes, but supported or directed by the owners. E.g.: a centralized access control, operated by security, that mitigates risks for several owners. |
| 3 | Asset owners do not manage by programs. With a risk approach, security works like them and collaborates better. |
| 4 | Security better understands the organization, its strategy, and its goals. It thus helps better to identify and prioritize risks. |
| 5 | Communication with internal and external stakeholders improves. Security learns what they consider critical and refines priorities and plans. |
| 6 | The focus on risk allows more innovation in solving problems and reducing risks. |
| 7 | The holistic approach provides more value and reduces security and related risks. |
| 8 | Security moves from reactive to proactive: fewer incidents and lower impact. |
| 9 | Better resource allocation. Budgets are more manageable and better supported when aligned with owners' goals and identified risks. |
| 10 | As a partner who understands the business, security enters other strategic areas. It gains visibility and value. |

**Benefits for the organization (9)**

| # | Benefit |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Critical risk decisions at the enterprise level, aligned with mission and objectives. Valid for multinationals and for single-facility organizations. |
| 2 | Owners and stakeholders have a broader, more coherent understanding of the security function and risk. |
| 3 | Senior management sees key risks better, because they are raised by managers who are asset owners. |
| 4 | Security resources are aligned with strategy to mitigate prioritized risks. |
| 5 | Early identification and proactive monitoring of a wide range of risks. |
| 6 | Better understanding and commitment by stakeholders regarding risk and the security function. |
| 7 | Better support for legal, regulatory, and contractual functions and issues. |
| 8 | Platform for integrating security into the organization's culture. |
| 9 | More resilience, better response to events, and better crisis management. |

### 1.2 Adopting an ESRM Approach

**The three components of ESRM**

| Component | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| **Context of ESRM** | Aspects of the organization that security must understand to adopt ESRM successfully. |
| **Foundation of ESRM** | Organizational concepts that sustain the approach and maximize its impact. |
| **ESRM Cycle** | The actual security risk management process; emphasizes understanding the assets. |

> **Exam note:** the book lists Context → Foundation → Cycle, but it develops the Cycle (1.3) before the Foundation (1.4).

#### 1.2.1 The Context of ESRM

- The organization is the environment where assets and risks live. Risks and incidents vary greatly from one organization to another.
- Understanding the organization is an **initial step** of ESRM. It is at least as important as understanding threats.
- Aspects that security must understand before implementing ESRM:

| Aspect | Why it matters |
| --- | --- |
| Mission and vision | They are the top of the strategy. Aligning the security strategy with the organization's is one of the most critical requirements. Reinforcing this knowledge is usually the first step when adopting ESRM. |
| Services and products | Basis for engaging with owners and stakeholders, and for identifying and prioritizing risks. The larger the organization, the more complex. |
| Key personnel, leadership, and operating structure | Makes it possible to move around the organization and build key relationships. |
| Legal requirements and regulations | Those that affect security and the organization in general. They help to see compliance risks and to understand stakeholders' positions. |
| Strategic plans and long-term goals | They are the road map. Security reviews goals and strategies looking for risks and raises them to leadership. |

- Resulting message to stakeholders: "security exists to help you achieve your goals" and "we want the same thing you do."
- Effect: more engagement and less perception of security as an **obstacle** to the business.

#### 1.2.2 Core Values

- They usually go beyond profit and shareholder value. Examples from the book: environmental care, community, employee safety, product quality, brand and image protection, new product development.
- ESRM emphasizes **partnership, collaboration, and transparency**. If the values fit with this, the organization will adopt ESRM more easily.
- Linking ESRM to the values ensures alignment with senior management. It is advisable to cite them in the security strategy and messages.
- Values usually define the culture. A culture that does not support security makes it hard to implement ESRM.

Questions to assess values and culture (6):

1. How does the organization handle change?
2. What is its risk tolerance/risk appetite?
3. Are some business units more open than others to working with security?
4. What do employee surveys say about values, culture, and change?
5. Is there effective communication with all stakeholders?
6. What are the organization's key motivators?

#### 1.2.3 Operating Environment

Three types of environment:

| Environment | Includes | What it is for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Physical** | Type of location, building, and surroundings; pedestrian and vehicle traffic; access by nonemployees; industrial control systems; criticality and sensitivity of processes and assets; products on site or stored. | Understanding processes and functions makes it possible to partner with owners and prioritize risks. Knowing the operations makes security a trusted advisor. |
| **Nonphysical** | Geopolitical environment; external pressures on the industry; legal, regulatory, and compliance requirements; intensity of competition; growth phase; speed of decision-making; impact of technology; complexity of continuous change, including leadership change. | They move owners and partners, and are sources of risk. They serve as a reference for designing mitigation options. |
| **Logical** | Types of information; digital assets and the network that connects them with each other and with stakeholders. E.g.: servers, workstations, network infrastructure, connectivity, other devices and end points. | Essential for advising on risks, because every business increasingly depends on digital assets and data. |

#### 1.2.4 Stakeholders

- **Definition:** anyone who interacts directly with the organization. They can affect, or be affected by, the organization, its assets, or its personnel.
- Security must know what matters to each one. That way it advises better and helps design mitigation.

Stakeholders cited by the book (7):

| Stakeholder | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| Executive team, Board of Directors, and those who set the strategy | With more mature ESRM, there is more contact with them and more need to know their concerns. |
| **Asset owners**, tangible and intangible | **Key partners** of ESRM; the ones worked with most. Some assets have shared or multiple owners. |
| People who work for or on behalf of the organization | They can be key allies. Some are owners of intangible assets: purchasing, human resources, legal, marketing. Others are partners in mitigating an owner's risks. Includes employees and contractor personnel. |
| Those who provide knowledge or support | Suppliers, distributors, consultants, audit firms. |
| Industry organizations | Their actions affect the organization. They can generate risk or be a mitigation resource. |
| Customers | Distributors, resellers, retailers, and direct customers. |
| Surrounding community | **Often forgotten.** The organization affects its surroundings, traffic, noise, and economy. There are joint risks; partnering with it helps reduce them. |

How to build the relationship with asset owners:

- First, identify them.
- If there is already a relationship, the partnership arises more easily.
- If there is not: directly ask for a brief meeting, or lean on a **champion**.
- The champion is an asset owner with whom you have already worked, at the same level as the others. Opens doors and defends ESRM and the professional.
- Initial meetings: short; get to know the owner and what matters to him or her.
- Follow-up meetings: focused on risk **as the owner sees it**. Look for an opportunity to propose a partnership.

Keys:

- Stakeholder support is critical: identify them, involve them, understand what matters to them, and align with their priorities.
- Understanding them does **not** mean harmonizing their interests. It means understanding their needs and their view of risk.

### 1.3 The ESRM Cycle

- It describes **how** security risks are mitigated. It resembles other processes in the sector.
- Distinctive feature: emphasis on understanding the assets and involving their owners.

| # | Process | What happens |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **Identify and Prioritize Assets** | Prioritization is by each asset's criticality to the mission and strategy. With guidance from senior management and input from owners and stakeholders. The prioritized list is the basis for the rest of the cycle. |
| 2 | **Identify and Prioritize Risks** | Prioritization is by each risk's potential to prevent fulfilling the mission and strategy. With guidance from senior management and input from stakeholders. |
| 3 | **Mitigate Prioritized Risks** | In order of priority, with controls recommended by security and **approved by the owner**. |
| 4 | **Continuous Improvement** | Spans the **entire** cycle. |

#### 1.3.1 Identify and Prioritize Assets

Key roles at this stage and throughout the cycle:

| Role | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| **Asset Owner** | Person most directly responsible for the asset operating successfully. ESRM assigns the risk to the owner because he or she best understands the asset. **The asset owner is the risk owner.** |
| **Top Management** | Highest executive level: the first executive level (c-suite) or an executive committee. Always **above** the security leader (CSO, Chief Security Officer) and **does not include** him or her. |
| **Security Professional** | Subject matter expert (SME) in security risk. Trusted advisor to owners, senior management, and stakeholders. Guides the owner in the risk decision. |

- ESRM considers **all** types of assets: physical, information, cyber, personnel, etc. It does not segment disciplines or work in silos.
- Everything within the scope of the security professional is within the scope of ESRM.

#### 1.3.2 Identify and Prioritize Risks

- Criterion: each risk's potential to undermine the mission and strategy.
- The prioritized lists of risks and assets form **strategic priorities**. They "cascade" down through the entire cycle and guide the owners' decisions.

#### 1.3.3 Mitigate Prioritized Risks

- The owner decides, with security's guidance.
- Security documents assets, risks, and owners' decisions, perhaps in a **security risk management plan**.
- The plan is ultimately **executed** by the security professional.

> **Exam note:** distinguish roles. Decides and approves = asset owner. Recommends, documents, and executes the plan = security professional.

#### 1.3.4 Continuous Improvement

- It is the final process, but it spans the entire cycle.
- It arises from ESRM results: better communication, more visibility of risks, effective prioritization and mitigation.
- Mechanisms: lessons learned and feedback loops.
- Functions that contribute most: **incident response, investigations and analysis, and information sharing**.

### 1.4 The Foundation of ESRM

To ensure sustained longevity and success, **four critical concepts** are incorporated:

| Component | Key idea |
| --- | --- |
| **Holistic Risk Management** | All stakeholders take part; covers all types of risk without silos. |
| **Partnership with Stakeholders** | Security as advisor and advocate for stakeholders. |
| **Transparency** | Clear, open, and honest approach to risks and process. |
| **Governance** | Rules and processes by which a function or organization is governed. |

#### 1.4.1 Holistic Risk Management

"Holistic" = inclusive, comprehensive, complete. Two meanings in ESRM:

1. **All stakeholders take part.** All have a role, an interest, and a benefit. Especially asset owners and senior management, who must participate actively.
2. **It covers all types of security risk**, without qualifiers or silos (physical, cyber, information, personnel). Whatever is within the scope of the security function falls under ESRM: one, several, or all disciplines.

- Scope does not make ESRM more or less effective. But the more it covers, the more positive impact it can have.

#### 1.4.2 Partner with Stakeholders

| In the Context (1.2.4) | In the Foundation (1.4.2) |
| --- | --- |
| Stakeholders are a component: identify them, involve them, and align with them. | The component is **forging partnerships**: collaborative role of advisor and advocate. |

- It is especially important with asset owners and senior management.

Expected conduct of the professional:

| Do | Instead of |
| --- | --- |
| Give **guidance** | Giving instructions |
| **Support** decisions about assets | Making them yourself |
| Rely on **measured risk** | Fear, uncertainty, doubt |

- Proactively define and "socialize" your own role with senior management and owners. If not, others will define it.
- This makes relationships more positive, valuable, and partnership-based.
- Other typical results (4):
  - Greater stakeholder engagement.
  - Greater sense of ownership and buy-in.
  - Better understanding of security risk on their part.
  - Organizational culture more favorable to security priorities.

> **Exam note:** the list in 1.4 says "Partnership with Stakeholders" and the title of 1.4.2 says "Partner with Stakeholders." It is the same component.

#### 1.4.3 Transparency

- **Definition:** a clear, open, and honest approach. It usually improves culture and morale; stakeholders feel like partners.

| Type | What it requires |
| --- | --- |
| **Risk Transparency** | Presenting risks as they are: neither exaggerated nor minimized. Use objective statements, quantitative analysis, and quantifiable metrics. Supports the owner as risk owner and enables informed decisions. |
| **Process Transparency** | Having owners and stakeholders understand the risk management process and each step. It almost never has value to keep it confidential. Sharing it increases engagement and acceptance. Analogy: the doctor who explains the procedure and calms the patient's defensive or anxious attitude. |

What to share with owners and stakeholders (5):

- a. Current and planned security measures.
- b. The reasons for those measures.
- c. Other relevant decision makers.
- d. Other mitigation options that may be considered.
- e. Final decisions on mitigation.

#### 1.4.4 Governance

- **Definition:** rules and processes by which a function or organization is governed.
- It manages expectations, provides clarity and consistency, and ensures that efforts cover the organization's needs.

| Type | Definition and notes |
| --- | --- |
| **Organizational Governance** | System by which the organization is directed and controlled. It addresses: role of senior executives and the board; audit and oversight; rights and responsibilities of stakeholders; decision procedures. ESRM governance must align with it. |
| **ESRM Governance** | Process of setting security risk policy and direction, allocating resources, and ensuring compliance. It is a **subset** of corporate governance and is modeled on organizational governance. It is exercised by a security governance body (committee, council, or other group). Its members must be **mostly risk stakeholders, not security professionals**. |

Typical documentary framework that governance produces:

| Document | Level | Feature |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Policies** | High level | Corporate strategy and direction. They are the "laws" of the organization. |
| **Standards** | Tactical | How to comply with policy. More detailed than policies. They set what "shall"/"must" be done. |
| **Guidelines** | Tactical | Like standards, but less rigid. They recommend what "should"/"may" be done. |
| **Procedures** | The most operational | Step-by-step instructions to comply with the preceding documents. |

- ESRM weighs heavily on physical security: from how products or solutions are adopted to their implementation and use across the enterprise.

### Key Terms

enterprise security risk management (ESRM) · CSO Roundtable / CSO Center · quick fix · program-based security management · asset owner · risk owner · top management · c-suite · security professional · trusted advisor · subject matter expert · convergence · enterprise risk management (ERM) · ERM operations committee · Context of ESRM · Foundation of ESRM · ESRM Cycle · mission and vision · core values · partnership, collaboration, transparency · risk tolerance / risk appetite · operating environment (physical, nonphysical, logical) · stakeholders · champion · Identify and Prioritize Assets · Identify and Prioritize Risks · Mitigate Prioritized Risks · Continuous Improvement · security risk management plan · lessons learned · feedback loops · holistic risk management · partnership with stakeholders · fear, uncertainty, doubt · ownership and buy-in · risk transparency · process transparency · organizational governance · ESRM governance · security governance body · policies · standards · guidelines · procedures

## Capítulo 2 — Functions of Physical Security (págs. 17–26)

**Central idea:** Physical security protects assets (people, property, and information) with physical measures. Its conceptual basis is the 4 Ds (deter, detect, delay, deny) and it is carried out with seven combined functions. Measures are chosen from a menu, stacked in layers, and coordinated with peripheral systems.

- Starting problem: security lacks universal definitions, physical security included.
- Knowing what physical security is and is not is key to an integrated protection and risk management strategy.
- It applies to assessment, planning, systems design, implementation, and program management.

### 2.1 Definition and Purpose of Physical Security

- **Purpose:** protect tangible, intangible, or mixed assets, whether people, property, or information.

| Source | Defines physical security as… |
| --- | --- |
| **Physical Asset Protection Standard** (PAP; ASIS International, 2021) | Part of security that uses physical measures to: protect people; prevent unauthorized access to equipment, facilities, material, and **documents**; and protect them against a security incident. |
| **U.S. Department of Homeland Security** (DHS) | Part of **homeland security** that uses physical measures to: protect personnel; prevent unauthorized access to equipment, facilities, material, and documents; and defend them against **espionage, sabotage, damage, and theft**. |
| **This volume** (Protection of Assets, *Physical Security*) | Same as the PAP, but with **information** instead of documents. |

- **Security, according to this volume:** the condition of being protected against hazards, threats, risks, tampering, or loss.
- Physical security combines physical measures against the full range of **realistic and credible** threats.
- Usefulness of the definition in any project or program:
  - **Limit** the scope: do not stray from the objective or mission.
  - **Broaden** the scope: include all relevant measures and techniques.
- It keeps focus while allowing broad thinking, without being limited to a single tool such as video surveillance (**Peterson, 2014**).

> **Exam note:** the volume's definition says "information"; the PAP's and the DHS's say "documents." Only the DHS's names espionage, sabotage, damage, and theft.

**The four Ds** — they must be the basis of **every** physical security project. Each measure is valued for its ability to:

| D | What the measure does |
| --- | --- |
| **Deter** | Deter the adversary. |
| **Detect** | Detect an attack. |
| **Delay** | Delay an attack. |
| **Deny** | Deny the adversary access to the target. |

### 2.2 Functions of Physical Security

An effective strategy uses **seven basic functions**, in an orchestrated and tailored way:

| # | Function | Definition and notes from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **Access control** | Any technique to limit or manage access to an area, facility, enclosure, system, person, or other asset. Access is controlled for a person, a vehicle, or an object (package, weapon, explosive device, or monitoring device). |
| 2 | **Deterrence** | Barriers, technology, and procedures to convince the attacker that he or she will fail, and so avoid the site. **It is the hardest measure to measure.** |
| 3 | **Detection** | Ability of the physical protection system to perceive that there is a possible intruder. On its own it is worth little: false positives depend directly on the system's sensitivity. |
| 4 | **Assessment** | Ability of the system to determine what was detected and decide whether to initiate a response. |
| 5 | **Delay** | Measures that slow the intruder's advance. Examples from the book: no cut/no climb fences, high-security locks, secure door installation and hardware, window bars. |
| 6 | **Response** | Actions to neutralize, contain, or mitigate an event. Often, the police respond and arrest the intruders. |
| 7 | **Evidence gathering** | Essential for prosecuting intruders. Every prosecution is good "publicity" for the site's security. It is usually CCTV (closed-circuit television) video. CCTV also supports other purposes, such as safety and operations. |

- In each project, consider **all** applicable measures. Value them for their contribution to the complete physical protection system.
- The measures are a **menu of options**. They are applied as **building blocks** in several **layers of security**.
- The selection of measures for a site must cover **all** the functions.

> **Exam note:** do not confuse the 4 Ds (conceptual basis) with the 7 functions. Also, the book is not consistent: the text speaks of 7 functions; Figure 2.2 shows 4; and the paragraph before 2.3 mentions "the four security functions."

**Figure 2.1 — Measures to Employ in a Physical Security Strategy** (measure × function matrix; "x" = the measure fulfills that function)

| Measure | Access Control | Deter | Delay | Assess | Respond | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Guards | x | x | x | x | x | x |
| Mobile Patrols |  | x | x | x | x | x |
| CCTV | x | x | x | x |  | x |
| FIDS (Fence Line Intrusion Detection System) |  | x | x |  |  |  |
| IDS (Intrusion Detection System) |  | x | x |  |  |  |
| Locks | x | x |  | x |  |  |
| Lights |  | x | x |  | x | x |
| Alarms |  | x |  |  | x |  |
| LIDAR (Light Detection and Ranging) |  |  | x | x |  | x |
| Fence/Wall | x | x |  |  |  |  |

- Only **Guards** mark all 6 columns. Mobile Patrols and CCTV follow, with 5 each.
- Reading note: the matrix was reconstructed from the position of each "x" in the PDF.

> **Exam note:** Figure 2.1 has no "Detect" column, even though detection is one of the 7 functions. It also clashes with the text: it puts FIDS and IDS under "Delay" and does not mark "Delay" for Locks or Fence/Wall. The text, by contrast, cites fences and high-security locks as delay measures. If that column were "Detect," the marks would fit the text. Possible erratum; do not memorize the figure as a rule.

**Figure 2.2 — Relationship of Physical Security Concepts, Functions, and Measures** (four levels, from the conceptual to the peripheral)

| Level | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Conceptual Basis | Deter · Detect · Delay · Deny |
| Functions of physical security (4) | Control Access · Observe · Detect Events · Respond |
| Measures/Tools (21) | Alarms · Access control · Barriers · Communications · Concierge · Doors/locks · Glass treatments · Incident management systems · Ingress/egress · Layout · Lighting · Natural surveillance · Patrol · Robotic systems · Screening · Security officer force · Sensors · Vaults · Video surveillance · Weapons · Working dogs |
| Peripheral Systems/Interfaces (5) | Life safety systems · Building controls · IT infrastructure · Liaison relationships · Outsourced services |

- Reading note: part of the text of this figure came with the font encoded in the PDF and was deciphered letter by letter. The initial capitals of Alarms, Access control, Barriers, Communications, and Building controls were inferred from context.

#### 2.2.1 Physical Security Measures

General features:

- They are the **hard framework** of the physical protection system.
- They are the **most visible** part of the program and help greatly with deterrence.
- They usually operate in environmental extremes: cold, heat, and precipitation.
- **All** are vulnerable to some type of attack.
- Choose them to meet the operational requirements. **Test them regularly** after installing them, especially if they have moving parts.
- The technology of attackers and defenders changes fast. Strategy for staying current: good relationships with reliable suppliers.

| Measure | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| **Barriers** | Mostly structural, but they can be of any of the "three formats." E.g., of a human barrier: a riot line or a human barricade. Broad category. |
| **Fencing** | Consider alternatives to chain link according to purpose, application, environment, and objectives. Decorative fencing is increasingly used in low to moderate risk. It often meets the objectives; there are products that combine security and aesthetics. |
| **Bollards** | Many styles and types. **Fixed or active** (e.g., retractable "pop-up"). Some are purely functional; others combine security and architectural appeal. |
| **Terrain** | Natural barriers, highly effective in certain cases. They can be preexisting (entirely natural) or built on purpose. |
| **Locks** | Mechanical or electronic. They must fit doors, hardware, environment, and objectives. The **door–lock mismatch** is common: it lowers security, raises costs, and complicates maintenance. E.g.: if there are windows beside the door, a **double-cylinder keyed deadbolt** is advisable. |
| **Design, architecture, and engineering** | Structural measures built in by taking advantage of the design. E.g.: the layout or traffic pattern helps minimize the number of openings in the building. It is critical to get involved **from the start** of the project to include security in design and budget. |
| **CPTED** (Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design) | Consider it in almost every project. It is often neglected or arrives late. It is a **force multiplier** and can reduce other physical measures, often more expensive. |
| **Landscaping** | Element of CPTED. It serves to deter, detect, and delay intruders. It guides pedestrian traffic and marks the boundary between **public, semi-private, and private** space. |
| **Lighting** | Value it for its effect on deterrence, detection, and delay, and for its support of observation and response. |
| **Glass treatments** | According to the objective (e.g., mitigating explosions): films, glazing, tinting, and the type of glass. Also window treatments: blast curtains, blinds, and meshes. |

> **Exam note:** the book says barriers can be of "three formats," but does not list them there. From the rest of the chapter they appear to be structural, electronic, and human.

> **Exam note (outside the book):** a double-cylinder deadbolt requires a key to exit as well. It can conflict with life safety and egress codes; it is advisable to check local regulations.

#### 2.2.2 Electronic Security Measures

| Measure | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| **Surveillance** | Although listed as electronic, it can also be **natural** (Chapter 10, CPTED). E.g.: employees who watch the area around their post, or a space designed for human observation. Electronic surveillance is more than cameras: power, field of view, getting the signal to where it is needed, camera control, analytics, storage, maintenance, and scalability. |
| **Access control** | It is thought of as electronic, but can be natural, structural, human, or mixed/hybrid. Common electronic elements: card readers, keypads, biometric systems, and locks. Key: an access database and communication between reader, database (local or remote), and lock. Consider maintenance, database updating, configuration, and scalability. It evolves fast: facial recognition and smart cards. |
| **Intrusion detection systems** (IDS) | They usually include **sensors; alarms or annunciators; and a communication or transmission mechanism**. Match the IDS to its purpose, to the type of facility or space, and to the environment. **False positives and false negatives** are a major problem. A poor initial design or installation can leave it totally ineffective. |
| **Communications** | Two fronts: communicating with employees and occupants in routine and emergency; and protocols so that electronic systems talk to each other. Systems cited: cameras, monitors, recorders, sensors, alarms, card readers, databases, electronic locks, control centers, and receiving stations. Plan for power outages, telecommunications failures, IT (information technology) network failures, users competing for the channel, and other factors. |

#### 2.2.3 Personnel and Guard Force Security

| Measure | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| **Security officer force** | Design it carefully so that it is effective and efficient. Planning factors (9): staffing, selection, training, equipment, post orders, specific duties, quality control, quality assurance, and supervision. |
| **Other human support functions** | Other departments take part: facilities, maintenance, reception, legal, risk management, and IT. Employees throughout the organization may have security duties in addition to their own. Planning must include this personnel; it can influence the structural and electronic systems in the plan. |
| **Visitor management/control** | Written logs or automated systems; usually the task of officers or reception. Automated ones are **much more effective**. They integrate with incident management, access control, and investigations. Their cost has dropped greatly. Do not leave it for last. |
| **Badging** | Identification strategy for: employees and staff; contractors or other regular visitors; other users; and occasional or one-time visitors. In many organizations, especially government, a badge gives physical and also **logical** access to IT systems. This dual use requires close, ongoing coordination between physical security and IT security. |
| **Security operations centers** | Nerve centers of security information. Also called **control centers** and **fusion centers**. Take care with location, design, staffing, and use. They affect and are affected by the whole plan: structural measures, electronic systems, and the human element. |
| **Weapons** | Irrelevant in some environments; very important and even controversial in others. Topics: whether to arm officers and which tactics and tools the response force will use. Guiding questions: What would the response to a major incident look like? What will the response force do? What role will weapons have in the plan? |
| **Incident management systems** | Some see them as peripheral, but they are a **key input** to any risk assessment architecture. Uses below. |

Uses of an effective incident management system (5):

1. Guide the response to an incident in real time.
2. Support post-incident analysis and reduce recurrence.
3. Inform the planning of physical security improvements or program changes.
4. Evaluate the effect of the facility's design and layout on prevention and response.
5. Compare characteristics of incidents among similar facilities, according to their physical security features.

**Not everything depends on the security department**

| Measure | Possible owner |
| --- | --- |
| Landscaping and natural or terrain barriers | Facilities or building maintenance department |
| Construction materials and methods | General contractor or building owner/manager |
| Loading dock operations | Logistics or transportation function |

- Even so, physical measures must be coordinated with each security function and with all physical security planning.

### 2.3 Peripheral Systems and Interfaces

- In modern facilities it is critical to address the relationship between security components and peripherals.
- Peripherals according to the text (6): **life safety systems**, **building controls**, **IT infrastructure**, **liaison relationships**, **outsourced services**, and **policies and procedures**.

> **Exam note:** the text lists 6 peripherals; Figure 2.2 only 5 (it does not include policies and procedures).

| Peripheral | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Life safety systems | A major challenge in new construction, remodeling, or system upgrades: the **conflict between security and life safety systems**. National and international standards and local codes usually ease it. Sometimes compensating measures are needed; they must be recommended within the planning. |
| Building controls | Automatic elevator control; automatic or motion-activated lighting; climate control; automatic zone lockdowns; environmental monitoring (temperature, humidity, smoke, chemical, and other sensors). Like life safety, they usually interoperate with security management systems. Plan to avoid conflicts. |
| IT infrastructure | Shared or dedicated, it usually supports the electronic security systems. Assess its reliability and its security: "the security of the security system." |
| Liaison relationships, outsourced services, policies and procedures | They can greatly influence the operation and effectiveness of physical security components, separately or as a system. |

- All peripherals deserve close attention in any project.

### Key Terms

physical security · security · Physical Asset Protection Standard (ASIS PAP-2021) · Department of Homeland Security (DHS) · internal security · espionage, sabotage, damage, theft · realistic and credible threats · four Ds (deter, detect, delay, deny) · access control · deterrence · detection · assessment · delay · response · evidence gathering · physical protection system · false positives / false negatives · menu of options · building-block · layers of security · FIDS · IDS · LiDAR · CCTV · hard framework · barriers · human barrier · fencing · chain link · decorative fencing · no cut/no climb fencing · bollards (fixed / active / pop-up) · terrain · double-cylinder keyed deadbolt · CPTED · force multiplier · landscaping · public / semi-private / private space · glass treatments · glazing · blast curtains · natural surveillance · card readers · biometrics · access database · annunciators · security officer force · post orders · quality control / quality assurance · visitor management · badging · logical access · security operations center · control center · fusion center · response force · incident management system · peripheral systems and interfaces · life safety systems · building controls · zone lockdown · IT infrastructure · liaison relationships · outsourced services

## Capítulo 3 — Planning and Conducting Physical Security Assessments (págs. 27–52)

**Central idea:** No physical security project should start without understanding the risk: assets, threats, and vulnerabilities. The basic tool is the risk assessment or the security survey (security survey or physical security assessment). The chapter gives a general risk assessment framework and a more concrete one for the physical security survey: methodologies, areas to review, tests, report, and automated tools.

### Chapter Introduction (unnumbered in the book)

- It applies to any project: from the complete design of a system to the upgrade of a single component.
- Fit within ESRM (Enterprise Security Risk Management):
  1. Understand the context.
  2. Identify the assets.
  3. Identify and prioritize security risks.
- Basic tool: **security risk assessment** or **security survey**.
- **Patterson (2013):** no security program without first identifying the assets, their threats, and how vulnerable they are to each threat.
- Problem: there are a great many models. They vary by sector, region or country, specialty, and even by consultant.
- National and international standard models exist, but here they are of little use because they are too general.
- The chapter offers a **general framework** (not a concrete model) for the risk assessment and a **specific** framework for the security survey.
- In the book, **security survey = physical security assessment** (synonyms).

| Tool | Expected level of mastery | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Comprehensive security risk assessment | Somewhat familiar | General concept |
| Security survey | Intimately familiar | It is the basis of every physical security project. It is most of the fieldwork. It collects data and evidence that justify countermeasures |

### 3.1 General Risk Assessment Models and Considerations

**Steps of a comprehensive risk assessment:**

| # | Step |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Identify the assets and assign them a value (absolute or relative) |
| 2 | Assess the relevant threats against those assets |
| 3 | Assess the vulnerabilities (vulnerability assessment) |
| 4 | Consider the impact of a loss event: attack, theft, or property damage |
| 5 | Summarize and prioritize the risks found |
| 6 | Recommend mitigation measures and merge them into a comprehensive protection strategy |

**Likelihood (or probability) factor:**

- Many models include it. It is the probability that a loss event will occur in the future.
- It is based on:
  - historical data from the site;
  - history of similar events at similar companies;
  - makeup of the neighborhood and the immediate surroundings;
  - general geographic location;
  - political and social conditions;
  - changes in the economy;
  - other factors (the book leaves the list open).
- Examples from the book:
  - A company in a flood-prone or coastal area: more likelihood of flooding than one inland. It applies even if it has never flooded, because the location favors it.
  - A business with a crime history in and around the property: high likelihood of more crimes if it does not improve its security posture. It assumes the other factors (economic, social, political) remain constant.

**Conditions that increase assets' exposure to the risk of loss (ASIS ESRM Guideline 2019):**

| Category | Factors |
| --- | --- |
| **Physical** | Types and location of facilities or campus; surroundings; volume of pedestrian or vehicle traffic; access by nonemployees needed to operate; operational technology or industrial control systems needed; sensitivity and criticality of processes and assets on site |
| **Nonphysical** | Geopolitical landscape; culture; sector pressures; legal, regulatory, and compliance requirements; intensity of competition; the organization's mode of growth; speed of decision-making; willingness to adopt technology |
| **Logical** | Information and digital assets, plus the network or digital space that connects them with each other and with users and stakeholders. Examples: network infrastructure, connectivity, servers, workstations, other network devices, and endpoints |

**Cyclical and continuous process:**

- The elements change over time. Best example: the rapid evolution of technology, especially electronic security systems (see Ch. 11, “Electronic Security Systems”).
- Also changing, sometimes without notice: asset value, threat environment, state of vulnerabilities, likelihood calculations, and impact of loss events. They must be monitored.
- Best method for monitoring and preparing: an **ongoing risk assessment program** (Peterson, 2014).
- Adopting ESRM pushes the security program toward constant improvement.
- **ESRM cycle** (ASIS ESRM Guideline, 2019): identify and prioritize assets → identify and prioritize risks → mitigate the prioritized risks → continuous improvement.
- **Exam note:** in 3.1 the book words the cycle as if it were three steps (“assets and risks” together). In 3.2.5 it says mitigation is “one of four processes.” The four are those in the line above.

### 3.2 Qualitative and Quantitative Methods

- Each component of the assessment is valued qualitatively or quantitatively.
- The value of assets is usually expressed in money, but it cannot always be. Typical case: intangibles such as intellectual property or workforce stability.
- Even when preferring monetary figures, it is hard to arrive at a defensible number.
- What is usually most suitable is to **combine both**. It depends on the strategic level, on what the executive who decides wants, and on the style of the security professional.

|  | Qualitative analysis | Quantitative analysis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Definition | Any approach that does **not** use numbers to describe the components of risk | Any approach that uses **numerical measures** for the value of assets or the level (severity or likelihood) of threats, vulnerabilities, impact, or loss events |
| Typical scale | Comparative terms: critical, high, medium, low, negligible | From simple scales (e.g., 1 to 5) to statistical methods and mathematical formulas |
| When it applies | Basic security applications | Measuring the effectiveness of a physical protection system whose main functions are to detect, delay, and respond |
| Advantages | Simpler and faster. Its results are usually as meaningful as numerical ones | Executives prefer graphs and tables that summarize a lot of data. The data are processed automatically with programs and algorithms |

- **Blended approach**: combine elements of both when appropriate.
- In any case, the most important things are:
  1. clearly define each level or descriptor used;
  2. ensure the best possible quality of input data;
  3. display the information clearly: trends, curves, and ranges.
- **Exam note:** the book puts “and” after the second point, as if there were two. There are three.

#### 3.2.1 Assets

- Identifying assets is key to estimating the risk of a site.
- Difficulty: there is no agreement on what exactly an asset is.
- The security professional and the asset owner must agree **at the start** on what counts and what does not count as an asset.
- It is advisable to walk through the operating processes to detect assets. Many owners only know the ones that appear on the balance sheet.
- **Asset:** anything that has tangible or intangible value to the company.
  - It is valued and prioritized in relation to the company's mission and objectives.
  - The maturity of the valuation process depends on the available resources. Over time it matures and becomes an integral part of the organization.
- **Factors for setting an asset's value** (nonexhaustive list):
  1. Purchase or replacement cost.
  2. Operational impact of its being unavailable (usually identified with a business impact analysis).
  3. Effects of damage to the asset.
  4. Time needed to replace it.
  5. Reputational impact of its being unavailable.
- **Valuation:** quantitative (numerical measurements), qualitative (interviews, surveys, industry publications), or mixed.
- Direct monetary value ≠ total value. An asset can be worth little in money and a lot in accumulated value to the organization (ASIS ESRM Guideline, 2019).
- **Criticality:** the impact of loss measured in currency.
  - It depends on: the mission; the resources used to fulfill it; how those resources interact; and how the organization would cope with the loss or maintain business continuity.
- Ways to assign value: economic cost, reputation, value of the information, and regulatory compliance.
- **Costs to consider:** immediate response and recovery; investigation; replacement; and **indirect** costs (often forgotten).
- **Indirect costs (the book's 19):**
  1. Temporary leased facilities.
  2. Equipment rental or purchase.
  3. Alternate suppliers.
  4. Alternate carriers and logistics.
  5. Temporary warehouses.
  6. Special employee benefits.
  7. Psychological counseling and employee assistance.
  8. Loss of market share (temporary or permanent).
  9. Lower employee productivity.
  10. Higher insurance premiums.
  11. Temporary staff.
  12. Recruiting costs for the permanent workforce.
  13. More security spending (temporary or permanent).
  14. More communication capabilities.
  15. Data recovery and restart or reconfiguration of computer systems.
  16. Administrative support.
  17. More travel.
  18. Marketing and public relations.
  19. Redoing emergency and continuity plans.
- **Intangible and mixed assets:** hard to value, but they suffer loss events and can greatly damage vitality and the mission. Executives must be educated about them.
- **Two ways to quantify value:**
  - Relative value: number from 1 (low) to 5 (high) according to priority.
  - Cost-of-loss formula (Patterson 2013):

```latex
K = C_p + C_t + C_r + C_i - I
```

| Symbol | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| K | Total cost of loss |
| Cp | Permanent replacement cost |
| Ct | Temporary substitute cost |
| Cr | Total related costs: removing the old asset, installing the new one (open list in the book) |
| Ci | Lost income cost |
| I | Available insurance or indemnity. It is **subtracted** |

- Own example (outside the book): Cp = 10,000; Ct = 2,000; Cr = 1,000; Ci = 5,000; I = 8,000 → K = 10,000.

**Four criteria for ranking assets by criticality:**

| # | Criterion | What it measures |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Workforce | Number and type of personnel located on site |
| 2 | Service delivery | Percentage of total service delivery that depends on the asset |
| 3 | Dependencies | Importance of the asset to other assets of the organization or of the network |
| 4 | Mission/objectives | Overall importance of the asset to the business mission or objective, combining all factors |

- **Exam note:** in criterion 3 the book says “within the organization of the network.” From context it should be read as “organization or network.”

#### 3.2.2 Evaluating Threats

- **Threat, according to ASIS International 2020:** any verbal or physical conduct that conveys intent to cause physical harm or to instill fear of suffering it. It also counts if it is reasonably perceived that way.
- **Exam note:** that definition comes from the 2020 ASIS standard on workplace violence (Workplace Violence and Active Assailant Standard) and centers on people. Even so, the book includes natural threats. Later it speaks of inadvertent threats, which do not appear in its list. In practice there are three categories: manmade, natural, and inadvertent.

| Type | How it is assessed | Keys |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Manmade threats** (intentional) | By identifying and studying potential adversaries | Think beyond the obvious: employees, terrorist organizations, organized crime, aggressive competitors, and activist groups (e.g., environmentalists). Growing challenge because of the global economy, worldwide demographic changes, and the **asymmetric** nature of today's threats (less conventional and harder to define). Adversaries are judged by their **capabilities** and their **intentions** |
| **Natural threats** | With historical trends and statistics | Long-term data on climate and natural hazards by geographic area, terrain, and environment. Sometimes there are data by sector or type of facility. The unexpected tends to happen, so comprehensive contingency planning and some all-hazard preparedness are recommended |

**Sources of information on adversary capabilities and intentions (11):**

1. Prior history.
2. Adversary sequence diagrams: they reflect the penetration paths into a closed building.
3. Rhetoric of the organization.
4. Public statements.
5. Intelligence and other open sources.
6. Internal communications (newsletters, websites, blog posts).
7. Police reports.
8. Crime analysis.
9. Professional organizations.
10. Automated databases.
11. Threat assessment professionals.

#### 3.2.3 Vulnerabilities

- **Vulnerability, according to ASIS International 2017:** state of being susceptible to harm or injury.
- **Exam note:** the citation “ASIS International 2017” does not appear in the chapter's references.
- Common approach: measure them by **observability** and **exploitability**.

| Type of threat | Observability | Exploitability |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adversary (intentional) | The adversary's ability to see and identify the vulnerability. E.g.: a hole in a chain link fence is highly observable; a video camera that does not work is not | The adversary's ability to take advantage of the vulnerability once he or she knows it |
| Natural | **It is inverted:** the ability of security personnel to observe, detect, or track an approaching threat (e.g., a storm). Requires early warning and notification mechanisms | The natural threat's ability to damage the facility, the mission, or the organization |
| Inadvertent | The approach changes to two questions: Do security personnel know the vulnerabilities? Does the opportunity for a loss event depend on the nature of the mission, the operation, or the facility? | The same two questions |

- Inadvertent threats and their vulnerabilities are the **hardest** to identify and measure. Even so, they must not be neglected.
- With this approach, mitigation is planned for the long term (strategic) and the immediate (tactical).

#### 3.2.4 Risk Analysis

- Assets, threats, and vulnerabilities are combined. Then the potential impact is considered and prioritization is done according to the consequences of a loss event.
- The assessment levels (threat, vulnerability, impact) are best set **by committee**, especially in quantitative analyses.
- That committee is a multidisciplinary team of subject matter experts. That way the numbers are credible and justifiable.
- **Justifying the numbers** is where clients, executives, and decision makers question the assessor most.
- One possible formula:

```latex
Risk = (Threat \times Vulnerability \times Impact)^{1/3}
```

- Why it works:
  - It **multiplies** instead of adding. If any factor is 0, the risk is 0 (at that time and place).
  - Each factor is rated from **0 to 100**. It is easy to understand because it resembles a percentage.
  - The **cube root** returns the result to the 0–100 scale. That way it displays well in graphs.
- **Exam note:** the book says “cubed root”; the correct term is “cube root,” exponent 1/3. In the PDF the exponent appears loose (“1/3”) after the parenthesis.
- Own example (outside the book): T = 80, V = 50, I = 40 → 80 × 50 × 40 = 160,000 → cube root ≈ 54.3.
- Presentation: it must help the decision maker understand and decide. Risks are ordered by priority or into categories, to show what to address first according to the assessor.

#### 3.2.5 Risk Mitigation

- It is one of the **four processes of the ESRM cycle**.
- The approach is chosen according to the organization's goals and mission.
- It is not practical to attack all risks. Priority: those that can cause significant impact or damage to the mission.
- **Good practice:** appropriate technologies + structural and architectural elements + the human element + strong policy and procedure controls.
- **Security-related options:**
  1. Measures to reduce the risk of the event.
  2. Architectural improvements.
  3. Security systems.
  4. Policies and procedures.
  5. Management practices.
  6. Security staffing.
- **Other options:**
  - **Transfer** the financial risk: insurance or contract clauses (e.g., indemnification clauses in security service contracts).
  - **Accept** the risk as a cost of doing business.
- Some options will not be available because they are infeasible or too costly (in money or otherwise).
- Every option is evaluated for **availability, affordability, and feasibility** for the operation.
- Frequent consideration besides cost: does it substantially interfere with the operation?
- Cases from the book:
  - Store with shoplifting: closing it keeps out the thieves, but also the customers; it is infeasible. Alternative: electronic article surveillance with VHF (very high frequency) or microwave technology.
  - Business open to the public that tightens access control so much that it scares off customers: it loses business.
- Challenge: balancing a sound strategy with operational needs and with the **psychological impact** on the people affected.
- **When choosing countermeasures (Patterson, 2013):**
  1. The effectiveness of each countermeasure and of the complete system depends on the adversary and the threat.
  2. Different threats require different levels of countermeasure effectiveness and system performance.
  3. If the threat becomes more sophisticated, the effectiveness of countermeasures must rise; if not, the extra risk is managed by other means.

#### 3.2.6 Leveraging Outside Expertise

- It is usually advisable to add a well-chosen outside provider or consultant to the assessment.
- Advantages:
  - Fresh eyes, without prior opinions, prejudices, preferences, or internal politics.
  - Experience and up-to-date knowledge of best practices, products, and industry standards.
  - With more regulation and government oversight, an outside assessment can **strengthen the company's position in an audit**.
- Selection criteria: specialty, experience, professionalism, and **degree of independence**.
- There must be clear agreement among all parties on purpose, scope, and expected product.
- The consultant must design the system **from the start** of the project. If not, **scope creep** appears: small undocumented changes with a large impact on the project.

### 3.3 Physical Security Assessments

- According to the ESRM guideline, risks are identified and assessed with a risk assessment process.
- The chosen methodology must **align with the organization's other risk frameworks and tools**.
- It must examine:
  1. general and specific threats;
  2. vulnerabilities;
  3. criticality of the assets;
  4. potential consequences;
  5. likelihood;
  6. severity;
  7. velocity.
- It must cover the risks to all relevant assets, whether **inherent or residual**.
- (Outside the book) Inherent risk: before applying controls. Residual risk: what remains after the controls. Velocity: how quickly a risk produces its impact.
- The physical security assessment or security survey is the tool for the risk to **physical (tangible)** assets.

#### 3.3.1 What Is a Security Survey?

- The concept of **adequacy** is weighing more and more. Reason: negligent security litigation is growing.
- Around the world, courts and regulators increasingly decide what is and is not adequate security.

**Two useful definitions:**

| Source | Definition (paraphrase) |
| --- | --- |
| ASIS International, Physical Asset Protection Standard (2021): “Security assessment (survey)” | Thorough physical examination of a facility, its systems, and procedures. It serves to evaluate the current level of security, locate deficiencies, and gauge the degree of protection needed |
| Momboisse (1977), classic definition cited by Broder (2012) | Critical **on-site** examination to learn the current state of security, identify **deficiencies or excesses**, determine the protection needed, and recommend improvements for the overall security of the operation |

- **Purposes of the security survey** (thorough on-site examination):
  1. Determine and document the current security posture.
  2. Identify deficiencies **and excesses** in existing measures.
  3. Compare the current posture with the appropriate level of security or protection.
  4. Recommend improvements to the overall situation.
- Done well, it benefits the professional (often together with a design team) and the client. Result: more security (less risk) for occupants and users.

**Key difference:**

| Comprehensive risk assessment | Security survey |
| --- | --- |
| Focuses **equally** on assets, threats, vulnerabilities, and consequences | Puts **more emphasis on vulnerabilities** |
| — | Its appraisal component consists of developing and communicating improvement recommendations |

**Factors to review when considering vulnerabilities (Patterson 2013):**

1. Lack of redundancy or backup in critical functions or systems.
2. Single points of failure.
3. Collocation of critical systems, organizations, or components.
4. Insufficient response capability to recover from an attack.
5. Ease of the aggressor's access to the facility.
6. Insufficient security measures.
7. Presence of hazardous materials.
8. Possible collateral damage from other businesses in the area.

**Cost-benefit analysis:** usually part of the assessment.

- Estimate the real cost of implementing the program.
- Compare it with the projected impact: loss reduction, financial savings, acquisition, life cycle, replacement, or other measures.
- Example (Patterson 2013): it makes no sense to spend **$100,000** on equipment to prevent the theft of a **$1,000** item. Better to insure it or move it to a safer place.

#### 3.3.2 Choosing a Physical Security Assessment Methodology

- There are almost infinite methodologies. Certain general protocols give **consistency and repeatability**.
- Methodologies can be used alone or combined.

**Two perspectives on topics to review in a survey:**

| Perspective 1 | Perspective 2 |
| --- | --- |
| CPTED (Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design) | Site topography |
| Access control | Monitoring |
| Intrusion detection systems | Access points |
| Security officer force | Building layout and construction |
| Communications | Blast protection |
| Emergency plans | Lighting |
| Identification approach | Surveillance systems |

- **Checklists:** they help avoid forgetting key elements and give the survey a sequence. But they must **not** be the center or the entirety of the assessment.

**Methodologies:**

|  | Outside-Inward | Inside-Outward | Functional / Security Discipline |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Team's role | **Adversary** (perpetrator). It is a penetration test | **Defender** (security professional) | Analyst of each discipline |
| Path | From outside (where the public circulates freely) → outer perimeter (fence, natural barrier, building wall, or other barrier) → outer, middle, and inner layers → asset | From the asset or target → toward the outer perimeter | Each function or discipline separately; then the findings are cross-checked |
| Question at each layer | “How do I defeat or evade this layer's measures and advance toward the asset?” | How do the measures operate, how effective are they, how do they contribute to deter-detect-delay-deny, and how can the layer be improved? | — |
| Recommendations | “What measure would stop me or slow me so much that I would abandon the attack?” | Several solutions per layer: architectural elements, structural security, CPTED, electronic security systems, officer support, policies and procedures (open list in the book) | — |
| Extras | Considers the criminal behavior of the surroundings, including actual crimes outside the facility. Thinks like the adversary against the 4 Ds: deter, detect, delay, deny | Analyzes **interdependencies** in each layer. E.g.: “How would a power outage affect it?” or “If a master key is stolen, how many layers does the intruder cross before being stopped or detected?” | Integrate with environmental factors, peripheral risks (neighbors), and policies and procedures |

**Layers of security (examples valid for outside-inward and inside-outward), from the inside out:**

| # | Layer | Typical measures |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Asset/target | Sensors, alarms, surveillance |
| 2 | Container, safe, or vault | Walls or sides, locks, sensors, alarms |
| 3 | Controlled or restricted area | Walls, doors, locks, surveillance, sensors |
| 4 | Security desk or entry control point | Security officer, turnstile, access control |
| 5 | Building perimeter | Walls, doors, locks, surveillance, sensors |
| 6 | Secure compound or campus | Surveillance, distance, lighting, patrolling |
| 7 | Property perimeter or fence line | Sensors, entry control, patrols, barriers |
| 8 | Neighborhood or surroundings | Police patrols, neighborhood watch, observation |

- They are only examples. The actual layers depend on the type of facility and the level of security. A shopping mall differs greatly from a government facility or a university campus.

**Disciplines of the functional method (in the book's order):**

- Security Architecture and Engineering (Ch. 7).
- Structural Security Measures (Ch. 9): barriers; locking mechanisms; lighting.
- Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design (Ch. 10).
- Electronic Security Systems (Ch. 11): access control systems; physical intrusion detection systems; surveillance systems; communication and annunciation systems; security of the electronic security systems on an IT backbone.
- Security Officers and the Human Element (Ch. 12).
- Almost all approaches are conceptually consistent. They differ in details of method or style.
- Conclusion: protocols must be **systematic, consistent, and repeatable**, even if the same model is not used in every case.

**Security Analysis Approaches (analysis tools):**

- Fishbone diagrams, Five Whys, MoSCoW method, and more (open list in the book).
- **SWOT** (strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, threats): complements the preceding approaches.
  - It originated in business management and adapts easily to security.
  - Walker (2021): business situational analysis; strategic assessment of key internal and external factors.

|  | Positive attributes | Negative attributes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Internal** (building, campus, facility, or security program) | Strengths | Weaknesses |
| **External** (may or may not be under the control of the professional, the organization, or the owner or manager) | Opportunities | Threats |

- **Figure 3.2 (SWOT Analysis Diagram):** 2×2 grid. Top row: Strengths and Weaknesses. Bottom row: Opportunities and Threats. Labels “Positive Attributes” and “Negative Attributes.”
- **Pending:** the figure's labels come out with a font encoded in the PDF; “Positive/Negative Attributes” was deciphered from the illegible text. In the figure's text there are no internal/external labels; that split comes from the body of the chapter.
- **“Threat” in SWOT** is not necessarily a security threat (terrorist or criminal). It can be the adverse effect of a power or network outage on security systems, or the risk of many false alarms in an intrusion detection system. It can also be a security threat: both senses must be distinguished.
- **“Opportunity”:** e.g., a new security technology becoming available, or the possibility of the company entering a new market.
- Usefulness: thinking creatively about how to do the assessment or how to structure a security program. More detail in POA (Protection of Assets): Business Principles.
- **Exam note:** the text refers to “Figure 3-2” and the caption says “Figure 3.2.” The chapter has no Figure 3.1.

### 3.4 General Guidelines – Areas to Assess

- These are general guidelines; they do not apply to every facility.
- **General team sequence:**
  1. Assess the surroundings, the neighbors, and possible peripheral risks.
  2. Walk the perimeter: fence lines, barriers, and gates (vehicle, rail, and pedestrian).
  3. Inspect exterior enclosures, power sources, storage tanks, boiler rooms, and storage of flammable liquids or hazardous materials (HAZMAT).
  4. Also inspect utility connections, similar spaces, building exteriors, rooftops, tunnels, and underground accesses.

**Rings of protection, typical case:**

| Ring | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| 1st (outer) | Property perimeter |
| 2nd (middle) | Building perimeter |
| 3rd (inner) | Internal controls |

#### 3.4.1 Typical Areas and Items to Assess

- **It is not a checklist:** it is a guide to possible areas, items, and topics.
- Each item is evaluated for: suitability to the situation, age, operability, maintenance, interoperability, aesthetics, and consistency with the current use of the space.

| Area | Items to review |
| --- | --- |
| **Barriers** | Gates; fences and fence top guards; bollards; other access barriers; natural barriers |
| **Doors** | Construction (wood, metal, glass, solid core; open list in the book) and direction of swing; frames; proper use of **astragals** (molding on the edge of the door that covers the gap between two leaves); hinges and hinge pins; locks and bolt travel (lock-throws) |
| **Windows** | Accessibility from ground level; materials and glazing; locks; frames; protection (bars, meshes, grilles; open list) |
| **Other Openings** (accessibility and protection) | Manholes; skylights; roof hatches; ventilation or air-conditioning grilles and ducts; penthouses and access to penthouse, roof, or terrace; sidewalk grates |
| **Locks** | Types suited to the application (quality and level of security; master-keyed; interchangeable cores); sufficiency; maintenance; key and card control protocol (responsibility and policy; records and inventory; key recovery procedures; change when appropriate, e.g., due to turnover of key personnel or after a theft or break-in) |
| **Safes and Containers** | Type and rating suited to the use; control of combinations and keys; alarms (if applicable); location and visibility; weight and anchoring (how easy they are to lift and carry out) |
| **Lighting** | Sufficiency (type, beam direction, controls); backup and emergency lighting; wiring (integrity and protection); inspections and maintenance |
| **Alarm (Physical Intrusion Detection) Systems** | Contracts (competitive price; up to date; in the facility's best interest; liability or hold harmless clauses if applicable; cancellation clauses); monitoring (central station; direct to police or fire department; local security personnel); response; sensors (type, range, coverage, maintenance, updates); signal integrity; certification (e.g., UL approval, Underwriters Laboratories); access authorizations; inspection, maintenance, and testing |
| **Electronic Security Systems (Other)** | Access control (hardware and software; database management; application and location; maintenance and inspection; scalability); video surveillance; correct equipment and application or planning (transmission; monitoring and storage; system management; maintenance and testing); mass communication and notification systems; watch tour |
| **Security Officer Services** | Staffing, schedules, and shifts; duties and tasks (patrol, fixed post, inspection, access control; open list); requirements, screening, training, and licenses; initial and recurrent training, and exercises; incident management system and automated visitor management; equipment, uniforms, and personal gear (including communications); supervision and quality assurance |
| **Vehicle, Traffic, and Parking Controls** | Entry and exit traffic patterns; parking areas; vehicle registration and control |
| **Protection of Utilities** (electricity, oil, gas, water, and the like) | Location and physical protection; access control; backup or emergency sources; protection of telecommunications and data lines |
| **Visitor Management** | Entry and exit points for visitors, delivery people, and contractors; identification systems (cards, passes; open list); entry and exit log, automated or manual; monitoring of entries and exits; escort procedures |
| **Package/Mail Handling** | Control and protection (incoming and outgoing); location of the mailroom and its access control; equipment and instructions for protection and inspection; receiving and distribution procedures; spot checks |

**Signage:** standardized, easy to read and understand, visible (unobstructed), and at eye level. It must also:

1. use images and letters large enough to be read from a distance;
2. use colors that stand out against the surroundings;
3. be well lit;
4. be kept clean and clear;
5. be in the right place according to its relevance (entrances, areas).

#### 3.4.2 Tests

- The survey may include tests of certain systems and procedures.
- They must be fully coordinated with the building owner or manager and, if applicable, with outside agencies (first responders, alarm monitoring services).

| Test | What is done |
| --- | --- |
| Shipping and receiving | Physically observe selected shipments (incoming and outgoing) and check them against bills of lading or inventory records |
| Alarms | Activate an intrusion alarm; evaluate the response and the reaction of occupants and officers |
| Computer/server room security | Attempt to gain access during working and nonworking hours to test security and access controls |
| General access controls | Attempt to enter the facility and internal areas during normal hours and outside them. See whether it succeeds and whether employees later challenge the “intruders” |

- **Exam note:** in the shipping test the book says “bills of lading of inventory records.” From context it is “or.”

### 3.5 Applying Assessment Results

- The report must give the **risk owner** enough understanding of the risk and of how it can affect the mission.
- The deepest study is useless if the results are not reported or applied well.

**Five criteria of a good report (Broder, 2012):**

| # | Criterion |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Accuracy |
| 2 | Clarity |
| 3 | Conciseness |
| 4 | Timeliness |
| 5 | Slant or pitch |

- The first four are straightforward. **Slant/pitch** is the tone of the report:
  - Courteous; without being picky or focusing on minor details.
  - Without a “gotcha” tone that highlights how bad the posture or strategy is.
  - Centered on **how to improve** and **why it matters**.
  - Tailored to its audience, but useful both to senior management and to those who will implement.
- **Roles:** the risk owner is responsible for mitigation and owner of the plan. The security team advises and supports.
- Highlight the **positive**: that way what works is not changed or eliminated, and best practices and effective measures are pointed out.
- **Exam note:** the book repeats that paragraph twice in a row (editing erratum).
- Assessments are not paper exercises or box-checking formalities (square fillers).
- ASIS International Risk Assessment Standard (2015): the risk assessment is the **cornerstone** for deciding with information how to address uncertainty in achieving objectives.
- Solutions must be orchestrated thoughtfully. They must not be a reaction to a loss event or to attractive electronic systems promoted by vendors.
- Uses beyond specific projects (renovations, designs, system upgrades):
  - Large organizations: a coordinated assessment program benefits the entire company.
  - Small organizations: review and compare reports periodically to inform management and generate improvement ideas.
- **Automated incident management system:** a tool for organizing and sharing reports.
  - McIlravey (2014): every risk assessment architecture has components that depend on incident management data (historical and empirical).
  - Those data include: history of loss events; threat frequency analysis; single and annual loss expectancy; impact assessment.
  - Storing survey reports in the same database improves filing, searching, and correlation with daily incidents and investigations.
- **Annual loss expectancy** according to the book:

```latex
ALE = \text{Cost of incident impact} \times \text{Frequency of occurrence}
```

- (Outside the book) The usual form is ALE = SLE × ARO: single loss expectancy times annualized rate of occurrence. Own example: a $5,000 incident that occurs 4 times a year → ALE = $20,000.
- Summary: the value of an assessment grows with reports, dissemination, good use of results, and updating. It applies to internal and external (consultant) assessments.
- Objective: turn the results into **actionable intelligence**, immediate and long-term, and not leave them gathering dust on a shelf.

### 3.6 Automated Assessment Tools

- There are many on the market. The key word is **assist**: the software must not be the only element.
- Main problem: many do not handle intangible factors or hard-to-quantify information well. Example: the **character or nature** of a risk.
- Peter Drucker (1970), on long-term planning: it is not enough to assess the **magnitude** of the risk; above all its **character** matters. It applies equally to physical security risks.

| Advantages | Disadvantages (arguments against) |
| --- | --- |
| They store, process, and manipulate large volumes of data | People without asset protection knowledge may believe that buying and installing software is enough to produce a valid assessment |
| They compare related data and project the benefit of different protection options | Commercial software may be too expensive for some organizations |
| Useful when there are many similar assessments or several complex ones | It may be unnecessarily complex |
| Systematic, repeatable process: similar results across assessments | It may not consider nonquantifiable characteristics with great weight in risk, such as the personality or culture of the organization |
| Assessments are kept and compared with earlier studies | They do not always cover all security risks |

- **Examples from the book (US):**
  - Federal Protective Service: uses software to assess hundreds of federal government facilities.
  - Interagency Security Committee: federal body created by **Presidential Executive Order 12977** to set security standards for federal facilities. It uses different software.
  - (Outside the book) Executive Order 12977 dates from 1995, after the Oklahoma City bombing.
- **When they are useful:** **low-risk** scenarios, or when the assessor knows little about security risk and wants to set a **baseline** according to best practices.
- Conclusion: the user must know how to calculate risk as a function of **threat, vulnerability, and consequence**.
- That requires a professional who understands the site's threats, the vulnerabilities to them, and the consequences of not mitigating them.
- Key to effective use: trained professional + credible, reproducible, and defensible assessment process + the tool's technology.

### Key Terms
