security risk assessment · security survey · physical security assessment · ESRM cycle · asset · intangible asset · mixed asset · asset owner · business impact analysis · criticality · indirect costs · cost-of-loss formula · loss event · likelihood · threat · manmade threats · natural threats · inadvertent threats · asymmetric threats · adversary sequence diagram · all-hazard preparedness · vulnerability · observability · exploitability · qualitative analysis · quantitative analysis · blended approach · risk analysis · subject matter experts · cube root · risk mitigation · risk transfer · risk acceptance · indemnification clause · electronic article surveillance · countermeasures · outside expertise · scope creep · inherent risk · residual risk · velocity · adequacy · negligent security litigation · deficiencies and excesses · appraisal · single point of failure · collocation · cost-benefit analysis · checklist · outside-inward methodology · inside-outward methodology · functional (security discipline) methodology · penetration test · deter-detect-delay-deny · layers of security · rings of protection · interdependencies · SWOT analysis · fishbone diagram · Five Whys · MoSCoW method · astragal · lock-throw · master-keyed · interchangeable core · hold harmless clause · central station · watch tour · mass notification · spot checks · bills of lading · risk owner · accuracy, clarity, conciseness, timeliness, slant or pitch · square fillers · automated incident management system · single loss expectancy · annual loss expectancy · actionable intelligence · automated assessment tools · baseline security · Federal Protective Service · Interagency Security Committee · Executive Order 12977

## Capítulo 4 — Measuring Effectiveness: Concepts in Physical Security Metrics (págs. 53–76)

**Central idea:** Metrics turn the physical security program into data that can be compared over time. They show status, trends, and return on investment (ROI) to management. They only work if they measure a sound process, have an interested consumer, and are planned before data are collected.

### 4.1 Understanding Metrics

- Starting motto: what is not measured cannot be managed.
- Context: tight budgets and more regulation. Management demands hard data and ROI to justify funds and resources.
- The budget limits the design of the physical security system.
- Performance metrics serve to:
  - show the status of the program;
  - identify performance trends;
  - demonstrate ROI or value-added, within the existing corporate culture.
- They are an important part of an ESRM (Enterprise Security Risk Management) program (Allen & Loyear, 2018).
  - Senior management or the risk owner must always know two things: how security helps protect the business and its mission, and what the current level of risk is.

**Definitions by Rathburn (2009)**

| Term | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Metric | A measure based on a reference with at least two points (e.g., quantity over time). Its main goal is to give insight into performance and operations. |
| Security | Protection from danger, or absence of danger. A security metric indicates the degree of security against a reference point. It also indicates what to do to avoid danger. |

- Metrics measure the effectiveness and efficiency of operations over time.

**Limits and challenges of metrics**

- If the organization has not suffered harm or been the target of attacks, success is hard to measure.
- Williams & Schultz (2016): the emphasis on metrics is sometimes poorly focused.
  - Risk: executing a bad process well. The numbers look good, but they measure an immature or broken process.
  - Example: IT boasts of having cleaned 17,000 viruses, but 5,000 got past it because of a deficient process or scope.
- Lesson: know what is measured, the quality of those metrics, and what is NOT measured.
- **Exam note:** the text cites William and Schultz (2016); in the references it appears as Williams, T. and Schultz, T. (2016), *Metrics and the Maturity Mindset*.

#### 4.1.1 Benefits of a Security Metrics Program

- Metrics make it possible to:
  - compare the effectiveness of the different components of the program;
  - measure the performance of a specific system, product, or process;
  - evaluate the response capability of security personnel.
- Properly applied, what is learned helps the security professional to (10 benefits):
  1. Better understand performance.
  2. Identify potential risks within the program.
  3. Detect problems and broken internal processes.
  4. Measure internal compliance with the organization's policy.
  5. Make better use of the security system's current capabilities.
  6. Measure the program against established references (benchmarks).
  7. Improve accountability.
  8. Communicate program performance.
  9. Drive performance improvement.
  10. Justify the allocation of resources.

#### 4.1.2 Designing a Metrics Program

- Order: first management understands what metrics are and how they are applied. Then the elements of the program that will yield useful metrics are chosen.
- Planning at the start avoids a lot of frustration later.
- Main tool: the measurement framework. It guides what performance means and what leads to sustainable success (Frost, 2007).

**Research sponsored by the ASIS Foundation**

- Work: *Persuading Senior Management with Effective, Evaluated Security Metrics* (Ohlhausen, Poore, McGarvey & Anderson, 2014). Free download at www.asisonline.org.
- Three products:
  1. **Security MET (Security Metrics Evaluation Tool):** self-administered tool to develop, evaluate, and improve metrics.
  2. **Library of metric descriptions,** each evaluated with the Security MET criteria.
  3. **Guidelines for effective use of metrics** to inform and persuade senior management, with emphasis on organizational risk and ROI.
- Purpose of the Security MET: identify the strengths and weaknesses of a metric so the weaknesses can be corrected.

| Security MET criteria group | Criteria (9 in total) |
| --- | --- |
| Technical Criteria | reliability · validity · generalizability |
| Operational (Security) Criteria | cost · timeliness · manipulation |
| Strategic (Corporate) Criteria | return on investment (ROI) · organizational relevance · communication |

- For each criterion, the MET offers: definition, illustration of the concept, behavioral summary scale, and sample applications.
- It includes a score sheet. A low score on a criterion indicates where to improve the metric.

**Enterprise Performance Framework (Frost, 2007) — Figure 4.1**

| Idea | Question it answers |
| --- | --- |
| Effectiveness | How well do systems and programs operate? E.g., at detecting, delaying, or annunciating. |
| Efficiency | How fast do programs respond to alarms, incidents, and customer requests? |
| Strategic improvement | How well aligned are security objectives with the organization's objectives? |

- Labels of Figure 4.1 (text recovered from a poorly encoded font):
  - Top goal: Sustained Quality Performance.
  - Operational Performance (managing today's business): Effectiveness → How do we accomplish the mission?; Efficiency → How are we operating?
  - Strategic Performance (preparing for future business): Strategic Initiatives → How do we prepare for tomorrow?
- **Pending:** exact arrangement of boxes and arrows in Figure 4.1.
- **Exam note:** the text calls the third idea Strategic improvement; the figure labels it Strategic Initiatives.

**Evaluating each element of the program**

- After choosing the framework, each element of the program is evaluated from its core functions. Examples from the book: physical security, personnel security, information protection, and emergency management.
- Two questions per element:
  1. What primary and secondary functions does it perform? They usually appear in the unit's mission statement.
  2. With what procedures, processes, tools, and resources does it carry them out?
- Example with the physical security program:
  - Function: protect assets by coordinating and integrating everything needed. Goal: create, maintain, and improve the capability to prevent, prepare for, mitigate, and respond to unauthorized access.
  - Tools and resources:

| Resource | Examples |
| --- | --- |
| Systems | intrusion detection systems, access control, CCTV (closed-circuit television), visitor management |
| Personnel | professional support staff (contractor and department), security officer force, other full-time employees |
| Regulations | compliance requirements; federal, state, and local regulations; policies and standards |

**Two principles for deciding which metrics to collect**

| Principle | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Start small | Security data are messy and there is no easy way to organize them. That is why few departments collect data, and even fewer act on them (McIlravey, 2009). Modern software can already collect and sort data. A few initial data points show the effort the full program will require. |
| Collect relevant data | Is the information useful to anyone? Some metrics only interest the manager and his or her team, and must not be circulated. It must pass the *who cares?* test (Rathburn, 2009). Without a consumer with a defined need, the metric only adds noise. |

- A metrics program wastes resources if the measures are not defined, bounded, collected, analyzed, and applied well.
- That is why it should only be created after serious introspection and planning.

**SMART metrics (Payne, 2006)**

| Letter | Criterion | What it requires |
| --- | --- | --- |
| S | Specific | It matters to someone and gives him or her clear, actionable intelligence. |
| M | Measurable | The formula gives a quantifiable value; preferably a whole number or a percentage. |
| A | Attainable | If gathering it takes too much time, it loses effectiveness. It may be better to use that time to fix known problems. |
| R | Repeatable | If it is not produced consistently and uniformly, it cannot be compared with anything, not even with itself. |
| T | Time-dependent | It is always collected over the same periods (day, week, month); otherwise it gives inaccurate results. |

- **Exam note:** in Payne's SMART, R = Repeatable and T = Time-dependent. In the classic management SMART they are Relevant and Time-bound (outside the book).

### 4.2 Physical Security Metrics

- Three main components of the physical security program: systems, personnel and compliance.

#### 4.2.1 Physical Security Systems Metrics

- Applies, e.g., to the PACS (physical access control system) and video surveillance.
- Useful perspective: operational efficiency.
- The SOC (security operations center) monitors and responds to alarms, among other tasks.
- An inefficient system floods the SOC with alarms and notices. The SOC does not respond in time and, over time, the alarms become noise.
- Goal of the metrics: reduce the noise on the operator's screen so that only legitimate alarms and notices get through.
- Modern PACS and video systems generate many user-defined alarms and system notices, with reports by alarm type.

**What can be measured? Common alarm types**

| Alarm | Definition | Causes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forced door | Entry through a controlled door without authorization or without an authorized device. | 1) User error: nighttime office cleaning staff who enter with a physical key instead of the tenant's card. 2) Malicious intent: someone forces their way in (e.g., kicking the door). 3) System device malfunction (hard to diagnose; frequent at the start of the program): faulty door position switch; faulty exit device; misaligned door; system programming error. |
| Door held open | The predefined bypass time of the position switch expires. The PACS considers the door held or propped open. | 1) User error: opens with a valid card and props the door so others can pass, typical at events with visitors without cards. Also out of courtesy, by holding it for someone else and exceeding the bypass time. 2) System device malfunction (hard to diagnose; common at the start): misaligned position switch; misaligned door; error when programming the bypass duration. |
| Unauthorized access attempts | Due to user error, programming error or malfunction. Useful as a metric to diagnose the system. Also as an investigation tool for patterns by location or by person. | 1) Card misread: generated by the user; frequent when migrating from a 125 kHz proximity card to a smart card. The user does not hold the card long enough. At a turnstile it is usually followed by a forced door alarm: the user presents the card and keeps walking. 2) Card expired: the cardholder ignores the renewal notices. 3) Unauthorized access: tries to enter an unauthorized area. Either their privileges are incorrectly programmed, or someone is trying to enter improperly; in that case, when, where and why are investigated. |
| User-defined actions/alarms | Order the PACS to take another action after an alarm (e.g., activating entry X unlocks door Y). They are the focus of SOC operators; they carry detailed information and response instructions. | Defined actions that are announced on screen are noise: the system only executes an action. A system report makes it possible to change how they are recorded and announced. |
| Communications failure | A component does not communicate with another within a set time. Occurs in PACS and CCTV. In PACS: reader ↔ control panel and panel ↔ PACS server. In video: network outage at a device or between it and the VMS (video management system) interface. | 1) Network availability: network outage; greatly affects the ability to monitor alarms. It usually cuts PACS server ↔ local panel; resolved by coordinating with IT. 2) Cabling/wiring issues: cable shorted against metal, cut, or with interference from nearby cables or power lines. Slow diagnosis and laborious repair; often requires new cable. |

- **Exam note:** the book says 13.53 MHz for the smart card. The standard frequency is 13.56 MHz (outside the book). It also writes KHz; the correct form is kHz.

**Examples of system metrics (reports from a real PACS, weeks of June–July 2021)**

- All the charts come from the same initial report. Each one adds actionable information.

| Figure | What it shows | Lesson |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4.2 *Total Forced Doors by Week* | Weekly total of forced doors, weeks 6/7/21 to 6/28/21 (axis up to 20,000). | Is it good or bad? Without a baseline, the totals give no useful information; more detail is needed. |
| 4.3 *Total Forced Doors and Turnstiles by Week* | Separates card reader doors, turnstiles and total. Most alarms come from turnstiles and drop sharply in the last week. | The turnstiles required a card on entry and exit. An executive decision removed card exit, but free egress mode was not programmed. Every exit generated an alarm. Once corrected, only the entry side alarms. |
| 4.4 *Forced Door Details by Location and Week* | Removes the turnstiles and shows the doors with the most alarms per week (axis 0–4,000): NY 9th Flr S Lobby Entrance; Jersey City 16th Flr Main Entrance; NY 10th Flr SE Entrance; HQ Mail Room Door; Chicago North Entry Door; Houston Equipment Rm. | Ideally the cause is found by reviewing video of the door with a reader. Sometimes a technician has to be sent. |
| 4.5 *Total Held Open Doors by Week* | Sharp drop in the last week because of a change in the calculation (axis up to 5,000; dates 6/7/21 to 7/3/21). | In the first 4 weeks, every state change of the position switch was counted. Video showed innocent errors. The alarm was reprogrammed to trigger only after 15 minutes. This located doors left open for long periods (training sessions, ventilation) and measures were taken per facility. |
| 4.6 *Total Unauthorized Access Attempts by Type and Week* | Classes: All Card Rejects, Expired, Access Privilege and Card Misread (axis 0–2,000), weeks 6/7/21 to 6/28/21. | Each class requires investigating the cause: user error, malfunction or malicious intent. |

- **Pending:** exact values of the bars in Figures 4.2 to 4.6; only axes and labels can be read.

#### 4.2.2 Physical Security Personnel Metrics

- They measure the human element of the program (e.g., the guard force).
- They indicate the appropriate spending and the number and type of personnel needed to operate effectively.
- They make it possible to see program growth, cost increases, efficiency gains and output costs.
- In sum, they give a view of the resources needed to meet goals and mission. More detail in Chapter 12 (Security Officers and the Human Element).
- First, the objectives of the human element within the program and how to measure them are defined.
- Two measurable categories: response and training.

**A. Response**

*1) Response to an alarm or incident.* It may already exist as a performance requirement in the guard contract or the integrator's contract. Two ways to apply it:

| Form | How it is measured |
| --- | --- |
| Guard force response | A stopwatch is enough. It is done within a contract surveillance plan. The COR (contracting officer representative) triggers an alarm; e.g., a duress alarm in the director's office. It measures how long the appropriate response takes (in person, call attempt). It is compared with the contract requirements (baseline requirements). This measures operational efficiency and suggests improvements. |
| Security systems integrator response | The integrator's contract sets performance requirements or SLAs (service level agreements). E.g.: time limit to start an installation after the task order or work order is issued. The best method to identify and resolve problems is a single contractor responsible for the whole system. The data are reported every month to the COR as a contract deliverable: each service call, location, duration, response time and other data. |

- Figure 4.7 (*Contractor Response Time by Completion Status and Month*): hypothetical integrator response requirement for service requests. It shows % completed and % not completed before 5 p.m. the next business day, from 6/20 to 5/21. Most responses met the deadline.
- Figure 4.8 (*Actual Contractor Response Time by Completion Status and Month*): another case, with a required response time of four hours.
- Audiences for these data: department heads, senior management, third parties and regulatory authorities.
- **Exam note:** the text cites Figure 4-8 twice; the first mention (results of the last year) corresponds to 4.7. Both figures carry the same title (*Service Calls Response Within 24 Hours*) and the same 5 p.m. next-business-day legend. However, 4.8 is described with 4 hours. It also says contact where it should say contract.
- **Pending:** monthly percentages in Figures 4.7 and 4.8 (not legible in the text).

*2) Response to customer requests.*

- It is one of the most important metrics any office can monitor.
- It directly affects its reputation (an intangible asset). Therefore, also its ability to obtain resources and fulfill the mission.
- It requires adequate technology. There are industry systems that measure and chart it well, but they usually require other areas to buy and implement them, with additional funds.
- Alternative: IT has long used this technology. By collaborating with IT, security can implement it with minimal cost and impact on the customer.
- How IT's customer service software works:
  - The request arrives online or by phone; it receives a tracking number and a time stamp.
  - A representative resolves it on the spot or routes it to the appropriate support team.
- Typical reports (the book clarifies there are more): number of requests; average response time; average resolution time; type of request; periods of high and low volume; most frequent requests.
  - Also: who resolved, how many each person processed and of what type. Useful for sizing resources and distributing the workload.
- Physical security elements that can be integrated: problems with access cards; requests for access to restricted areas; lost or stolen cards; key requests.
- Advantages of integrating them into the existing customer service process (one-stop shop model):
  - the employee visits fewer places to resolve a matter;
  - less time lost and lower local travel expenses;
  - greater employee satisfaction;
  - better understanding among IT, physical security and other areas;
  - helps security management detect process inefficiencies.

**B. Training Metrics** — developed from two perspectives:

| Perspective | Scope | Metrics |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Inward-facing | Training required of the security staff themselves: annual performance plan, job requirements, contract or professional development. | Baseline of 100%. The % achieved at the close of the period can count toward the employee's annual evaluation. Others: % of staff who meet all requirements; average score on end-of-course evaluations; professional development objectives achieved; proficiency demonstrated in exercises. |
| Outward-facing | Training that the security office designs and delivers to employees or other audiences. Topics: policies and procedures, IT security, handling of sensitive information, privacy rules, emergency procedures, business ethics, workplace violence prevention and travel safety. | Training needs identified; courses developed; courses delivered; total % of students who attend. Baseline of 100% in each to detect areas for improvement. E.g.: a low value in courses delivered may indicate a lack of resources to deliver them. |

- Numerical example from the book (inward-facing):

```latex
\text{Completion rate} = \frac{9 \text{ courses completed}}{15 \text{ courses required}} \times 100 = 60\%
```

- **Customer satisfaction survey:**
  - Security collaborates with IT to host and manage the survey on an electronic site.
  - It is distributed, e.g., with a link in selected emails from the security staff.
  - Customers give anonymous feedback on the service received.
  - It is tabulated weekly, monthly and quarterly to see areas for improvement and areas of success.

#### 4.2.3 Physical Security Compliance Metrics in the Public Sector

- Government agencies' physical security programs must comply with various regulations and requirements of their own.

**Compliance of Facilities**

- Requirement for US federal facilities: conduct a security assessment (facility security assessment) at each site under the agency's charge.
- Two phases:
  1. Initial assessment.
  2. Recurring assessment, with an interval according to the facility's security level. E.g.: low level, every 5 years; higher levels, every 3 years.
- Key component: identify vulnerabilities and recommend countermeasures to mitigate their possible exploitation.
- Metrics by phase: assessments completed; vulnerabilities identified; time to deploy countermeasures; total cost of the assessment program.
- Formula 1 — % of facilities with a current assessment (goal: 100%):

```latex
\text{Facilities with a current assessment (\%)} = \frac{\text{Total number of facilities with a current security assessment}}{\text{Total number of facilities}} \times 100
```

- Formula 2 — MTTMV (mean time to mitigate vulnerabilities) (Center for Internet Security, 2010):
  - Indicates how the organization reacts to detected vulnerabilities. It is measured in units of time.
  - Gives the average time to mitigate a known vulnerability and points to processes that need to be sped up.

```latex
MTTMV = \frac{\sum \left( \text{Date of mitigation} - \text{Date vulnerability was detected} \right)}{\text{Total number of mitigated vulnerabilities}}
```

- **Exam note:** the summation (Σ) is not visible in the extracted text. Since it is an average, the differences for all mitigated vulnerabilities are added (outside the book: this is how the CIS defines it). Also, the book cites CIS 2010 but does not include it in its references.

**Compliance of Systems**

- Sometimes physical security must collect metrics to meet regulatory standards.
- In the US, PACS are critical and depend on IT infrastructure. Therefore they must comply with FISMA (Federal Information Security Management Act of 2014).
  - Federal agencies' FISMA information systems must operate within the controls and limits set by law.
  - They go through a C&A (certification and accreditation) process when deployed and then at predefined intervals.
  - Auditors identify vulnerabilities or conditions outside acceptable limits. They deliver a final report to the executive committee.
  - The findings touch the PACS infrastructure and operation: server configuration, network communications, disaster recovery operations and operating environment.
- Not all physical security managers are familiar with these topics. They matter more and more because the systems run on the IT network.
- Metrics (similar to those for facilities):
  1. % of mitigations successfully deployed.
  2. Total mitigation time per finding.
  3. Total mitigation cost per finding.
  4. Average mitigation cost per finding.
- **Exam note:** the reference cites FISMA 2014 as 44 USC § 3541, which is the section of the 2002 FISMA. FISMA 2014 is at 44 USC § 3551 et seq. Today NIST calls C&A assessment and authorization (A&A) (outside the book).

**Biometrics**

- More and more US states have laws against the unlawful collection and storage of biometric information, which uniquely identifies the person. Many countries have similar laws.
- Compliance with applicable state, local and federal laws must be ensured.

#### 4.2.4 Presenting Aggregate Status for Physical Security Metrics

- Purpose of collecting metrics: give the organization assurance about the program's effectiveness and, ultimately, facilitate its improvement.
- Steps:
  1. Set performance requirements for each main component. They may come from external demands (regulatory compliance), existing baselines or established levels of risk acceptance.
  2. Measure each task or process of the component against its requirement → component rating.
  3. With the component ratings → program rating.
- Figure 4.9 (*Metrics Summary Chart*): table by fiscal year, with quarters Q1–Q4 for Systems, Personnel, Compliance and Overall.
  - Gives management a quick snapshot of the program's effectiveness and efficiency.
  - If management questions a rating, the security staff show the data from the individual metrics.
- **Pending:** contents of the cells in Figure 4.9 (ratings or colors, not legible in the text).

### 4.3 Additional Recommended Metrics

- Senior security staff can be creative in choosing which metrics to implement and how to use them.

| Metric | How it is measured and what for |
| --- | --- |
| Number of security requests received | Against prior periods (week, month, quarter); measures efficiency and resource allocation. Types: new access credentials; changes to existing credentials; requests for system activity reports. |
| External dependency responsiveness | Time in which external dependencies (e.g., contracting office, IT technical support) handle security requests. |
| Change in the number of security-related incidents | Against fixed periods (month, quarter, year). Shows trends and changes in incident type according to the countermeasures deployed. |
| Security cost per square foot | Formula A. |
| Security cost per employee | Formula B. |
| Facility security assessments performed | Formula C. |
| Security system preventive maintenance inspections performed | Formula D. |

- Formula A — security cost per square foot:

```latex
\text{Security cost per ft}^2 = \frac{\text{Total system cost} + \text{Total contractor labor cost} + \text{Total FTE} + \text{Indirect cost}}{\text{Total square footage}}
```

- Formula B — security cost per employee:

```latex
\text{Security cost per employee} = \frac{\text{Total security department cost (Contract cost + FTE cost + Indirect cost)}}{\text{Total number of employees}}
```

- Formula C — facility security assessments performed:

```latex
\text{Facility security assessments performed} = \frac{\text{Total number of facility security assessments performed}}{\text{Total number of facility security assessments planned}}
```

- Formula D — preventive maintenance inspections performed:

```latex
\text{PM inspections performed} = \frac{\text{Total number of preventive maintenance inspections performed}}{\text{Total number of facilities with security systems}}
```

- **Exam note:** in Formula A the book adds Total FTE (full-time equivalent positions) to costs. It should be read as FTE cost, as in Formula B.
- Conversion to m² (outside the book): 1 m² = 10.764 ft².

```latex
\text{Cost per m}^2 = \text{Cost per ft}^2 \times 10.764
```

**Why metrics matter (chapter close)**

- Security competes with other areas for limited funds and resources.
- Metrics tell executives whether their investment improved security, reduced vulnerability and lowered risk to an acceptable level.
- They are used by program managers, system owners and security directors. They serve to isolate problems and justify funds and resources.
- They must drive improvement and focus on the organization's strategic objectives and goals.
- Avoid collecting data for the sake of collecting.
- Starting small makes it possible to detect errors in what is collected and in how it is collected and used.
- A program under way reveals new applications and process improvements. With metrics, every aspect of the program is measured and also better managed.

### 4.4 Application of Metrics Throughout This Book

| Chapter | Application of the metrics |
| --- | --- |
| 11 — Electronic Security Systems (ESS) | They add value in selecting, scoping, upgrading and maintaining the ESS. When selecting one, the requirements document covers communication protocols, number of cardholders and readers per panel. The reporting function is often forgotten: does the system work well? how does it generate reports? Manufacturers neglected it: little operational experience and users who did not use report-based metrics. This is changing in new designs. |
| 12 — Security Officers and the Human Element | Metrics fit into the guard force's QA (quality assurance) and QC (quality control) programs. Financial: contract fund burn rate, management of overtime cost and cost of turnover. Performance: guard deficiency reports, response times, training compliance and accuracy of what is entered in the automated incident management system. Trends guide changes in staffing, post locations, allocation of duties and program design. |
| 13 — Principles of Project Management | They are applied in the WBS (work breakdown structure), with activities, time and effort. Also in requirements, design, costing and contracting, and test protocols. Highlighted approach: earned value, the measurement of project progress to date. The book describes it as the cumulative budgeted cost compared with the actual cost of the work completed at a point in the schedule. Early warning of schedule and cost deviations, when correcting is most effective. |
| 16 — Follow-On Support and Activities | ESS metrics detect failures and components about to fail. They help define the maintenance contract, for one site or for dispersed facilities. Stable system with few failures → time-and-material contract. High failure rate in a complex integrated system → fixed-price maintenance contract. Region with more needs → the contract prioritizes it in price or in the location of the main provider. They also guide upgrades, deployment of new technology and transition of legacy systems. |

- **Exam note (earned value):** the book's definition is confusing. In project management, EV (earned value) is the budgeted cost of the work performed. Compared with the actual cost (AC) it gives the cost variance; with the planned value (PV), the schedule variance (outside the book).

```latex
CV = EV - AC \qquad SV = EV - PV
```
