test · SMART (Specific, Measurable, Attainable, Repeatable, Time-dependent) · Baseline · Security operations center (SOC) · Physical access control system (PACS) · Noise · Forced door · Door held open · Door position switch · Bypass duration · Propped open · Exit device · Unauthorized access attempt · Card misread · Card expired · Access privilege · User-defined actions/alarms · Communications failure · Network availability · Video management system (VMS) · Turnstile · Free egress · Guard force response · Contract surveillance plan · Contracting officer representative (COR) · Duress alarm · Baseline requirements · Service level agreement (SLA) · Task order / work order · Contract deliverable · Response to customer requests · Intangible asset · One-stop shop · Inward-facing training metrics · Outward-facing training metrics · Customer satisfaction survey · Facility security assessment · Countermeasure · Mean time to mitigate vulnerabilities (MTTMV) · Federal Information Security Management Act (FISMA) · Certification and accreditation (C&A) · Biometric information · Aggregate status · Metrics Summary Chart · Risk acceptance · External dependency responsiveness · Security cost per square foot · Security cost per employee · Full-time equivalent (FTE) · Preventive maintenance inspection · Electronic security system (ESS) · Quality assurance (QA) · Quality control (QC) · Burn rate · Work breakdown structure (WBS) · Earned value · Time-and-material contract · Fixed-price contract · Legacy systems

## Capítulo 5 — Basic Design Concepts (págs. 77–94)

**Central idea:** Every physical security project must apply risk management and basic design principles from the planning stage. The two pillars are the 4 Ds and layered security. The chapter adds concepts (point vs. area, avoiding conflicts, balance, design criteria, procedures) and cases of good and bad practices.

### Chapter Introduction (pp. 77–78)

- The chapter ties together the four parts of the book:
  1. Risk Management: The Basis for Physical Security
  2. Design Principles and Practices
  3. Physical Security and Protection Strategies
  4. Managing Physical Security Projects and Programs
- Security risk management is applied to all decisions about:
  - facilities: design, layout and use;
  - overall protection strategies;
  - structural and electronic security systems;
  - security officer operations and other human support;
  - physical security program management.
- The professional's mental model: plan, implement, evaluate and monitor protection programs.
- It is applied to every aspect of the integrated strategy, not just the building or the electronics.
- ASIS PAP standard (Physical Asset Protection Standard, 2021): to choose and apply measures properly, a risk assessment must first be done.
  - Its results must be interpreted, reported and used correctly.
- Reviewing good and bad examples makes it possible to benchmark protection strategies.

### 5.1 Design Principles

| Foundational principle | Content |
| --- | --- |
| The four Ds | Deter, Detect, Delay, Deny |
| Layered security | Also called defense-in-depth or security-in-depth |

- A layer can be:
  - **Physical:** a wall or an entry control point.
  - **Functional:** e.g., an intrusion detection layer + a video surveillance layer + a response force layer, together (with or without a physical layer) to protect an area.
- Layers must reinforce and back each other up, without overdesign or unnecessary duplication.
- **Critical detection point:** the point where the remaining delay time exceeds the response force time.
- Defense in depth: the adversary must avoid or defeat several measures in sequence.
  - Example: defeating a sensor and penetrating two barriers to reach a room or a file cabinet in a restricted area.
  - The times and effectiveness of each layer may vary, but each layer requires a separate act by the adversary.
- Effects of layers on the adversary:
  - they create uncertainty in the adversary's mind;
  - they increase the attack preparation time;
  - they add steps to the intrusion;
  - they give the security or police response time;
  - they may convince the adversary to abort and look for a softer target.

> **Exam note:** the book says that at the critical detection point the remaining delay "exceeds" the response time. (outside the book) In the physical protection systems literature (Garcia) it is the last point along the path where detecting still allows interruption: remaining delay ≥ response time. Detecting after that is too late.

> **Exam note:** this book uses deter, detect, delay, deny. (outside the book) Other sources replace "deny" with "respond" or use 5 Ds. Answer with this book's list.

#### 5.1.1 Point Versus Area Security

|  | Area security | Point security |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Outer perimeter | Strong perimeter protection | Lax control |
| Entry control | Very strict, at a single portal | Focus on each building or on restricted areas within them |

- Context from the book: sensitive research campus (intellectual property, high-end laboratory equipment, hazardous or controlled materials) or high-security government complex.
- Usual answer: a bit of both. It is not always practical because of budget, convenience or mission.
- The choice depends on the nature of the threat:
  - Under normal conditions, point security may be enough.
  - If a threat from radical activists arises, it is advisable to move to area security.
- On a large campus (e.g., a dozen multistory buildings) the conclusion may change. Key questions:
  - How does it affect the budget, staffing, equipment, mitigation approach and overall strategy?
  - What additional information is needed?
- Source: Tapestry Technologies (2010).

#### 5.1.2 Conflict Avoidance

- Video surveillance, sensors, doors, access control, lighting and security officers can clash with each other.
- More electronic measures → greater likelihood of false alarms caused by proactive employees.
  - Example: a security officer sets off the intrusion alarm when inspecting a cut or damaged fence.
- Physical measures must be seen as parts of a whole: an integrated strategy or program.
- Conflict between security and safety:
  - Example: fail safe or fail secure electronic locks? This is usually decided by building or fire codes.
  - Hardware, glazing, audible alarms, lighting and access control must meet security objectives, safety objectives and regulations at the same time.
- Conflict with the mission or culture: measures must be consistent with the organization's operations, people and purpose.

> (outside the book) Fail safe: unlocks on loss of power (prioritizes evacuation). Fail secure: stays locked on loss of power.

#### 5.1.3 Balance

Several dimensions of balance make the architecture practical, efficient and effective:

| Dimension (book order) | What it requires | Example or key |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Among types of measures | Electronic, structural (includes mechanical), human and procedural, orchestrated as a system | Do not overvalue electronics over the building layout and structural measures |
| 2. Among access points or methods | Equivalent protection at all accesses | Heavily controlled front door and open back door. Case: high school with magnetometers at the 2 main entrances and other entrances uncontrolled, open or with broken locks |
| 2 (expansion). Through any attack path (balanced protection) | Wherever the adversary comes from, they find effective measures | All the surfaces of a room (list below) |
| 3. Among objectives or assets | Not just goods: also people, information, intangible assets, mission and other objectives | Applies to any facility: shopping center, research center, warehouse, hospital, school, data center |
| Related: security, safety and operations | They can clash; good planning reduces the conflict and seeks mutual support | Plant with large bay doors between two zones for moving machinery, one zone being restricted |
| Final: among threats | Do not eliminate a measure because it overprotects against another threat | Adequate protection against all threats by all paths, balanced with cost, safety and structural integrity |

- Surfaces surrounding a room (book example):
  - walls, floors and ceilings of various types;
  - windows and doors of various types;
  - equipment hatches in floors and ceilings;
  - HVAC (heating, ventilating, and air conditioning) openings with different types of grilles;
  - crawl spaces, fire escapes or ladders, skylights and ledges; the book leaves this list open.
- **Balanced system:** the minimum time or difficulty to penetrate each barrier is equal, and so is the probability of detecting penetration of each barrier.
  - It is an ideal, probably unattainable.
  - Conceptual goal: a balanced level of deterrence, delay, detection and denial on all sides of the enclosure (building, room, vault).

#### 5.1.4 Additional Design Elements

- The type or use of the facility greatly conditions the design and strategy.
- Having special needs: data centers, communications centers, hospitals, laboratories, manufacturing plants and universities.
- Data centers and biotechnology laboratories need specialized environmental control and air handling (Figure 5.1).
  - Decide early where that equipment goes and whether it needs protection against vandalism, tampering, intentional attack or sabotage.
- Manufacturing plants: the location of hazardous materials areas is key.
- The location of certain elements can hinder the security or first responder response and evacuation.
  - Collaborate in advance with designers and stakeholders and rehearse scenarios.
- Review for safety, security and operations the location of: air intakes, exhaust vents, water drainage and utility entry points.
- In addition to the physical layout, consider operating procedures.
- **Component failure:** a much-overlooked aspect; components fail, perhaps at the worst moment.
  - Evaluate the timing and consequences of failure and include them in the plan.
  - The plan covers: initial design, maintenance, testing and contingency operations of electronic and mechanical systems.
  - It is unlikely that a complex system will operate its entire life without failures.
  - Causes: from environmental factors (expected) to adversary actions that exceed the design threat.
  - Knowing the cause helps restore the system. More important: contingency plans to keep operating or maintain the security level for a set period.
  - Automatic contingency (redundant equipment that takes over the function): very desirable in some cases. Example: generators or batteries if the adversary cuts the main power.
  - Some contingencies need outside help. Example: local police who reinforce the airport at an elevated alert. Here the "failure" is the temporary lack of response force against the new threat.
- Figure 5.1 "HVAC/Environmental Control Systems at High-Tech Facilities" (photographs). **Pending:** the content of the photos cannot be read as text.

#### Design Criteria (unnumbered subsection)

- Every design needs criteria to evaluate its elements.

|  | Performance criteria | Feature criteria |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How it chooses | By each element's contribution to overall performance | To meet the requirement that certain elements be present |
| Measure of effectiveness | Overall system performance | Presence of those features |
| What it allows or causes | Compare current (baseline) and improved system, quantify the improvement and do cost-benefit analysis | Leads to the checklist method |
| Recommendation | Preferred: overall performance is what matters | Avoid it in standards or requirements, or use it with great care |
| Example (perimeter detection) | Detect an intruder running by any attack method | Include two specific sensors: motion and fence |

> **Exam note:** after the example, the book states that the fence-mounted sensor is "the best" for monitoring the perimeter. It is an absolute statement that clashes with the performance logic of the section itself. On the exam, prioritize the performance criterion.

Performance measures:

| Function | Measures |
| --- | --- |
| Detection | Probability of detection; communication and assessment time; frequency of nuisance alarms (nuisance alarms = false positives); frequency of undetected intrusions (false negatives) |
| Delay | Time to defeat obstacles |
| Denial/response | Probability of accurate communication to the response force; time to communicate or assess; probability of deployment to the adversary's location; deployment time; response force effectiveness |

> **Exam note:** the book equates nuisance alarms with false positives. (outside the book) In physical protection systems, a nuisance alarm is caused by a real non-intruder stimulus (animal, weather). A false alarm is caused by the equipment itself.

#### Procedures (unnumbered subsection)

- Effective strategy = people + procedures + equipment, integrated against the expected or reasonably expected threat.
- The emphasis is usually on the human element and technology, but the value of procedures is enormous.
- Changing procedures can be a cost-effective solution, always together with the other existing or recommended elements.
- Procedures include:
  - operating and maintenance functions;
  - training and awareness of employees and users;
  - training of security officers and the response force;
  - business practices (e.g., whether visitors are escorted in certain areas).
- **Investigations** as a procedural tool:
  - Types: incident and exploratory (e.g., due to a complaint or a detected discrepancy).
  - They may respond to a loss or anticipate a threat (e.g., background investigations of candidates).
  - Their results go into an incident management system, together with: routine incident reports, access control records, video surveillance information and visitor logs, among other sources.
  - Review those data periodically to evaluate and readjust the program; they also feed design and improvement projects.
  - Frequent error: not using investigative information to refine objectives or recommend measures.
- Procedures complement good technical design, layout and systems (ASIS International, 2012).

> **Note:** the text cites "ASIS International, 2012", but the chapter's bibliography only includes the 2021 ASIS PAP standard. Possible citation error.

### 5.2 Examples of Design Practices: Good and Not So Good

| Figure | Title in the book | Assessment |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 5.2 | Design Features of a Professional Office Campus | Good overall layout |
| 5.3 | Using a stylish wall to partially hide protected assets | Good |
| 5.4 | A Unique Terrain-Based Approach to Standoff | Good, with one weak point |
| 5.5 | A Good Practice: Separating Shipping and Receiving | Good |
| 5.6 | Inefficient Placement of Surveillance Cameras | Bad |
| 5.7 | First-Floor Office Lobby Using Compartmentation | Good, with one flaw |
| 5.8 | Bollards Protecting Half of a Driveway | Bad |

#### Figure 5.2 — Office campus: overall layout (facility layout)

- Basic practice, applicable to any multibuilding complex or enclosed site.
- **Surroundings:**
  - Behind: interstate highway. It gives advantages and risks; both must be considered.
  - On the left: transportation facility (perhaps a bus terminal and commuter parking). It may be a crime generator, though not always.
  - That facility has partial perimeter fencing → another layer of security for the complex.
  - In front and on the right: not shown in the diagram. Probably ordinary streets, but this should not be taken for granted.
- **Perimeter:** attractive berm instead of a fence.
  - There was enough land; it was judged at least as effective as a mechanical measure (fence).
  - Landscaping chosen for security objectives and for the aesthetics of the office park and the neighborhood:
    - certain trees or large boulders → effective structural barriers;
    - thorny shrubs → visual privacy and a somewhat lesser physical barrier;
    - low stone wall or decorative fence → deterrence, demarcation and some barrier.
  - Key factor in a barrier's contribution to system effectiveness: the time to defeat it.
  - Risk: landscaping can block cameras (poor maintenance or seasonal changes in foliage).
- **Changes over time:** case of a new commuter rail station to the right of the campus.
  - It generated foot traffic between the terminal and the station; people cutting across the campus or parking on it.
  - Options: perimeter fence + staffed checkpoint, or simply a low wall or decorative fence + signage.
  - No solution fits everything; risk assessment and knowing the area help.
- **Internal layout:**
  - Buildings that receive visitors, at the front; the most secure or restricted, at the back.
  - Visitor parking separate or well defined, well located.
  - Data center and service area somewhat isolated (behind Building 1).
- Lesson: a well-thought-out layout gives present and future benefits and makes the other measures (structural, electronic and human) cheaper.
- **Pending:** the Figure 5.2 diagram cannot be read as text; this summary is based only on the book's explanation.

#### Figure 5.3 — Decorative wall that hides assets

- Loading dock, trash containers and backup generator, behind a brick wall that matches the building.
- Small bollards (low security) protect the generator from accidental impacts. Sometimes sturdier bollards or barriers are recommended.
- The three areas and the vehicle access are easily covered with cameras → fewer cameras and less load on control center operators.

#### Figure 5.4 — Separation distance through terrain (standoff)

- Standoff distance matters for blast protection.
- The terrain gives natural separation and grade difference: vehicles stay at least 40 feet (≈ 12 m) from the building exterior.
- The slope isolates the lower level; it provides privacy and keeps away or gives away intruders or unlawful observers.
- Weak point: egress is limited to the ramps (bridges). Solution: enough ramps on all sides.
- Berms, slopes, vegetation and natural terrain can be integrated into the strategy at many types of facility.

> **Exam note:** the book gives the figure in feet: 40 ft. The conversion (40 × 0.3048 = 12.19 m) is mine; on the exam, think in feet.

#### Figure 5.5 — Separating shipping and receiving

- Recommended in commerce, warehouses and freight transport: plants, warehouses, docks, transshipment points and stores.
- It segregates incoming goods from outgoing goods; it frustrates thefts that occur when they are mixed or close together.
- It also reduces paperwork and inventory errors or losses.
- High-value items: in a cage or closed room → physical barrier and psychological deterrence.

#### Figure 5.6 — Poorly placed cameras

- There is one camera for each entrance door; a single one could cover both.
- There are no cameras at the loading docks.
- If the docks are covered, a parked trailer can block the view of the next doors.
- The docks deserve surveillance equal to or greater than the entrances: easy access for intruders, vandalism and improper movements of goods.
- Plan mounting location, field of view and number of doors per camera.

#### Figure 5.7 — Lobby with compartmentation

- The conference room is outside the restricted space.
  - Attendees do not sign in at the desk or receive a badge.
  - They are separated from the private areas; they usually have no direct link to the organization.
  - Useful where there are sensitive activities; it protects information, assets, occupants and employees.
- Security office in a central position; mail and loading dock somewhat segregated.
- Elevators behind the desk: only for those who signed in and were authorized by a security officer.
- Flaw: the emergency exit on the right is partly blocked by sofas. An inspector or the fire marshal would have it corrected on the spot.

#### Figure 5.8 — Bollards at half a vehicle entrance

- After several truck bomb attacks, many buildings extended the standoff with bollards or large concrete planters.
- Here the owner, the manager and the security director did not coordinate with the neighboring building, which has no bollards.
- The attacker can go around the bollards and attack either building.
- It is more a security management failure than a physical security failure; both disciplines influence it.
- Lesson: avoid myopic vision (only one's own); consider the neighbor and peripheral threats. Collaborative planning (Peterson, 2014).

#### Case: the vehicle barrier and the attack on foot

- An attacker wanted to enter by car with explosives, tailgating another vehicle.
- The retractable barrier at the checkpoint was too fast and he could not enter.
- Plan B: he got out with the explosives and ran on foot to the building perimeter. The controller, focused on the car, reacted late.
- Lesson: the barrier fulfilled its mission but did not stop the attack. A vehicle barrier may not stop a pedestrian, and vice versa.
- Think outside the conventional about threats and scenarios; avoid the piecemeal or silo approach.

#### Risk management approach

- Collaborate with architects, designers, systems consultants and other stakeholders.
- Review scenarios, benchmark, evaluate similar environments and think outside the conventional.
- Goal: an integrated, well-orchestrated strategy, not just buying and installing hardware.

#### Purchasing and vendors (Purpura, 2013, pp. 262–263)

- Warns against relying too much on vendors' advice or pressure.
- Unethical sales practices it cites:
  - selling obsolete technology and unnecessary equipment;
  - pushing intrusion detection systems with excess inventory, useless or unnecessary for the client;
  - omitting extra staff, additional hardware, software problems and expensive maintenance.
- The same can happen with computer security products (IT: information technology).
- Rules for buying well:
  1. Apply the "buyer beware" principle.
  2. Decide as a team, with several specialists.
  3. Evaluate the organization's needs, not the vendor's.
  4. Stay up to date on the technological state of the art.
  5. Think critically; do not decide on emotion.
  6. Avoid panic purchases or instinctive (kneejerk) reaction.
- Risk analysis helps the buyer to: identify areas of potential loss, point out weaknesses, develop and install countermeasures and evaluate needs.
- Vellani, *Strategic Security Management* (2007): "match product to need and not need to product".
- The designer must choose equipment only on determined requirements and needs.

### Key Terms

four Ds · deter · detect · delay · deny · layered security · defense-in-depth · security-in-depth · critical detection point · response force time · softer target · area security · point security · single portal · conflict avoidance · fail safe · fail secure · balance · balanced protection · balanced system · protection objectives · component failure · contingency plans · redundant equipment · backup power · design criteria · performance criteria · feature criteria · checklist method · baseline system · cost-benefit analysis · performance measures · probability of detection · assessment · nuisance alarms · false positives · false negatives · time to defeat obstacles · response force effectiveness · procedures · training and awareness · incident investigation · exploratory investigation · background investigations · incident management system · facility layout · berm · crime generator · standoff distance · bollards · shipping and receiving separation · compartmentation · tailgating · vehicle barrier · silo approach · buyer beware · kneejerk reaction purchases · match product to need · Physical Asset Protection Standard (PAP)

## Capítulo 6 — Influencing Factors in Physical Security Design (págs. 95–102)

**Central idea:** Many factors determine whether a physical security strategy is appropriate, effective and cost-effective. The team must analyze them at the start and throughout the project: the asset, the building, the environment, the location and others (management support, budget, vendors, integration). The mitigation chosen must fit the mission, the risk profile and the operation.

### Chapter Introduction (p. 95)

- Applies to new construction, expansion, renovation, upgrade or protection of another type of asset (vehicle, person, intellectual property).
- Starting with the right mindset gives a greater chance of success and eases the process.
- Some factors apply only to certain environments or assets. Even so, the team must discuss them at the start and during the project.
- They influence, sometimes greatly, three things: relevance, operational effectiveness and cost-effectiveness of the strategies and measures.

### 6.1 Characteristics of the Assets Under Protection

- Sometimes the project's focus is not a building. The program may protect:
  1. a person (executive, official, celebrity);
  2. an item of intellectual property (trade secret or physical model of a device);
  3. an event or a series of events;
  4. a vehicle (automobile, train, ship, aircraft or other means of transport);
  5. data stored or processed by a computer, computer network (IT: information technology), device or telecommunications system;
  6. a high-value object (painting, sculpture, jewel, cash);
  7. animals, plants, bioorganisms or other living things;
  8. a capability (e.g., the bandwidth of a communications or transmission system).
- Each type of asset requires a different approach and different metrics to measure effectiveness.
- Guiding questions:
  - Must the measures be mobile or travel with the asset? Does that limit size, weight, connectivity, personnel or power?
  - Must they adapt to conditions or are they essentially static?
  - Is the threat constant or variable? If it varies: over time, in intensity, in nature, by location, by season or time of day.
- They are critical if the asset is outside the usual domain of the professional, the team or the vendors. An outside consultant can be hired.

### 6.2 Characteristics of the Building or Facility

If the asset is a building or facility, other factors apply:

#### 6.2.1 Ownership and Occupancy

- Key question: who is the owner? It affects the ability to modify the structure, sign contracts and do other business.
- Dealing with the owner gives more leverage and flexibility than dealing with a tenant.
- Direct communication with the owner helps, though it is a distraction if the owner wants to get too involved.
- If the owner occupies the building, they are usually more interested in the problems and details.
- Special arrangements if the owner occupies it: executive protection, robust communications, high-level or sensitive meeting rooms, operations center.
  - Especially if the owner is the CEO (chief executive officer) and the building houses senior management (C-suite: CEO; CFO, chief financial officer; CIO, chief information officer).
- Case: a large financial corporation built, occupied for a few years and sold.
  - Buildings tailored to it, but designed for the next occupant (perhaps several tenants).
  - Lesson: architectural and security elements that are easy to remove or install.
- **One or several tenants:**
  - Multi-tenant building: more adaptable design, maintained throughout the life of the facility. Each tenant has different and changing requirements.
  - Also in government: a mix of government and commercial occupants, or of several agencies with their own policies (Gilmore, 2009).
  - Even more so with international or multinational organizations: culture, social customs, language, business practices and risk profile have an influence.
- Case: a US (United States) government agency vacated a leased building in an office park.
  - By policy it removed vault doors, special floors, cabling, security systems and other equipment.
  - Another agency with a sensitive mission arrived and installed the same things, with the same specifications.
  - Lesson: great cost and waste of resources, plus longer transition delays.

#### 6.2.2 Purpose of the Facility

- How and by whom will it be used? It greatly influences the strategy, design and layout.
- Facilities with a specific purpose (the book's full list):
  - power plants;
  - dams;
  - other utilities;
  - telecommunications facilities;
  - large retail facilities;
  - manufacturing plants;
  - facilities that handle classified or sensitive information;
  - transportation facilities;
  - military installations;
  - hospitals;
  - laboratories;
  - chemical plants;
  - schools and educational facilities.
- The purpose determines: access control, standoff distance, types of sensors, nature of the security force and dependence on utilities, among other factors.

#### 6.2.3 Access

- Intended access: private, public or mixed.
  - Public: e.g., shopping centers. Private: only authorized personnel (employees).
- It affects: layout, parking, security systems and the role or use of security officers.
- Related variable: hours. Normal hours, extended (store open late) or 24/7 (24 hours, 7 days)?
- Are there dual-egress doors, required by local code, that prevent locking down the main building from an adjacent parking lot?

### 6.3 Characteristics of the Surroundings

- Consider the terrain and the neighbors.
- Neighbors influence the threats, the vulnerabilities and the potential impact of an event.
  - A school would not be placed next to a hazardous waste depot or a dangerous chemical plant.
  - Neighbors are almost never chosen, but they must be part of the risk assessment and the strategy.
- Neighbors and ingress and egress: can a neighbor's incident cut off one's own access?
  - Incidents cited: chemical spill, vehicle accident, facility lockdown, labor dispute or other.
- Museum case: a facility depended on deliveries and shipments day and night, next to a museum threatened by hate groups.
  - A shooter entered the museum; police cordoned off the whole neighborhood for several hours.
  - The plant's only transportation route was inaccessible for a long time.
  - Lesson: a neighbor's incident can paralyze one's own operation if there is only one access route.

### 6.4 Characteristics of the Location

- The environment changes security: dense urban area, suburb, town, rural or remote area.
- The response time of first responders varies greatly. It may require more self-reliance in an incident.
  - The risk assessment helps determine those needs.
- Other location factors:
  - proximity to a body of water (can be positive or negative);
  - reliability of supporting utilities and infrastructure;
  - proximity to transportation facilities;
  - access by roads and bridges;
  - telecommunications infrastructure.
- These elements can be a valuable asset in operations or in an emergency.
- Do trains or expressways pass next to the site or beneath it, posing a threat?
- Climate and natural disasters (key factor): tornadoes, lightning, wind, extreme temperatures, sandstorms, floods, hurricanes, earthquakes, typhoons, tsunamis, snow or ice and other weather conditions.
  - How do they affect security systems and elements?
  - How to bring in more security officers in an emergency if transportation and access are cut off?
  - How to compensate for electronic systems out of service for a time?
  - The answers go into the security strategy and into the continuity-of-operations and disaster management plans.

### 6.5 Additional Influencing Factors

| Factor | Key |
| --- | --- |
| Senior management support | Level of management support for security |
| Budget and resources | Budget process and availability of resources |
| Human capital and services | Access to personnel, outsource providers and special expertise or services |
| Relationship with vendors | Systems integrators, installation and maintenance, door and hardware suppliers, central station services and consultants. They must be responsive, capable and open to discussing the client's needs |
| Effective integration | All elements work as a comprehensive protective system (see below) |

- Elements that must be integrated: electronic systems, incident management system, visitor management system, structural elements and security staff.
- The physical infrastructure must act in concert (transparently) with:
  - personnel security;
  - information assets protection;
  - IT security;
  - investigations;
  - training and awareness (which give credibility and authority).
- And with related disciplines: safety, regulatory compliance, business continuity and facilities or real estate management.
- Business continuity (the book inserts it in this section):
  - The greatest predictor of the success or failure of business continuity planning is senior management commitment.
  - Processes for developing the business continuity plan (BCP), according to p. 100: readiness, prevention, response and recovery.

> **Exam note:** internal contradiction. On p. 100 the BCP processes are 4 (readiness, prevention, response, recovery). On p. 101 they are 3 (readiness, response, recovery). Memorize both versions; if an option includes "prevention", it is the one from p. 100.

#### 6.5.1 Selecting Mitigation Options Based on Influencing Factors

- General goal: cohesion among the organization, mission, risk profile and protection strategy (measures and tools).
- When choosing mitigation, consider goals and mission to protect people and assets and reduce legal liability.
- It is not practical to address all identified risks.
  - Prioritize the asset–threat/vulnerability pairs (threat vectors) that can cause great mission impact or damage.
  - Also consider their likelihood.
- There are more and more options, but some are unfeasible or too costly (in money or other terms).
- A sound strategy combines the measures in a well-orchestrated way.

| Type of option | Content |
| --- | --- |
| General security categories | 1. Architectural improvements. 2. Security systems. 3. Policies and procedures. 4. Management practices. 5. Security staffing |
| Transfer the financial risk | Insurance or contractual terms (e.g., indemnification clauses in security service contracts) |
| Accept the risk | As a cost of doing business; always an option |

- Every option is evaluated by four criteria for the company's operation:
  1. availability;
  2. affordability;
  3. feasibility;
  4. risk acceptance.
- Besides cost: does the strategy substantially interfere with operations?
  - Obvious example: closing the store to stop shoplifting also locks out customers; the store would go under.
  - Less obvious example: access control so severe that it discourages customers also loses business.
- The professional's challenge: balance a sound strategy with operational needs and the psychological impact on people and culture (Patterson, 2013).
- Business continuity (again, p. 101):
  - Processes: readiness, response and recovery (see the Exam note above).
  - Guiding principle: the plan must contain a risk assessment and a business impact analysis (BIA). It must be flexible and adapt to the situation.
  - The BIA includes data analysis. It helps management and the planner decide which functions are critical to continue operating.

### Key Terms

influencing factors · assets under protection · intellectual property · trade secret · high-value object · capability (bandwidth) · constant vs. variable threat · outside consultants · ownership and occupancy · owner-occupied facility · tenant · leverage · executive protection · C-suite · multi-tenant facility · purpose of the facility · standoff distance · private, public or mixed access · business hours · 24/7 · dual-egress doors · lockdown · surroundings · neighbors · ingress and egress · location · dense urban area · rural or remote area · first responders · response time · self-reliance · supporting utilities and infrastructure · natural disasters · continuity of operations · disaster management plans · senior management support · senior management commitment · systems integrators · central station services · integration · incident management system · visitor management system · personnel security · information assets protection · IT security · mitigation options · risk profile · threat vectors · liability · architectural improvements · security staffing · risk transfer · insurance · indemnification clauses · risk acceptance · availability · affordability · feasibility · shoplifting · business continuity plan (BCP) · readiness · prevention · response · recovery · business impact analysis (BIA)

## Capítulo 7 — Security Architecture and Engineering (págs. 103–126)

**Central idea:** Security is built into the design from the start of the project, not added at the end. Everything starts from the risk assessment and the design basis threat (DBT). Site, structure, materials, utilities and life safety systems must work together and in layers.

**Security architecture and design**

- Discipline that designs facilities and complexes with security built in from the origin.
- Applies to new construction, renovation and expansion.
- Ideal: the security professional collaborates with architects, engineers and the general contractor from the start and throughout the process.
- Using the results of a risk assessment saves money and improves protection.

**Castles: defensive design factors (6)**

| Factor | What it consists of |
| --- | --- |
| location | geography, terrain and position |
| structural design | size, shape and construction materials |
| clear zones | cleared zones for surveillance, threat detection and standoff distance |
| access control | moats, drawbridges and few access points |
| defensive points | points for deploying weaponry |
| multiple layers or zones of protection | several layers or zones of protection |

- Lesson: these ancient techniques serve modern design, in physical and procedural security. If attacks evolve, defenses do too.

| Figure | Castle | Dates | Protection features |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 7.1 | Castel dell’Ovo, Naples (Italy) | built in 1128 | water on 3 sides; entrance with portcullis; thick, slightly sloped curtain walls; battlements; loopholes for arrows and cannons |
| 7.2 | Castel Nuovo, Naples (Italy) | completed in 1343; renovated in the 1500s | star-shaped plan; several ramparts; crenellated towers; curtain walls; portcullises |
| 7.3 | Häme Castle, Hämeenlinna (Finland) | 13th or 14th century, gray stone, later reinforced with brick; bastions in the 18th century; prison since 1837 | excellent vantage points; berms; defined clear zone around the perimeter |

### 7.1 Design Overview

- First priority of every project: **determine the client's requirements**.
- In engineering this includes site layout, structure size, number of occupants and building activities.
- Sources: the client and the codes and regulations of the jurisdiction.
- **Basic factors that the security design must know from the start (9):**
  1. governing codes and regulations
  2. protection requirements
  3. company standards and guidelines
  4. type of construction
  5. site layout
  6. material selection
  7. utilities
  8. life safety
  9. infrastructure

### 7.2 Codes and Regulations

- Projects have federal, state and local requirements.
- **Ways they arrive (5):**
  - code regulations: mandatory by law
  - trade or industry guidelines / best practices
  - standards
  - permitting requirements
  - contractual requirements
- In addition: the client's own standards or guidelines. They are not code, but the designer must know them.
- Some codes and many guidelines address security design.
- Risk: a security requirement can violate another regulation, e.g., life safety codes. Solution: coordinate with the other design disciplines (holistic approach).
- **US federal guidelines:**
  - Department of Defense (DoD) → must comply with the Unified Facilities Criteria (UFC). The **Series 4** of the UFC deals with security.
  - Whole Building Design Guide (www.wbdg.org): resource of federal guidelines by agency.
  - Guidelines for commercial sites: Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) and American Society of Civil Engineers (ASCE).
- Federal guidelines are useful even if the project is not federal. Full list in 7.9.

### 7.3 Project Requirements

- Necessary part of the design: do a risk assessment.
- There are many risk models, but all must evaluate asset value, threat and vulnerability.
- Simple risk equation:

```latex
\text{Risk} = \text{Vulnerability} \times \text{Threat} \times \text{Asset Value}
```

- You cannot protect against everything. The client must:
  - set the necessary level of protection;
  - define the acceptable levels of risk;
  - apply reasonable mitigations according to cost-benefit.
- The client may not share the detail of the assessment and deliver only the DBT.
- **Design basis threat (DBT):** profile of the adversary on which the facility's engineering and security operations are based. It includes:
  - type, composition and capabilities;
  - methods: tactics, techniques and procedures;
  - goals, intent and motivation.

**FEMA model: 2 classes of threats or hazards (FEMA 2003)**

|  | Natural hazards | Human-caused hazards (*intentional threats*) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Examples from the book | catastrophic weather events, earthquakes, wildfires (non-exhaustive list) | technological hazards, terrorism, others |
| Distinguishing feature | their probability is estimated with historical data | they originate in human activity |

- Typical cases: disgruntled employees, students and even soldiers who attack what they see as the source of their problems, often public institutions.
- **Challenge:** balance public access with public safety. People do not want to shop or conduct business in a fortress.
  - Some mitigations protect without losing the welcoming look.
  - Others show the protection in plain view: they reassure the public and deter threats.
- **Trap when choosing technology:** not being able to verify the product's advertising before installing it.
  - Research products and services diligently and verify that they meet the client's requirements.
  - Some systems are tested and certified; others have no valid test method; some companies refuse to pay for tests.
  - For some clients theoretical calculations are not enough: they demand real tests (certification).
  - If the client wants a certified system, they must require it in the design specifications.

### 7.4 Type of Construction

- Retrofit or renovation vs. new construction can change the design greatly. The requirements depend on use; their implementation, on the type of work.
- Consult the stakeholders about pros, cons and limitations of the project.
- **Change review board:** representatives of each main project partner. Its most important duty: to be the central point (clearinghouse) for all changes.
- **Failure points:** in any type of work, ask what will fail first in a catastrophic event. A system is only as good and strong as its weakest point. Taken up again in 7.6.

**Figure 7.4 — Considerations in New Construction versus Retrofit/Renovation** (non-exhaustive list)

| Type | Pros | Cons |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New construction | integrated system and facility; based on own design criteria; systems designed and tested to be compatible; warrantied systems; incorporates future growth or expansion | most costs up front (front-loaded); requires complete design of structure and systems; the land must be acquired; more design and construction time; permit requirements |
| Retrofit construction or renovation | takes advantage of the existing structure; only installation and minor work (system and components); schedule normally shorter | interrupts current activities; existing utilities and systems may not provide capacity; the cost may exceed that of new construction; existing systems may be incompatible or hard to match |

- Exam note: the text speaks of “figures” in the plural, but it is a single figure (7.4). It has typos (“Majrity”, “Constrction”) and two lines without bullets (“Warrantied systems”, “Permit requirements”), which read as separate items.

### 7.5 Site Layout

- Premise of perimeter design: **the 4 Ds — deter, detect, deny, delay**.
- Exam note: do not confuse with the functions of a physical protection system, *detect, delay, respond* (outside the book).
- The easiest: move the threat away from the target. It is not always realistic:
  - Urban area: little space; land is very expensive or nonexistent.
  - Rural area: convenience is sacrificed; it is less desirable.
  - *Standoff* enlarges the site footprint and adds costs and environmental issues.
- The facility must look like less of a target of opportunity than its neighbor.
- If the attacker is not deterred: the more time between detection and reaching the target, the more opportunity to respond.
- All systems (security and building) must be in good condition. Broken cameras or breached fences say that security does not matter → easy target.

#### 7.5.1 Perimeter

- One of the first lines of defense. Depending on the level of protection, it combines: fence line, security officers, cameras, vehicle barriers, intrusion detection systems (IDS), signs, natural barriers.
- **Fence:** the most common perimeter defense.
  - It marks a line that must not be crossed and gives some delay.
  - In reality it only “keeps honest people honest”. It does not stop someone who wants to get in.
  - A standard fence topped with barbed wire is climbed in seconds.
  - Some industries require it: the cargo industry asks for 6 ft in height (≈1.8 m, conversion outside the book).
- **Fence + IDS** (pressure plates, vibration sensors, laser/LiDAR, other electronic sensors): the fence deters; the IDS alerts and the response starts sooner.
- **Good practice:** do not put the fence right at the property line, but a little inside.
  - The outside space allows maintenance and visibility of the boundary.
  - It prevents neighbors from planting plants or placing objects that could be used to breach the fence.
- Inside the fence, maintenance and landscaping remain key to detecting and monitoring.
  - Vegetation should be lower the closer it is to the building. That way explosives, espionage equipment or hidden people can be seen.
  - If trees or other factors block the main camera or sensor of a critical area, add coverage from another angle.
- Not all fences are equal: cheap battery-powered tools cut chain-link in seconds. For more delay there is **no cut/no climb fencing**, at greater cost.

#### 7.5.2 Lighting

- Each type of visual surveillance has minimum light requirements: normal vision or special cameras (infrared or digital).
- The type of lamp affects color perception:

| Lamp | Light it emits |
| --- | --- |
| Metal halide | white → the best for color CCTV (closed-circuit television) |
| High-pressure sodium | yellowish |

- Design according to local code. Codes seek to reduce light pollution.
- The code may set maximum and minimum lumens according to site size or light spill.
- **Spillover:** light that extends onto neighboring properties. It is a nuisance in residential neighborhoods at night and can dazzle drivers on nearby roads.
- The book expands on security lighting in a later chapter.

#### 7.5.3 Building Design Against Blast

- Site and building design can reduce or worsen the effect of an explosion. The building suffers more than the initial wave.
- **Blast pressure terms (5):**

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Incendiary thermal effect | flash or fireball on detonation; insignificant unless there are highly combustible materials |
| Shock or blast wave | leading edge of the energy wave; expands in a circular pattern from the source |
| Reflective pressure | wave energy redirected by objects in its path; can be much stronger than the initial wave at the same distance (Wilkinson & Anderson 2003) |
| Overpressure | resulting pressure above ambient |
| Fragmentation | materials that surround the explosive or are nearby, thrown by the pressure waves |

- **Urban environment:** reflected pressures greatly increase damage, through multiple impacts from several angles and at different times.
  - The book's analogy: dropping a bucket of ping-pong balls onto a miniature town. They bounce until the energy dissipates; the waves do too.
- **Building shape:** changes its vulnerability. The wave is reflected at an angle in keeping with the surface it hits.
- Figure 7.5 — Blast Pressure Effects on a Structure (FEMA 2003).
- Figure 7.6 — Effects of Building Shape on Air Blast (Hinman 2011): examples of building shapes and their effect against the wave.
- **Pending:** the graphic content of figures 7.5 and 7.6 cannot be read (they are images).
- If explosion is a valid risk: design the structure (new construction) or the site to lower the risk to an acceptable level, with qualified personnel.

#### 7.5.4 Building Access

- **Standoff:** distance between the threat and the building. It must be enough to detect and respond.
- If there is land, standoff easily adds delay and improves detection and characterization of the threat.
- Figure 7.7 — Security Zones: several measures create standoff, establish security zones and lengthen the penetration time. **Pending:** graphic detail of the figure (image).
- Without detection there is no response. To gain response time you need:
  - secondary detection, e.g., IDS;
  - delay tactics, e.g., serpentine drives and parking standoff.
- Know the response time of internal security or local police. Design the site so that the delay comes as close as reasonable to that time.
- Physically effective and cost-effective approach: **serpentine barriers in front of retractable bollards**.

### 7.6 Material Selection

- Architecture: the material gives the design its look. Engineering: it gives strength and protects assets.
- The structure includes glazing, wood, steel, walls, floors, ceilings and furniture. Everything can become a projectile if poorly designed.
- Materials also affect physical penetration and access to building utilities or controls.

#### 7.6.1 Building Structure

- Every system is designed to the requirements set at the start; the structure protects against the design hazards.
- Design against natural hazards that also works against human hazards: **seismic anchoring of suspended equipment** reduces debris in an explosion.
  - The wave breaks windows and weak points in the facade and reaches the equipment; if the mounting is weak, it falls and damages assets.
- **Progressive collapse:** uncommon; it is considered in buildings of **3 or more stories**.
  - Definition (ASCE/SEI 59-11, *Blast Protection of Buildings*, ASCE 2011): chain failure of the structural system, disproportionate to the initial local damage, such as that from an explosion.
  - Mechanism: a column or floor support falls, those above fail and the building collapses on itself.
  - Examples from terrorist attacks: Khobar Towers (Saudi Arabia) and Murrah Federal Building (Oklahoma City). Figure 7.8: Khobar Towers (DoD photo).

#### 7.6.2 Facades

- It determines whether the building looks attractive or ugly. In engineering, the facade protects or causes harm.
- Some materials break under force or pressure; their fragments become hazards. Wind or explosion throws them against buildings, key assets or people.
- Example: ballast gravel on rooftops comes loose in a tornado and becomes a projectile.
- **Sound and sight lines:** the material must be neutral or a countermeasure, never help the attacker.
- More glass = better view for the attacker. It may require treatment against viewing inside or against listening devices.

#### 7.6.3 Interior Layout

- Open, closed (compartmentalized) or mixed floor plans. The user decides according to their operations.

|  | Open floor plan | Closed / compartmentalized floor plan |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Advantages | great visibility of activities and people; collaboration; overall situational awareness (reduces threats); easy to detect confrontations and leave quickly | privacy to work; more material that blocks fragments |
| Disadvantages | little privacy; can be an “open attack zone” with few places to take cover | much less awareness of what is happening; restricted escape routes; more material that can fragment |
| Adjustments | mid-level screens for sensitive or offensive material; low ceilings shorten the columns (useful in an explosion) but reduce spaciousness | — |

- **Layered placement:** high-security activities in the center; low-security ones in the outer layers. That way no one without authorization crosses secure zones.
- **Special risk areas** (e.g., mail rooms with unknown threats): next to exterior walls, to limit damage if something goes off. Even better: screen the mail elsewhere.
- **Key or highly skilled personnel:** away from parking lots, vehicle access points, structural overhangs and walls with a lot of glass.
- The client, with the designer's help, chooses; it is almost always a mix, and the percentage is decided objectively.

#### 7.6.4 Glazing

- **Benefit:** natural light; open and welcoming atmosphere. It earns points toward LEED (Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design) certification from the U.S. Green Building Council.
- **Risk:** fragmentation due to pressure imbalance, caused by an extreme storm (tornado) or explosion.
  - If the exterior pressure exceeds the interior pressure, the window fails and the fragments enter the occupied space.
  - The design must anticipate fragmentation and choose mitigations according to the required level of protection.

**Figure 7.9 — Performance Conditions for Window System Response (GSA 2020)** (GSA: General Services Administration)

| Performance condition | Protection level | Hazard level | Glass response |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Safe | None | does not break; no visible damage to glass or frame |
| 2 | Very High | None | cracks, but the frame retains it; dust or very small fragments near the sill or on the floor are accepted |
| 3a | High | Very low | cracks; fragments enter and fall to the floor no more than 3.3 ft (1 m) from the window |
| 3b | High | Low | cracks; fragments fall to the floor no more than 10 ft (3 m) from the window |
| 4 | Medium | Medium | cracks; fragments fall to the floor and strike a vertical witness panel no more than 10 ft (3 m) away, at a height no greater than 2 ft (0.6 m) |
| 5 | Low | High | cracks and the window system fails catastrophically; fragments strike the witness panel no more than 10 ft (3 m) away, above 2 ft (0.6 m) in height |

- Rule to remember: the higher the condition number, the less protection and the more hazard.
- Exam note: in the book, row 3b appears at the end of the table; here it is in its logical order. The table text came with a garbled font encoding and was read by deciphering it.

**How to reduce fragmentation**

- First: ensure the minimum standoff from blast sources, such as improvised explosive devices (IED) in a vehicle or container.
- Designing without windows is neither desirable nor practical. **Other options (7):**

| Measure | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| Locate windows away from blast sources | if the wall facing the parking lot or threats has a lot of glass, add a secondary interior wall that captures fragments; that curtain wall also reduces heating and cooling losses |
| Choose the type of glass carefully | standard construction annealed glass fragments easily; look for reinforced options; the glass also affects heating and cooling |
| Reduce the window size | large unsupported windows fragment more: more surface receives the pressure |
| Reinforce the glass | film on the surface or between panes, or wire mesh; in a retrofit, verify that the structure holding the window withstands the same; if it is weaker, it fails and the whole window enters as a large projectile |
| Construction and design that support the panels | if the bite (overlap of the frame over the glass) is insufficient, the glass breaks and fragments more easily |
| Blast curtain | fixed to the ceiling and floor; traps the fragments and collects them at its base; good retrofit option; lets light through; only works in its position: if occupants pull it aside or cut it at the bottom, it becomes useless |
| Catch bar, in a retrofit | together with a reinforced window; it goes at the center point of the window; if the glass fails, it folds over the bar instead of entering the room |

- **Double benefit:** blast countermeasures also slow down intrusions.
  - Smaller, higher windows reduce fragmentation and access.
  - Do not put windows next to doors: it avoids breaking the glass and opening the lock from inside.

### 7.7 Site Utilities

- Determine what special requirements protect the facility against the DBT.
- **Aspects to consider (4):** power redundancy; how utilities enter the site; maintenance needs; access to utilities.
- Protect the entry points of critical utilities.
- Critical utility → loop design or redundant feed: if lost, it is fed from another point (back-feed).
- **Utility provider access:**

| Option | Implication |
| --- | --- |
| Outside the fence | technicians can provide maintenance and emergency support at any time |
| Inside the fence | requires coordination and monitoring |

- Evaluate whether an intruder can enter through utility access points or attack the utilities themselves: natural gas, chemicals, telecommunications or power.

#### 7.7.1 HVAC

- HVAC (heating, ventilation, and air conditioning): fresh air, temperature and humidity. It provides comfort and protects mission-essential equipment.
- **Temperature:** server rooms require much lower temperatures than people. Heat damages equipment and cuts services → redundancy (secondary chiller, air handling units).
- **Humidity:** high humidity damages equipment and over time causes mold. Keep it **below 60% relative humidity**.
- **Air quality:** mix of conditioned recirculated air and fresh air.
  - Natural ventilation is the preferred and typical one, but fresh air intakes are targets for introducing chemical or biological weapons.
  - Protect the intakes and keep them away from sources of contamination, such as the emergency generator exhaust.
  - Elevation protects: on the roof or high on the wall, next to the roofline, it is hard to introduce contaminants.
  - Intake near the ground: physical barrier with walls and an inclined mesh cover. The mesh lets air through, prevents throwing objects, and its slope makes them roll off.
- Figure 7-10 — Protecting Intakes for HVAC Systems (AHU = air handling unit). **Pending:** graphic detail of the figure (image).
- High-risk facilities: sealed HVAC with filtration against chemical, biological or radiological attack.

#### 7.7.2 Emergency Power

- In the design, define which equipment or systems need power at all times and what interruption they tolerate.
  - That sets how much power the emergency generator must provide.
  - It also indicates whether a UPS (uninterruptible power supply) is needed and with what capacity.
- Not everything has to go on emergency power; the client decides.
- Key staff's computers: individual UPS units with time to shut down properly and lose less data. It reduces the size and cost of the main backup.
- **Maintenance:** generator load tests; UPS battery inspection. Without maintenance the risk of failure at the critical moment rises.
- Even generator fuel goes bad if stored too long → regular maintenance plan.

#### 7.7.3 Other Utilities

- **Other site utilities (4):** communications (telephone and internet); potable water; gas; sanitary sewer.
- They are usually protected at the source because the U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS) considers them critical infrastructure and key resources.
- Even so, they can be attacked along their supply lines.
- **SCADA** (supervisory control and data acquisition): system with which owners or managers control settings and operation of building utilities. It is not a utility, but it must be protected: a breach can bring down critical components and damage operations.

### 7.8 Life Safety Systems

- In the US the requirements come from two codes: the Life Safety Code of the National Fire Protection Association (NFPA 101) and the International Building Code (IBC).
- They make it possible to maintain security together with safe egress.
- **Golden rule: security cannot override the life safety code.**
- Some states, such as California and New York, have additional requirements.
- Exam note: the book calls both “building codes”; NFPA 101 is strictly a life safety code, not a building code (outside the book).
- **Life safety and security considerations (10):**
  1. evacuation and exit points, especially those affected by access control locks, elevator lobby closures or delayed locking
  2. door alarms
  3. smoke detection and trouble alarms
  4. fire detection systems
  5. sprinklers or other suppression system
  6. chemical and biological hazard detection
  7. mass notification systems
  8. emergency lighting and wayfinding
  9. annunciator panels
  10. emergency planning
- **Locks:** NFPA 101 regulates the types of locks on doors. None may prevent the occupant from reaching a safe place or an exit.
- Every system must **fail open or be failsafe** (automatic release of locks) in an emergency.
- Functions such as motion sensors that unlock the door on approach may be required. According to the book, that arrangement also prevents unauthorized access once the building is empty.
- **Doors to sensitive, hazardous or exterior areas:** with alarm and monitoring. If it sounds, send a response to investigate and secure the area again.
  - They become a nuisance if staff leave them open to get back in, e.g., when going out to smoke. Train: that breach is not acceptable.
- **Trouble alarms in important rooms:** a nuisance if there is a lot of dust. Even so, always monitor and attend to them: there may be a greater hazard, such as fire.
- **Emergency lighting:** key for evacuation; test and maintain it regularly.
- **Wayfinding:** not mandatory in every facility, but it guides staff in a distressing event. E.g.: photoluminescent signs low down, in case smoke forces people to crawl.
- **Response time:** the panel must be next to on-site monitoring, or there must be a system that alerts outside emergency services. The sooner the threat is identified, the sooner it is stabilized and the less damage there is.

#### 7.8.1 Evacuation

- Security systems cannot get in the way of life safety; they must work together.
- **Key steps for planning evacuations (7):**
  1. Designate primary and secondary (or more) evacuation routes from all areas, including exterior ones such as rooftops.
  2. Identify muster points away from the facility and other hazards.
  3. Identify and train the personnel (security or employees) who will help with the evacuation.
  4. Identify those who need special assistance and locate the special equipment they require immediately.
  5. Locate the special equipment needed to evacuate (e.g., escape ladders).
  6. Locate fire extinguishers, smoke detectors and first aid kits.
  7. Locate the shutoff valves for gas, water and electricity.
- **Evacuation route:** the easiest and closest, free of obstacles, to an exit. Corridors wide enough.
- Routes and areas of refuge: always clear, never used as storage.
- If possible, avoid exits that lead directly to parking lots or public areas with secondary hazards: car bombs or snipers.
- On exiting, go straight to the muster point for the count by the designated safety officers.
- **Muster points:** easy for staff to recognize, not very obvious to an attacker. Identify them with colors or letters, not with department names that give away a target.

#### 7.8.2 Shelter-in-Place

- Sometimes it is better to shelter inside rather than go out. Examples: extreme weather (tornado) or an active shooter outside the facility.
- Every facility must have designated areas to gather occupants. They usually have isolated walls for more protection and are in the interior, e.g., stairwells.
- **Air quality when evacuation is not possible:**
  - HVAC mechanisms that shut off the air supply in a fire;
  - duct filters that remove particles, including some biological hazards;
  - ultraviolet (UV) light in ducts that kills some biological hazards;
  - sensors for specific chemicals that shut off the airflow.

**Chapter close**

- Architecture and engineering are part of the integrated asset protection strategy.
- A facility does not need to look like a fortress, but it can be protected like one.
- Correct design: several layers of protection; it starts with the risk assessment; then elements, systems and materials are chosen.
- Man-made hazards: explosion, intrusion, workplace violence, crime, cyberattacks. Natural: catastrophic weather, earthquakes, wildfires. All systems must work together.
- Total security is not guaranteed, but risks can be managed.

### 7.9 Publications Relevant to Security Architecture and Engineering

| Issuer | Publications |
| --- | --- |
| American Society of Civil Engineers (ASCE) | Blast Protection of Buildings, ASCE/SEI 59-11 |
| Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) | Guidance for Protecting Building Environments from Airborne Chemical, Biological, or Radiological Attacks |
| FEMA | E155, Building Design for Homeland Security · FEMA 426, Reference Manual to Mitigate Potential Terrorist Attacks Against Buildings · FEMA 427, Primer for Design of Commercial Buildings to Mitigate Terrorist Attacks · FEMA 428, Primer to Design Safe School Projects in Case of Terrorist Attacks · FEMA 429, Insurance, Finance, and Regulation Primer for Terrorism Risk Management in Buildings · FEMA 452, Risk Assessment: A How-To Guide to Mitigate Potential Terrorist Attacks Against Buildings · FEMA 453, Design Guidance for Shelters and Safe Rooms |

## Capítulo 8 — Integrated Security and Protection Strategies (págs. 127–134)

**Central idea:** “Integrated” and “integration” are used with different meanings in the profession. The chapter organizes them into four levels, each broader than the last. It goes from electronic systems to enterprise-wide risk management (ERM and ESRM).

- Two complementary perspectives:
  - integration **within** the physical security domain;
  - integration **among** security disciplines, including physical.

**The 4 levels of integration** (from narrowest to broadest)

| Level | English term | Section |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | integrated electronic security systems | 8.1 |
| 2 | integrated physical security elements | 8.2 |
| 3 | integrated security programs | 8.3 |
| 4 | integration in enterprise risk management | 8.4 |

- All four are highly relevant to the profession and to creating physical security strategies.
- Exam note: the list calls level 4 “integration in enterprise risk management”; the title of 8.4 is “Integration of ERM and ESRM”. It is the same level.

### 8.1 Integrated Electronic Security Systems

- Video surveillance, access control, sensors, alarms and other elements interact and usually work together.
- That complexity helps, but it demands attention to detail, a lot of planning and testing.
- **Network backbone:** almost all systems travel over a network.

| Option | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Independent network | **preferred approach** |
| Part of the enterprise network | performance problems due to limited bandwidth or sporadic traffic spikes; difficulties with permissions to use the network |

- Systems have become more complex and integrated because of network complexity.
- **Good option: a single source** responsible for preventive and remedial maintenance. As a single point of contact, it diagnoses the cause and manages the solution.
- **System integrators:** third parties that supply or maintain specific components. Other providers: network services, telecommunications and application software.
- **Example (renovation, expansion or new project):** the security professional forms a team of stakeholders to:
  - choose the system (or systems) that meets everyone's needs;
  - make the most of interoperability;
  - correct the deficiencies of the current system;
  - anticipate future uses.
- Key to success: an open and cooperative relationship between the integrator and the IT (information technology) team that supports the network.
- Integration is an opportunity and also new challenges. It requires planning and collaboration throughout the project life cycle.
- **Other implications:** costs, dependencies and the challenge of securing the security systems themselves.

### 8.2 Integrated Physical Security Elements

- All elements must operate as part of a comprehensive physical security strategy.
- **3 general categories:** electronic security systems; structural security measures; human capital.
- **Potential components of any strategy (22; non-exhaustive list):**
  1. architecture and engineering
  2. facility layout and design landscaping
  3. natural access control
  4. electronic access control systems
  5. natural surveillance
  6. video surveillance systems
  7. doors and locking systems
  8. fencing, gates, and walls
  9. structural barriers (e.g., bollards and wedge barriers)
  10. visitor management systems
  11. threat intelligence sources
  12. incident and case management systems
  13. sensors
  14. alarms
  15. intrusion detection systems
  16. communications systems
  17. lighting (security and general)
  18. security officer force
  19. concierge or reception staff
  20. auxiliary personnel
  21. outsource providers
  22. canine services
- Exam note: “facility layout and design landscaping” appears this way on a single line; it could be two elements (“facility layout and design” and “landscaping”).
- **Objective:** choose tools from the list and other measures for a tailored strategy, according to the facility's mission, nature, location and threat profile. Everything must be coordinated like an orchestra.
- **Human element:** security officers are often a key component. What is critical is assigning the right person to the role and to the job requirements.
  - Benefits of a good fit: shift scheduling, client satisfaction, retention and greater effectiveness.
  - It can make the difference between a lawsuit or none, keeping a client or even saving a life.
  - Example: an officer who is good at a fixed post or patrol may not be ready for the security operations center (SOC). They may not know how to operate or interpret electronic or building control systems.
  - A staffing mismatch can be disastrous, whether intentional, due to understaffing or due to a sudden increase in requirements.

### 8.3 Integrated Security Programs

- Physical security is only one element of the total protection strategy.
- **The protection target (asset) can be:** facility, campus, community, project, person, physical object, other property (e.g., money) or an intangible.
- **Disciplines with which physical security is integrated (9):**
  1. personnel security
  2. IT security
  3. information assets protection
  4. technical security
  5. investigations
  6. training and awareness
  7. executive protection
  8. product security
  9. crime prevention
- **Successful security design:** recognizes technological advances on all fronts, not just in electronic systems. It integrates **3 primary elements:**

| Element | Points from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Architectural aspects | one of the most significant factors; limiting access to the building and routing corridors through control points makes it easier to control access |
| Security systems | electronic measures complement procedures |
| Operational factors | good operating procedures from the design phase; otherwise the human-machine interface will be inefficient |

- **Technology = force multiplier:** it does not replace personnel. It increases their capabilities and provides checks and balances against individual misconduct.
- **Human element** (decision-making, common sense, awareness): it is integrated in the design phase, not after the system is designed and implemented.
- Successful organizations know that their greatest security resources are their employees.
  - Understanding how they will use the technology and how they will respond makes it possible to prevent most incidents and respond well to the rest.
- **Operating procedures by threat level: before buying or installing any system.**
  - Installing equipment and software without an operating philosophy can leave systems that increase the risk of harm.
- If everyone follows established, easy procedures, abnormal activity stands out (Patterson 2013).

### 8.4 Integration of Enterprise Risk Management and Enterprise Security Risk Management

- It is the final and broadest aspect of integrated asset protection: the enterprise level.

| Concept | Definition and features |
| --- | --- |
| Enterprise Security Risk Management (ESRM) | strategic security management approach that links security practice to the organization's mission and goals, with globally accepted risk management principles. Foundations: holistic risk management, transparency, governance and partnership with stakeholders. Operates in the context of mission, vision, values, operating environment and stakeholders. Detail in the POA (Protection of Assets): Security Management |
| Enterprise Risk Management (ERM) | process or structure with which the organization identifies and understands its risks, plus the strategic plans to prepare for and manage them. Covers all key risks: financial, operational, market, strategic, reputational and security |

- **Key difference:** ERM covers all of the organization's key risks; ESRM, the security ones.
- **Relationship between the two:**
  - Not all organizations have an operating ERM structure, even if they address risk in various ways.
  - Since almost all have security risk, ESRM can be a subcomponent of ERM.
  - High-impact security risks may fall among those that ERM manages. Not all ESRM risks reach that level.
  - That is why ESRM is an independent risk management system, whether or not there is ERM.
- Both combine many functions of the organization to focus on its key assets and on a very complete strategy to protect them.
- **Integration chain** (from smallest to broadest): components → electronic security system package → physical security package → total security program → ERM domain.

**Figure 8.1 — Enterprise Security Domain Functions** (8 functions)

| Zone of the figure | Functions |
| --- | --- |
| ESRM side | Emergency management · Business continuity · Occupational health and safety · Security/asset protection |
| Central zone | Legal strategy and liability management · Insurance and indemnity |
| ERM side | Public relations · Competitive intelligence |

- **Pending:** the figure is a graphic; the split by zones comes from the position of the text. Confirm in the image whether the central zone is the intersection of ESRM and ERM.
- Part of the figure's text came with a garbled font encoding and was read by deciphering it.

**What it means for the security professional**

- Physical security usually takes part in an ERM approach. It is worth knowing the concept and incorporating it into planning.
- Example: the security director of a financial institution must know the liability for thefts at or near automated teller machines (ATM). They must create a program to reduce that risk.
- Security takes part in almost any incident, whether or not it is within its direct scope. Examples: strategic risks such as legal or credit liability, or support for a civil investigation.
- Those incidents are risks for the organization and may require (or be expected to prompt) a security response.
- Holistic integration = seeing the big picture. It is crucial in an increasingly connected and fast-moving world.

### Key Terms

integration · integrated electronic security systems · integrated physical security elements · integrated security programs · enterprise risk management (ERM) · enterprise security risk management (ESRM) · network backbone · single source · preventive and remedial maintenance · systems integrator · interoperability · securing the security systems · electronic security systems · structural security measures · human capital · natural access control · natural surveillance · threat intelligence sources · visitor management systems · security operations center (SOC) · staffing mismatch · force multiplier · checks and balances · architectural aspects · security systems · operational factors · operating procedures · human element · holistic risk management · governance · transparency · stakeholder partnership · Enterprise Security Domain Functions · big picture

## Capítulo 9 — Structural Security Measures (págs. 135–210)

**Central idea:** Physical security has three components that must work in coordination: structural security, electronic security systems and the human element (security officers and response forces). This chapter covers the structural part: barriers (9.1), containers and vaults (9.2), locks (9.3) and lighting (9.4). Each structural element is a layer that provides delay, and the weakest part sets the value of the whole.

- **Barrier** — ASIS definition (ASIS International 2021): natural or man-made obstacle to the movement or direction of people, animals, vehicles or materials.
- Structural security includes: overall structure of buildings and facilities, physical barriers, locking systems and lighting.
- Natural barriers are expanded on in Chapter 10.

### 9.1 Barriers

- A building's structural components form a barrier, that is, a layer of security. They must be understood, leveraged and managed within the physical security strategy.
- Components: walls, floors, ceilings, roofs, doors, windows and other structures.

#### 9.1.1 Walls

- Walls resist penetration better than doors, windows, vents and other openings. Even so, almost all can be breached with the right tools.
- Sometimes the wall is the adversary's best route for a forced entry.
- Threats: cutting tools; vehicles that ram cinder block, wood frame and other common walls; explosives that open holes to get in.
- Thickening or improving the wall gives only a **moderate** delay against explosives, although the amount of explosive needed grows greatly with thickness.
- Improvements pay off more against hand, power or thermal tools.

**Most common wall types (8):**

| Term | Gloss |
| --- | --- |
| reinforced concrete | reinforced concrete |
| expanded metal/concrete | expanded metal/concrete |
| concrete block | concrete block |
| clay tile | clay block/tile |
| precast concrete tee sections | precast concrete T sections |
| corrugated asbestos | corrugated asbestos |
| sheet metal | sheet metal |
| wood frame | wood frame |

**Reinforced concrete:**

- It is used where there are sensitive materials and is believed to be formidable. Tests show that ordinary reinforced concrete is penetrated quickly.
- Reason: it is designed for structural loads, not to delay. In conventional construction, the strength, thickness, size and spacing of the reinforcement are set by the structure, not by security.
- **Two or more walls in series delay more than a single wall of the same total thickness.** They require several efforts and carrying tools through each wall.
- With explosives, the contained pressure can bring down the roof and neighboring structures; the debris acts as an extra barrier.
- Rebar lengthens the delay: after an explosion it is usually intact and has to be removed. Removing the rebar usually takes longer than removing the concrete.
- More delay: more rebar, thicker rebar or smaller center-to-center spacing.
