#### 9.1.2 Doors

- **The weakest part of a barrier determines its final value.** That part is usually the door.

**Door classification (7):**

| Term | Gloss |
| --- | --- |
| standard industrial doors | standard industrial |
| personnel doors | personnel (pedestrian) |
| attack- and bullet-resistant doors | attack- and bullet-resistant |
| vehicle access doors | vehicle access |
| vault doors | vault |
| blast-resistant doors | blast-resistant |
| turnstile gates | turnstiles |

- Walls gain delay with thicker or composite materials. Doors are a weak link because of their functional requirements and their hardware.
- Typical example: heavy concrete walls with hollow steel doors, ordinary frames and hinges.
- **Balanced design principle:** door, frame, hinges, bolts and lock must give the same delay as floors, walls and roofs.
- If the door assembly cannot be improved enough, it may not be cost-effective to improve the building structure.
- Large manufacturers offer attack- and bullet-resistant doors; properly installed, they increase resistance to penetration.

**Typical exterior door:**

- Thickness 1¾ in (44 mm); 16 or 18 gauge steel sheets (1.5 or 1.2 mm).
- Hollow core or composite core; it may have glass or louvers.
- Composite core: noncombustible material that dampens sound, normally polyurethane foam or board.
- In hollow doors, light-gauge vertical channels are sometimes added for rigidity.

**Steel personnel doors:**

- Single or double. Exterior ones open outward, with the door closer on the inside.
- Mortised hinges with removable or non-removable pins.
- **Panic bars:** make the door a one-way barrier. They are easier to defeat when entering and make the attacker's escape easier.
- **Egress delay of 30 to 45 s:** prevents opening during that time. It is overridden if the fire alarm or the automatic extinguishing system is activated.

**Door attack times:**

| Attack | Time or data |
| --- | --- |
| Power tools on a light sheet-steel door | crawl-through hole in 3 min |
| Explosives | noisy and leave evidence, which helps detection |
| Picking a standard lock | \~1 min average for a skilled locksmith |
| Pipe or strap wrench on a key-in-knob lock | well under 1 min |
| Exterior door hinges (normally 3) | \~1 min |
| Crawl-through hole in flat, tempered or wired glass (hand tools) | 15 s |
| Forcing louvers or mesh | 30 s |
| Industrial doors | highly variable, from 10 s |

- Picking only works if there is a keyway; the pipe wrench, only with exposed hardware.
- Exit-only doors: flush mounting, no exterior hardware.
- If a keyway is needed: high-security locks with long picking times. Door sensors reduce the lock's vulnerability.
- Exposed exterior hinge pins = natural target. Even non-removable ones fall quickly to hand tools, thermal tools or explosives.
- Exterior doors are vulnerable to vehicle ramming and to rescue tools: breaching shotgun rounds (police) and hydraulic spreaders (firefighters).
- Industrial doors need improved designs to match the structure's delay.
- Fire and building codes may require interior panic bars.
- If it cannot be replaced, the old door is upgraded. In new construction or full replacement: new high-security attack-resistant doors.

**Upgrade of steel personnel doors in pressed steel frames:**

| Order or measure | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| 1st Eliminate unnecessary doors | first step |
| 2nd Eliminate unnecessary exterior windows, louvers, knobs and keyways | next step |
| Steel plates on the surface | more resistance to hand tools and light power tools |
| Heavy-duty hinges | support the added weight |
| Grout the frames with concrete | reinforces the supporting structure |
| Wood core (especially redwood) between plates | delay against thermal cutting 3 to 4 times greater than an air gap |

**Prying attack on the lock/frame area:**

- The frame strike is separated from the latch. A separation of ½ in (13 mm) to ¾ in (19 mm) is enough.
- **Astragal:** strip of sheet steel welded or bolted to the door. Same height as the door, ≥ 2 in (51 mm) wide and 1 in (25 mm) of overlap over the frame.
- Fill the frame with concrete at least 18 in (45 cm) above the frame strike, on both sides of the frame.
- Exterior personnel doors: high-security locks. A multiple-deadbolt system with key on both sides almost eliminates the prying attack.

**Hinges:**

- They are defeated in \~1 min by removing pins or cutting the knuckles.
- Welding the pin tip only works against hand tools. If the knuckles are cut with a power or thermal tool, it is still \~1 min.
- Upgrades: stud-in-hole hinges and full-length hinges.
- **Z-strip:** bolted or welded to the back face of the door. When prying, it butts against the frame or the stop, and forces more force and larger tools.

**Panic bars (panic or crash bars):**

- They are defeated in \~1 min with small hand tools, with less noise than thermal cutting. If noise does not matter, with power tools.
- Upgrade 1: bent metal plate with drill-resistant steel section. It prevents chiseling and wire hooking of the bar.
- Upgrade 2: electronic control. The bar must be held pressed for a set time before an electronic bolt is released. It gives the officer time to assess by video and respond.
- Upgrade 3: remove exterior knobs and hardware on emergency exits. It makes prying harder without affecting evacuation.
- Louvers and glass in exterior doors: minimize them. If needed, reduce them so that no one fits and reinforce them from the inside with mesh or a bar grille.

#### 9.1.3 Windows and Other Openings

- Without upgrades, windows barely delay. They must follow balanced design so as not to be the weak link.

**Types of commercial glass (ASIS International 2021):**

| Type | Characteristics | Use or limitation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Annealed or plate glass | manufactured to control residual stresses and be workable. Examples: plate, float, sheet, rolled and some patterned | breaks into large, dangerous fragments; codes may restrict it in door panels and fire exits |
| Tempered | treated to resist breakage (reheated and rapidly cooled); more mechanical and thermal strength | required by codes because it breaks into small pieces. Even so, it falls in seconds to impact tools |
| Wired | some resistance to large objects, but may shatter. ¼ in (6 mm), diamond, square or hexagonal mesh | main use: maintaining fire rating in fire doors and windows. It is penetrated in \~20 s |
| Laminated | 2 or more sheets of glass (annealed float, sheet or plate) bonded to plastic layers. The pieces stay stuck together | must be in a frame fixed to the structure. **Preferred type against explosions** |
| Bullet- or burglar-resistant | laminate of several layers of glass, polycarbonate and other films | various levels of ballistic resistance |

- Standard glass: very fragile; it is penetrated in a few seconds. Upgrades: thick security glass or grilles of expanded steel mesh or other metal grilles.
- ¼ in (6 mm) laminated safety glass: 30 s. 9/16 in (14 mm) security glass: 15 min with hand tools to open a hole.
- Security glass is not transparent armor, but it resists forced penetration better than standard glass.

**Transparent plastics (glass substitutes):**

- Some are combustible; fire codes may restrict them.
- Acrylics (Lucite, Plexiglas) under 1 in (25 mm): they break in under 10 s.
- Polycarbonates: they resist impact almost like bullet-resistant glass. ½ in (13 mm) Lexan: up to 2 min against hand tools.
- Thermal attack on polycarbonate: \~1 min, but it burns and releases toxic gases.
- **Glass/polycarbonate composite:** polycarbonate core between two layers of glass. Created for prisons, now used in all types of facilities. Good delay against hand tools.

**The window as a system** (glass + frame + mounting structure), for both delay and explosion:

- The strength and weight of frames vary greatly. Some manufacturers reinforce the sash (security sash) but not the frame.
- A window in a door is penetrated in seconds.
- Some special frames hide materials that resist cutting.
- On windows that open, the latch can be the weak link; it must not be reachable from outside. Options: fixed windows or sturdier latches.
- Fastening the frame to the structure: more or heavier hardware, or weld the frame's fin.

**Other window materials:**

| Measure | What it does | Limitation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Window bars | steel bars that prevent using the opening as access | only where building and fire codes allow |
| Window film or fragment retention film | adheres on the inside; reinforces and holds broken glass. Uses: (1) protection against intrusion or "smash and grab"; (2) fewer injuries from shards in an explosion; (3) fewer injuries from projectiles in extreme weather (hurricane, tornado) | defeated by repeated attacks |
| Blast curtains | fabric reinforced against material flying out in an explosion | do not protect against intrusion; they are a people protection measure |
| Security shutters | roll-up (horizontal aluminum or PVC —polyvinyl chloride— slats that rise into a box) or accordion (vertical slats that slide to the sides) | manual or electric operation with remote control, weather sensors or timers |

**Other openings:**

- Industrial facilities have many unmonitored openings: ventilation ducts, utility tunnels and service openings. They are possible intrusion routes.
- Few delay a determined adversary much, especially if they were designed for easy maintenance. Since they are hidden routes, they must have **barricade and sensors**.
- **Utility ports:** any unmonitored framed opening that is neither a door nor a window.
- Their grilles serve for personnel safety, decoration or against pests, but give little security. They may have covers without a lock or interior barrier.
- Standard windows and utility ports are weak links. **Most unimproved windows are penetrated with hand tools in under 30 s.**
- Upgrades: grilles, bars, expanded metal mesh or screens. Gratings of steel mesh, expanded metal, bars or tubes reduce the opening so that no one fits.
- **These covers are placed at the detection point or after it.**
- Tunnels between buildings: poorly protected, sometimes only with covers or manholes without a lock.
- Pipe chases: congested, but with room for maintenance. HVAC (heating, ventilation and air conditioning) ducts can be a route.
- Tunnels, manholes, roof and wall openings for equipment and ducts: reinforce with interior barriers.
- New buildings: windows smaller than a person and several small openings for ducts.
- Very narrow windows, 4 in (102 mm) or less: even without glass the opening has to be enlarged to get through.
- In existing buildings windows can be eliminated and the opening brought to the same delay as the wall.

#### 9.1.4 Roofs and Floors

- Functions: they protect from weather, provide work surface and are barriers.
- Evaluate threats from determined adversaries with hand, power and thermal tools, and explosives, combined.
- They are built the same way, but differ in thickness, type and amount of steel, and concrete strength according to load.
- **Floors resist more than roofs:** the main structure protects them and they carry more load.

**Current roof types (7):**

| Term | Gloss |
| --- | --- |
| prestressed concrete tee beam | prestressed concrete T beam |
| metal subdeck and reinforced concrete | metal subdeck and reinforced concrete |
| metal roof deck with lightweight concrete | metal roof deck with lightweight concrete |
| metal roof deck with insulation | metal roof deck with insulation |
| metal roof | metal roof |
| reinforced concrete beam and slab | reinforced concrete beam and slab |
| wood sheathing with membrane | wood sheathing with membrane |

**Roof upgrade methods (7):**

1. Membranes with embedded mesh.
2. Several inches of rigid insulation.
3. Concrete reinforced with deformed steel bars and expanded steel mesh.
4. Thicker rebar arranged in several rows or layers.
5. More fasteners and more structural members in corrugated roofs.
6. In metal systems: mechanical fasteners or joints, continuous welding and thicker-gauge material.
7. Thicker rebar to reinforce the flange of precast T beams.

- Upgrades **below the roof** usually give the best value. Tests suggest that a barrier under the roof is more effective than reinforcing the roof itself.
- Sometimes it is added without major modifications. It forces a second penetration, in a reduced space and perhaps with other tools.
- **Optimal distance between roof and secondary barrier: 10 to 12 in (25.4 to 30.5 cm).** The small space creates a "hole effect" or hinders the attack.
- Materials: quarry screen, bank-vault-type expanded steel mesh and floor gratings.
- Earth covering: increases delay through roof and walls. Buried and cut-and-cover structures use it against access and explosions.

#### 9.1.5 Fencing and Perimeter Walls

- One of the most common perimeter measures, in government and the private sector.
- Dual function: visual line of demarcation and barrier that gives delay and some resistance to intrusion or attack.

##### Chain Link Fencing

- **Advantages:** the most popular, durable and inexpensive for most uses. It installs quickly. Properly installed, it gives reasonable protection against vehicles, people and animals. It allows seeing from one side to the other, if desired.
- Fabric: wire coiled and interwoven into a continuous mesh, without knots or ties, except at the selvage.
- **Knuckling:** pairs of neighboring ends are interlaced and bent into a closed loop.
- **Twisting and barbing:** pairs of ends are twisted into a closed helix of one machine turn (3 full twists) and cut at an angle to leave a point.
- Coatings: zinc, aluminum or plastic/polymer.
- **Constant conclusion of tests:** it is crossed easily, even without aids such as blankets, wire cutters or shears. It gives minimal protection against determined adversaries.
- It does deter some vehicles, animals, casual intruders and opportunists.
- **Its greatest use: marking the property boundary**, so that the intruder cannot claim not to have known they were trespassing.
- There are many heights; it serves for almost any perimeter you want to define or where you want some delay.

**Causes of poor-quality or ineffective fences (4):**

1. Large-mesh fabric.
2. Wire of insufficient gauge.
3. Light, thin-walled posts and rails.
4. Shallow post holes.

- Manufacturing and installation standards: CLFMI (Chain Link Fence Manufacturers Institute), ASTM (formerly American Society for Testing and Materials) and BSI (British Standards Institution).
- Different ASTM standards apply depending on the fence material and the site.

**Heights:**

| Use | Height |
| --- | --- |
| Marking boundaries or stopping small animals | 4 ft (1.2 m) |
| Signs on boundary fence (property and danger warning) | normally every 50 ft (15 m) |
| Deterring people | not less than 7 ft (2.1 m) |
| Typical security fence (Figure 9.1, CLFMI) | 8 ft (2.5 m) + top guard of 1 ft (0.3 m) with 3 strands of barbed wire |

- **Exam note:** the book converts 8 ft as 2.5 m here and as 2.4 m for the electric fence. The correct value is 2.44 m.
- The installation points are indicative; the professional must consult ASTM and CLFMI for their application and area.

##### Chain Link Fence Installation

**Posts:**

- **Terminal posts:** ends, corners and gate supports.
- **Pull posts:** terminals that brace a long run or a change in grade.
- **Line posts:** all the others.
- Line posts: equal intervals, **maximum 10 ft (3 m)** on average center to center, measured parallel to the slope.
- Hole diameter: 4 times the largest section of the post; local conditions may change it.
- Depth: **minimum 24 in (0.6 m) + 3 in (76 mm) for each foot (0.3 m) of height above 4 ft (1.2 m).**
- Own example: 8 ft fence → 24 + (4 × 3) = 36 in depth.
- Fill: 2,500 psi concrete (pounds per square inch; 17.2 MPa), projecting 2 in (50 mm) above ground. Crowned surface to shed water.
- Valid alternative if it equals or exceeds concrete: driven post with **two steel blades** driven diagonally through metal footplates bolted underground.
- The blades are at right angles to the fence and brace it front and back. It is compared to a tree root: the post bends or breaks before the anchor fails.
- Since the blades pass below the frost line, frost and thaw do not affect them.
- **In solid rock:** depth = 3 times the largest section of the post; diameter = 0.5 in (13 mm) larger than that section.
- In rock: fill halfway with non-shrink hydraulic cement, sink the post plumb, fill without voids and crown the surface.

**Bracing:**

- Terminals are braced diagonally to the nearest line post when required. Otherwise: loose posts, loose fabric, poor appearance and a fence that invites penetration.

| Condition | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| Fabric of 6 ft (1.83 m) or less with top rail | no braces required |
| Fabric of more than 6 ft (1.83 m) | braces mandatory |
| Fabric of more than 12 ft (3.66 m) | center rail mandatory |
| No top rail, any height | braces at all terminals |

- Angle between brace and ground: **maximum 50°**. The brace runs from each terminal to the adjacent line post or its footing.
- No top rail: tension wire from end to end of each run, fastened to the fabric within the top foot (0.3 m).

**Figure 9.1 (Typical Chain Link Security Fence Installation):** front and side views. Labeled elements: concrete; fabric selvage; fabric tie; truss rod; brace rail; fabric; post spacing 10' max.; barb arm; line post; top rail; 3 strands of barbed wire; post cap; corner or end post; rail end; tension bands at 15" centers; tension bar.

- **Pending:** the barb arm dimension reads "4-5”" in the extracted text; it is not clear whether it is 45° or a measurement.

**Fabric:**

- Gauge **No. 9 AWG** (American Wire Gauge) or heavier. Mesh openings **of 2 in (50 mm) maximum**.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** the diameter for gauge 9 that the book gives (0.148 in) corresponds to steel wire gauge, not AWG.
- Selvage on fences of 5 ft (1.5 m) or less: knuckled at both ends.
- Selvage on fences of more than 5 ft: knuckled at one end and twisted at the other.
- Mesh of less than 2 in (51 mm): all selvage knuckled.
- Fabric stretched to \~2 in (51 mm) above hard ground or pavement and well secured to the posts.
- On soft ground, the fabric must go below the surface to compensate for ground movement.
- Against passing underneath: concrete apron at least 6 in thick; fabric extended below the bottom rail and embedded in concrete; pipe at the base of the fabric; or U-shaped stakes of \~2 ft (0.6 m).
- **Exam note:** the book writes 6 in (52 mm); the correct conversion is 152 mm.
- Culverts, channels, streams or other openings of 96 in² (619 cm²) or more: protect them with more fencing, grilles or other barriers without impeding drainage.
- Each run is fastened separately at all terminal posts after cutting the fabric.

**Wire breaking loads:**

| Gauge | Diameter | Breaking load |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No. 6 | 0.192 in (5 mm) | 2,170 lb (984 kg) |
| No. 9 | 0.148 in (3.8 mm) | 1,290 lb (585 kg) |
| No. 11 | 0.120 in (3 mm) | 850 lb (386 kg) |
| No. 11 (sic) | 0.113 in (2.9 mm) | 750 lb (340 kg) |

- **Exam note:** the book repeats "No. 11" in two rows. According to its own Figure 9.2, 0.113 in is gauge 11½.
- Fastening: fabric to line posts every **15 in (38 cm)**; edges to top and bottom rails or tension wires every **24 in (60.9 cm)** at most.

**Fence top treatment:**

- **Top rail:** horizontal member; fabric fastened with ties or clips every 2 ft (60.9 cm) at most. It improves appearance, but gives a handhold for climbing. Without a rail, use a top tension wire.
- **Top guard (barbed overhang):** barbed wire overhang, normally at 45°. Its orientation depends on whether the threat is getting in or getting out.
  - Outward: makes it harder to enter from outside. It may encroach on the neighbor's land.
  - Inward: makes it harder to climb out.
  - Double overhang (inward and outward) or inverted-V arm.
- 3 strands of barbed wire spaced 6 in (15.2 cm). The arms (outriggers) can be lengthened and strands added.
- The arms are fixed on top of the posts and must withstand **250 lb (113 kg)** applied on the outer strand.
- **Clear zone:** remove boxes, ladders, containers, vehicles and equipment next to the fence, and prune overhanging branches (natural ladder).
- **Failing to maintain the clear zone is one of the most common mistakes and nullifies the fence's effectiveness.**

**Fence gates:**

- The minimum compatible with efficient operation. All with locks.
- Common failure: the lateral stress when opening and closing weakens the corners. Tubular frames (round or square) welded at all corners.
- Same fabric as the fence, fastened to the frame every 15 in (38.1 cm).

| Type | Use |
| --- | --- |
| Single-swing | pedestrian walkways |
| Double-swing | vehicle entrances |
| Multifold | any opening up to 60 ft (18.2 m) |
| Overhead slide, single or double | no room to swing |
| Cantilever slide, single or double | entrances where an overhead track would obstruct equipment taller than highway vehicles |
| Vertical-lift | special uses, e.g., loading docks |
| Hangar-type slide on narrow track | very large openings |

- Any of them can be motorized.

**Turnstiles:**

- Uses: stores, supermarkets, amusement parks, subways and corporate and industrial facilities.

| Height | Use | Security |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Waist-high, \~36 in (0.9 m) | crowd control, charging admission, counting people | little, unless always attended |
| Full-height, \~7 ft (2.1 m) | completely surrounds the person passing | can be automated with card, keypad or biometric |

**Chain link variants:**

- Visual privacy: redwood or plastic slats inserted in the mesh, or fabric hung and well secured.
- Colors: hot-extruded PVC resin coating, up to 22 mil (0.022 in or 0.5 mm). Smooth and does not rust, ideal in marine areas.
- Standard PVC colors: green, olive green, brown and black.
- Interior use: e.g., in a warehouse, compartment for valuable material or information; it can also be closed on top.

**Figure 9.2 — Typical wire and mesh sizes** (ASTM A392-11a, Standard Specification for Zinc-Coated Steel Chain-Link Fence Fabric):

| Coated wire diameter (in) | Gauge | Mesh (in) | Fabric heights (in) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0.192 | 6 | 2 | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.148 | 9 | 2 | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.148 | 9 | 1 | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.128 | 10½ | 2 | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.120 | 11 | 2 | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84 … |
| 0.120 | 11 | 1¾ | … 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.120 | 11 | 1 | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.120 | 11 | 6/8\* (sic) | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.120 | 11 | ½\* | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.120 | 11 | 3/8\* | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, 120, 144 |
| 0.113 | 11½ | 2 1/5 (sic) | 36, 42, 48, 60, 72 … |

- \* Sizes designed for security (small meshes).
- **Pending:** incomplete cells ("…") in the 11/2, 11/1¾ and 11½ rows of the original. "6/8" and "2 1/5" appear that way in the text; probably 5/8 and 2⅛ (outside the book, verify).
- The professional uses this standard to choose wire, mesh and diamond count.

##### Applications for Decorative Fencing

- Factors when choosing a fence: security objectives, type, size, cost, location and appearance.
- Appearance depends on: level of security needed, purpose, environment, surrounding area, local codes or requirements and desired ambiance.
- Decorative fencing varies by material (wood, metal, composite), color, style or design.
- **Wall** of brick, stone, concrete or others: it blends better with the architecture, but usually costs more and limits visibility inward and outward.
- Fences and walls serve as supports for lighting, sensors and other systems.
- They serve low, medium or high security objectives.
- **Physical Asset Protection Standard (ASIS International 2021):** ornamental fences of carbon steel, galvanized steel or aluminum, assemblable in various forms.
- Depending on the application, the following is defined: type, style, height, picket and pale spacing, rail spacing, top treatment (including curved anti-climb system) and structural performance.
- High-security novelties: integrated barbs on top (Figure 9.4) and top inclined outward, like the chain link arms (bars bent at 45° with barbs).
- Combination used at some sites: decorative medium-high security vehicle gates with a chain link perimeter.
- With current offerings and costs, it is often just as sound or sounder to use decorative fencing with a uniform design.
- Figure 9.3: decorative fence next to a masonry wall, for elevation and landscaping.

##### Other Fencing Types

**Cable System Fencing (Figure 9.5):**

- High-strength cables; used mainly for vehicle access control.
- They allow considerable deflection and partial penetration before resisting. The deflection depends on the distance between concrete anchors (dead men), typically **\~200 ft (61 m)**.
- Therefore their installation differs somewhat from that of other fences and walls.
- Figure 9.5: cable fence combined with vegetation (Department of Defense, 1999).

**Expanded Metal and Welded Wire Fabric:**

- Cost: more expensive than chain link and cheaper than perforated metal or wrought ironwork. They look like a net.
- Expanded metal: does not unravel, is hard and very difficult to cut. It comes in uncoated, painted or galvanized steel, aluminum and stainless steel.
- **4 types of expanded metal:** standard or regular; grating; flattened; architectural or decorative.
- Welded wire fabric: cheaper than expanded metal; for low-risk applications.

**Wooden Fences:**

- For low security. They must be hard to climb and strong enough for the desired level.
- Upgrades: barbed wire, razor wire or metal spikes.
- To delay: vertical boards of **1¾ in (44 mm)** wide at most; horizontal sections spaced **50 in (1.27 m)** apart and on the protected side.

**Electric Security Fencing:**

- Tight grid of wires, on posts with insulators.
- From 5-wire systems (wall top) to multizone with **up to 50 wires** (high security).
- Typical industrial: **8 ft (2.4 m) high with 20 wires**, mounted **inside** the perimeter chain link fence.

| Form | How it works |
| --- | --- |
| All live-wire deterrent fence | relies on human fear of shock |
| Monitored fence | besides fear, detects cutting or climbing of wires and triggers an alarm |

- Monitored ones are integrated with intrusion alarm or access control and, increasingly, with cameras.
- Non-lethal, but unpleasant. They discharge energy in **pulses \~45 times per minute**.
- There are international safety standards for the energizers and for fence construction.
- **Exam note:** the book says they "do not use electric current" but pulses. Understand it as meaning there is no continuous current (outside the book).

**Barbed Wire and Razor Wire:**

- Barbed wire varies in gauge, coating weight, number of barbs and spacing between them.
- Barbed-wire-only fence against people: **at least 7 ft (2.1 m)** not counting the top guard. Strands well tensioned, on posts **less than 6 ft (1.8 m)** apart.
- **Concertina:** barbs or razor in coils. It serves as a top guard or as a fence by itself.
- Temporary or tactical concertina barriers are laid quickly. Local codes may regulate this use.

#### 9.1.6 Blocking Barriers

- Physical barriers can control pedestrians or materials. **Blocking barriers** serve mainly to control or prevent **vehicle access**.
- A light vehicle goes through almost any ordinary fence by ramming it at high speed. A heavy one breaks sturdier barriers.
- If the first vehicle is disabled, the attacker removes it and drives a **second vehicle** through the gap.
- **Highway guard rail:** effective only at impact angles **under 30°**. A light vehicle can get through, but is badly damaged.
- The book's conclusion: it is almost always best to use a **security barrier designed for that purpose**.

**Two categories of barriers**

|  | Passive / fixed | Active / operable |
| --- | --- | --- |
| What they are | Fixed; they do not let vehicles in | They open and close to let authorized vehicles through |
| Where | Perimeter, away from vehicle entrances | Vehicle access control points on the perimeter, or the entrance to a specific building (parking, garage) to check or inspect vehicles |
| Who operates them | No one | An operator (e.g., security officer) or an electronic access control system (card reader, keypad) |
| Types (all) | 1) fixed bollards; 2) engineered planters; 3) Jersey barriers; 4) heavy objects and trees; 5) water obstacles; 6) walls and fences (seen in 9.1.5) | 1) retractable bollards; 2) rising wedge systems; 3) rotating wedge systems; 4) drop arm crash beams; 5) crash gates; 6) surface-mounted wedges and plates |
| Source |  | U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS), 2011 |

**Bollards: design implications**

- Typical uses (full list): airports; corporate campuses; data centers; educational facilities; government facilities; military installations; nuclear facilities; ports; stadiums and arenas; water treatment plants.
- A bollard is an intrusion in the streetscape. The system must be well thought out, limited in extent and integrated into the perimeter and the urban landscape.
- Objective: little visual impact and not obstructing legitimate traffic, including **emergency entry and exit**.
- **Height:** to reduce visual impact, normally **no more than 30 in (0.76 m)**. That height may not be enough for some vehicle threats.
- The slope of the roadway and the height of the curb can improve effectiveness and make the bollard less visible.
- **Sidewalk width:** the bollard takes away the curb–bollard space, normally **24 in (0.6 m)**, plus its own width. A problem in downtown areas with heavy foot traffic and narrow sidewalks.

**Other guidelines for bollard systems (all)**

1. Spacing between bollards: **36–48 in (0.9–1.2 m)**, depending on expected traffic and the needs of pedestrians and people with disabilities.
2. In unavoidable long lines, interleave trees and large bollards that serve as seating. Over the years, the trees dominate the landscape and the barrier goes unnoticed.
3. Keep bollards out of ADA (Americans with Disabilities Act) ramps and street corners.
4. Linear layout: the axis of the bollards parallel to the street axis.
5. They can be custom designed to harmonize with the building, but must be **tested at an independent laboratory**.
6. Bollards set very close together make it hard for wheelchairs to reach curb cuts.
7. **In no case more than 38 in (1 m) tall**, including the decorative sleeve.

- **Exam note:** two different heights. 30 in (0.76 m) is the typical height for aesthetics; 38 in (≈1 m) is the absolute maximum.

**Bollards at urban sites**

- The typical foundation (Figure 9.6, cross-section of a bollard, U.S. Department of State) only works where the subsoil does not conflict with utilities, vaults or other urban infrastructure.
- Place them **as close to the roadway as possible**. If it is a public way (e.g., municipal sidewalk), a permit may be required.
- In cities like **New York and Washington, D.C.**, individual approvals must be obtained from several utility companies before building.
- Always check what is underneath before digging. The companies may demand proof that gravity and impact loads do not damage their networks.

**Bollard types by operation**

| Type | How it works | Use / pros and cons |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fixed | Permanently anchored | Passive barrier |
| Removable | Fits into a socket in the ground; secured with a padlock-type device against tampering | Traffic control and low security |
| Retractable (Figure 9.7, Delta Scientific Corporation) | Manual, electric, hydraulic, pneumatic or gas-assisted. When retracted it is flush with the ground. New variant: **twist-up** mechanism, which rotates the bollard into position | Cons: more maintenance than fixed; requires training officers and maintenance staff; risk of **accidental activation** with damage or injuries and lawsuits against the owner or the security provider |

**Other functions and aesthetics**

- They are combined with fences or trees to provide **multiple layers** of protection (Figure 9.8: decorative fence resting on bollards at 1.5–3 ft, 0.5–0.9 m).
- **Exam note:** Figure 9.8 spaces the bollards 1.5–3 ft (0.5–0.9 m), less than the general guideline of 36–48 in (0.9–1.2 m). In the figure the bollards are combined with a fence.
- They can carry lighting, sensors or other electronics, and even exterior **power outlets**.
- Lighting offered: metal halide, CFL (compact fluorescent), induction fluorescent or LED, for low, medium or high security.
- There is a growing market ("cottage industry") of decorative bollards, many security-rated. Traditional manufacturers already offer catalog and custom models.
- Sleeves in brushed aluminum, stainless steel, bronze or custom colors; some with the logo engraved.

**Vehicle impact standards and ratings**

- Credible standards cited: **U.S. Department of State SD-STD-02.01**; **ASTM F2656-07**; **BSI (British Standards Institution) PAS 68**.
- **Exam note:** the book writes "PAS68:2103"; it is a typo for **PAS 68:2013**.
- They measure a barrier's ability to stop a test vehicle of a given **weight** at a given **speed**.
- **ASTM F2656-07** gives impact conditions, designations and penetration levels. It serves to choose passive perimeter barriers and active access barriers at facilities exposed to **VBIED** (vehicle-borne improvised explosive device).

**Figure 9.9: Barrier Protection Ratings (Calpipe 2013)**

| Vehicle weight | Speed | Department of State | ASTM |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 15,000 lb (6,800 kg) | 30 mph (48 km/h) | K4 | M30 |
| 15,000 lb (6,800 kg) | 40 mph (64 km/h) | K8 | M40 |
| 15,000 lb (6,800 kg) | 50 mph (80 km/h) | K12 | M50 |

- **Exam note:** the figure prints "48k/km/h"; it is 48 km/h.
- These ratings are valid for judging effectiveness in general. For a detailed analysis you must add: angle of attack, bumper height, load height and other factors.
- (outside the book) In ASTM, the "P" indicates penetration; **P1** = the vehicle's load passes at most 1 m (3.3 ft) beyond the barrier.

**Mounting: shallow vs. deep**

- **Shallow mount** or **deep mount** bollards, depending on terrain and environment.
- Deep is more effective. Shallow is reinforced with special footings and installation techniques.
- In both cases: local code restrictions, buried utilities and soil type.

**Other blocking barriers**

| Barrier | Key characteristics |
| --- | --- |
| Wedge barriers, in general | They can be fixed, but are almost always **retractable**. Retractable ones are sometimes called **rotating plate barriers**: metal plate in the roadway that an operator raises or lowers from a booth. They can have an impact rating (crash rated) |
| Rising wedge (Figures 9.10 and 9.11) | Among the **first active systems**. Utilitarian look compared with retractable bollards or rotating wedges. Provision must be made for the passage of bicycles, cars and emergency vehicles. Like every active barrier, it must be **attended at all times** |
| Mobile wedge (Figure 9.10) | A medium pickup truck places it in **15 minutes**. Useful in a planned temporary system under elevated threat |
| Wedge mounting | On the surface or in an excavation about **18 in** deep. There are shallow foundation systems rated **ASTM M50 P1** or **Department of State K12**. Raised height: **21–38 in (0.5–1 m)**. Standard width: **10 ft (3 m)** |
| Rotating wedge (Figure 9.12) | Action similar to the rising wedge, but with a **curved face** (better appearance) and embedded at **greater depth**. Height **24–28 in (0.6–0.7 m)**; standard width **10 ft (3 m)**. Driven by heavy **hydraulic cylinders**. About **3 seconds per movement** |

- **Exam note:** the book converts 18 in as 0.48 m; it is actually 0.46 m.
- **Exam note:** Figure 9.12 says "Rotating Edge Barrier"; it is a typo for rotating **wedge** barrier.

**Maintenance and operation of active barriers**

- Retractable bollards, and especially wedges, require careful maintenance and monitoring.
- Data sheets give **cycles per unit of time** or **cycles between maintenance**. Take them seriously and consider the expected use.
- Key question: is the barrier normally down and deployed only in the face of a threat, or is it normally deployed and lowered for each authorized vehicle?
- The difference can be **1 cycle a month versus hundreds of cycles a day**, with a large effect on power consumption and maintenance.
- Draft operating procedures (standard operating procedures or post orders) that set: when and how they are deployed; notification requirements; periodic test protocols; maintenance intervals.

**Security planters**

- Decorative planters designed as a barrier; many shapes, sizes, styles and designs.
- They are mounted above or below ground level. They are fixed with rebar dowels or larger steel tubes, or with special footings for more protection against impact or explosion.
- Common on the perimeter of commercial, municipal and federal buildings worldwide. They blend into the architecture and can be customized in style and level of protection.
- Figures: 9.13, custom planters in an urban office area (Wausau Tile); 9.14, planters with logos at a sports arena (Wausau Tile); 9.15, cross-section of a planter with a **highway-type barrier core** inside (Stonewear Force Protection).

**Jersey barriers (Figure 9.16)**

- Standardized **precast concrete** element. Created in the **1940s–1950s** as a highway median to keep cars from crossing into the opposite lanes.
- Massive use in security **after 9/11**, as an anti-ram and traffic control barrier against terrorist attacks.
- They were believed to protect through their mass: a **12 ft (3.6 m)** barrier weighs about 5,700 lb (2,585 kg).
- But **resting on the ground they are ineffective** against a vehicle attack. To work they must be **embedded** and have **vertical steel reinforcement anchoring** through the barrier to the pavement.
- Cons: little adaptability; standard lengths of **12.5 and 20 ft (3.8 and 6 m)**, which makes them inflexible. Poorly installed, the overlaps leave unwanted gaps or narrow sidewalks and streets until they are impassable.
- They serve as a permanent or temporary barrier.
- **Exam note:** the weight example uses a 12 ft barrier, but the standard lengths cited are 12.5 and 20 ft.
- Every barrier system must be well designed, built and maintained, especially active barriers with moving parts (DHS 2011).

**Effective improvised barriers**

| Barrier | Specification | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Heavy machinery tires | **7–8 ft (2.1–2.4 m)** in diameter, **half buried**, with the soil around them well compacted | A **3/4-ton** pickup does not get through |
| Metal posts or railroad ties | Partially buried in concrete; inclined **outward, in the direction of the possible penetration, at 30–45°**; spaced **4 ft (1.2 m)** apart | A vehicle at high speed suffers serious damage and its occupants, severe injuries |

#### 9.1.7 Symbolic and Natural Barriers

- A barrier does not have to be mechanical or manufactured.
- Symbolic and natural ones can be a very effective **alternative or complement** to typical security hardware.

| Type | Examples (all those in the book) | How it acts |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Symbolic | Landscaping; curbs; small decorative fences; changes in floor color or texture | Normally **not a solid physical barrier**. It sends a **psychological signal** that helps control movement or makes anyone who wants to leave the designated routes or zones uncomfortable |
| Natural | (the book gives no examples here) | Usually very effective and **blends into the setting** of the place |

- The topic is expanded in **Chapter 10**.

### 9.2 Containers and Vaults

- Another layer of physical security, often **the closest to the asset**: containers, safes and vaults.
- The intrusion protection of a safe or vault can be measured by its **lock resistance**.

#### 9.2.1 Safes

**Basic ideas**

- That something is called a "safe" does not guarantee that it protects everything stored in it. You must know the characteristics and limits of each type.

|  | Fire safe | Burglary safe |
| --- | --- | --- |
| How it protects | Insulating material that dissipates heat | Thick solid steel walls |
| Weak point | The insulation resists a hammer blow poorly: it is no good against forced entry | Steel quickly transmits heat to the interior: paper is soon destroyed in a fire |

- **UL** (Underwriters Laboratories) has a well-established protocol for testing and rating safes according to the expected protection. Understanding its labels is essential for choosing well.
- The safes in this section are generally **portable**: unanchored, they can be moved with basic equipment.
- Even if burglary-resistant, they can be carried off whole and opened at leisure. **A safe on wheels is not burglary-resistant.**
- In an isolated area with no other protection (alarms, surveillance) it is considered vulnerable.
- **UL requires anchoring any safe under 750 lb (340 kg).**
- Two general categories: 1) **record safes**, fire-resistant records safes; 2) safes for **valuables** against forced penetration.

**Record safes for fire protection**

- Records have changed: more electronic management, offsite and cloud storage. Storing them properly is still necessary and requires the right container.
- They protect, up to a point, with special insulation and construction against heat and flames.
- The insulation **contains moisture** that dissipates heat; this way the interior does not reach the temperature that destroys the records.
- That moisture is **not replenished**: a safe that has been through a fire may not protect afterward.
- The moisture also evaporates over time: a fireproof safe keeps its rating for about **20–30 years**, depending on the climate.

**Labels**

- The level of protection is read from the product's **UL label**.
- Old products may carry a label from the defunct **SMNA** (Safe Manufacturers National Association). It is usually inside the door or on its upper interior part.
- Distrust "ratings" from "manufacturer testing" or "built to UL class specifications". There was probably no independent test and they perform worse than with a UL label or one from another recognized independent laboratory.
- **Exam note:** the book gives as an example "UL 687, burglary safe standard" within the fire section. The fire standard for records equipment that the book itself cites (9.2.2) is **UL 72**.
- UL used **letters until 1972**. Since then the label indicates the type of container and the level of protection.
- Today it indicates whether it is a fire-resistant safe or an insulated records container, and the **hours of protection**.
- Example **"350–4 Hours" (350-4 HR)**: with the exterior at **1,700 °F (927 °C)**, the interior does not reach **350 °F (177 °C)** for **at least 4 hours**. At 350 °F almost all paper is damaged.
- The same classes are in **NFPA 232**, Standard for the Protection of Records (NFPA 2012). NFPA = National Fire Protection Association.

**Figure 9.17: pre-1972 and current equivalents (fire-resistant safes)**

| SMNA | Previous UL | Current UL |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A | A | 350–4 hours |
| B | B | 350–2 hours |
| C | C | 350–1 hour |

**Three UL classes of records protection equipment**

| Class | For which media | Maximum interior | Maximum relative humidity inside |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 350 | Paper and paper products | 350 °F (177 °C) | 65% |
| 150 | Magnetic media, tapes, discs and photographic materials | 150 °F (66 °C) | 80% |
| 125 | Floppy discs and similar flexible computer media | 125 °F (52 °C) | **Pending:** the book does not give it |

- In class 150, **temperature and humidity** matter. More humidity (80%) is allowed than in class 350 (65%).
- More humidity = more protection against temperature rise: the interior stays cooler for the same time.
- In addition, paper is more sensitive to humidity than magnetic, photographic or disc media.

**Fire-resistant safes (fireproof safes for paper)**

- Three classes: **350-4, 350-2 and 350-1**, according to the hours they protect paper in a fire.
- All three must pass **fire, explosion and impact** tests:

| Test | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| Fire endurance | The interior temperature is recorded: it may not exceed 350 °F (177 °C) at the limit of 4, 2 or 1 h. At the end, all papers **legible** and not crumbling when taken out and examined |
| Impact (drop test) | Maintain the same temperature conditions after **dropping 30 ft (9.1 m)**. Simulates falling **three stories** in a collapse |
| Explosion | Must not explode when suddenly exposed to **2,000 °F (1,093 °C)** |

- The same impact and explosion tests apply to records protection equipment (see electronic records, below).

**Insulated filing devices**

- **Class 350-1 (formerly Class D)** and **Class 350-½ (formerly Class E)**. They protect considerably less than the three fire-resistant safes.
- In the test, the thermocouples go in the **center** of the compartment. In fire-resistant safes they go **1 in** from the sides and the door.
- They are not designed for impact and do not pass the drop test (fire-resistant safes and insulated records containers do).
- **Exam note:** it is very easy to confuse the **350-1 insulated filing device** with the **350-1 fire-resistant safe** and the **350-1 insulated records container**. The label says which it is: fire-resistant safe, insulated record container or insulated filing device (or file drawer).
- They only protect against total burnout in **fire-resistant buildings** with little combustible material around.
- **NFPA 220** defines a fire-resistive building: all its structural elements (columns, beams, floors and roof) are of approved noncombustible or limited-combustible materials, with specified resistances.
- Do not place them on a floor that could collapse: on falling they may open and damage the contents.
- **Exam note:** the book writes "first-resistant safes"; it is a typo for fire-resistant.

**Electronic Data Processing Record Protection**

- Magnetic media deteriorate from **150 °F (66 °C)**, and sooner with humidity above 80%. A container for paper is **not enough**.
- You need one that withstands extreme heat and high humidity.
- **"Safe within a safe"** option: insulated, sealed inner compartment for the media, inside an outer safe with a thick insulation wall.
- It protects against **125 °F (52 °C) or 150 °F (66 °C) and 80% humidity** for 4, 3, 2 or 1 hour. Other models achieve the same without that design.
- Classes 150 and 125: the label must say **"Record Protection Equipment"** and indicate the class and the time (1 to 4 h).

**Figure 9.18: UL designations and labels of fire-resistant containers**

| Container type | Designations |
| --- | --- |
| Fire-resistant safes, paper | 350-4 (4 h); 350-2 (2 h); 350-1 (1 h) |
| Insulated records containers, electronic media | 150-4 (4 h); 150-3 (3 h); 150-2 (2 h); 150-1 (1 h); 125-3 (3 h); 125-2 (2 h); 125-1 (1 h) |
| Insulated filing devices | Class 350-1 or ½; Class 150-1 or ½; Class 125-1 or ½ (1 h or ½ h) |
| Insulated file drawers | Class 350-1; Class 150-1; Class 125-1 (1 h) |

- **Exam note:** the text gives 4, 3, 2 or 1 hour for 125 °F and 150 °F, but Figure 9.18 does not include 125-4.

**Safes designed to protect valuables**

- They are rated with test data and specifications from the **ISO** (Insurance Services Office) and UL.
- **They do not protect against fire.** If both are needed: put the valuables container inside a larger fire-resistant safe or vault, or use a safe rated for fire and burglary.
- Their interior is smaller than that of records safes: money and valuables take up less space than papers.
- So that it is not carried off: install it in a **steel-clad concrete block** or anchor it well.

**UL Ratings and Performance Requirements**

| Concept | What it requires |
| --- | --- |
| Net working time | The safe resisted entry for **15 net min**. Entry = opening the door or making a 6-inch square opening through the door or front. Tools: common hand tools, drills, punches, hammers and pressure devices. Rule: **"when the tool leaves the safe, the clock stops"**. There are **more than 50 types of attack**; technicians usually test only 2 or 3, depending on what they know about the product |
| Safe construction requirements | UL **Group II, 1 or 1R** combination lock; it may have a UL-rated **"Type 1"** high-security electronic lock. Minimum weight **750 lb**, or instructions for anchoring it in a larger safe, a concrete block or the premises. Walls equivalent to **≥ 1 in of open-hearth steel** with tensile strength **≥ 50,000 PSI** (pounds per square inch). Joints equivalent to **¼ in** continuous penetration weld of the same steel. One hole **≤ ¼ in** for electrical conductors, with no direct line of sight to the door or the mechanism |
| Performance requirements | Body and door resist entry (opening the door or a 6-inch square opening through body, door or front) for **30 net min**. Tools: common hand tools, picking tools, portable mechanical or electric tools, grinding points, carbide drills, pressure devices, abrasive discs and power saws |

- **Exam note:** the book does not label these rows. The 15 min row corresponds to TL-15 and the 30 min row to TL-30.

**Common Misunderstanding of UL Ratings**

- It is believed that **5 min (RSC), 15 min (TL-15) and 30 min (TL-30)** are how long it takes to open the safe. **That is false.**
- A professional safe cracker, with knowledge, tools and time, opens it after several hours. It depends on their skill, what they know about the construction and luck.
- **Any safe can be opened.** The higher the UL rating, the more time it costs.
- **TL-30:** construction identical to TL-15. Same test except for the time (30 min) and two more tools: abrasive discs and power saws.
- The TL-30 label means they could not open the door or make the 6-inch square opening in 30 min.
- The test engineers have the **manufacturing drawings** and can take the safe apart before testing it.
- **TL-30X6:** the TL-30 test done on **all six sides**. Combination safe with maximum body and door protection on 6 sides against common hand-held mechanical and electric tools, and their combinations.

**Figure 9.19: how to read UL ratings**

| Code | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| TL | Tool Resistant |
| TR | Torch Resistant |
| X6 | Test done on all six sides of the safe |
| 15, 30 or 60 | Minutes of the test |

- Example **TRTL-30-X6**: torch- and tool-resistant on all six sides. It is among the highest burglary ratings on the market.
- According to the book, the highest of all is **TRTL-60-X6**: it withstands torch, explosives (**8 oz of nitroglycerin**) and tools (prying and other striking) on all 6 sides for **1 hour**.
- **Exam note:** in UL 687 resistance to explosives is marked with **TX**; the highest is **TXTL-60X6** (outside the book). The book attributes the explosives to TRTL-60-X6.

**Burglary-Resistant Containers**

- They resist an attack with tools, torch or explosives, given enough time to work.
- They are usually solid steel plate, or steel with a composite fill such as concrete.
- They are divided by level of protection and tests passed:

| Category | Construction or test | Is there a test? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| B-Rate | Industry catch-all for almost any safe with a lock. Unwritten standard: **¼ in body, ½ in door**. Today many use **1/8 in** in the body (or make both 1/8 and ¼) to cut costs because of the price of steel and shipping. When buying, check the lock work, hard plates and relockers | No |
| RSC (Residential Security Container) | UL rating: **5 net min**, on all sides, with several tools. UL tests and certifies the manufacturer. Combination or key unit against common mechanical tools; the whole unit is tested. Base standard: **UL 1037**, Anti-theft Alarms and Devices | Yes |
| UL 1037 rule applied to the RSC | The device resists **≥ 5 min** of attack; the disassembly needed to remove it counts within those 5 min. Tools: hammers, chisels, adjustable wrenches, pry bars, punches and screwdrivers. Hammers with head of **≤ 3 lb**; no tool **over 18 in**. Product mounted in its position; attacked by **a single operator** | Yes |
| RSC II | Like the RSC, but **10 min**, **2 technicians** and more aggressive tools: picks, high-speed carbide drills and pressure devices. They try for a "six-square-inch" opening in the door or front | Yes |
| B/C-Rate | Catch-all: body **≥ ¼ in**, door **½ in**, plus layers of **10 or 12 gauge** metal with fireproof composite material. Check lock work, relockers and the rest | No |
| C-Rate | **½ in** steel safe with **1 in** door and lock. Check lock work and relockers | No |
| TL-15 | Passed the standard **UL 687** tests, with the same tools and almost always the same engineers | Yes |

- **Exam note:** RSC II says "six-square-inch opening" (6 in²), while TL speaks of "6-inch square" (6 × 6 in). Possible typo.
- **Exam note:** the text says "see TL-15 and TL-30 below", but those descriptions come earlier.

**Burglary/Fire-Resistive Containers**

- Since **1975** there have been single containers that pass the UL fire and burglary tests.
- Burglary: **30 min or 1 h**. Fire: **1 or 2 h**.
- Thick insulation around the contents, with no gaps through which heat can enter.
- High tensile strength steel body, electrically welded. Doors of that steel and of armor plate, against electric carbide drills, abrasive discs and other tools.
- Insurers look at the rating when setting premiums: the right safe can **lower the premium**. Sometimes the annual savings soon equal the cost of the equipment.

**Fire tests of class 350 safes (according to the book)**

| Rating | Heating | Interior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ½ h | ½ h up to **1,550 °F** exterior | < 350 °F during heating and cooling |
| 1 h | 1 h up to 1,550 °F, then cooling | < 350 °F throughout the test |
| 2 h | 2 h up to 1,550 °F, then cooling | Does not exceed 350 °F in the whole process |

- Paper begins to char at about **410 °F**; that is why the interior limit is 350 °F.
- After the test time: it is taken out of the furnace, hoisted **30 ft (3 stories)** and dropped onto concrete rubble.
- It is left to cool and put into a furnace preheated to **2,000 °F**. This is the **explosion test**: to see whether the door pops open. The contents must remain intact.
- **Exam note:** here the book gives 1,550 °F exterior for ½, 1 and 2 h; earlier it gave 1,700 °F (927 °C) for 350-4. (outside the book) The standard curve rises with time: 1,550 °F at ½ h, 1,700 °F at 1 h, 1,850 °F at 2 h and 2,000 °F at 4 h.
- **Exam note:** earlier the book treats drop and explosion as separate tests; here it chains them.
- **Exam note:** this list includes a ½ h class 350 safe. According to Figure 9.18, ½ h exists only in insulated filing devices.

**GSA-Approved Safes**

- The **GSA** (U.S. General Services Administration) tested and approved **six classes** of safes.
- They protect against **forced or surreptitious** entry and serve to store **classified government information**. They are not intended for valuables.
- The standards are at www.gsa.gov.

#### 9.2.2 Vaults

- **Vault:** room or area of special construction to limit access and protect the assets it contains. In general, it prevents **forced entry and removal** of the asset.
- The term also covers rooms made to protect the contents from **fire**, not necessarily from theft.

|  | Burglary vault | Fire vault |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cost | Higher | Generally lower |
| Construction standards | Very different from each other | Very different from each other |
| Burglary protection | High | Much lower |

- To choose which standards to follow (fire or security), the owner considers: 1) the asset and its vulnerability; 2) the custodian's legal liability under requirements of a regulator, an insurer or a government agency.

**Fire-Resistive Vaults**

- Main standard in the US: **NFPA 232**. **It does not consider forced entry.**
- Key factor: whether the vault will be in a fire-resistant building. According to **NFPA 220**, Standard on Types of Building Construction: walls, partitions, columns, floors and roofs of noncombustible or limited-combustible material.
- In non-fire-resistant structures **more vault protection** is required.
- Records of exceptional value or rarity: individualized measures, outside existing standards.
- Records essential for reconstructing other records: special protection.
- The vault demands excellent design and construction to withstand everything a fire imposes.
- **Avoid below-grade vaults**, for three reasons: 1) burning or smoldering debris accumulates in the basement and causes a prolonged "cooking effect" that defeats the protection; 2) humidity that lets mold damage the records; 3) flood risk.

**Traditional vault construction**

| Element | Specification |
| --- | --- |
| Reinforced concrete | Steel bars of **≥ 0.5 in (13 mm)** diameter, at **6 in (15.2 cm)** centers, at right angles in both directions. Wire-tied at crossings at no more than **12 in (30.5 cm)** in both directions, and centered in the wall or panel |
| Structural steel frame | Protected with **≥ 4 in (10.2 cm)** of concrete, brick or equivalent. Tied with steel ties or mesh equivalent to **No. 8 ASW gauge** wire with **8 in (20.3 cm)** spacing. The brick is filled solid with concrete down to the steel |

**Minimum wall thickness** (fire resistance depends on thickness)

| Vault | Brick | Reinforced concrete |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4 hours | 12 in (30.5 cm) | 8 in (20.4 cm) |
| 6 hours | 12 in (30.5 cm) | 10 in (25.4 cm) |

- **Exam note:** the book gives 12 in of brick for both 4 and 6 hours; only the concrete changes.

**Media Storage and Protection**

- Magnetic media are usually kept in the same vaults as paper. It is neither practical nor cost-effective to build the whole vault to their level: better an **additional container inside the vault**.
- Store them according to the manufacturer's specifications and/or industry standards.
- Long-term retention: update the media (software, versions) so the data can be recovered. Keep an inventory and a backup, preferably **off-site**.
- Digital media (memory sticks, USB, CDs and DVDs): safe that withstands **238 °F (≈114 °C) or more**.
- Backups, internal hard drives and tapes are **the most vulnerable**: safe that withstands **150 °F (≈66 °C) or more**.
- Hard drive: maximum non-operating temperature, about **70 °C (≈158 °F)**. It is about 10 degrees more than the operating one, because in use it heats up a lot.
- (outside the book) "Withstand 238 °F or 150 °F" is understood as keeping the interior below that temperature, like the UL 150 and 125 classes.

**Data safes**

| Rating | Protects | Interior during furnace and cooling |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 h – UL class 150 | Computer data 1 h | < 150 °F |
| 2 h – UL class 150 | Computer data 2 h | < 150 °F |
| 1 h – UL class 125 | Computer data 1 h | < 125 °F |

**Standards for media storage (full list)**

| Organization | Standard |
| --- | --- |
| ANSI (American National Standards Institute) | ANSI IT9.11, Standard for Imaging Media — Processed Safety Photographic Film Storage |
| ASTM (American Society for Testing and Materials) | ASTM E 119, Test Method for Fire Test of Building Constructive Materials |
| FM (Factory Mutual Research Corporation) | FM Approval Class 4200, Storage of Records and Valuables |
| NFPA (National Fire Protection Association) | NFPA 232, Standard for the Protection of Records; NFPA 40, Standard for the Handling and Storage of Cellulose Nitrate Film Records; NFPA 232AM, Standard for Fire Risk Evaluation of Structures Containing Vital Records; NFPA 75, Standard for the Protection of Electronic Computer/Data Processing Equipment |
| UL (Underwriters Laboratories) | UL 72, Test for Fire Resistance of Record Protection Equipment; UL 155, Tests for Fire Resistance of Vault and File Room Doors |

**Vaults for Protection Against Forced Entry**

- Location: **avoid an exterior wall**. It would give the intruder a convenient point to attack from outside.
- All **six surfaces** must protect equally. Typical mistake: installing a heavy door and assuming the concrete walls around it are enough.
- Concrete looks strong, but it is drilled through quickly with modern tools. Those walls are designed for structural loads, not to delay.
- Concrete is brittle and resists tension and bending poorly: it is relatively easy to penetrate.
- **Never use unreinforced concrete** against forced penetration.
- Reinforcement with deformed bar (**rebar**). It is numbered in multiples of **1/8 in (3 mm)** of diameter: **No. 3 = 3/8 in (10 mm)**.
- The current ASTM specification for building vaults **requires rebar**.
- All surfaces are vulnerable. **A vault only delays, it does not stop**, a determined intruder.
- It is impossible to build an impenetrable vault. It is designed to resist for a **defined period**. Even the best concrete yields to someone with skill, personnel and tools.
- Cast-in-place concrete vault: **walls, roof and floor of at least 12 in** to delay enough, provided there are other **detection** countermeasures.

**Penetration Techniques**

- **Explosives**: very effective against concrete. Shock waves travel through all of it and cause internal fragmentation and splaying of both faces.
- Steel reinforcement **increases the delay**: the explosion breaks the concrete, but the rebar usually remains intact and has to be removed.
- The gauge of the rebar greatly influences protection:

| Rebar | Tool needed to cut it |
| --- | --- |
| No. 4 or smaller | Handheld bolt cutters |
| No. 5 to No. 8 | Motorized hydraulic shear, cutting torch or thermal lance (burning bar) |
| Larger than No. 8 | Only torch, thermal lance, power saw or explosives |

**Wall Construction and Penetration**

- Advantage of the thick reinforced concrete wall: it forces the use of sophisticated tools and explosives, and more supplies (e.g., acetylene tanks).
- Because of the tools, supplies, noise, smoke and heat, the attack must be made in a **hidden place**.
- Cases: buildings next to banks have been rented as a front to dig a tunnel and enter through the floor.
- A gang rented a building across from a bank and dug under the street. The vault had an alarm: it sounded when the floor was drilled and they were discovered. Lesson: alarm the floor as well.
- But if the vault has a **time lock**, like modern bank vaults, it does not open until the programmed time even if the alarm sounds. This makes it hard to isolate an attempt through the floor.
- Solution: **CCTV** (closed-circuit television) with audio and lighting **inside the vault**. It allows observation and, if recorded, leaves a record of the intrusion.

**Vulnerabilities of Vaults**

- Poor **opening and closing policies and procedures** are often the undoing of the best-designed vaults.
- The **two-man rule** with a combination lock is vulnerable.
- Case: disc lock with a **three-position changeable combination**. They thought it had 4 numbers because they counted the **opening index** (the last one) as part of the combination.
- They gave the first two numbers to one person and "third and fourth" to another. Since there are only 3 numbers, the first could deduce the rest in **fewer than 40 attempts**.
- Common practice: limits on the first and last number of the combination (see the Exam note below). The gates of the lock wheels are **two digits wide**.
- **Exam note:** the book says not to put the first and last number "above 10 or below 90". It is inverted: the logical thing is to avoid numbers **below 10 or above 90** (outside the book).
- If a locksmith does not change the combination and the instructions are not followed exactly, the lock can end up **set to a single number**. It opens by dialing the last number and turning to the opening index. A simple and serious error from not following the dialing sequence.
- **Test:** the custodian turns the dial **three times to the left**, stops at the **third number** and then turns **right to the opening index**. If the bolt opens, the combination is on a single number: **change it immediately**.
- Another error in the case: each person stood next to the door while the other dialed and could see their part.
- Another vulnerability: the **escape mechanism** (handle or release inside the vault).
- Resistance ratings are **invalidated** if any door, fan or other port is installed without following the manufacturer's instructions.
- A trusted locksmith must, periodically: 1) inspect the locking mechanism; 2) perform preventive maintenance on lock and door; 3) check that there are no unauthorized modifications.

### 9.3 Locks and Locking Mechanisms

**Central idea**

- The lock secures the movable parts of a barrier (doors, windows, covers).
- It is the most widely used method of controlling physical access.
- It should not be the only protection for an important area: with skill and time, any lock can be defeated.
- Supplement it with periodic rounds by security officers or with electronic security systems.
- Uses cited: homes, vehicles, offices, hotels, safes, desks, cabinets, file cabinets, briefcases, display cases, jewelry boxes and computer systems.

**Two general classes**

| Class | Principle | Associated with |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mechanical | Physical parts only | Key or combination |
| Electrified | Electrical power + mechanical operation | Automated access control systems |

#### 9.3.1 Mechanical Locks

- **Definition:** an arrangement of physical parts that acts as a barrier and prevents the bolt or latch from being opened.

**Four functional sets**

| Component | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Bolt or latch | Joins the movable part (door, window) with the fixed part (jamb, frame) |
| Keeper or strike | Housing the bolt enters. It is not an integral part of the mechanism |
| Tumbler array | Barrier or maze that must be overcome to move the bolt |
| Key or unlocking device | Overcomes the barrier and operates the bolt |

- Normally: the bolt and the barrier are in the installed hardware (lockset); the key is separate.
- Exception: locks with physical logic devices, which form a single piece:
  - integrated digital keypad that mechanically releases the bolt with the correct sequence;
  - dial combination lock.

##### Primary Types

| Type | How it works | Uses | Exam points |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Warded lock** | The first and oldest. Open keyhole that can be seen through; long barrel-type key | Old houses, farms, old inns. Very simple | Weak against spring manipulation with any key the wards do not stop, and against corrosion from weather and age. A modern program does not include it: phased replacement or reinforce with other locks |
| **Lever lock** | 18th century. Flat metal levers on a common pivot, held by a wire spring. Each lever has a cut, opposite the pivot, for a projection of the bolt called the **fence**. If all of them line up, the fence enters and the bolt retracts | Desks, cabinets, lockers, bank safe-deposit boxes, U.S. mail boxes | More secure than the warded lock. With two or more fence cuts per lever, several keys open it → allows master keying. Inherently vulnerable to picking, but can be designed to be resistant. Do not confuse with the lever handle |
| **Pin tumbler lock** | 19th century, Linus Yale (who also created the dial combination lock). Same elements: mechanism that moves the bolt, maze and keyway; the maze changes | The most widely used in the U.S. on exterior and interior building doors | The most important advance in the history of the mechanical lock. With recent improvements it reaches very high security |
| **Wafer tumbler lock** | Fairly late development. Flat wafers (metal or other material) lock the plug to the shell | — | Allows master keying and double-bitted keys |
| **Dial combination lock** | Keyless. Similar to the lever lock: aligns the gates of the wheels so that the bolt's fence enters. Wheels are fully circular and interdependent: moving one moves the others | Safes and containers | The order of movement matters → it is a true **combination** lock, not a **permutation** lock |

**Pin tumbler: conventional cylinder vs. high security**

- Conventional: key pins evenly spaced in a single row; usually 5, 6 or 7 pins.
- When master keying, split pins are added and the number of possible key changes drops considerably.
- High security cylinder: interlocking pins and drivers. Picks or uncoded keys do not align them.
  - The keys are cut with precise depth and angle.
  - The key raises each pin to the shear line and at the same time rotates it.
  - In this way the interlock passes through a special slot at the base of the plug, and the plug turns enough to move the bolt.
- **Medeco** (variant): instead of a slot at the base of the plug it uses a **side bar**.
  - The bar enters a recess in the shell or retracts into slots in the pins.
- In both high security types, copying the key is very difficult except with special equipment from the manufacturer.

**Number of combinations (dial combination locks)**

- Theoretical maximum = positions per wheel raised to the number of wheels.
- A good quality padlock usually has 100 positions per wheel.

```latex
N_{\max} = p^{t} \qquad p = 100,\; t = 4 \;\Rightarrow\; N_{\max} = 100^{4} = 100{,}000{,}000
```

- Exam note: in the PDF text the exponent appears as "1004"; it is 100 raised to the 4th power (10^8). The figure of 100,000,000 is correct.

**Electronic combination locks**

- They replace the mechanical dial on safes and secure document cabinets.
- They are powered by the turning of the dial by the user.
- The numbers appear on an LCD (liquid crystal display) or other display, not on the dial markings.
- The display can only be seen from a limited angle.
- The number dialed bears no relation to the physical position of the dial.
- Additional functions:
  - mandatory wait (time-out) of a certain number of seconds between each number;
  - two-person rule: two numbers must be dialed to open;
  - own number per user → audit trail of which combination opened it and when;
  - it memorizes failed attempts.
- Because of their electronic precision, no combinations are lost to unusable adjacent numbers.
- They are claimed to resist all the typical attacks on mechanical combinations and also electrical and magnetic ones.

##### Master Keying

- **Principle:** several different keys open the same lock. Each acts on different tumblers or on different aspects of the same ones.
- It creates an **access hierarchy**: one lock → increasingly larger groups → all the locks in the population.
- **Rationale:** with hundreds or thousands of locks, it would be impractical for someone with broad access to carry one key per lock.
- Master groups are formed: a single key opens its entire group.
- Three security problems, which must be weighed against the supposed convenience:

| # | Problem | Detail and mitigation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Control of master keys (accountability) | Losing, compromising or using a master key without permission exposes its entire group. Restricted-access key cabinets and PC software help |
| 2 | More points for surreptitious opening | Several shear lines or gate openings are created |
| 3 | More maintenance (in cylinders) | The extra master pieces, often fragile, get displaced or break; the lock jams and has to be disassembled |

##### Security Vulnerabilities of Mechanical Locks

- Some attacks require a great deal of skill; others are within reach of amateurs and vandals.
- According to the book, **rim-mounted, mortise and vertical rod** locks offer optimal security.

| Attack route | What it consists of |
| --- | --- |
| Attack by force | Separating the door from the jamb; pulling the lock out of its housing and manipulating the bolt; breaking the pins so the plug turns freely |
| Surreptitious attack | Picking or manipulation with tools |
| Impression-making and try keys | Objective: a facsimile key. (a) Impression: insert a blank key of the correct profile and force it to turn so that slight marks are left before cutting it. (b) Try keys or "jingle keys": blanks well milled for that keyway with random cuts; they are turned and "raked" until the lock opens |

##### Rearranging Mechanical Locks

- **When:** change of authorized personnel; system compromise; loss or loss of control of keys; major changes in occupancy.

| Method | How | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Relocation | Rotate locks between doors, between cabinets, etc. | Only works if the lock carries no markings that a former key holder would recognize |
| 2. Reordering the tumblers (most effective) | Levers and wafers: disassemble and change the order. Pin tumbler: the same pins in other positions | The same tumblers are reused many times |
| 3. Interchangeable / convertible core | The core is changed on site for another one already combinated | Very quick redistribution; offered by some pin tumbler manufacturers |

#### 9.3.2 Electrified Locking Mechanisms

- They lock and unlock the door from a remote device: electric button, motion sensor, card reader or digital keypad.
- **Boolean logic:** organizes conditions into sets. Example: "if A and B are closed, C can be opened".
  - Useful in mantraps and other high security arrangements.
- When analyzing failures and ways of defeating the lock, also include the remote-control devices.

##### Fail safe vs. fail secure

- They are used mainly in relation to fire and life safety codes.
- They apply to doors that must be open throughout occupancy or only during a detected fire.
- The codes require exit with **a single action, no special knowledge**.
  - Exceptions: banks, jewelry stores and other high security environments.
  - It is a single action: turning the handle; pushing the exit bar (panic bar).
  - It is not a single action: pressing a button, turning a key, using a reader or entering a code before turning the handle.
- Local fire codes vary by municipality: check them before specifying the lock.

|  | Fail safe | Fail secure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| In case of failure | It **opens** | It **stays locked** |
| Failure considered | The most common: power outage. Also failure of the mechanism or of a connected control | Power outage or other failure |
| When it is used | Almost all (not all) locks on code-required exits, for free exit if a blackout and a fire coincide. Required by the IBC (International Building Code) and the NFPA LSC (National Fire Protection Association, Life Safety Code) when exiting requires special knowledge, e.g. electromagnets (Maglocks) | On the egress route, only if free exit is guaranteed whatever the state of power or controls |
| Example | Electromagnet | Electrified exit bar: when pushed it opens mechanically, with or without power; therefore it does not need to be fail safe |

- Exam note: the book writes "fail safe" and "failsafe" interchangeably; it is the same concept.
- Exam note (outside the book): the NFPA LSC is the NFPA 101 standard.

##### Continuous Duty vs. Intermittent Duty

| Continuous duty | Intermittent duty |
| --- | --- |
| Designed to be energized for long periods | Designed to be energized for short periods |
| Sturdier construction; more resistant to physical attack, especially in electric strikes | Less robust |
| Use when an attack on the lock is expected | — |

##### Door States

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Open | Ajar |
| Closed | Together, but no hardware prevents it from being pushed. Depending on the case, it can meet the fire code |
| Latched | Sealed: it is opened by turning the handle or releasing the latch, not by pushing. A change in air pressure does not open it |
| Locked | It does not open without a key or credential; once unlocked, the handle must be turned to unlatch it |

##### Fire Rating

- Each door assembly (door + frame) has a fire resistance rating; it is set by building codes.
- Installing electrified hardware, or drilling the door or frame for wiring, can affect that rating.
- The hardware that goes on fire-rated openings, frames and doors must also be fire-rated.
- Rated openings must be **latched even when unlocked**.

##### Locking Devices (lists from the book)

- Electronic access control can lock and unlock by electrical means alone, with keypads and readers, together with electromagnets.

| Magnetic locks | Electronic locks | Mechanical locking devices |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Continuous duty: shear locks and direct hold | Electronic locks | Grade 1: 800,000 cycles, heavy commercial |
| Continuous duty with delayed egress, direct hold | Electric strikes | Grade 2: 400,000 cycles, light commercial and heavy residential |
|  | Mortise-style | Grade 3: 200,000 cycles, most residential |
|  | Cylindrical-style | Panic and latching hardware |
|  | Electric doorknobs and deadbolts | Dead bolts |
|  |  | Mortise and cylindrical |

- Exam note: in the book, "Examples of Electronic Locks include" appears as a bullet inside the list of electromagnets (a formatting error). The mechanical list mixes durability grades with hardware types.
- Exam note (outside the book): grades 1 to 3 correspond to the ANSI/BHMA (American National Standards Institute / Builders Hardware Manufacturers Association) classification; the book does not name the standard.

##### Primary Types of Electrified Locking Mechanisms

| Type | Operation | Advantages and uses | Limitations and precautions |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Electric deadbolt** | The oldest and simplest. A solenoid (electromagnet) moves a bolt, normally in the frame, in or out of a strike in the door | Can be fail safe (opens when power is removed) or fail secure (stays locked) | Not recommended on doors that must open on their own with the fire alarm: the bolt binds if people push the door when power is cut (panic). There are tapered bolts to prevent binding; consult local codes |
| **Electric latch** | Also with a solenoid, in the frame, with a strike in the door. Uses a beveled latch | Advantage over the deadbolt: it does not have to be retracted to close; the spring yields as it passes over the strike | — |
| **Electric strike** | Complement to any mechanical lock. A solenoid releases or blocks the keeper | Widely used for years in apartment buildings, offices and stores. **It is not a lock**: it works with one | The handle is fixed on the controlled side; access is remote (interior button, reader or keypad). If the interior handle turns, exit is free; if both are fixed, exit is made with the same devices. With a cylinder, it opens with a key |
| **Electric lockset** | Normal mortise lock, electrified to control whether the handle turns | Increasingly popular in access control. Storeroom function: fixed exterior handle controlled by a reader; interior handle always free for exit | The door must be drilled and the cable run through the hinge or around it. Option: a sensor that, when the interior handle is turned, sends a request-to-exit to shunt the magnetic contact or the local siren. Requires a 4-wire transfer hinge instead of 2 |
| **Exit device** (panic bar / crash bar) | On egress routes of high-occupancy buildings. Rim: barely modifies the door. Mortise: mechanism in the edge.
