Vertical rod: double doors without a fixed frame or mullion; rods to the header and to the floor, exposed or concealed | Can be electrified for remote re-entry (button or reader/keypad) | See delayed egress below |
| **Electromagnetic lock** (magnetic lock, Maglock) | Electromagnet + metal armature. When energized, it attracts the armature and holds the door. Usually mounted on the header; on the side it is called **direct hold**: it reduces the clear opening but is much more secure | No moving parts; low maintenance if the surfaces are clean and aligned and power is guaranteed | Inherently fail safe. In high security, backup power. Force of 500 to 2,000 lb (227 to 907 kg); several can be installed per door. The best ones monitor the magnet-armature bond and the door position, against the trick of a non-metallic sheet between the two |

**Delayed egress**

- A compromise between life safety and asset protection; for emergency-exit-only doors.
- When the bar is pushed, a delay of **15 or 30 seconds** begins; afterwards the door opens.
- Requirements:
  - special signage that warns of the delay;
  - connection to the fire alarm system;
  - no delay in a fire or other defined life safety emergency.
- It does not usually give time to intercept the thief, but it activates a local alarm and reports it to a monitoring center.
- Video identifies the perpetrator and what he is carrying, and records the incident.
- On the electromagnet, a switch detects the push and starts the countdown; the same precautions apply.
- Electromagnets and electric bolts or strikes can be combined. Example: an area with entry and exit control that must allow free exit in an emergency.
- These locks are regulated according to the occupancy or occupancy group and its risk, in the IBC and the NFPA LSC.

##### Wireless Locks

- Useful where wiring is impractical or aesthetics matter; in large projects they save cable and labor.
- The lock is replaced with a **battery-powered** electrified one with an integrated reader (proximity or keypad).
- It almost always communicates by Wi-Fi with a hub.
- The hub connects to the LAN (local area network) or directly to the access controller, depending on the manufacturer.
- Battery life depends on the number of card reads and openings.
- Styles: cylindrical, mortise and electrified trim for exit bars.
- Exam note: the book says "number of card readers"; from context it refers to card reads.

#### 9.3.3 Designing Security Locking Systems

- Despite software-based access control, the mechanical key lock is still the most widely used in corporate and institutional facilities.
  - Reasons: low cost, simple operation and reliability.
- If the threat or risk justifies it, use high security locks and restricted keys.
  - Objective: to resist system compromise or picking by a skilled adversary.
- Everything is lost if the system is not planned, managed and installed professionally:
  - losing a master key can force the replacement of all the cylinders (thousands of dollars);
  - failing to control the issue and return of keys can cause losses of millions.
- ALOA (Associated Locksmiths of America) certifications: Registered Locksmith, Certified Locksmith and Certified Master Locksmith. They indicate the locksmith's knowledge and level.

**What a locking system controls**

- The opening of doors or portals: building perimeter, room, enclosure, confidential file, container, file cabinet or safe.
- The use of equipment (e.g., cash register operations).
- Door operators.
- Loose assets (e.g., security cables for portable computer equipment).
- Every application must be included in the plan.

**Evaluate the entire area**

- Consider the strength of all the barriers that give access: door, frame, walls, windows, ceiling and floor.
- Examples from the book:
  - an expensive lock on a hollow wooden door: it deters the amateur, but does not stop the professional (it only leaves evidence);
  - a strong metal door in a weak frame or in a sheet rock wall: it does not stop the determined intruder.

**Professional's preliminary questions (8)**

1. What assets must be protected (people, structure, equipment, negotiables, operations, information) and how much are they worth?
2. What threats exist and how likely are they to occur?
3. Who needs access and how often?
4. How much flexibility must the system have?
5. What impact will the controls have on normal operations?
6. How much weight should be given to the organization's culture or image?
7. Are there staff and skills to plan, design, implement, operate and maintain, or must it be outsourced?
8. What budget is there to implement and, often more important, to operate and maintain?

##### The Design Plan

- A locking system is a coordinated arrangement of elements that support and complement each other.
- The plan considers:
  1. different, simultaneous levels of control for enclosed spaces;
  2. the likelihood that those levels will change over time;
  3. users who need common access in some spaces and exclusive access in others;
  4. the possible loss, compromise or damage of keys, cards or tokens;
  5. effective means of adapting the system to planned and unforeseen changes.
- **It is designed, not just installed.** Adding or removing locks "as needed" is not a locking concept.
- Without planning, security degrades to mere **privacy**. In addition:
  - unauthorized persons get in;
  - control of the devices issued is lost;
  - management by access levels is sacrificed.
- Quick test:
  - if it is not clear who accesses what → **it is not secure**;
  - if there are no multiple levels of access → **it is not convenient**;
  - in both cases → **it is not economical** (money is misapplied).
- Security vs. convenience tension: more convenience usually subtracts security, and vice versa. The balance comes from defining needs well at the outset.
- Choose the mechanism suited to each application. Example: the **latch bolt** extends by spring and retracts by pressure on its end or with a lever. This distinguishes it from the **deadbolt** and the **dead latch**.
- They can be mechanical or electronic, with codes or with personal physiological characteristics (biometrics). The planning criteria are common to all.

##### Lock System Planning Considerations (7)

1. Total number of locks.
2. Main categories or sectors of the system.
3. Security objectives.
4. Size and turnover of the population.
5. Related or supporting security subsystems.
6. Intelligence or information requirements.
7. Criticality of asset exposure.

##### Locking Policy

- The program must be based on a **written policy**, especially with many employees or many sites.
- The policy must:
  1. require a systematic approach to the use of locks for security purposes;
  2. assign specific responsibility for the program: to the security department if there is one; if not, to any responsible manager;
  3. hold accountable for compliance everyone who uses or has access to keys, locks, access devices or combinations.
- That compliance is enforced through the chain of command and evaluated in the salary review.

### 9.4 Lighting and Security Applications

- Disciplines involved: lighting science and technology; electrical systems; power sources; design of luminaires and housings (aesthetics and function); initial and recurring costs; light trespass; environmental factors (e.g., the mercury in lamps).
- There is specific security lighting, but general lighting also affects security.
- **Benefits:** deters crime; supports video surveillance; improves observation of the scene; supports response; distinguishes public from private space; possibly reduces legal liability.
- **Disadvantages:**
  - cost of equipment, installation and maintenance;
  - light pollution and light trespass;
  - luminaires that break the aesthetics or the architectural design;
  - drawing attention to a site that would prefer a low profile.

#### 9.4.1 Types of Lighting Equipment and Lamps

**Security lighting equipment (5)**

| Equipment | Beam | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Streetlight | Downward, circular | — |
| Searchlight | Very narrow and high intensity, on a specific area | Penitentiaries, construction sites, industry; supplement to other lights |
| Floodlight | Medium to wide, over a large area | Commercial, industrial and residential perimeters; usually mounted on buildings near the perimeter |
| Fresnel | Narrow and horizontal | Illuminates the potential intruder and leaves security personnel hidden; common on industrial perimeter fences |
| High mast lighting | — | Parking lots and highways. Height of 70 to 150 ft (21 to 46 m). Main determinant of the light level outside the perimeter |

**Lamps or light sources** (Purpura, Fennelly, Honey & Broder, 2012; ASIS International, 2004)

| Lamp | How it works | Advantages | Disadvantages and notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Incandescent | Current through a tungsten filament that becomes white hot | — | The least efficient, the most expensive to operate and short-lived |
| Halogen, quartz halogen | Incandescent filled with halogen gas | ≈ 25% more efficiency and life than incandescent | — |
| Fluorescent | Electricity through a gas in a glass tube; 40 to 80 lumens | Twice the light and less than half the heat of an incandescent of equal wattage | Does not give high light levels; hardly used outdoors except for signs |
| Mercury vapor | Current through a gas | Long life | Takes several minutes to give full light; poor color rendition for video (bluish light) |
| Metal halide | Gas lamp | Imitates daylight and colors look natural (stadiums); goes well with video surveillance | The most expensive to install and maintain |
| High-pressure sodium | Gas lamp | Efficient and long-lived; streets and parking lots; in fog it lets more detail be seen at greater distance | Poor color rendition |
| Low-pressure sodium | Gas lamp | Even more efficient than high-pressure | Expensive to maintain; poor color rendition for video |
| LED (light-emitting diode) | — | Very cost-efficient alternative; lasts longer without losing illumination | See below |
| Induction | — | Long life | Like fluorescent, mainly indoors; exceptions: covered parking lots, underpasses and tunnels |

- Exam note (outside the book): the fluorescent lamp's "40 to 80 lumens" should be read as lumens **per watt** (efficacy).
- **Infrared (IR):** it may be invisible to the naked eye, but it is useful for illuminating video scenes.
- Each lamp differs in color rendition, service life, consumption and warm-up time.
  - Choose lamp and luminaire carefully, both in security lighting and in general lighting that affects security.

**LED and security** (David Beausoleil, president and founder of CAST Lighting, LLC; Beausoleil, 2014)

- Before: floodlights, wall packs and parking lot poles. "More light was better"; lumens were set by local code or contractor recommendation.
- Almost nothing changed since the 1950s; little attention to glare, placement and levels. Control by dusk-to-dawn photocell, timer, or both.
- LED brought:
  - better light quality, beam patterns and optical performance;
  - lower consumption and longer lamp life;
  - much smaller luminaires (from HID luminaires with ballast to printed circuit boards);
  - more and more lumens per watt.
- With LED "there can be no waste": minimal internal reflector losses, spill and unnecessary light.
- **Key lesson:** over-lighting and glare **reduce** security.
  - Extreme contrast creates black zones where people hide, cameras cannot see and personnel do not observe.
  - The eye takes **20 to 60 seconds** to refocus after glare.
  - The following must be resolved: luminaire placement, light distribution, glare control and lumen level.
- Other lessons: better placement and design; emergence of the wall pack; LED in floodlights, streetlights, high mast and warning signs; more efficiency and better optics.
- It is not a panacea: a poorly placed LED that causes glare saves energy but lights poorly. Design according to the objectives and the other measures.

**24 V perimeter LED**

- Operates at 24 V (instead of the typical 120 V), with lights mounted on a fence or wall.
- Spacing of 10, 20 or 30 ft (≈ 3, 6 or 9 m); gives reflected light without glare.
- The eye sees beyond the light into the darkness; it almost eliminates the contrasts of over-lighting.
- It can improve the image of cameras and Internet monitoring.
- Origin: derived from low-voltage decorative garden lighting.
- Simple installation: it is attached to existing poles and the cable is run with zip ties to the top rail.
- Estimated savings: up to **80%** compared with the traditional high-voltage system (installation, labor, materials and energy).
- Each LED consumes only **7 W**; the typical pole consumes much more.
- Objective: to put the light where it is needed without a prohibitive cost in the short or long term.
- Uses: storage yards, refineries, tank farms, oil fields, ports, train stations, airports, reservoirs and very large sites with an illuminated fence.
- Future: wireless, signal-over-power line and Internet control; custom products thanks to miniaturization; better color, optics, lumens per watt and life. **Moore's law** will raise performance and lower prices.

#### 9.4.2 Lighting Challenges

- **Three security purposes of lighting (Purpura, 2013):**
  1. psychological deterrence;
  2. enabling detection;
  3. enhancing video surveillance.
- To achieve them: the right quantity, type and intensity, over the right space and efficiently.

| Ineffective lighting | Too much lighting |
| --- | --- |
| Strong contrasts of light and shadow disorient human or electronic surveillance | Glare blinds officers and response forces |
| Poor light helps the intruder hide or evade detection | The exterior/interior imbalance allows seeing in from outside |
| Inadequate light makes officers more visible and vulnerable than the intruder | Over-lighting creates blind spots or "white out" in cameras |
|  | More contrast of light and shadow makes observation difficult |

- **Contrast:** cases (Kurasz, 2007):
  - A short tunnel with very bright lights: on leaving at night, the contrast with the darkness blinded drivers and caused crashes. One of every two lamps was turned off, with costly rewiring. Lesson: more light is not better.
  - A complex that would look well lit in a dark area looks poorly lit next to a very bright neighbor (car dealership, sports field). Lesson: in remote areas with no light around, a lower level is enough.
- **Glare:** it can be an advantage or a problem.
  - Intentional glare: luminaires pointed outward from the premises to inhibit the intruder's vision. Take care that it does not affect cameras, personnel or legitimate activity.
  - To reduce the unwanted kind: increase the mounting height and use steeper aiming angles. This also reduces light pollution and light trespass.
- **Light pollution** includes:
  - **Light spillage:** light that goes beyond the area or point where it is needed.
  - **Light trespass:** spillage that enters neighboring property or interferes (or could interfere) with others.
  - Consequences: from wasted energy and annoyance to hostility or lawsuits.
- **Beam direction:** depends on lamp, luminaire design, mounting, location and environmental conditions. The luminaire and the mounting weigh heavily.
  - **Figure 9.20** (Lighting straight down, © 2013 Innovative Protection Solutions): luminaire pointing straight down; it lights the island but not the stalls or the aisles of the parking lot. Example of ineffective direction.
- **Beam overlap / dead spaces:** applies to poles and wall packs.
  - **Figure 9.21** (Appropriate Overlap Versus Coverage Gap for Pole-Mounted Outdoor Lighting, © 2014 Innovative Protection Solutions): on the left, good overlap in width at ground level and in height; on the right, ineffective design with a coverage gap at ground level and above.
  - Rule of thumb: **pole spacing = 2 × mast height**. It varies greatly with the luminaire, the lamp and the angle.

```latex
d_{\text{posts}} \approx 2\, h_{\text{mast}}
```

- High mast outdoors: wider and more natural distribution, fewer poles (fewer obstacles) and more aesthetic (FEMA, Federal Emergency Management Agency, 2003). Drawback: it is struck by lightning (ASIS International, 2021).
- Directed LED evens out the lighting and makes visibility more efficient.

#### 9.4.3 Characteristics of Light and Lighting

- Four characteristics: quantity of light; level or intensity (illuminance); reflectance of materials; color temperature.
- Design depends on the site's objectives. A vehicle checkpoint where the officer reads IDs and looks for contraband needs much more light than a walkway.
- In addition: comply with local codes and standards and sustainability initiatives. The video system can also dictate the light level and the color temperature.

**Quantity of light: lumens**

- Flow of light emitted by a lamp.
- Example: a 100 W household bulb ≈ 1,700 lumens.
- A spot and a floodlight can emit the same lumens: the spot concentrates them and the floodlight scatters them.

**Illuminance (light level, intensity or illuminance)**

- Concentration of light on an area.
- Guide in the U.S.: *Guideline for Security Lighting for People, Property, and Critical Infrastructure* (Illuminating Engineering Society, IES, 2017).
- Lux (metric) = lumens per m². Foot-candle (fc; English) = lumens per ft².
- For the camera or the eye, what is critical is the light that reaches the iris area of the lens: its illuminance.

```latex
E_{\text{lux}} = \frac{\Phi_{\text{lm}}}{A_{\text{m}^2}} \qquad E_{\text{fc}} = \frac{\Phi_{\text{lm}}}{A_{\text{ft}^2}} \qquad 1\ \text{fc} = 10.76\ \text{lux} \;(\approx 1{:}10)
```

**Figure 9.22: Natural and Visual Light Levels (levels in foot-candles)**

| fc | Natural source | Visual experience |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 50,000 | — | Upper limit of visual tolerance |
| 10,000 | Direct sun | Fresh snow on a clear day |
| 1,000 | Full daylight | Average earth on a clear day |
| 100 | Overcast day | Average earth on an overcast day |
| 1 | Twilight | White paper 1 ft from a standard candle |
| 0.1 | Deep twilight | — |
| 0.05 | — | Snow with a full moon |
| 0.01 | Full moon | — |
| 0.005 | — | Average earth with a full moon |
| 0.001 | Quarter moon | — |
| 0.0001 | Starlight | Snow with starlight |
| 0.00005 | — | Grass with starlight |
| 0.00001 | Overcast night | — |
| 0.000001 | — | Absolute limit of vision |

- **Pending:** in the figure, the word "Candle" appears on a separate line in the natural source column. It seems to complete "standard candle" from the 1 fc row.
- Exam note (outside the book): by historical definition, 1 fc is the light at 1 ft from a standard candle.

**Reflectance**

- If an object does not reflect light it cannot be seen, except as a silhouette against the background.
- With light that is not white, colors look false.
- Light surfaces reflect more: a concrete parking lot reflects more than an asphalt one.
- Mirror or shiny surface: high; matte surface: low. In manufactured objects it varies with the material and age.
- Measure: ratio between incident light and reflected light (in lumens), expressed in %.

```latex
\rho\,(\%) = \frac{\Phi_{\text{reflected}}}{\Phi_{\text{incident}}} \times 100
```

- Exam note (outside the book): the book states the ratio the wrong way around (incident over reflected). Reflectance is reflected over incident; that is why it never exceeds 100%.

**Figure 9.23: Reflectance Measurements**

| Material | Reflectance |
| --- | --- |
| Asphalt | 5% |
| Old concrete | 40% |
| New concrete | 25% |
| Red brick | 25% |
| Grass | 40% |
| Snow | 95% |

- Exam note (outside the book): the book gives 40% to old concrete and 25% to new. The opposite would be expected (new is lighter in color); probable inversion. Know the table as it stands and the possible erratum.

**Color temperature (CCT)**

- The book calls it "corrected color temperature" (CCT): it measures how warm or cool a light is.
- It is measured in kelvin, an absolute scale; the book says that 0 K ≈ −272 °C.
- Exam note (outside the book): the standard term is "correlated color temperature". Also, 0 K = −273.15 °C, and in the international system one says "kelvin", not "degrees Kelvin".
- Analogy of metal in the furnace: bright red ≈ 2,700 K; white ≈ 4,100 K; blue ≈ 5,000 K (similar to daylight).
- Red is perceived as warm; white or blue, as cool. Color temperature strongly influences mood and atmosphere.

**Figure 9.24: Color Temperature**

|  | Warm 3,000 K | Neutral 3,500 K | Cool 4,100 K | Daylight 5,000 K |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Effects and mood | Friendly, intimate, personal, exclusive | Friendly, welcoming, nonthreatening | Orderly, clean, efficient | Bright, alert, exact coloring |
| Applications | Restaurants, hotel lobbies, boutiques, libraries, offices, retail stores | Public reception areas, showrooms, bookstores, offices | Meeting rooms, classrooms, hospitals, offices, mass-market stores | Galleries, museums, jewelry stores, medical offices, print shops |
| Lamps | Fluorescent, incandescent, halogen | Fluorescent, mercury vapor | Fluorescent, mercury vapor, metal halide | Fluorescent, mercury vapor, metal halide |

- Figure note: CCT of low-pressure sodium = 1,750 K; of high-pressure = 2,000 K.

**Color rendition and CRI**

- Security personnel must describe colors accurately: it helps to stop and process criminals captured on video.
- **Color rendition:** the lamp's ability to distinguish, grade and faithfully reproduce the colors of an object.
- It is measured with the **CRI**, a scale from 0 to 100. "Color rendition index" and "color rendering index" are equivalent.
  - 70 to 80: good. Over 80: excellent. 100: daylight.
- High- and low-pressure sodium and mercury vapor have a very low CRI: do not use them with color cameras or where identifying color is critical.
- Example from the book: under low-pressure sodium, a green shirt looks bluish.
- Exam note: the book says "100 percent"; the CRI is an index, not a percentage.

**Figure 9.25: Color Rendition Index for Various Lamp Types**

| Lamp | CRI |
| --- | --- |
| Incandescent | 100 |
| Halogen | 100 |
| Fluorescent | 75–100 |
| Metal halide | 70 |
| LED | 75–90 |
| Mercury vapor | 50 |
| High-pressure sodium | 20 |
| Low-pressure sodium | 5 |

- A high CRI also matters in retail, restaurants and precision manual work. It gives visual clarity and is associated with higher morale and productivity.
- At night outdoors, a high CRI gives pedestrians a greater sense of security: they see farther and perceive depth better.

**Brightness and glare (more subjective terms)**

- **Brightness:** perception of the amount of light reaching the eyes.
- **Glare:** excessive brightness. It hurts the eye, lowers its efficiency, creates excessive contrast, makes people look away and makes seeing difficult.
  - It can deter at the perimeter, but it harms patrols and response forces just the same.
  - It can cause light trespass onto neighboring properties, sidewalks and streets. It must not dazzle or create excessive contrast for drivers and pedestrians.
- **Transitional lighting:** standard practice to reduce risk when moving from a brightly lit place to the parking lot.
- Many communities limit the light level and its spillage or trespass through zoning.

#### 9.4.4 Overview of Lighting Systems

| Component | What it is | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lamp (light bulb) | The manufactured light source: filament or arc tube, glass cover and connectors | "Incandescent", "mercury vapor", etc., name the technology |
| Luminaire (fixture) | Complete unit: lamp, socket, reflectors and diffusers that distribute and focus the light | Some lamps (spots, floods) come with an integrated reflector. Includes the electrical connection and, depending on the technology, **ballasts** (correct voltage, current and waveform to start and operate) and **photosensors** (turn on or off according to ambient light). Chosen for performance and aesthetics. With industrial vibration: secondary retention, e.g. metal cable, so it does not fall on people |
| Mounting hardware | Wall or pole bracket | Secures the luminaire at the right height and in the right place |
| Electrical power | Feeds lamp, ballasts and photocells | See HID below |

- **HID (high-intensity discharge):** metal halide, mercury vapor and high-pressure sodium.
  - Sensitive to voltage drops: they give light with a high-pressure arc; if the voltage drops enough, the arc goes out.
  - Restarting usually takes a long time: up to **20 minutes**.
  - In high security or life safety areas (vaults, cash registers, evacuation routes and assembly points): consider batteries, generators and UPS (uninterruptible power supply).

#### 9.4.5 Economic Considerations

- Cost weighs heavily in deciding the lighting level.
- Lighting required by code or regulation: fire safety, retail banking, NRC (Nuclear Regulatory Commission) or OSHA (Occupational Safety and Health Administration).
- It is often an optional expense: it is justified by identifiable savings or a quantifiable reduction in risk.

**Breakdown of operating cost**

| Item | Approx. % |
| --- | --- |
| Capital goods (lamps, ballasts) | 8% |
| Maintenance | 4% |
| Energy | 88% |

- That is why the most important thing is energy efficiency, called the lamp's **efficacy**.
- The next cost is replacement lamps; it varies with the technology and quality.

```latex
\text{Effectiveness} = \frac{\text{lumens emitted}}{\text{watts consumed}} \quad [\text{lm/W}]
```

**Cleaning and replacing lamps**

- Maintenance includes the labor to change and clean lamps.
- Dirt on the luminaire lowers the light emitted:
  - clean environment (computer room, offices): drops ≈ 3 to 4% per year; cleaning every **3 years** is recommended;
  - very dirty environment: after **1 year** it can give only **80%** of its design output (−20%).
- Consumption is the same with or without dirt: it pays to clean regularly.
- Lamps perform less with age: at the end of their rated life they may give only **80%**, even when clean.
- **Planned replacement** of all lamps or by groups: costs less labor than changing them one by one and avoids dark zones.
- It is advisable to replace at a multiple of the cleaning cycle. Example from the book: average life 6 years and cleaning every 2 → change all of them every 3 cleaning cycles.

```latex
\text{cleaning cycles between replacements} = \frac{\text{service life}}{\text{cleaning interval}} = \frac{6\ \text{years}}{2\ \text{years}} = 3
```

**Number of luminaires:** depends on the area, the required light level, the height and design of the luminaires and the technology.

**Uniformity**

- A uniform distribution, especially outdoors, is expensive; some variation is accepted.
- It is measured as the ratio between the average level and the minimum level.

```latex
U = \frac{E_{\text{average}}}{E_{\text{minimum}}}
```

| Environment | Typical ratio |
| --- | --- |
| Work environments | 1:0.7 |
| Pedestrian walkway | 4:1 |
| Street or roadway | 10:1 |

- More uniformity → better depth perception and a greater sense of security.
- Exam note: the book mixes notations. 4:1 and 10:1 are average:minimum. 1:0.7 appears to be written backwards (minimum = 0.7 × average, ≈ 1.4:1). More uniformity = ratio closer to 1:1 (outside the book).

#### 9.4.6 Starting and Restrike

- Some lamps take time to come back on after being turned off (on purpose, by blackout or by voltage drop).
- It is typical of HID: they depend on an arc, and the tube must cool before it is restruck (restrike).
- HID (and, to a much lesser degree, fluorescent) lamps also take time, when cold, to reach their design light output.
- For the security professional: scheduled turn-on covers startup, but a total or partial outage, even a brief one, can leave the area without light for quite a while.

**Figure 9.26: Lamp Starting and Restrike Times (minutes)**

| Lamp | Starting | Restrike |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Incandescent | Instant | Instant |
| Halogen | Instant | Instant |
| Fluorescent | Instant\* | Instant\* |
| Metal halide | 5–8 | 10–20 |
| LED | Instant | Instant |
| Mercury vapor | 5–8 | 10–20 |
| High-pressure sodium | 2–5 | 1–20 |
| Low-pressure sodium | 5–8 | 0–8 |

- (\*) Fluorescent needs time, especially in cold weather, to reach full output.
- New technologies seek to reduce these times. Example: HID with **two tubes**; one is used and the other stays cold for quick restrike.

#### 9.4.7 Security Lighting for Selected Applications

- **Four applications of security lighting:** standby, continuous, movable and emergency. The book only lists them.
- **Rule of thumb** for pedestrians or normal cameras (minimum reflected light):

| Task | Minimum |
| --- | --- |
| Detection | 0.5 fc |
| Recognition | 1.0 fc |
| Identification | 2.0 fc |

| Area | Recommendations and levels |
| --- | --- |
| Perimeter fencing | Light deters, just like the physical barrier. With perimeter intrusion detection, it supports alarm assessment by CCTV (closed-circuit television) and the response force in delaying and apprehending. NRC: **0.2 fc** at the perimeter and in the clear zone between the two fences. Consider light trespass onto neighbors |
| Site landscape and perimeter approaches | Streets and walkways are lit for life safety and asset protection. Vertical light on the ground helps to see potholes or objects. For pedestrians to see each other, or for cameras, some of the light must be horizontal (lighting vertical surfaces). Trees and shrubs that offer hiding places: difficult and expensive. Continuous lighting can give glare projection and controlled results. Ground light directed at trees and shrubs is the most effective way to keep them from serving as hiding places, and it provides a high-contrast background for seeing movement. Levels: walkways **1–4 fc**; streets **0.5–2 fc**; entrances **10 fc**; open yards **2 fc** |
| Building facade | If exterior objects cannot be lit well, high contrast identifies shape and movement: light the facade with floodlights. If it reflects well, it contributes horizontal light for an observer (person or camera) between the facade and the object. **0.5–2 fc** |
| Parking structures | Difficult: few vertical elements that reflect or give contrast. Some municipal codes require a horizontal white stripe at waist height. The low ceiling calls for luminaires that spread light horizontally (excellent for vertical surfaces). With cameras, choose luminaires that do not dazzle the lens. **5 fc horizontal with 4:1 uniformity** |
| Open parking | Height is less limited, except for codes or light trespass; high sources give horizontal illumination. High-pressure sodium: high efficiency, many lm/W, cost-effective. From **0.2 fc** minimum (low activity: general parking and pedestrians) to **2 fc** (heavy vehicle activity). Cash collection and vehicle access control: **5 fc minimum** |
| Loading docks | Depends on after-hours activity. Exterior with no trucks parked: **1 fc** on the facade (rolling doors, stairs, ramps) and **0.2 fc** in the open yard. Nighttime loading and unloading: **5 fc**. Interior: bays **15 fc**; unpacking and sorting **20 fc**; packing and shipping **30 fc** |
| Security control and monitoring rooms | Computer work: **30–50 fc**; task areas such as the console: **50–70 fc**. Reflection on screens is a problem: watch the position of luminaires and the angle of screens; use flat or anti-glare screens. If monitoring (alarms, video) predominates, lower the ambient light considerably to reduce reflections and raise contrast. The security manager should discuss dimmers with the designer |
| Guard and gate houses | Well-lit exterior at night: **2–5 fc**. Interior by day: high task light, **30 fc**; at night, lower it below the exterior level in order to see the surroundings and approaching pedestrians and vehicles well |

- Exam note: several figures do not match Figure 9.27 (see 9.4.9).
  - Walkways: 1–4 fc here vs. 0.20 fc in the table.
  - Gate houses: 2–5 fc exterior here vs. 30 fc in the table (the 30 fc is the interior daytime light).
  - Entrances: 10 fc here vs. 5 fc pedestrian and 10 fc vehicular in the table.
  - Open yards: 2 fc in landscape vs. 0.2 fc in loading docks.

#### 9.4.8 Lighting Considerations for Electronic Surveillance Systems

- Additional considerations with cameras:
  1. CRI, to reproduce and identify colors accurately;
  2. reflectance of materials;
  3. directionality of reflected light.
- **Wavelength:** the eye sees the visible spectrum, from **400 nm (violet) to 700 nm (red)**; nm = nanometers.
  - It is a small part of the electromagnetic spectrum. At its ends are ultraviolet and infrared.
  - The sun and artificial sources emit energy outside human sensitivity.
  - Cameras usually see what we see, but many capture the **near infrared (700–1,100 nm)**.
- **IR illumination:** with a special-sensor camera it makes it possible to see without visible light.
  - Useful where zoning limits light trespass or covert surveillance is sought.
  - It only works with **monochrome** cameras, not color.
  - Place the illuminator next to the camera, with a beam aperture matching the lens setting.
  - On PTZ (pan/tilt/zoom) cameras, mount the IR on the mechanism so that it follows the camera. Verify that it supports the weight of camera + luminaire.
- **Color temperature and cameras:**
  - Technical data sheets are usually based on incandescent tungsten filament (**2,700 K**).
  - No usual color camera in video surveillance works well at the **1,700 K** of low-pressure sodium.
  - New CCD (charge-coupled device) sensors greatly improve color with high-pressure sodium (**2,200 K**).
- Exam note: the book refers to "Figure 9-25" for color temperatures; it is **Figure 9.24** (9.25 is the CRI one).
- Exam note: the book gives different CCTs. Low-pressure sodium: 1,750 K (Fig. 9.24) vs. 1,700 K here. High-pressure sodium: 2,000 K vs. 2,200 K.

|  | Color camera | Monochrome (black and white) camera |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Light needed for equal quality | Twice as much | Half as much |
| Minimum video signal | At least 50% of the full signal; otherwise the color record fades | Only 20–30% |
| Works with IR | No | Yes |

**Other specifications on camera data sheets**

- Minimum light levels quoted at **75% or 89.9% reflectance**.
- Minimum levels tied to a specific lens. Example: standard color camera with 0.25 fc at f/1.2; with f/2.0 it rises to 0.68 fc, that is **2.7 times** more light.
- **White balance:** automatic adjustment of the camera to the color temperature of the source. Range of **2,200 K to 7,000 K**; it must be compatible with the existing or designed lighting.

```latex
\frac{0.68\ \text{fc}}{0.25\ \text{fc}} \approx 2.7
```

#### 9.4.9 Standards for Security Lighting Levels

- Standards are numerous and vary widely.
- Exam note: the book's introductory sentence is truncated ("The following guidance."); the guidance is Figure 9.27.

**Figure 9.27: Guidelines for Minimum Lighting Levels**

| Application | Minimum (IES standards) | Comments (other standards) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Perimeter fence | 0.50 fc | NRC 0.20 fc |
| Outer perimeter | 0.50–2.00 fc | NRC 0.20 fc; DoAFM 0.15 fc |
| Open areas | 2.00 fc | — |
| Open parking | 0.20–0.90 fc | IES, high vehicle activity: 2.00 fc |
| Parking structure | 5.00 fc | — |
| Pedestrian walkways | 0.20 fc | IES: 7.50 fc at automated teller machines (ATM) |
| Pedestrian entrances | 5.00 fc | DoAFM 2.00 fc |
| Vehicular entrances | 10.00 fc | DoAFM 1.00 fc |
| Facade | 0.50–2.00 fc | — |
| Gate houses | 30.00 fc | — |
| Loading dock exterior | 0.20–5.00 fc | — |
| Loading bays | 15.00 fc | — |
| Offices: general | 30–50 fc | — |
| Offices: task | 50–70 fc | — |
| Interior public areas | 10–20 fc | — |
| Retail stores | 50.00 fc | — |
| Bank: lobby | 20.00 fc | — |
| Bank: teller | 50.00 fc | — |
| Bank: automated teller machine (ATM) | 15.00 fc | IES: 30 fc at the preparation table |

- Acronyms in the table: IES = Illuminating Engineers Society of North America; NRC = Nuclear Regulatory Commission; DoAFM = Department of the Army Field Manual (U.S. Army field manual).
- Exam note: the table says "Illuminating Engineers Society"; elsewhere in the book it is Illuminating Engineering Society.
- Exam note: in the PDF, the label "(or other standards)" falls under the "Application" column; by content it belongs to the comments column.
- Exam note: for automated teller machines the table gives 7.50 fc (comment on walkways) and 15.00 fc (bank row).

- National Lighting Bureau (www.nlb.org).
- Illuminating Engineering Society (www.ies.org).
- National Council on Qualifications for the Lighting Professions (www.ncqlp.org).

- Philips Lighting Company.

- Sylvania Lighting.

## Capítulo 10 — Crime Prevention through Environmental Design (págs. 211–252)

**Central idea:** CPTED (Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design) uses the design and use of the built environment to reduce crime and the fear of crime. It rests on three elements: territoriality, surveillance and access control, applied through natural, mechanical and organizational means. It is cheaper to integrate it from the planning stage and to combine it with traditional security.

**Context (chapter introduction):**

- CPTED is a cost-effective tool, but it is often forgotten when designing or renovating facilities.
- Causes of the oversight: lack of knowledge, excessive reliance on electronic systems, management concerns and the fact that the concept has detractors.
- Academic response: universities are creating CPTED certificates and courses in architecture, urban planning, criminal justice and security management.
- **1972 — Oscar Newman** (architect at the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development, HUD): publishes *Defensible Space*.
- Newman showed how crime opportunities are "designed" into the built environment. From this CPTED was born (Kennedy 2006).

### 10.1 Principles of CPTED

- **Origin of the term:** first used by Ray Jeffrey in his book *Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design* (1971).
- Exam note: the book writes "Jeffrey"; the criminologist's name is C. Ray Jeffery (outside the book).
- **Central principle (Jeffery):** proper design and effective use of the built environment reduce the incidence and fear of crime, and improve the quality of life.
- Basis: common sense and awareness of how people use space, for legitimate or criminal purposes.
- It is best applied with multidisciplinary teams: planners, designers, architects, landscape architects, law enforcement, security professionals and users (residents, employees).
- The security professional must master CPTED in order to work with local prevention officers, design authorities and architects, in new construction or renovation.

**CPTED as a set of management tools: its targets:**

| Target | What it means | Example from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Places | Physical environments designed to produce behavioral effects that reduce opportunity and fear. Includes offices, parking lots, parks and public spaces, multifamily buildings, warehouses, schools, places of worship and shopping centers | In a parking lot, emergency call posts and more light overcome the sense of distance and isolation |
| Behavior | Some places predict, create, promote or allow crime or disorder; others induce law-abiding behavior | — |
| Design and use of space | Redesigning or making better use of a space encourages desirable behavior and discourages crime | Placing the parking lot in front of a convenience store |

**Three underlying elements of CPTED:** territoriality · surveillance · access control.

**Types of CPTED measures (Crowe, 1991):**

| Type | What it involves | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mechanical | Physical security hardware or electronic systems | Locks, fencing, CCTV (closed-circuit television) |
| Organizational | People or activities, not equipment | Guards, police patrols, neighborhood watch |
| Natural | Natural elements and nonmechanical objects | Terrain, layout, landscaping, gates, walls |

Exam note: Crowe places "fencing" among the mechanical measures, but "gates" and "walls" among the natural ones.

**CPTED tools (examples from the book):**

| Tool | Idea | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Natural territorial reinforcement | Create a sense of ownership, responsibility and accountability in owners, managers or occupants, so that they watch and detect intruders. People defend the space they feel psychologically they own. It can be physical (building codes as a defense strategy) or symbolic. It is called "natural" because it arises from the normal, routine use of the environment | Low edge hedges next to the sidewalks of an apartment complex (they mark each dwelling and prevent shortcuts). Color, texture, surface variations, signage and wayfinding. Real barriers (fences, walls) and symbolic markers (warning signs, low hedges, low wooden picket fences) |
| Natural surveillance | More visibility for occupants and casual observers means more detection of intruders or misconduct | A tall wooden fence that hides a loading dock invites theft; a chain-link fence that allows seeing through discourages it. Windows, door viewers, mirrors. Open sidewalks and streets, designs without hiding places, adequate nighttime lighting, activity areas in front of the building (lobby–street visual connection) |
| Natural access control | Real and symbolic barriers (doors, fences, shrubs) that define and limit access | Dense, thorny shrubs under low windows, window locks or alarm. Hinges not accessible from the outside, secure access to roofs and to ladders, strikeplates anchored to the frame, closed windows, key control policies, neighborhood watch |
| Management and maintenance | The space must look cared for. "Broken windows" theory (Wilson & Kelling, 1982): unrepaired deterioration gives an impression of abandonment and of the absence of a capable guardian, and increases criminal opportunity | Markers of deterioration: graffiti, trash, abandoned furniture, weeds. A car parked for a long time with a broken window will soon have more. Maintain lighting, paint, signage, fences, walkways and repair what is broken |
| Legitimate activity support | For places that are hard to protect because of their location or geography. Drugs and crime thrive where residents and management do not "claim" the space with legitimate activities | A hotspot can disappear if the police set up a substation or if maintenance staff occupy the space |

- **Key element of CPTED:** designing or redesigning a place to reduce the opportunity and fear of crime, by natural, mechanical and organizational or procedural means.
- CPTED is a prevention theory based on **environmental criminology**.
- Premise: well-designed buildings, parks and parking lots deter opportunities, reduce fear and improve quality of life.
- Benefit to the organization: it improves its security posture and security awareness.

#### 10.1.1 Criminal Behaviors and Patterns

- Applying CPTED requires knowing the basic theory and practice of crime prevention.
- The **American Crime Prevention Institute** (University of Louisville, Kentucky) set operating assumptions. Two apply especially to CPTED:
  1. Knowledge in prevention evolves and is interdisciplinary: successful practices and emerging technologies must be analyzed, and findings shared.
  2. Strategies must be flexible and creative: what works in one case may fail in a similar one with a different culture or environment.
- CPTED is a living discipline. It changes with design and construction, the use of space, social mentality and criminal behavior.
- **Flash mobs case:** many young people burst into a store at the same time for mass theft and vandalism. They organize almost instantly through social networks. They are hard to stop because of the number of perpetrators.
- The physical characteristics of the site influence the offender's decisions, because they alter his chances of being detected.
- **Key rule:** crime is more likely when the offender perceives a low probability of detection and identification and a high probability of escape.

**Target Selection**

- Priority: first prevent the most serious crimes and protect the most victimized people and places.
- Steps against repeat victimization:

| Step | How it is applied |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Quickly remove signs of victimization | Repair visible damage as soon as possible; sometimes the victim must be helped. In apartments: cover or replace glass, repair door jambs, change locks and keys, repair broken or burned-out lights, trim shrubs that block the view and apply CPTED strategies |
| 2. Improve physical security | Improves natural surveillance, visibility, sightlines and access control. Reduces piggybacking (slipping in behind someone authorized), intrusion and other unauthorized access. Redesign exterior entrances to see everyone who enters or leaves |
| 3. Block easy access to targets | Doors, gates, screens and real or symbolic barriers. Relocate cash registers, vending machines, service vehicles, automated teller machines (ATM) and other valuable assets. In stores, expensive items in locked display cases. At gas stations, the employee behind impact- and bullet-resistant polycarbonate |
| 4. Protect especially vulnerable targets | If they cannot be moved: removable bollards, roll-down gates, fences and screens to deny access after hours |
| 5. Regulate access to high-risk assets or areas | Require credentials, permits or fees for restrooms, parks, schools and parking lots |

**Capable Guardian Concept**

- It comes from the **routine activity theory**: the presence of capable guardians can deter crime.
- Offenders avoid victims they perceive as armed, able to resist or dangerous.
- They avoid areas heavily patrolled by police, guards or "nosy neighbors".
- They also avoid passive barriers: alarms, fences and locks.
- They look for the easiest, lowest-risk path. Natural surveillance and capable guardians lower the site's perceived vulnerability.

**Situational Crime Prevention**

- Origin: Great Britain, late 1970s and early 1980s.
- It reduces opportunities in all behavioral contexts, not just in buildings and spaces.
- At first it attacked the opportunity for specific crimes. Recent versions also attack motives.
- Those social prevention strategies connect it with second generation CPTED.
- **Definition:** to manage, design or manipulate the environment systematically and as permanently as possible in order to:
  1. increase the effort required of the offender;
  2. increase his risk;
  3. reduce the reward he perceives.
- Impact: it changed British crime control policy. It shifted from a focus on the offender (personality, behavior, background) to a focus on the environment that creates opportunities.

#### 10.1.2 Later Developments in CPTED

| Generation | When | Focus |
| --- | --- | --- |
| First generation | Jeffery 1971 and Newman 1972 | Reduce physical opportunities: territoriality, surveillance, access control, maintenance and legitimate activity. "Fortified city" mentality. Local scope |
| Second generation | Approx. 2003–2008 | In addition to removing physical opportunities, create a sense of neighborliness to reduce the motives for crime. Identifies the community's social resources. Reinforces legitimate activity with community cohesion. Seeks a socioeconomically balanced community and a well-maintained city for everyone. Local scope |
| Third generation | Current (modern-day) | Environmental sustainability and green technology. Reprograms urban space by digital means and green technologies. Retains surveillance and control (1st) and effective physical design and sociocultural diversity (2nd). A green and sustainable urbanity is perceived as safe. Treats security as a global matter |

- Sources of the table: UNICRI and MIT 2011 (United Nations Interregional Crime and Justice Research Institute and Massachusetts Institute of Technology); Fennelly and Perry 2018 (local vs. global scope).

**Second generation CPTED: 4 strategies (Saville & Cleveland, 2013):**

| Strategy | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Cohesion | Community groups, neighborhood associations and personal development programs: leadership, financial and organizational skills, conflict resolution |
| Capacity threshold | Also called tipping point theory. It balances land uses and urban elements. Too many abandoned properties tip the area toward crime. A balance of legitimate commerce, recreation and diverse housing improves livability |
| Community culture | Cultural, artistic, sports and recreational activities that unite neighbors around a common purpose |
| Connectivity | Links the neighborhood with neighboring neighborhoods and with funding and political support from businesses and higher levels of government |

**CPTED 3-D and Beyond**

- It looks at space through three Ds: **Designation** (its purpose), **Definition** (management and identity) and **Design** (according to the desired function and behavior).
- Figure 10.1 evaluates the 3-D factors. It also includes the traditional **"4 Ds"**: deter, detect, delay, deny, and other considerations.

**Figure 10.1 — Tool for Evaluating CPTED 3-D Factors (questions by factor):**

| Factor | Key questions |
| --- | --- |
| Designation | What is the designated purpose of the space? What was it originally intended for? How well does it support its current or intended use? Is there conflict? How and where? |
| Definition | How is the space defined? Is its ownership clear? Where are its boundaries? Do social or cultural definitions influence its use? Are there clear legal and administrative rules in policy and effectively enforced? Do signs indicate the correct use or the limits of access? Is there conflict or confusion between purpose and definition? |
| Design | Does the physical design support the intended function? Does it support the desired or accepted behaviors? Does it clash with the productive use of the space or the intended human activity? Is there confusion about how the design aims to control or modify behaviors? |
| Deterrence | Does security personnel deter the illegitimate and promote the intended behavior? Does the design allow good surveillance and access control to and from the property? Does the intended behavior itself discourage the illegal or illegitimate? |
| Detection | Can entry be controlled? Is there a process to assess whether an intrusion is legitimate or not? Is detection achieved by physical design, mechanical technology or operational manpower? Is a person or agency notified to respond? |
| Delay | Are there active barriers? Are there passive barriers? Are there guards or other designated responders? How much delay is needed to detect and respond? |
| Response | What are the roles and post orders of the responding guards? What equipment is needed for a complete response? What tactics allow a quick and clear response? What training do they receive? |
| Reporting | What communication network documents intrusions or calls for help? What is the written incident reporting protocol? How is the documentation organized and stored? Is it sufficiently detailed and clear? |
| Discrimination | Does the staff know how to distinguish legitimate users or threats from illegitimate ones? Is the equipment sensitive enough to distinguish false threats from real ones? |
| Neutralization | Was the threat sufficiently deterred? Was the system reset and tested to avoid complacency or false alarms? Were the attackers controlled, the police notified and the scene secured again? In the case of fire, smoke or flood: was it neutralized, was damage assessed and was the scene secured against contamination or pilferage? |

- After answering, the space is rated by how well it supports natural access control, natural surveillance and territoriality.
- Goal of the questions: that there be no conflicts between the space, its activities and the expected behaviors.
- **Case:** an access control that is hard to use or that fails frequently leads employees to prop doors open. It shows that the system was poorly chosen and users were not trained.
- Exam note: the text announces the "4 Ds" (deter, detect, delay, deny), but Figure 10.1 has no "Deny" section. In its place it adds Response, Reporting, Discrimination and Neutralization.

### 10.2 Tools of CPTED

#### 10.2.1 Tools That Address the Three Elements of CPTED

**Territoriality**

- People take better care of a space when they feel ownership or responsibility for it, formal or informal.
- Effect on users (employees, residents, visitors): they are more observant, report what is suspicious, notice and correct risks, and take better care of the property.
- **Most basic tool:** a good training and awareness program. People know what to look for and feel empowered to participate.
- **Simple tool:** signage. The environment communicates its designated use and appropriate behavior.

| Concept | Definition | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Graphic | Symbol that conveys a message with an image | Figure of a man on the men's restroom |
| Signage | Message in letters or words | "No trespassing" |

- A business that has already suffered crimes is "on notice": the crime may be considered foreseeable.
- Signs put users on notice and try to shift part of the responsibility to them. To achieve this, they must set clear rules.
- Examples of rules from the book: "Do not walk on the grass" · "Enter at own risk" · "Lock your valuables" · "No trespassing" · "Don't even think of committing a crime".
- In a parking lot, signage indicates: entrances and exits, speed bumps, speed limits, direction of traffic, keeping valuables put away, direction of parking, use of video surveillance, fire exits and alarms, and panic buttons and intercoms.
- **Note:** posting a sign does not relieve the building owner of legal liability.
- Signage directs people to destinations and areas, especially in multitenant buildings. It reduces wandering by strangers or people with bad intentions.
- At the same time it creates a welcoming environment and protects the security and privacy of the occupants. An information kiosk or a well-placed concierge/receptionist achieves the same.
- Consider the demographics of employees, visitors and neighbors: it may call for signs in several languages.

**Considerations for security signage and graphics:**

| Category | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Architectural design considerations | The architect or graphics consultant advises on letter size and type, reading distance, reflectivity, lighting, location and intended audience. Some typefaces are more readable from a distance |
| Systems considerations | Consistent, uniform and well-distributed signs. Like the fire exits on all stairways, security signs must be in all critical areas |
| Procedural considerations | They clarify procedures (Figure 10.2): wearing an ID badge, warning of a change in floor level in a restaurant, warning shoppers of anti-theft surveillance, indicating areas where sensitive information is not discussed |

**Signage figures (for a person with 20/20 vision):**

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Reading distance | 50 ft (15 m) |
| Minimum letter height | 6 in (15 cm) |
| Minimum height of graphics or symbols | 15 in (38 cm) |
| Minimum indoor illumination | 215 lumens per square meter (= 215 lux, about 20 fc; equivalence outside the book) |
| Lamps | Placed to avoid glare on the sign |

- If the user does not follow the posted rules, the burden of responsibility shifts to him and his intent can be questioned.
- Without notice, the behavior is left to personal interpretation and is hard to question.
- Early involvement of the architect is advisable in order to use signage well.
- **Warning (Figure 10.2):** the sign that keeps vehicle exhaust from entering the building also tells a terrorist where to place a chemical or biological device. Signage can harm security.

**Surveillance**

- Definition: the act of observing.
- It is not just cameras, monitors and digital recorders. Natural surveillance can be just as effective for certain objectives.
- **Figure 10.3 (Typical Elevator Lobby):** the office has glass doors. The receptionist, well positioned, observes the elevator lobby and notices suspicious activity or risks.

**Access Control**

- It can also be natural.
- **Classic example: the castle.** Surveillance with high towers and clear zones. Access control with walls, moats, berms, drawbridges, terrain, water, rock/stone and few access points.
- **Figure 10.4 (Berm and Tree Line):** an office complex uses a landscaped berm and a line of trees to control the perimeter and provide privacy.
- The berm has a single vehicular entrance/exit. It was chosen from the start instead of a standard fence.
- Result: perimeter security, an aesthetic environment for employees and easy approval from residential neighbors.

#### 10.2.2 Reducing Crime Through Architectural Design

- The security professional integrates CPTED into the design by working with community groups and professionals.
- **Integrating CPTED into initial planning is more cost-effective** than changing once construction has begun.
- Designing without thinking about security can cause: lawsuits, injuries, expensive retrofitting and more security staff.
- Measures added later: distort building functions, raise personnel costs and end up exposed and unsightly.
- A building must: allow efficient work, meet the needs of users and protect them from hazards and crime.

**Traditional building planning process (5 steps):**

| Step | What happens |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Programming | The owner informs the architect of the purpose of the building and its occupants |
| 2. Schematic design | The architect makes bubble diagrams with circulation patterns and proximity relationships. They evolve into floor plan, site plan and elevations, the start of engineering |
| 3. Design development | The architect presents ideas and makes corrections. The drawings add structural, mechanical and electrical engineering, ventilation and site planning. Larger scale (in the U.S., typically 1/4 in = 1 ft) |
| 4. Construction documents or working drawings | Final drawings with all technical data and technical specifications |
| 5. Bids for construction and selection of contractor | Drawings and specifications go out to bid |

- **Security must be addressed in the programming phase.**
- Main responsibility of the owner or client: to define threats to people, information and property; to set the required level of security and the budget. He may consult a security professional.

**Three security challenges for architects:**

| Challenge | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Determining requirements | Analyze the designated purpose of the space. Examine the cultural, legal and physical definitions of prescribed, desired and acceptable behaviors. Design to support them. Ask about existing policies and practices and integrate them into programming |
| Knowing the technology | Technology advances quickly. Many projects add specialists and systems integrators. The architect needs a grounding in security principles to evaluate and work with specialists and manufacturers |
| Understanding architectural implications | Integrate security and life safety goals, sometimes in conflict, with other requirements. Plan space, function and people to deter, detect, delay, deny and respond (deter, detect, delay, deny, response) |

- The architect converts the requirements into an **architectural program**. Like a restaurant menu: it defines what will be produced and how much it will cost.
- The architect decides circulation, access, materials, fenestration and other features that help or frustrate security.
- After that point, every security change requires modifying drawings and specifications, with more time and money.
- Some jurisdictions require a security review by the police to approve the building permit. Inspectors look for points for assaults, muggings, burglaries and crimes of opportunity.
- Many jurisdictions have security ordinances: lighting levels and security doors and windows. Some companies require similar reviews.
- If security is just one more design requirement, it carries no more weight than fire protection or landscaping.

**CPTED vs. target hardening:**

| Approach | What it uses | Limitation or advantage |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Traditional target hardening | Barriers: locks, alarms, fences, gates | Often overlooks natural access control and surveillance |
| CPTED | Natural and normal uses of the environment | Sometimes achieves the same effects as mechanical hardening and surveillance |

**The 3 basic CPTED strategies** (natural access control, natural surveillance, natural territorial reinforcement) are applied through three methods:

| Method | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Organized | Staffing |
| Mechanical | Technological products |
| Natural | Site planning, design, landscaping and signage |

- A checklist helps incorporate CPTED principles into proposed projects.

#### 10.2.3 Access Control, Surveillance, and Territorial Reinforcement

**Where to consider access control:**

1. All entrances and exits of the site and the building.
2. Internal access points to restricted or controlled zones.
3. Features of the environment and the building used to enter: approach routes, trees, ledges, skylights, balconies, windows, tunnels.
4. Security screening devices: guard posts, surveillance, identification equipment, turnstiles, card readers.

- **Objective of access control:** to deny access to the target (asset) and to create in the potential offender a perception of risk, detection, delay and response.
- Surveillance reinforces access control.

| Method | Access control | Surveillance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Organized | Security guards | Police and guard patrols |
| Mechanical | Hardening: locks, card systems, windows with protective glazing, special door and window hardware, reinforced walls, floors or doors | Lighting and video |
| Natural | Spatial definition and circulation patterns, sometimes with security zoning | Windows, low landscaping, elevated entrances |

**Site Development and Security Zoning**

- Security planning should begin, if possible, at **site selection**. Goal: a site that meets architectural requirements and provides security advantages.
- The analysis evaluates conditions inside and outside the site: topography, vegetation, neighboring land uses, circulation patterns, neighborhood crime patterns, police patrol patterns, sightlines, hiding zones, location of utilities and current and planned lighting.
- Also: access points and circulation of vehicles, employees, service personnel, visitors and external pedestrians.
- Questions at this stage:
  1. What is the site physically like and how does it influence security?
  2. What land uses surround it?
  3. What crimes occur in the area and how often?
- A high-risk site is not automatically ruled out. The owner may choose it and address the threats with design, technology, personnel and security management.

**The three levels of defense the architect designs:**

| Level | What it protects | Key measures and data |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1st Site perimeter and grounds | Site analysis is the first level of defense | Walls, dense plantings, fences, berms, ditches, lighting and natural topographic separations. Against intruders, the most common: walls, chain-link and other barriers. Landscaping marks the property line |
| 2nd Building perimeter/exterior | A critical "second line of defense". Think of the area as having four sides, a roof and a floor | Main entry points: windows, doors, skylights, storm sewers, roof, floor and fire escapes. Doors: frame, latches, locks, hinges, panic bars and the wall around them. Windows: type of glass, frame, hardware and size of the opening |
| 3rd Internal space protection | Sensitive zones inside the facility | Security technology, personnel and restricted circulation. Zones with different levels of security |

- Doors and windows are inherently poor at resisting penetration.
- **Key figure:** most stud walls and metal deck roofs can be breached with hand tools in **less than 2 minutes**.
- Unreinforced concrete block walls are quickly broken with a sledgehammer or a car.
- Zoning lets each person reach his destination and keeps him from entering where he has no business.
- Controlling access by department filters out unwanted visitors, reduces congestion and helps employees identify and challenge unauthorized people.

**Types of security zones:**

| Zone | Rule | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Unrestricted zones | Open to everyone during hours of use. The design must invite people to do their business and leave without entering controlled or restricted zones | Lobbies, reception areas, snack bars, certain personnel and administrative offices, public meeting rooms |
| Controlled zones | A valid reason is needed to enter. Once inside, one moves between departments without severe restriction | Administrative offices, staff dining rooms, security offices, office work areas, loading docks |
| Restricted zones | Sensitive areas only for assigned personnel. Some sections require additional access control | Vaults, sensitive records, chemicals and drugs, food preparation, mechanical areas, telephone equipment, electrical equipment, control rooms, laboratories, laundry, sterile supply, special equipment and sensitive work areas |

- Exam note: administrative offices appear in two zones. "Certain" offices are unrestricted; the rest are controlled.
- Logical order: first resolve circulation with zoning; then consider mechanical solutions.

**Visibility: Privacy versus Security**

- Balancing privacy and security is difficult.
- A hedge or a low fence defines, physically and psychologically, the public and the private.
- A picket fence marks the edge without blocking the view or limiting surveillance.
- Adding trees gives a sense of enclosure, but it allows seeing between the fence and the tree canopy.
- Block or brick walls secure, but they also hide thieves. Bare walls invite graffiti.
- Walls with thorny plants (bougainvillea, carissa, wild lime) protect more and form a better barrier. Certain plants deter even without a wall.
- Disadvantages of thorny plants: risk to small children and a maintenance challenge.
- Carissa sizes: emerald blanket (dwarf), boxwood blanket (up to 6 ft / 1.8 m) and carissa grandiflora (7–8 ft / 2.1–2.4 m).
- In housing: hedges, shrub beds or low ground covers discourage entry through windows. Avoid tall, large-leaved plants that hide the intruder.
- Pygmy date palms in front of windows let the breeze through, but the thorns at the base of their leaves stop anyone who climbs. Similar: Jerusalem thorn and cinnecord.
- Even if the thief enters through a door and leaves through a window, carrying large objects out through shrubs and hedges is much harder.
- **Earth berms:** very effective for natural access control, but they can block the view.
- **Park case:** some berms broke up the monotony of flat terrain, but kept the police from seeing play areas used by gangs. They were lowered to **no more than 2.5 ft (0.7 m)**.

**Landscaping rules for a secure design** (in addition to climate):

| Rule | Figure or detail |
| --- | --- |
| Plants must not block large parts of a main path or recreation area | — |
| Consider growth rate and maintenance | — |
| Low plants set back from the edge of paths | 1 yd (1 m) |
| Maximum height of low shrubs | 32 in (81 cm) |
| Thorny shrubs at possible hiding places, areas of illegitimate activity or walls with windows that must be kept away from people | They can attract trash; a low fence may be needed against wind-blown debris |
| Hard landscaping that is vandal-resistant and without "projectiles" | No pavers or loose gravel |
| Furniture and landscape elements must not serve for entry or for seeing over walls or hedges | Furniture for brief use, not suitable for sleeping |
| Tree canopies pruned from below | Up to 8 ft (2.4 m) where appropriate: clear line of sight, fewer hiding places and ambushes |

- The type and location of trees greatly affect the coverage of exterior lighting. Security lighting should go from the tree canopy downward.
- The security professional should take part in the landscaping and lighting plans.
- Know whether trees are deciduous (lose their leaves) or evergreen like the pine.
- **Trees and video:** on the plan the camera seems to have a clear view, but trees create blind spots. Consider height and foliage when locating cameras.
- Exam note: here the shrub limit is 32 in (81 cm) and the berm limit 2.5 ft; do not confuse with other "2 or 3 feet" rules from other sources (outside the book).

### 10.3 CPTED Applications in Various Settings

- Considerations, problems and challenges when applying CPTED in specific settings or sectors.

#### 10.3.1 Commercial Office Buildings

- An office project (renovation, expansion or new construction) obliges security and owners to work with designers in new ways.
- The architect needs information for the architectural program. Their typical source is the **security director**.
- If the client has no in-house security professional, it can hire one to advise the company and the architect.
- The security professional identifies the vital assets. **Three categories of assets: people, information, property.**

**Asset to Be Protected: People (the most valuable asset)**

Six questions for the architect's needs assessment:

| Question | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Who: who are the users? | Visitors, staff, service crew, sales |
| What: what can they do in the building? | Tasks, recreation, work |
| Why: why are they there? | Official business, visiting as guests |
| When: when do they arrive and leave? | Shifts or other patterns |
| Where: where can they go? | Horizontal, vertical |
| How: how do they get there? | Access methods, circulation |

**Figure 10.5 — Access and Hours (table the security professional gives the architect):**

| Who | Why | What | When | Where | How |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vice President | Company business | Administration, management | 0800–1800, M–F | All areas, total access | Staff elevator |
| Custodian/cleaning crew | Clean offices, vacuum | Clean, collect trash | "0111–0400", M–F | Lobby floors | Officer lets in, has access to special service… |

- Exam note: the cleaning crew's time "0111" appears to be an erratum in the book. **Pending:** confirm the start time and the end of the "How" cell (cut off in the extracted text).

**Custodian/cleaning crew case:**

| Security implications | Design implications |
| --- | --- |
| Control of after-hours access | Sign-in desk for service trades |
| Verify that the cleaning employee is who he says he is | Access control system to control entry and record movements |
| Security personnel who log and supervise entries and exits | Location of trash containers |
| Key control | Location of the service elevator |
| — | Location of service doors |
| — | Alarms for offices and for critical wiring to and from the control room |
| — | Infrastructure and structure lines |

- It is only a sample of what the architect must resolve with the security professional's information.
- The answers to the six questions go into the architectural program or "problem-seeking" stage. Later they are used for the schematic, development and construction drawings.

**Asset to Be Protected: Information**

Critical needs assessment questions:

| Question | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Who has access to the information? Who enters after hours? | Staff, management, contractors/consultants, vendors, joint venture partners, mail room |
| What information is it? | Data, trade secrets, personnel files, blueprints, computer programs, classified government information, third-party information, operational or business plans |
| What format is it in? | Personal knowledge, paper, electronic media, models/prototypes, equipment |
| How transportable and transferable is it? | — |
| Why is it worth protecting? | Competitive advantage, critical technology, strategic value, regulatory requirements, privacy protection, contractual or legal restrictions |
| When is it accessible or vulnerable, and for how long? | — |
| Where is it accessible or vulnerable? | — |
| How can it be obtained or compromised, legitimately and illegitimately? | — |

- With the answers, the security professional prepares an **information asset protection plan** for the architect.
- With it, architectural, technological and organizational responses are designed for a comprehensive strategy.

**Architectural features to protect information:**

| Element | Recommendation |
| --- | --- |
| Doors and windows | Minimum of exterior openings, easy to observe. Controlled and monitored doors for accountability. Main entrance defined architecturally. Service entrance secured and supervised. Storerooms monitored and where a supervisor can see movement. Take care what information, activities and equipment can be seen through internal and external windows |
| Reception desk | Screens visitors, vendors and outsiders. It must see the entrance doors and the elevators. It marks the layering between public and private entrance. Screens, visitor logs and security data not visible to visitors |
| Controlled areas | Distinguish and restrict access: VIP zones, computer/data center and sensitive operations areas |
| Computer/server room | Strict access control, protected service lines, high security glass for easy supervision and central location |
| Computer anchoring | Secure laptops, tablets and computers with security cables |
| Employee traffic patterns | Control entry and exit. A controlled and supervised exit allows checking packages, briefcases and bags. Locker areas well lit and supervised against theft |
| Elevators | They should open onto the supervised core. Special floors or VIP offices may require access control in the elevator or dedicated elevators |
| Loading dock | Its own path, away from employees and visitors. Ground loops and an intercom to alert security to a truck when no one is supervising. If possible, physically separate shipping and receiving |
| Mail room | At the end of a clear, unobstructed path from the loading or mail delivery area. Secure room with a monitored door to control access and for accountability |
| Vaults | Locate vaults, fireproof safes and files according to the site and frequency of use. Supermarkets put the vault at the front so it can be seen; other stores hide it |
| Conference or meeting facilities | Outside the restricted zone, normally next to the lobby and before the access control point. In this way attendees need not be given a badge and are kept out of private or semiprivate areas |

**Asset to Be Protected: Property**

- The same process applies: ask the key questions and set security and architectural criteria.
- Market advantage: "designing out" shoplifting, pilferage, espionage, assaults and terrorism.
- A building can have environmental conflicts that give opportunities to the offender. CPTED and defensible space principles help prevent them.
- The owner does not control his whole surroundings: neighborhood streets; nearby stadiums, bars, parks or dumps; public services such as the police.
- Even so, if the security professional assesses the risks well and does a good needs assessment, the right solutions emerge.

**CPTED Considerations for Offices and Office Buildings**

- Typical office risks: walk-in thefts, burglary, theft by deception and fraud, vandalism, loss of information and employee theft.
- After identifying risks, consider site, building and internal security requirements:

| Element | Recommendation |
| --- | --- |
| Site location | Study zoning and building code restrictions of the site and surroundings. Review current and planned landscaping |
| External areas | Design garages and parking lots with care. Arrange entrances, exits, paths and streets to avoid traffic conflicts. Identify and prioritize fences, gates and lighting. **Design the video after laying out circulation.** Address delivery and trash areas |
| Access points | For employees, visitors and service personnel. Limit the number of entrances; if needed, use exit-only doors. Consider fire exits and the life safety code early so as not to undermine security. Secure loading docks. Needs of basements and mechanical rooms. Exterior stairs, roof access, doors and windows |
| Internal locations | Arrange with care: lobby entrances, secondary entrances, reception, cashier, computer areas, electrical or telephone services, executive areas, dining rooms, staff restrooms, security command center, vaults and special equipment |
| Lobby entrance | Make it clear to everyone that the lobby is the right place to enter. Higher quality materials to project success, stability and power |
| Reception desk | A layer of building security. The receptionist identifies and approves visitors before letting them through. Good view of who enters; can block passage to nonpublic areas, elevators and stairs. Do not overload with tasks that distract from screening. Emergency call button. Can hide video equipment (angles and ventilation). Design that slows attacks, like the wide counters in banks that prevent reaching or jumping over |
| Access control and video surveillance | Resolve them in the design and the architectural program. Review stairs, elevators and corridors. Choose a key system that allows for growth and change |
| Pedestrian access | A direct path from the street to the front of the building |
| Orientation | Orient the building to allow seeing into the site |
| Doors and windows | Extra attention, especially on the ground floor |
| Conduits | Plan conduits for security, with future capacity, from the design stage; avoids expensive renovations. Consider shielding lines to protect communications |

#### 10.3.2 Industrial Buildings and Facilities

- High risk of: employee theft, burglary, robbery, industrial espionage, vandalism and arson.
- Steps for the architect or the security professional:

| Element | Recommendation |
| --- | --- |
| Traffic | Clearly define incoming and outgoing traffic |
| Perimeter | Define the boundaries with landscaping, fences, walls, etc. |
| Paths of travel | Separate the paths of public, private and service vehicles, and of pedestrians. Pedestrian routes and accesses easy for others to see |
| Building shell | Minimum of openings. Clear lobbies and entrances, as a transition from the security perimeter to the plant area. **Reinforce every opening larger than 96 sq in (619 cm²) and less than 18 ft (5.5 m) from the ground:** polycarbonate, window sheets, meshes or others. If a perimeter door must be left open for ventilation, add a chain-link door (air and visibility with security) |
| Monitoring | Exterior emergency doors with alarm, video and security monitoring |
| Service doors | They should lead directly from outside to the service areas, to reduce interior traffic. Under in-person or video surveillance |
| Shipping and receiving | Separate both functions as much as possible to reduce collusion and pilferage. Dock inside the building to expose the material less. Waiting room with restrooms for drivers, so they do not cross the building |
| Trash | Cleaning staff should reach compactors or incinerators without leaving the building |
| Research and development (R&D) | R&D and sensitive activities away from normal circulation routes |
