| Employee entrances | Directly from the employee parking lot. Wide doors for flow and so that staff can supervise against pilferage |
| Punch clocks | Near the employee entrance, separated by barriers for controlled entry and credential checks by security |
| Personnel office | At the outer edge of the building, so that applicants do not walk through it |
| Elevators | Separate freight and personnel elevators to expose cargo less to theft and pilferage |
| Warehouse | Finished product away from operating areas. Access control at its doors |
| Other storage | Tool rooms and storerooms with ceiling enclosure |

#### 10.3.3 Parking Facilities

- Parking lots and garages use CPTED with surveillance, access control and territorial reinforcement.
- It is achieved through the design interface, security patrols and technology.
- Common threats: vehicle theft, theft from vehicles, attacks on people (assault, robbery, rape) and vandalism.

**Vulnerability Assessment: the first step**

- In the U.S., the standard of care requires that it include:
  1. crime history of the site;
  2. review of landscaping, lighting, stairs, elevators, surveillance capacity, access control equipment, signage and restrooms;
  3. inspection of supervision or payment facilities;
  4. examination of operating and personnel policies and procedures.
- Who, why, what, when, where, how questions:
  1. Whom does it serve: shoppers, commuters, students or employees?
  2. How many cars use it and how fast do the spaces turn over?
  3. Are the sightlines clear or blocked by walls, columns or ramps?
  4. What are its hours and how do they affect the user's environment?
  5. Is the light natural or artificial? Are the luminaires at ceiling height? If so, what color is the ceiling and how are they placed?
  6. Is there video surveillance? With what details?
  7. Is there street-level protection: gates, screens or other barriers?
- Additional questions: vehicle and pedestrian entrances, accessible routes for the disabled, condition of elevators, location and visibility of stairs, and whether little-used areas can be selectively closed.

**On the Ground**

- Surface parking: where it can be seen from the street and from nearby occupied buildings (natural surveillance).
- Defining the perimeter and controlling access deters unwanted pedestrian passage: fences, changes of level, ground-floor protection and other barriers that channel toward designated accesses and avoid hiding places.
- **Ground floor with wire mesh; upper floors open** with tensioned cables so cars do not go past the stall.
- Mesh instead of wall and open floors: allow natural surveillance and let calls for help be heard.
- The ground-floor mesh should not run from floor to ceiling: it would serve for climbing to upper floors.
- Low shrubs next to the perimeter wall discourage climbing or cutting the mesh.
- Exterior garage doors: **egress only**.
- Varied landscaping: better low hedges with tall-canopy trees than a solid hedge. Keep everything pruned for good visibility and without hiding places.

| Vegetation rule in parking lots | Figure |
| --- | --- |
| Plants over 3 ft (1 m) prohibited near entrances | Not within 10–15 ft (3–4.5 m) |
| Pruning of low branches of mature trees | Up to 8 ft (2.5 m) |

- Exam note: the book converts 8 ft as 2.4 m in 10.2.3 and as 2.5 m here. The figure that counts is 8 ft.

**Vehicle and pedestrian access**

- Traffic engineers prefer several accesses; but more entrances make it harder to control users and uses.
- **CPTED prefers a single entrance and exit for all vehicles.**
- If volume requires more, each access needs: booth with an attendant, gate arm, roll-down shutter to close after hours, video and good lighting.
- Pedestrian access is often forgotten or poorly designed. **Primary rule: do not force the pedestrian to cross the path of the cars.**
- If unavoidable, create a safe path to a marked crossing that alerts drivers.
- Pedestrian routes can pass by the attendant's booth to make surveillance easier.
- Accessibility: reserved spaces and attention to ramps, railings, floors, crossings and pedestrian routes, stairs and elevator location and design.

**Booths, restrooms and payment machines**

- Booths: **360-degree** field of view, monitored by video, with security glass, duress alarms and drop safes with a sign that the attendant cannot take money out.
- Booth lighting sufficient for the video and dimmable, so that the attendant can see outward at night.
- Attendant's restroom: near the booth, in a surveillable area, locked and with a personal alarm inside.
- **Public restrooms: leave them out of the design.** They are a meeting point for victims and predators, and hard to secure because of privacy.
- If unavoidable: doors visible from the attendant's post; open maze-type entrances to hear screams; panic and help buttons; motion-activated light.
- Automated payment machines: in view of users and staff, against vandalism, theft and attacks on customers.

**Structural Elements**

- New construction: **round columns**, because they give more visibility than rectangular or square ones.
- Ramp most consistent with CPTED: **exterior loop**. It leaves floors flat and sightlines clear.
- Where a solid wall is needed: portholes with mesh, windows or openings to give openness and allow casual observation.
- Stairs and elevators: central location and visible from the attendant's post. If not possible, video to watch entrances and exits.
- Panic alarms and door position switches alert the attendant that someone is in the stairwell.
- Stairs visible from street level and of transparent glass.
- The bottom of the stairs must not offer hiding places. Roof exits, if it is not a parking level, must be secured.
- Rooftop mechanical room doors: always locked.
- Basement and roof doors: position switches, intercoms, screech alarms and signal transmission to security or police.
- Elevators: all the glass and visibility the structure allows. Those with glass walls on the exterior of the building are seen from the street and the garage. With intercom and audible alarms.
- High-rise or underground mixed-use garages: elevators that lead into a **lobby**, not directly to office or residential floors. From the lobby another elevator or stair is used, with screening, access control and surveillance.

**Surveillance**

- Cameras in areas of constant light, by day or with luminaires.
- Low-light cameras: possible, but more expensive and they tacitly admit that the lighting may be poor.
- Cameras with an unobstructed view and out of reach. On surface lots, good sightlines and maximum coverage.
- Protected in dark polycarbonate domes: they resist vandalism and hide where they are looking.
- Video monitored in real time and recorded digitally to replay and enhance images.
- Color cameras: better identify vehicles and people; useful as evidence.
- Panic buttons (call boxes) integrated with the video: when pressed, a camera is activated.
- Video integrated with access control: captures license plates on entry or exit.

**Lighting**

- Without good light, video surveillance and natural surveillance are weakened.
- The book says that "at least 175 yards of lighting" suffice to detect human movement. Exam note: the sentence is confusing (yards is not a unit of illumination); it appears to be an erratum.
- Reference: *Guideline for Security Lighting for People, Property, and Critical Infrastructure* (Illuminating Engineering Society of North America, IESNA, 2017).

| Area | Recommended level |
| --- | --- |
| Gathering areas: stairs, elevators, ramps | 5–6 fc (foot-candles) = 54–65 lux (lm/m²) |
| Walkways around garages | 5 fc |
| Open parking lots (also in commercial zones, hotels, motels and apartment buildings) | Minimum 3 fc (32 lux) |
| Entrances | 10 fc (108 lux) or twice the level of the surroundings, so that they stand out |
| Perimeter fences | At least half of the average horizontal illumination on both sides of the fence |
| Paths to garages | 3 fc (32 lux), to see a person at 30 ft (9 m) or more; average/minimum ratio no greater than 4:1 |

| Luminaire height | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| Typical poles of 30–45 ft (9–14 m) | Wide band of light, but deep shadows between cars |
| Pedestrian-level light at 12–14 ft (3.6–4.2 m) | Passes through car windows and reflects off them; reduces shadows and dark areas |

- Ideal in an open parking lot: combine high and low light for more coverage and fewer shadows and hiding places.
- Garage interior painted in light colors to reflect more light.
- Luminaires with polycarbonate lenses against vandalism and breakage.
- Maintenance protocol: repair damaged lights and replace burned-out bulbs promptly; replace those that work according to their service life.
- **Fort Lauderdale (Florida) case:** a garage painted white circles on the ceiling to reflect light. Its ceilings were higher than average, which improved the distribution.

| Light source | Service life | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Metal halide | About 20,000 h | Preferred by most CPTED practitioners; renders the color of cars, clothing and people well | More expensive than high-pressure sodium and mercury vapor |
| Low-pressure sodium vapor | About 50,000 h | The most energy-efficient | Poor color rendition; not suitable for capturing crime scene details |
| High-pressure sodium vapor | Shorter than metal halide | Cheaper than metal halide | Lasts less and renders color worse |
| Mercury vapor | Shorter than metal halide | Cheaper than metal halide | Lasts less and renders color worse |

- There is no single answer. CPTED allows for diversity of lighting according to the risk and threat assessment and the desired user experience.

**Signage**

- Well lit, with letters or symbols **at least 8 in (20 cm)** high.
- Wall signs for pedestrians and vehicles: graphic whenever possible, for universal comprehension and clear direction.
- Graffiti is illegitimate signage: it usually marks the territory of gangs or vandals. Remove it as soon as possible.
- Prevention: anti-graffiti epoxy paint on walls and more light in problem areas to increase natural surveillance.
- Trying to prevent graffiti communicates that the property is the territory of its legitimate owner.

**Mixed Uses**

- Trend: integrating parking into mixed-use developments. More legitimate users and more "eyes on the street" reinforce territoriality.
- Many garages add shops: copy centers, fast food or car washes, compatible activities that attract legitimate users.
- By day, spaces reserved for businesses; at night, flat rate for clubs, restaurants and neighbors with a night permit.

#### 10.3.4 Schools

- Challenge: large population, many entrances and many windows on the ground floor.
- **Conditions that generate problems:** (1) poorly defined campus boundaries; (2) informal gathering areas out of sight; (3) layout with isolated points (Figure 10.6); (4) bus loading that conflicts with cars; (5) student parking as far as possible from the building; (6) students parked on the street, in conflict with the neighborhood; (7) parking lots hidden by plants; (8) lockers that create confusion and help hide contraband; (9) excessive use of hallways with blind spots; (10) restrooms far from supervision.
- **Figure 10.6 (Layouts to Avoid):** avoid "crime generators": remote, little-used or unobserved areas.
- CPTED contributes with natural access control, surveillance, territoriality, boundary definition, management and maintenance, **without turning the school into a fortress**. Technology complements.
- **Focus areas:**
  - *Site:* landscaping, perimeter boundary, exterior pedestrian routes, vehicle routes and parking, recreation areas.
  - *Building design:* building organization, covered exterior walkways, entry points, enclosed exterior spaces, auxiliary buildings, walls, windows, doors, roofs, lighting.
  - *Interior spaces:* lobby and reception, hallways, restrooms, stairs, cafeterias, auditoriums, gymnasiums, libraries and media centers, classrooms, locker rooms, laboratories, workshops, music rooms, computer rooms, administrative areas.
  - *Systems and equipment:* alarms and surveillance, fire control, HVAC (heating, ventilation and air conditioning) and mechanical equipment, vending machines, drinking fountains, elevators, telephony, information systems.
- The school communicates its relationship to the surroundings through its **edges**. Landscaping marks boundaries and can restrict access. Fences and gates must allow seeing through; plants must not create hiding places.
- Administrative offices with a clear view of play areas, gathering areas and parking: raises the risk of identification and intervention for the intruder. Allow seeing toward and from courtyards, classrooms and high-risk areas.

**Design recommendations for schools:**

- **Observation from classrooms:** parking and circulation in view of the classrooms (many students, more casual observation).
- **Observation of vehicular traffic:** offices with a line of sight to entry roads and parking lots. No one should enter without being detected.
- **Surveillance points:** view of problem areas from spaces of public use, such as a common stairway.
- **Exterior circulation:** wide paths that do not serve as an informal gathering place; bicycle racks in a highly visible area.
- **Traffic calming:** parking lots with few or no long straight stretches; speed and stop signs; bus without conflict with other traffic.
- **Signage:** announce intended and prohibited uses; clear, reasonably sized and easy to see.
- **Spatial/temporal issues:** safe activities in unsafe places (hallways next to offices; adult education at night). Separate cafeteria entrance and exit. Different lunch times for older and younger students. Driving classes in the parking lot.
- **Lighting:** dusk-to-dawn light on the grounds.
- **Covered circulation:** minimize blind spots and entrapment points; eliminate "door in the face" incidents.
- **Main entry:** obvious and with few entrances; planned access to other areas; little confusion. Weapons detectors can be integrated.
- **Recessed entries:** avoid blind spots; chamfer corners to see around the turn and avoid ambushes.
- **Doors and doorways:** poorly designed recessed doors create blind spots. Institutional-grade doors and frames. Faceplates over locks against jimmying.
- **Courtyards and gathering places:** formal areas that are defined, lit and observed. If the courts attract problems at night, remove nets and hoops at the end of the day. If the gym is used after hours, be able to isolate the rest of the building.
- **Walls:** no hiding places; graffiti- and vandal-resistant materials; landscaping can protect them. **Perimeter wall: at least 7 ft (2.1 m) with barbed wire on top.**
- **Windows:** several small windows give the same as one large one with more security. High windows (clerestory) give light and air without easy access. Glass block wall with clerestory: few openings, security and light. All with self-engaging locks.
- **Video surveillance:** continuous operation and archived recordings.
- **Duress alarms:** in isolated areas such as restrooms and lockers; integrated and with top response priority.
- **Computers/Laptops:** secure them one by one and check them regularly.

#### 10.3.5 Automated Teller Machines, ATMs

- They are "where the easy money is": they are often isolated and the user carries or deposits cash. CPTED reduces assault, robbery and homicide of customers.
- **1. Adequate lighting:** the user sees suspects and witnesses (including the police) see the crime. Light at the building corners next to the ATM and in the nearby parking lot, without shadows or hiding places.
  - **The U.S. has no national standards for bank lighting**; some cities and states do.
  - Typical minimums, measured at 3 ft (1 m) from the ground: **25 fc (269 lux) at the front of the ATM; 10 fc (107 lux) within 5 ft (1.5 m); 2 fc (22 lux) at 50–60 ft (15–18 m).**
  - Exam note: here 10 fc = 107 lux and in parking lots 108 lux. It is rounding; the figure is 10 fc.
  - The minimums do not cover everything: shadows, type, color, direction and uniformity of the light, glare and obstacles. A lot of light at the front and little around prevents seeing dangers.
  - Photosensors that turn on at dusk; monitor levels; long-life bulbs; automatic monitors that alert the operator; protected luminaires.
- **2. Visible landscaping:** prune routinely; **the best is to have no shrubs**; if there are any, slow-growing. Remove containers, benches or walls that block the view.
- **3. Mirrors:** rearview mirrors at the ATM and at nearby corners.
- **4. Where natural surveillance abounds:** in view of pedestrians, drivers, patrols and cameras. The opportunist avoids open sites, and help for the victim increases. Vestibules with large vision panels and duress alarms with real response. Protect the person servicing the machine (alarms, secure rooms). In stores: no plants or blinds, visible from the street. Against attacks on the machine: alarms, impact and seismic sensors, weight and resistance to pulling, heat detectors and locking mechanisms.
- **5. At police stations:** if the extra traffic does not fit, only at night, when the risk of robbery is higher. Also at post offices or fire stations.
- **6. High-risk sites:** review local crime rates. Not in drug trafficking areas, next to abandoned buildings or problem bars. If needed: move, close or limit hours.
- **7. Cameras:** two purposes, deterring robbery and fraud and identifying the offender. Record the close-up of the user and what is behind. Visible ones deter more but suffer vandalism. **Dummy cameras only if there are also real ones**; otherwise, false security and a negligence lawsuit for the "illusion of security". One operator used off-camera heat sensors that trigger a voice message or silent alarm.
- **8. Calling for help during the robbery:** panic button (risk of false alarms); telephone next to the ATM; monitored open microphone; alarm if the vestibule door is left open too long; Reverse PIN (PIN, personal identification number, entered backwards or with an extra digit triggers a silent alarm; expensive and hard under stress, which is why it is rarely installed).
- **9. Private guards:** only at high-risk ATMs or patrolling several at random. It is expensive.
- **10. Prohibit loitering and aggressive panhandling:** laws give the police authority to move opportunists away.
- **11. Locked vestibules:** they can help, but the customer can be trapped with the offender; people hold the door for others; they attract homeless people.
- **12. Daily withdrawal limits:** reduce the loss and discourage some; but most street robbers do not expect much money and take the risk anyway.
- **13. Training users:** put the money away discreetly before leaving; check whether they are being watched; go accompanied at night; look inside the vestibule before entering; keep others from seeing their PIN over the shoulder; watch the parking lot (loiterers, containers, cars).

#### 10.3.6 U.S. Federal Buildings

- **1995: bombing of the Alfred P. Murrah Federal Building (Oklahoma City).** It generated security standards for all federal facilities; several states did the same.
- The standards of the **GSA (General Services Administration)** promote the defensible space/CPTED approach: edges that define circulation and filters between legitimate and illegitimate users.
- They cover: security glazing, blast-resistant design and construction, landscaping, site lighting and natural and mechanical surveillance.
- They vary by level: a **Level 1** may not require entry control; a **Level 4** may require electronic controls and video.
- Context (outside the book): the 1995 Department of Justice report defined five levels (I–V); today the federal standards are set by the ISC (Interagency Security Committee).
- **Four GSA categories:**
  - *Perimeter and exterior security:* parking area and controls; video monitoring; lighting with emergency backup; physical barriers.
  - *Entry security:* intrusion detection system; upgrade to life safety standards; screening of mail, people and packages; entry control with video and electric door strikes; high security locks.
  - *Interior security:* employee credentials and visitor control; control of access to utilities; emergency power for critical systems; location of day care centers.
  - *Security planning:* locate tenants according to their security needs; blast standards.
- These criteria balance cost-effectiveness, acceptance of some risk and public access to the buildings.
- **Measures against bomb attacks:**
  - Secure perimeter as far out as possible: **setback of 100 ft (30 m)**.
  - Concrete barriers disguised as planters or works of art, next to the sidewalk and far from the building, **spaced less than 4 ft (1.2 m) apart** to stop vehicles from passing.
  - New buildings with a simple rectangular plan: less diffraction of the blast wave that bounces off U- or L-shaped buildings.
  - Reduce ornaments that can come loose; lightweight exterior cladding.
  - Eliminate hiding places; clear views and building in view of other occupied facilities.
  - Eliminate vehicle approaches perpendicular to the building; minimize vehicle accesses.
  - Eliminate or strictly control parking under the building; parking as far away as possible and in view of occupied rooms.
  - Light the building exterior.
  - Secure access to power or heating plants, gas pipes, water, and electric and telephone service.

### 10.4 Integration of CPTED and Traditional Security

- Only in rare cases is CPTED enough by itself to manage risk.
- Within a comprehensive asset protection strategy it is an excellent, cost-effective complement to typical physical security. A single tool rarely solves everything.
- **How it can reduce costs:** (1) reduce or reassign guards; (2) reduce the necessary reach of the video system; (3) natural access control that replaces some structural barriers; (4) lower insurance premiums.
- It also improves life safety: better evacuation routes and fewer incidental crimes against employees, residents and users.
- In a "big picture" program, protection objectives are better met, normally at lower cost or with the cost shared among departments or cost centers.
- Future threats: street crime and workplace violence will continue; sabotage and terrorism against critical infrastructure may be added.
- CPTED may not stop the most determined terrorist. But terrorism often begins with intrusion and unauthorized access to study vulnerabilities.
- If the target is not easy to access or has good security, the attacker may look for a more vulnerable one.
- Conclusion: CPTED is a legitimate strategy for reducing the opportunity for terrorism and ordinary crime.

### Key terms

CPTED · Defensible Space · built environment · territoriality · natural surveillance · natural access control · natural territorial reinforcement · management and maintenance · legitimate activity support · broken windows theory · capable guardian · routine activity theory · situational crime prevention · repeat victimization · piggybacking · target hardening · mechanical / organizational / natural measures · first / second / third generation CPTED · cohesion · capacity threshold (tipping point) · community culture · connectivity · CPTED 3-D (designation, definition, design) · 4 Ds (deter, detect, delay, deny) · programming · schematic design · design development · construction documents · architectural program · security zoning · unrestricted / controlled / restricted zones · berm · clerestory windows · foot-candle · metal halide · low-pressure sodium · crime generators · duress alarm · illusion of security · reverse PIN · setback · GSA security levels

## Capítulo 11 — Electronic Security Systems (págs. 253–416)

**Central idea:** Electronic systems decide who enters, detect what must not enter or leave, and warn of intrusions. They then make it possible to view, assess and communicate alarms in order to respond in time. The chapter closes with the integration of all these subsystems and their trends.

- Sections: 11.1 Access Control Systems · 11.2 Contraband Detection · 11.3 Physical Intrusion Detection Systems · 11.4 Video Surveillance · 11.5 Communications and Annunciation Systems · 11.6 Trends and Issues in Electronic Systems Integration.
- The chapter has no introduction of its own: it opens directly with 11.1.

### 11.1 Access Control Systems

- **Electronic access control system:** validates one or more credentials of three types:
  - something you know (*what you know*): PIN, password;
  - something you are, inherent to you (*what you are*): biometrics;
  - something you carry (*what you have*): card, key, credential.
- **Components of a complete system:** *credential reader*; communication wiring; *distributed processor*; central database; software; interfaces with external systems; applications for *request-to-exit* (REX) devices at the doors that require them.
- **Personnel access control:** the part of the system that authorizes entry and verifies the authorization of whoever asks to enter a controlled area. It checks whether the person:
  1. carries a valid credential;
  2. knows a valid *personal identification number* (PIN);
  3. has the unique physical trait that matches the one recorded at *enrollment*: fingerprint, hand geometry.
- **Key difference between methods:**
  - Except for biometrics, the devices can be used without the authorized person (they are lent or stolen).
  - Only the match of a physical trait verifies the person's identity.
  - A credential or a number only proves that someone has a valid credential or knows a valid number.
- **Combining technologies:** reduces *throughput*, but makes the system harder to defeat.
- **Entry authorization methods:** PIN; credentials; *positive personnel identity verification* or biometrics.

| Forms of identification required | Name in the book |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Single factor authentication |
| 2 | Dual factor authentication |
| 3 | Three factor authorization |

- **Exam note:** the book calls the third level *three factor authorization*. For consistency with the other two, it is *authentication* (three-factor authentication).

#### 11.1.1 Personnel Access Control

| Measure for deciding whom to let through | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Credentials or objects the person carries | Metal key; proximity device; chip insertion card; magnetic stripe card; photo credential (basic access control tool) |
| Private information the person knows | Password; PIN |
| Biometric traits (give the highest level of security) | Fingerprint; hand geometry; iris and retina patterns; signature; speech patterns |

- The most secure systems combine several methods to authenticate and validate.
- A company with several sites, even if far apart, can control them all with a single electronic system.
- There are *remotely managed access control services* run by third parties. They are not recommended for high security.
- Sites with classified material, such as SCIFs (*Sensitive Compartmented Information Facilities*):
  - follow the requirements of the security agency that oversees them;
  - use only approved products and services.
- Other sites may use electronic *pushbutton locks*: no central administration or connection, with minimal protection.

##### Means of Defeating Access Control Points

| Attack | What it consists of |
| --- | --- |
| *Deceit* | False pretexts to convince the guard or an employee to let someone through. *Social engineering* is a real threat. Training staff in the security plan helps, though it is not infallible |
| *Direct physical attack* | Force and tools to get in |
| *Technical attack* | Forging a credential; guessing or obtaining passcodes or PINs |

##### Objectives of a *comprehensive access control system*

1. Let in and out only authorized people and vehicles.
2. Allow auditing of compliance with access control policies.
3. Detect and prevent the entry of contraband.
4. Detect and prevent unauthorized removal of assets.
5. Provide information to assess and respond in time to exceptions and alarms.

##### Key design questions

- Will it be integrated with alarms, video surveillance, building automation and elevators?
  - Almost all products allow it: some out of the box, others with extra software modules.
  - Review the product's limitations and licenses carefully.
- What *throughput* is wanted at each controlled point?
- Are entries and exits of people and vehicles recorded? If so, how are authorizations, rejections and exceptions coded for later investigation?
- Once integrated, does each component meet building and fire codes?
- Who maintains the database? Will it be integrated with the employee information system? This is a major concern for Human Resources.
- Balancing access control and *life safety* creates challenges. Codes and life safety almost always prevail.

##### Personal Identification Number (PIN)

- **How it works:** the user types a memorized number on a *keypad*.
- **Variant with credential:** the coded credential locates the reference file for that badge number in the database. Then the person types the PIN. If it matches the stored one, the person enters.
- The PIN is chosen by the person or assigned. Usual length: 4 to 6 digits.
- **Weaknesses:**
  1. The PIN and the credential can be passed to someone unauthorized.
  2. An adversary can see it on the sly, looking over the shoulder (*shoulder surfing*).
  3. It can be obtained under duress.
  4. In addition, people tend to write it down.
- **Against observation:** *scrambler-type PIN reader*.
  - The digits change places on each use.
  - It can only be seen from a very narrow angle.
  - It is usually paired with credential readers, such as proximity readers.
- **Two criteria for a secure PIN:**
  1. Enough digits so that it cannot be guessed. This is key if the PIN is the only entry criterion.
  2. That it have no special meaning for its holder.
- **Numerical example:** for a few hundred people, 4 digits are enough. They give 10,000 combinations, many more than people; guessing is unlikely.
- **Numbers to avoid if the person chooses it:** birth dates; parts of the social security number; telephone numbers; the year; easy sequences such as 1-1-1-1 or 1-2-3-4.
- **Duress code:** several integrators offer a panic code:
  - swapping the last two digits of the PIN; or
  - typing the PIN backwards.
  - Requirement: the last two digits (when swapping) or all four (when reversing) must be different. Otherwise, the panic code would be the same as the normal PIN.
- **Maximum attempts:** some systems, after several failed attempts, cancel the credential or send an alarm to the central station.
- Combining PIN with credential and biometrics raises security.
- **Scenarios the software allows, by time of day and day of the week:**
  - credential without PIN;
  - PIN without credential;
  - PIN and credential together.

##### Credentials

| Type of credential | How it is checked | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| *Photo identification badge* | By hand, by a very attentive guard | The most practical and accepted |
| *Exchange badge* | By hand, by a very attentive guard | The inside badge never leaves the area |
| *Stored-image badge* | By hand, by a very attentive guard | Compares against a stored image |
| *Coded credential* | Automatically | Basis of electronic systems |

**Photo identification badge**

- Common, but not always effective:
  - it can be forged;
  - someone can use makeup to look like the photo on a stolen badge;
  - the check is manual and the guard gets distracted, especially when many people are entering.
- Modern printers curb forgery with *overlays*, holograms and *dye sublimation* (the photo is printed directly on the card).
- Many access control packages include a photo module: enrollment, photo capture and issuance in a single step.

| Printer function | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| *Bulk card printing* | After photographing many people, it prints unattended for later distribution. Options such as alphabetical order make distribution easier |
| *Dye sublimation printing* | Prints directly on the badge. May require lamination against UV (ultraviolet) rays and scratches |
| *Overlays* | Can be customized, with holograms or other elements that are hard to copy |
| *On-board encoding* | Some print bar codes and encode magnetic stripe and smart card |

- The software must be synchronized: a single integrated credential database.
- **Control against the *insider threat*:**
  - Keep blank cards and those already encoded locked in a secure container.
  - Whoever makes the card requests the blank material from a second person, preferably a supervisor.
  - Reason: an intruder with a valid card is very hard to stop.
  - Protect the encoding information in the same way. If it leaks, the adversary can clone a credential in use, with the same access.
- **Update photos every 5 to 10 years:** reprinting costs money, but people change a lot in a decade.

**Exchange badge**

- At each control point a twin badge of each employee is kept.
- The guard compares the person with the photo on the twin. If they match, he exchanges badges and lets the person through.
- The twin can carry more information and be a different color.
- The employee's stays at the control point until he leaves; then they are swapped back.
- **Advantage:** the badge used inside the secure area never leaves it. Reduces forgery, loss and theft.
- **Limit:** it does not prevent someone from using makeup to look like the photo on a stolen badge.

**Stored-image badge (stored image or *video comparator*)**

- The guard compares a securely stored image with the real-time image of whoever asks to enter.
- **Its two most important features:**
  - *Enrollment capability*: maximum number of images the system stores.
  - *Access time*: from when the identification number is entered until the image appears.
- The image is retrieved with a coded credential or keypad: by hand or by passing the credential through a reader at the guard post.
- **It is not *personnel identity verification***: it does not measure a unique, measurable trait, like a fingerprint.
- **Advantage over the manual photo:** the stored image is hard to alter. In that respect it equals the exchange badge.
- **Limit:** it is still vulnerable to makeup.
- The function of displaying the stored image (*call-up*) comes in most software. If needed, confirm that the vendor offers it.
- More advanced systems show the photos on portable devices:
  - they read the card at a distance and request that the image be sent;
  - they reinforce checkpoints and extend the perimeter.

**Coded credential: functions the system can offer**

- Record of entry authorization for each credential.
- Unique machine-readable identification codes.
- Deactivation of a person without having to recover his credential.
- Several levels of authorization: only certain access points or certain hours.
- *Antipassback* on entrances and exits, such as parking lots or controlled laboratories.
- *Time zone control and holiday management*: who goes where and when, according to time zones and local holidays.
- Integration with visitor management and issuance of temporary credentials.
- Photo software: capture, printing and link to employee data.
- Interface with Human Resources for payroll and other records.
- Contractor management and payment.
- Interface for *biometric template enrollment*.
- Alarm management: prioritization, response notes, passwords, maps and other graphical interfaces.
- Notification of doors held open beyond the access time or forced.
- Manual opening and closing of access points by authorized operators.
- Integration with video to assess alarms and analyze past events.
- Interface with *building management*.
- Administrator user levels to create, modify and delete records.
- Limits on which parts of the database and which functions each operator uses.
- Record of who modified or attempted to modify a file, field or database (system audit).
- Each use of the credential can be recorded: action, time, place and credential number, listable on request.
- Many coded credentials are a badge worn on the premises.

##### Badge Coding and Types

Most common techniques: magnetic stripe, Wiegand wire, bar code, proximity and smart card.

**Magnetic stripe**

- Widely used in credit and debit cards. A magnetic stripe on one edge stores the data; it is read when swiped or inserted in the reader.
- ***Coercivity*:** the material's resistance to having its data changed by a magnetic field. It is the field strength needed to change the information.
- **Unit:** oersted (Oe).

| Material | Coercivity | Typical use | Risk of erasure |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Low-coercivity* | 300 Oe | Credit cards | Relatively easy to erase |
| *High-coercivity* | 2,500–4,000 Oe | Security credentials (the most used) | Very unlikely by accident. Household magnets do not erase it; *rare-earth magnets* can alter it |

- Alphanumeric encoding allows including name and credential number. There is little space on a credit-card-size card: take care with the design.
- **Disadvantage:** forging is relatively easy. With commercial equipment the data are decoded or duplicates are encoded.
  - Mitigation: proprietary, nonstandard encoding and reading.
  - Cost: it limits connection with other equipment and the options for expansion or upgrade.

**Wiegand wire vs. OSDP**

- **Wiegand:** old technology that is now hardly used. Its signal format has been a de facto standard since the 1980s, and its data protocol is still widespread.
- **OSDP (*Open Supervised Device Protocol*):** communication protocol for access control that improves interoperability between products.
  - It became an international IEC (International Electrotechnical Commission) standard and was adopted by the SIA (Security Industry Association) in 2020.
  - It provides more security, functionality, interoperability and consistency than Wiegand.
  - **Wiring:** Wiegand requires 12 or more wires between door and panel (reader, electric strike, alarm contact and REX). OSDP needs only 2 wires for everything.
  - It protects readers against hacking with AES-128 encryption (*Advanced Encryption Standard*).
- **Exam note (outside the book):** in practice OSDP runs over RS-485 and usually uses 4 conductors (2 for data and 2 for power). The IEC standard is 60839-11-5 (2020). For the exam, stick with the book's figure: 2 wires versus 12 or more.

**Bar code**

- Widely used in retail; sometimes on credentials. The width of the bars and spaces forms the code.
- An optical sensor scans it and sends the data to a decoder. It is printed on the credential and used like the magnetic stripe.
- **Disadvantage:** easy to duplicate, unless it carries an opaque cover. That cover is increasingly common on security credentials.
- *2-D bar codes* store more information than one-dimensional ones.

**Proximity badge**

- It is read without physically inserting it into the reader. It is a small transponder or radio frequency (RF) transmitter.
- It is classified by three criteria:

| Criterion | Types |
| --- | --- |
| Power source | **Active:** long-life battery. Some use the battery only on entering the interrogation field; others always transmit and the antenna picks up the data as they approach. **Passive:** takes its energy from the reader's RF signal on entering the field |
| Frequency range | **Low frequency:** 125 kHz range. **High frequency:** from 2.5 MHz to over 1 GHz |
| Data capacity | **Read-only:** code set at the factory, does not change. **Read/write:** larger data field; programmed by the system administrator |

- **Advantages:**
  - widely used; somewhat hard to duplicate;
  - readers with no moving parts, suitable for any climate;
  - readers in vandal-resistant housings, protected from almost all vandalism;
  - long-range readers exist, convenient for parking gates and vehicle entrances.
- **Risks and countermeasures:**
  - Long range also allows reading and copying poorly secured cards with an RFID (*Radio Frequency Identification*) tool. Countermeasure: sleeves, wallets or badge holders with RFID shielding.
  - Readers with compromised firmware: the attacker gains access to what is stored in the reader. Countermeasure: dual factor enabled (proximity + PIN or proximity + biometrics).
- ***Facility code*:** extra number encoded on the credential, unique to the company.
  - It keeps another tenant with the same credential number from entering your facility.
  - It is read together with the credential number.

**Smart card**

- Credit card size, with an integrated circuit inside.
- **Contact:** metal contacts on the surface that communicate with the reader.
- ***Contactless*:** communicates by RF; it has no metal contacts.
- **Memory-only card vs. true smart card:**
  - Memory-only: works like the magnetic stripe (number, name and other data).
  - True: carries a microprocessor; that is what makes it "smart".
- **Memory:** from 8 KB to 64 KB, with a projection of 1 MB in the future.
- **Main advantages:** lots of memory and strong resistance to forgery or compromise. Many encrypt the communication. They are valued relative to their cost.
- **When it makes sense:**
  - **Does not make sense** (poor return on investment): large population and a security level that is not extreme.
  - **Makes sense:** small population in a very high security facility.
  - **Makes sense:** limiting access to certain areas of a large facility, such as precious metals or executive suites.
  - **Can be cost-effective:** if it combines security with administrative records (training, medical files or asset control).

**Hybrid cards**

- They combine several technologies: smart card functions, high-coercivity magnetic stripe, holder photo and even bar code.
- **Limit:** the space available for more encoding arrays.

**Mobile credentials: NFC and Bluetooth**

- NFC (*Near Field Communication*) and Bluetooth on smartphones are gaining a lot of traction as a credential.
- Banking apps and other trusted transactions ("I am who I say I am") are quickly raising the bar for this type of credential.
- Many smart card manufacturers are developing NFC credential and provisioning software.

#### 11.1.2 Locks

- They secure the movable parts of barriers.
- They should not be the only protection for important areas: with skill and time they are defeated. Always with complementary measures.
- They are the most widely used method for controlling physical access: homes, vehicles, offices, hotels, safes, desks, cabinets, file cabinets, briefcases, display cases and jewelry boxes.
- They are among the oldest security devices.
- **Two general classes:**
  1. Purely mechanical.
  2. Those that combine electrical power with mechanical operation; they are associated with automated access control systems.
- They vary by physical type, application and mode of operation.

##### Mechanical Locks

- Examples: door locks, cabinet locks and padlocks. An arrangement of physical parts prevents the bolt or latch from being opened.
- **Two main components:**
  - *Coded mechanism*: key cylinder, or *wheel pack* in a combination lock.
  - *Fastening device*: latch or bolt.

| Aspect | *Latch* | *Bolt* |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Behavior | Retracts by itself when the door is closed | Stays fixed until someone moves it |
| Convenience | More convenient | Less convenient |
| Security | More vulnerable | More secure, especially if it projects at least 1 in (2.5 cm) from the edge of the door |

- Many locks that used to be "mechanical" now come with an integrated battery and credential reading. Some have wireless communication: they bridge the mechanical and the electrified.
- *Disc tumbler* lock: born for the early automobile industry. Today it is used in doors, cabinets, padlocks and cylinders.
- **Four functional sets of a mechanical lock:**
  1. *Bolt or latch*: joins the movable part (door, window) to the fixed part (jamb, frame).
  2. *Keeper or strike*: where the bolt enters. It is not part of the mechanism; it is its secure housing when closed.
  3. *Tumbler array*: the barrier or maze that must be overcome to move the bolt.
  4. Key or unlocking device: designed to overcome the barrier and move the bolt.
- Normally the bolt and the barrier are in the fixed hardware (*lockset*) and the key is separate.
- **Exception, physical logic devices:** the whole lock is a single assembly. Examples: integrated digital keypad that mechanically releases the bolt; dial combination locks.

| Type | Origin | How it works | Uses | Security |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Warded lock* | The first developed and the oldest in use | Open, visible keyhole; long barrel-type key | Old houses, farm buildings, old inns | Very simple. Weak against spring manipulation with any key the wards do not stop, and against corrosion. A modern program does not include it: replace or reinforce it in phases |
| *Lever lock* | Lever principle perfected in the 18th century | Flat metal levers on a common pivot, held by a spring. Each has a cut for the *fence*, a projection of the bolt. When aligned, the bolt retracts | Desks, cabinets, lockers, bank safe-deposit boxes, U.S. mail boxes | More secure than the warded lock. With 2 or more cuts per lever it admits a master key. Prone to *picking*, although there are anti-pick measures. Do not confuse with a lever handle |
| *Pin tumbler* | 19th century, Linus Yale (who also created the dial combination lock) | Mechanism that moves the bolt + maze + keyway; it is distinguished in the maze. Conventional cylinder: equidistant pins in a row; 5, 6 or 7 pins | Probably the most widely used in the U.S. on exterior and interior doors | The most important development in the history of the mechanical lock. With recent improvements it achieves high security |
| *Wafer tumbler* | Fairly late development | Flat wafers of metal or other material lock the *plug* to the *shell* | Not indicated by the book. Admits *double-bitted keys* | Admits a master key |
| *Dial combination lock* | Also by Linus Yale | Keyless. Similar to the lever lock: aligns the *gates* of the wheels so that the fence enters. Circular, interdependent wheels: moving one moves the others | Safes and cabinets | Order matters: it is a true combination lock, not a permutation lock |

**Pin tumbler details**

- With a master key, *split pins* are added. The number of *key changes* drops considerably.
- **Change keying system:** the key that opens a single lock within a master keyed system.
- **Basic countermeasure against all manipulation:** tight tolerances and more pins, discs or levers.
- **High-security cylinder:**
  - Pins and *drivers* are interlocked. Neither a pick nor a key without the exact code aligns them.
  - The key is cut at precise angles, in addition to depths. It raises each pin to the *shear line* and at the same time rotates it.
  - In this way the interlock passes through a special slot at the base of the plug, and the plug turns enough to move the bolt.
  - The book calls this design "paracentric"; it seeks to make picking difficult.
  - **Medeco variant:** instead of the slot at the base of the plug, a *side bar* enters a recess in the shell or retracts into slots in the pins.
  - In both types, keys with specific angles are very hard to copy without the manufacturer's special equipment.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** in locksmithing, "paracentric" usually describes a zigzag-profile keyway, not rotating pins. Memorize the book's version.

**Calculating combinations (dial lock)**

- Theoretical maximum = positions per wheel raised to the number of wheels. A good lock typically has 100 positions per wheel.

```latex
\text{combinations} = P^{n} \qquad 100^{4} = 100{,}000{,}000
```

- Example from the book: 4 wheels of 100 positions give 100,000,000 possible combinations.

**Electronic combination locks**

- They replace dial locks on safes and secure document cabinets.
- The user generates the power by turning the dial.
- The number is seen on an LCD (*liquid crystal display*), not on the dial markings.
- The display can only be seen from a limited angle. The number bears no relation to the dial position.
- **Functions:**
  - time limit of a certain number of seconds between each number dialed;
  - *two-person rule*: two numbers must be dialed to open;
  - one number per user: audit trail of which combination opened it and when;
  - memory of failed attempts.
- Because of their electronic precision, they do not lose combinations to unusable adjacent numbers.
- They are claimed to be immune to the typical attacks on mechanical ones and to electrical and magnetic attacks.

##### Master Keying

- **Principle:** a lock is opened by more than one key. Each key acts on different tumblers or on different aspects of the same tumblers.
- *Reusable cores* provide security and an economical solution to the loss or theft of keys.
- **Access hierarchy:** from a single lock, to increasingly larger groups, up to all the locks.
- **Rationale:** with hundreds or thousands of locks, it is not feasible for someone who needs broad access to carry one key per lock. Master groups are created where one key opens the whole group.
- **Three security difficulties** (weigh them against convenience):
  1. **Key control:** requires very strict accountability. Losing, compromising or misusing a master key exposes the whole group. Restricted-access key cabinets and PC software help.
  2. **More weak points against manipulation:** several shear lines or gate openings are created for opening on the sly.
  3. **More maintenance in cylinders:** there are more parts. The master pieces, often fragile, get displaced or break; the lock jams and has to be disassembled.

##### Security Vulnerabilities of Mechanical Locks

- Some attacks require a great deal of skill; others are almost invited by the very look of the lock.

| Attack | How it is done |
| --- | --- |
| *Attack by force* | Separating the door from the jamb; pulling the lock out of its housing and moving the bolt by hand; breaking the pins so the plug turns freely |
| *Surreptitious attack* | Picking, raking, bumping or manipulation of design flaws with special locksmith tools. There are tutorial videos of these "hacks" |
| *Impression-making and try keys* | **Impression:** a blank key of the correct profile, without cuts, is inserted and turned with pressure to leave slight marks. **Try keys** (or *jingle keys*): well-milled blanks for that keyway, with random cuts; they are inserted, turning and raking until the lock opens |

##### Rearranging Mechanical Locks

- **When it is needed:** changes in authorized personnel; system compromise; loss or lack of control of keys; major changes in occupancy.
- **Ways of doing it:**
  1. **Relocate the lock:** rotate locks between doors or cabinets. Only works if nothing on the lock lets a former key holder recognize it.
  2. **Reorder the tumblers (most effective):** new combination inside each lock.
     - Levers and wafers: disassemble and change the order; the same tumblers serve many times.
     - Pins: the same pins, in different positions.
- **Convertible or interchangeable core:** option from some pin tumbler lock manufacturers.
  - Allows combinations to be redistributed very quickly.
  - It is swapped on the spot for another core already prepared with the new scheme.

##### Electrified Locking Mechanisms

- They are locked and unlocked from a remote device: electric button, motion sensor, card reader, digital keypad or biometric reader.
- They can be mechanical or electromagnetic.
- **Power:** direct current (DC), alternating current (AC), batteries or PoE (*Power over Ethernet*). It must be compatible with the available power and with the other access control functions.
- **Boolean logic:** organizes conditions into sets. Example: "if door A and B are closed, C can be opened". Useful in *mantraps* and other high security measures.
- When analyzing failures and ways of defeating a lock, also assess the vulnerability of the remote-control devices.

**Fail-safe, fail-secure and fail-soft**

| Term | Definition | Where it applies |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fail-safe | Unlocks on any failure. The typical failure is a power outage; failures of the mechanism and of the connected control also count | Most (not all) of the locks on code-required exits: free exit if a blackout and a fire coincide |
| Fail-secure | Stays locked on loss of power or other failure | Can be on the egress route if free exit exists whatever the state of the power or the control |
| Fail-soft (earlier term) | Reduced capacity of the system due to the failure of some element | System as a whole |

- Fail-safe and fail-secure usually refer to doors on the *path of egress*. They must open throughout occupancy or only on a detected fire.
- Fail-safe is not needed if the door already lets people out by turning the handle or pushing the bar from the secure side. Example: the electrified panic bar opens mechanically when pushed, with or without power.
- **Single action rule:** codes require exit with a single action and without special knowledge.
  - **Valid:** turning the handle; pushing an *exit device* or *panic bar*.
  - **Not valid:** pressing a button, turning a key, using a reader or entering a code before turning the handle.
  - **Exceptions:** banks, jewelry stores and other high security environments.
  - Fire codes vary by municipality: consult them when specifying any lock.

**Primary types of electrified locks (1): deadbolt, latch, strike and mortise lock**

| Type | Operation | Advantage or use | Limit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Electric deadbolt* | The oldest and simplest. A solenoid (electromagnet) moves a bolt, normally mounted in the frame, in or out of a strike plate in the door | Can be fail-safe or fail-secure | Not recommended on doors that must open with the fire alarm: the bolt binds if people push before power is cut. There are tapered bolts against binding; consult codes |
| *Electric latch* | Solenoid in the frame and strike plate in the door, like the deadbolt, but with a beveled latch | It does not have to be retracted to close: the spring pushes it as it passes over the strike plate | The book does not indicate limits |
| *Electric strike* | Complement to any standard mechanical lock. A solenoid opens or closes the strike plate. It is not a lock: it works with one | Classic use in apartment buildings, offices, stores and occupancies in general | The book does not indicate limits |
| *Electric lockset* | Normal *mortise* lock, electrified to control whether the handle turns | Increasingly popular in automated access control | The door must be drilled and the cable run to the hinge side, through the hinge or around it |

- **Electric strike in practice:**
  - Controls passage in one or both directions. The handle is fixed on the controlled side or sides.
  - Entry is only remote: button or switch inside the secure area, or card reader or keypad.
  - If the handle on the secure side turns, exit is free.
  - If it is fixed on both sides, exit is made with the same devices as for entry.
  - If the lock has a cylinder on one or both sides, it also opens with a key.
- **Electric lockset in practice:**
  - *Storeroom function*: a reader controls the exterior handle, normally fixed. The interior one always turns for unobstructed exit.
  - Some models come with a switch that activates when the interior handle is turned. It sends a request-to-exit (REX) signal.
  - That signal shunts the magnetic contact or the local siren: no alarm sounds on a valid exit.
  - It requires a 4-wire transfer hinge, instead of 2, for the power and the sensor.

**Primary types of electrified locks (2): exit device and electromagnet**

**Exit device (panic bar or crash bar)**

- Common on egress doors of high-occupancy buildings.

| Mounting | Features |
| --- | --- |
| *Rim-mounted* | Requires little modification of the door |
| *Mortise-mounted* | The mechanism is mortised into the edge of the door, like a mortise lock |
| *Vertical rod* | For double doors without a fixed frame or *mullion* to receive the latch. The rods enter recesses or strike plates in the header and floor. They are exposed or inside a hollow door |

- Electrified, they allow controlled re-entry from a distance with a button or reader/keypad.
- ***Delayed egress locking system*:** compromise between life safety and asset protection.
  - For doors used only for fire evacuation.
  - When the bar is pushed, a delay of 15 or 30 seconds begins; afterwards the door opens.
  - **Requirements:** special signage that warns of the delay; connection to the fire alarm system; no delay in a fire or other defined life safety emergency.
  - It does not usually give time to intercept a thief. But a local alarm sounds and it is reported to a monitoring center.
  - A camera can identify the perpetrator and what he is carrying, and record the incident.

**Electromagnetic lock (or *maglock*)**

- Electromagnet + metal armature or strike plate. When energized, it attracts the armature and keeps the door closed.
- **Mounting:** normally on the header. Also on the side: it reduces the clear width, but the door is much more secure.
- **Where it is useful:** doors of architectural value and where mechanical locking must be coordinated with life safety codes. It is governed by the authority having jurisdiction (AHJ).
- **Rating:** by the force to separate the armature from the electromagnet, from 500 to 2,000 lb (≈ 227–907 kg, outside the book).
- One per door is almost always enough; in high security several are installed. Consider the composition and materials of the door.
- **Advantages:** no moving parts; less maintenance than mechanical or electromechanical ones. It works if the surfaces are clean and aligned and power is assured.
- **Sensors:** the best models monitor the magnet–armature bond and the door position.
  - They prevent the trick of inserting a nonmetallic sheet between magnet and strike plate to weaken the bond.
- **Inherently fail-safe:** without power, it releases the strike plate.
- **Backup power:** the book recommends it in high security, provided they are not on designated egress doors or on accesses to emergency exits.
- **Combination with electric bolt or strike:** area secure against unauthorized entry, with free exit in a defined emergency.
- **Functions added by some manufacturers:**
  - passive infrared (PIR) sensor that detects whoever approaches from the secure side and opens;
  - miniature camera;
  - piezoelectric "embarrassment alarm" sounder if the door is held or left open beyond the programmed time;
  - finishes in metals and designer covers.
- **In delayed egress:** a switch in the magnet detects the push and starts the countdown.
- **Legal requirements:**
  - strictly comply with occupancy codes;
  - AHJ approval before activating them;
  - fail-safe wiring and connection to the fire alarm to release.
  - According to the book, in most jurisdictions it is a crime to put a backup battery on a maglock.
- **Exam note:** contradiction in the book. First it recommends backup power in high security (outside egress doors). Then it says that a backup battery on maglocks is a crime almost everywhere. Safe answer: maglock on egress = fail-safe, no battery and tied to the fire alarm.

##### Designing Secure Locking Systems

- Despite cards and biometrics, the mechanical key lock remains the most widely used system for restricting access in businesses and institutions.
- **Why:** low cost, simple operation and reliability.
- **Those advantages are lost without professional planning and management:**
  - Losing a master key can force the replacement of all the cylinders: thousands of dollars.
  - Without control of the issue and return of keys, losses from theft or destruction can reach millions of dollars.

| What a locking system controls | Example from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Door or portal giving access to an area | Building perimeter door |
| A room | Confidential records file |
| A smaller container | File cabinet or safe |
| The use of equipment | Cash register |
| Individual assets | Cable lock for portable computer equipment |

- **Evaluate the whole area:** the strength of all the barriers that give access. Door and frame, walls, windows, ceiling and floor.
  - Expensive lock on a hollow wooden door: it deters the amateur, but for the professional it only leaves evidence of forcing.
  - Strong metal door: does not stop a determined intruder if the frame is weak, the wall is standard sheet rock or he can get through the drop ceiling.
- **Professional's initial questions:**
  1. What assets must be protected (people, structure, equipment, negotiable instruments, operations, information) and how much are they worth to the organization?
  2. What threats do they face and how likely are they?
  3. Who needs access to them and how often?
  4. How much flexibility must the system have?
  5. What impact will the controls have on daily operations?
  6. How much weight do the organization's culture or image carry?
  7. Are there staff and skills to plan, design, implement, operate and maintain the system? Or will part or all of it have to be outsourced?
  8. What budget is there to implement? And, often more important, how much to operate and maintain?
  9. Is there a need to audit who went where and when for regulatory compliance?

##### The Design Plan

- A locking system is a coordinated set of elements that support and complement each other.
- **The plan considers:**
  - different, simultaneous levels of control for enclosed spaces;
  - that those levels will change over time;
  - that some (or many) users will need common access in some spaces and exclusive access in others;
  - that keys, cards or *tokens* will be lost, compromised, damaged or stolen;
  - effective means of adapting to planned and unforeseen changes.
- **A locking system is designed, not just installed.** Adding or removing locks "as needed" is not the same as a concept that withstands changes in size, occupancy or use.
- **Without planning:**
  - security degrades to mere privacy;
  - unauthorized persons get into secured spaces;
  - control of the devices issued is lost;
  - the convenience of *status-level management* is sacrificed.
- If it is not clear who accesses what, the program **is not secure**. If there are no multiple levels of access, **it is not convenient**. In both cases it fails and is not economical: money is misapplied.
- **Convenience–security tension:** more convenience usually means less security, and vice versa. It is balanced if the needs are well defined from the outset.
- Choose the mechanism suited to each application: conventional mechanical or electronic, with codes or physiological traits.

##### Lock System Planning Considerations

Study systematically before deciding on hardware or scheme:

1. Total number of locks.
2. Major categories or sectors of the system.
3. Security objectives.
4. Size and turnover of the population.
5. Related or supporting security subsystems.
6. Intelligence or information needs.
7. Criticality of asset exposure.
8. Drawings or floor plans of the facility.
9. Door numbering scheme.
10. Door type (metal, wood, glass).
11. Locked perimeter assets (utility switches, storerooms, gates, building hatches).

##### Locking Policy

- The program must be based on a written policy, especially in large organizations or those with many sites.
- **The policy must:**
  1. Require a systematic approach to the use of locks for security purposes.
  2. Assign someone specific to develop the program: the security department if there is one; if not, any responsible manager.
  3. Hold accountable for compliance everyone who uses or has access to keys, locks, access devices or combinations.
- That responsibility is enforced through the chain of command. It is evaluated in the salary review, along with overall performance.

##### Rapid Entry Systems

- They allow emergency services to enter when no one is there to open.
- ***Rapid entry key vault*:** fixed, weatherproof box holding the facility's essential keys.
- The building alarm can monitor it to detect unauthorized opening or tampering.
- The key to the box is handed to the emergency services when it is installed.
- It usually goes at the main entrance. It is usually required by the AHJ when there are life safety requirements.

##### Credential-Operated Locks

- A card or other unique device is presented to a reader at the controlled point.
- The system checks the holder's identity and the time and place requested. It compares them with what is stored and opens or denies access.

##### Combination Locks

- Mechanical or electrical. An integrated alphanumeric keypad receives a predetermined sequence of numbers or letters.
- Sometimes combined with:
  - a key that only works after the correct sequence;
  - a card reader;
  - a biometric trait.

##### Biometric Locks

- They verify a specific physical trait: fingerprint, hand geometry, face, iris or retina.
- Once the trait is verified, they open only if the point's parameters are met: authorized place and time.
- ***Personnel identity verification*:** corroborates the claimed identity with one or more unique biometric traits.
- There is commercial equipment for hand or finger geometry, handwriting, eye pattern, fingerprints, voice, face and other physical traits.
- **Every system of this type considers three factors:**
  1. how unique the trait is (*uniqueness*);
  2. how much the trait varies (*variability*);
  3. how hard it is to implement the system that processes it.

| Mode | Who initiates | How it works |
| --- | --- | --- |
| *Verification* | The person claims an identity | Presents the trait and the equipment confirms that it matches |
| *Recognition* | No one claims an identity | The equipment tries to identify the person; if it matches the database, the person enters |

**Error rates (performance indicator)**

| Error | Other name | What it is | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Type I error* | *False reject* | Rejecting a valid user | Authorized people are denied passage; the security manager receives complaints, especially if it affects executives |
| *Type II error* | *False accept* | Accepting an unauthorized person | Security breach; the adversary never reports it |

- **Equal error rate (EER):** crossover point where Type I and Type II errors are equal. It is obtained by combining both curves on a graph.
  - It is not necessarily the point at which the equipment should operate.
  - It does not coincide with the point where both errors are lowest.
  - It is useful for comparing biometric devices with each other.
- **Exam note:** the book says the curves are shown "in a fault tree". It is a graph of error curves, not a fault tree (outside the book).
- **Both errors cannot be minimized at the same time.** The balance must be decided according to the security objectives.
  - Setting for few false rejects: all authorized people get in, but security is compromised.
  - Setting for few false accepts: high security, but authorized people are sometimes denied passage.
- **Exam note:** the book writes that "a *low* false accept rate compromises security". It is the other way around: a *high* false accept rate compromises it.
- **Environment:** some biometric technologies do not adapt well to outdoors or harsh environments.

**Biometric technologies**

| Technology | Mode | Key figures | Security level according to the book |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Iris (*eye pattern*) | Recognition (no PIN) | Reads at 10–12 in (25–30 cm). Transaction of 4–5 s (with practice) to 15 s (novices). \~2% cannot be enrolled | No false acceptances in the lab |
| Hand geometry (*hand geometry*) | Verification (with credential or PIN) | Type I and II errors < 1% at Sandia | Not rated; gives only the error figure |
| Two-finger geometry (*two-finger geometry*) | Same as hand geometry | A single guide pin | The book does not state it |
| Handwriting (*handwriting*) | Signature verification | No figures | Low |
| Fingerprint (*fingerprint*) | Verification | Used for more than 100 years | Among the most reliable |
| Voice (*voice*) | Verification by phrase | No figures | Low today |
| Face (*face*) | Verification; potential to identify people in crowds | No figures | Inferior to mature technologies |

**Iris (*eye pattern*)**

- The iris is the colored part of the eye; it regulates the light that enters. A video camera captures its structure, which is unique.
- It operates in recognition mode: there is no need to type a PIN.
- **Advantages:** no contact between the face and the reader; the eye is illuminated from outside with visible light, with no LED inside the eye.
- **Laboratory test of a prototype (Bouchier, Ahrens & Wells 1996):**
  - glare from eyeglasses caused some false rejections (Type I);
  - there were no false acceptances (Type II);
  - later equipment added glare detection and compensation.
- **Cannot be enrolled (\~2%):** blindness or other damage to the iris, very dilated eyes, or very dark irises. They need another secure access method.

**Hand or finger geometry**

- It characterizes the shape of the hand in three dimensions: width and length of the fingers and thickness of the hand.
- **Process:**
  1. Presenting an encoded credential or typing a PIN starts the reading.
  2. The hand rests on a reflective plate (*platen*) with guide pins to align the fingers.
  3. A camera captures the hand, with a side view for thickness. With infrared light and a reflective plate, the hand appears as a silhouette.
  4. The system measures lengths and widths and creates a feature vector (*feature vector*).
  5. It compares it with the enrollment template (*template*). If they match within tolerance, the verification is positive.
- The guide pins are designed for the right hand. The left hand is enrolled and read with the palm facing up.
- **Tests at Sandia National Laboratories:** Type I and Type II errors below 1% (Holmes, Wright & Maxwell 1991).
- Ruehle and Ahrens (1997) reported on its use in an operational environment.
- **Two-finger variant:** measures the length and width of the index–middle pair. A single guide pin between the two: it works equally with the left or right hand.

**Handwriting**

- Banks have used signature verification for years, although signatures are easily forged.
- Automatic systems measure the dynamics of writing: displacement, speed, and acceleration.
- Statistics indicate that each person's signature is unique and fairly consistent.
- The sensors (*transducers*) are in the pen or in the tablet.
- Low security. Better where transactions are already signed. No significant advances for access control or credentials.

**Fingerprints**

- A means of identifying people for more than 100 years; it remains among the most reliable.
- Automatic systems use image processing and pattern recognition.
- Most use minutia points (*minutia points*): endings and bifurcations of the ridges. Some compare the whole image.
- All require correct finger placement and precise analysis and comparison.

| Capture method | How it works | Advantage | Drawback |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Optical | Prism + solid-state camera | It is the most widely used method | Dry or worn fingerprints are difficult. The plate is coated to improve the optical coupling with the finger |
| Ultrasound | Scans beneath the superficial skin, where the fingerprint is not damaged | Less sensitive to dry or worn fingerprints | Slower: the transducer performs a raster scan (*raster scan*) |
| Direct solid-state sensor | Capacitive, electric-field, or thermal | Cheap silicon chips: expected to appear on desktops for logging in | Hardening delicate chips delayed their adoption |

- Silicon sensors are gaining popularity. New materials and manufacturing methods overcome electrostatic discharge, grease, and sweat on the finger.

**Voice**

- A useful trait for verifying identity and suitable for automatic processing.
- **Measurements to distinguish the speaker:** waveform envelope; voice pitch period; relative amplitude spectrum; resonance frequencies of the vocal tract.
- The system asks the person to say a predetermined word, or to repeat words or numbers that it chooses.
- **Low security today, but attractive:**
  - easy to deploy and well accepted by the public;
  - it is enough to install it at one end of a telephone system, perhaps centralized: fewer units;
  - almost everyone knows how to use a telephone: little training and little distrust.
- **Procedural problem:** the voice changes with illness or stress. An alternative procedure or access must be provided.
- Little evidence of new developments.

**Face**

- It uses distinctive features of the face. Almost all capture the image with a video camera; one system uses thermal imaging with an infrared camera.
- It extracts features and compares them with those stored. If they match within tolerance, the verification is positive.
- Studied for years, but commercial only recently. **Two difficult problems:**
  1. Variation in how the face is presented: tilt and turn of the head; with or without eyeglasses; changes in facial hair; changes in expression.
  2. Variation in lighting: day versus night; one place versus another.
- Its performance is still not close to that of mature biometric technologies.
- **Attractions:** no contact; potential to identify known or wanted criminals in a crowd (*face-in-the-crowd*). Useful in casinos, shopping malls, and crowded places.
- Test it thoroughly in the real environment before implementing it.
- It is improving thanks to megapixel cameras and greater computing speed.

**Other techniques**

- **Keystroke patterns (*keystroke technology*):** developed and marketed for logging in to computers.
- **Studied but not yet in use:** ear shape, gait (*gait*), fingernail bed (*fingernail bed*), and body odor.

**People who cannot use biometrics**

- Every technology leaves out some people at enrollment: procedures must be in place for them.
  - Examples: very dry or very damaged skin; signatures and voice patterns that are not repeatable.
- Temporary injuries of authorized users: broken fingers or hands; eye injuries or surgery; other medical conditions.

##### HSPD-12 Implementation

- **HSPD-12 (*Homeland Security Presidential Directive 12*):** U.S. presidential directive signed by George W. Bush in August 2004.
- It requires the entire federal government and its contractor agencies to use a single high-security credential.
- **Technical basis:** the FIPS 201 standard (*Federal Information Processing Standard 201*). It uses a contact and contactless smart card.
- According to the book, its implementation is still under way.
- **Who is affected:** mainly federal and contractor facilities. It may also affect private industry.
  - Example: someone who regularly enters those facilities with a vehicle may need a federal ID.
- **Oversight:** GSA (*General Services Administration*) and NIST (*National Institute of Standards and Technology*). They oversee the development and testing of credentials, readers, access control systems, and issuance procedures.
- If a company must comply, the details are agreed case by case with the government.
- Be careful when searching the internet: many vendor sites come up. Some claim to comply with HSPD-12 without mentioning certification.
- (outside the book) The resulting credential is known as the PIV card (*Personal Identity Verification*).

##### UL Standards

- **UL (*Underwriters Laboratories*):** prepares safety standards, mainly as a guide for manufacturers. It then certifies whether the equipment it receives meets them.
- The standards arise from broad feedback: the public, insurers, government, academia, inspection authorities, consumer organizations, and end users.
- **UL 294, 7th edition (2018):** standard for access control system units.
  - It evaluates the construction, performance, and operation of the system.
  - The listing evaluates its safety and that it works reliably without creating hazardous conditions.
- **Exam note:** the book presents UL as "perhaps the best known of these" without naming any other body beforehand. It is a leftover from an earlier edition.

##### Code Considerations and Governance

| Year | Milestone |
| --- | --- |
| 2000 | The IBC (*International Building Code*), from the ICC (*International Code Council*), replaces the UBC |
| 1997 | Last edition of the UBC; until then it was updated approximately every 3 years |
| 1990 | ADA (*Americans with Disabilities Act*) |
| 1927 | The ICBO publishes the UBC (*Uniform Building Code*): the same safe construction requirements from one city to another |

- **The ICC was born from the merger of three bodies:**

| Body | Code it published |
| --- | --- |
| ICBO (*International Council of Building Officials*, according to the book) | Uniform Building Code |
| BOCA (*Building Officials and Code Administrators International*) | BOCA National Building Code |
| SBCCI (*Southern Building Code Congress International*) | Standard Building Code |

- **Exam note (outside the book):** the official name of the ICBO is *International Conference of Building Officials*. The book says "Council."
- The IBC seeks uniform safe construction standards and the elimination of differences among the three earlier codes. It is used mainly in the U.S.
- **NFPA 101 Life Safety Code** (*National Fire Protection Association*): used in the U.S. where the IBC was not adopted as the building code.
- **Canada:** the NBC (*National Building Code*) and the NFC (*National Fire Code*) play a similar role. Note: here NFC is not *Near Field Communication*.
- **ADA of 1990:** federal anti-discrimination law, based on the *Civil Rights Act* of 1964.
  - It is not a building or fire code, but it affects access control at entrance and exit doors.
  - **Two ways it affects access control and life safety:**
    1. If it requires the employer to accommodate an employee with a disability: all communication systems must be equally effective for that person, with auxiliary aids where needed.
    2. If the building falls under the public accommodation (*public accommodation*) requirements, the system must meet them.
- **Greatest concern when installing access control:** being able to leave the building, especially in emergencies such as a fire.
- **Means of egress (*means of egress*), three parts:**
  1. Path of travel to an exit (*path of travel to an exit*).
  2. The exit itself (*exit*).
  3. Exit discharge (*exit discharge*): the path to a safe area outside.
- **The code also regulates:**
  - the number of exits according to the intended use, how many people can be present at one time, and where they are;
  - special places where evacuation is difficult: hospitals, nursing homes, and prisons;
  - specific hazards, such as industries with flammable or toxic chemicals.
- **Before installing:** review the design for code compliance. Some jurisdictions require prior design review and later testing before the system is activated.

### 11.2 Contraband Detection

#### 11.2.1 Metal Detectors

**Magnetometer**

- **Passive** device: it monitors the Earth's magnetic field and detects the changes caused by **ferromagnetic** materials (those attracted by a magnet).
- It does not detect copper, aluminum, or zinc.
- Many weapons are made of steel, but many others are made of composite materials: the magnetometer may not see them.
