- “Magnetometer” is used as a synonym for any metal detector, but the traditional one differs greatly from modern active detectors.
- **It should not be used to detect contraband.**

**Typical use and operators**

- Most common use: screening people who enter to prevent firearms and other weapons from getting in.
- For this reason the operators are usually **armed**: they will have to disarm anyone who tries to enter with a weapon.
- Normally **two operators**: one searches manually; the other observes and protects the searcher from an attack by the person being screened.

**Active metal detectors**

- They generate a variable magnetic field for a short period.
- They detect one of two things: the change in the field when metal enters it, or the eddy currents that a pulsed field induces in the object.
- The magnitude of the response depends on:
  - Conductivity of the metal.
  - Magnetic properties of the metal (relative permeability).
  - Shape and size of the object.
  - Orientation of the object within the magnetic field.

**The two active methods**

| Aspect | Continuous wave | Pulsed field |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Availability | No longer sold commercially | The one used today |
| Signal | Steady-state, sinusoidal field, from 100 Hz to 25 kHz | Fixed-frequency pulses, 400–500 pulses per second |
| Coils | One transmitter on one side of the arch; balanced receivers on the opposite side; the person passes between them | Several low-inductance transmitters; it does not need balanced receivers |
| Principle | Balanced differential amplifier: with no metal there is no difference and no output; metal unbalances the receivers and generates a signal | Field bursts of up to 50 microseconds, 200–400 times per second; receivers off during the pulse and on a few tens of milliseconds later; the metal retains an eddy current that is read |
| Alarm | Signal amplified and phase-verified; alarm if it exceeds the threshold | Same: amplification, phase detection, and threshold |
| Extra | Phase allows optimizing for ferromagnetic (high permeability) or nonferromagnetic (low) metals | Same; in addition, the multiple coils reduce the effect of object orientation |

- In the pulsed field, the eddy current decays quickly according to the metal's resistivity, but lasts long enough to be read.
- Modern digital technology analyzes the signal better: it distinguishes types of metal and separates real threats from harmless objects.

> **Exam note:** the book gives two different figures for the pulsed field: 400–500 pulses per second and, a few lines later, 200–400 times per second.

**Very small quantities of metal (for example, gold)**

- Very hard to detect with a portal metal detector.
- With continuous wave, a higher-than-normal frequency improves detection.
- In all cases very high sensitivity is needed, which triggers nuisance alarms.
- An area may be required to remove steel-toed footwear and other metal objects.
- Handheld detectors detect very small quantities: they are better for tiny objects.

**Handheld detector: disadvantages**

- It requires active participation by the guard.
- Time the search takes.
- It is intrusive because of its closeness to the body; even more so if the searcher and the person searched are of different sexes.
- Many sites, especially airports, provide same-sex operators.

**Detector placement**

- The field is not limited to the space between coils: it detects moving metal outside the arch.
- Nuisance alarms: moving metal objects in front or to the side, such as doors, forklifts, and carts.
- False alarms: electromagnetic transients, such as radio transmitters, power line fluctuations, and flickering fluorescent lights.
- They tolerate some stationary metal: reinforcing bar in floors and walls and other metal construction materials.
- Installing it right next to a steel beam is not recommended.
- Too much metal severely distorts the field. Two possible effects:
  - The detector does not operate and gives an error alarm.
  - It keeps operating with zones of extremely low or high sensitivity: undetected threats or many nuisance alarms.
- Nearby objects such as security equipment, metal trash cans, or chairs do not disable it, but they distort the field.
- Solution: a **no-move rule** for those objects near the detector after the installation tests.

#### 11.2.2 Package Search

- Two paths: **manual** search or **active interrogation**.
- Active interrogation uses a family of X-ray techniques:
  1. Single-energy transmission X-ray.
  2. Multiple-energy X-ray.
  3. Computed tomography (CT).
  4. Backscatter X-ray.
- In general they are **not safe for people**. Exception: low-dose backscatter for people (see 11.2.3).
- Single energy: useful for finding **metal objects** (weapons, tools, metal components of bombs).
- The other techniques are useful for seeing **low atomic number (low-Z)** materials.
- Atomic number (Z): number of protons in the nucleus of the atom.
- Low-Z contraband: explosives, drugs, and some foods.
- Low-Z atoms: hydrogen, oxygen, carbon, and all elements up to aluminum.

> **Exam note:** the book says that aluminum has Z = 26. This is an erratum: aluminum is Z = 13 and Z = 26 is iron (outside the book).

**Conventional single-energy X-rays**

- They produce an image that an operator inspects.
- They are effective if the operator is well trained and attentive, and if the image is not cluttered.
- Metals attenuate X-rays heavily; low-density and low-Z materials attenuate them little.
- They do not penetrate the heavy materials of some shipping containers or vehicles.
- For large, dense containers and vehicles: high-energy or multiple-energy X-rays.
- Almost all low-Z development is aimed at explosives, but most of those technologies can be adjusted to look for drugs.

#### 11.2.3 Explosives Detection

| Family | What it looks for | Characteristics |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Bulk** | Macroscopic, detonable quantities of explosive | Targets specific threat quantities; usually uses ionizing radiation, not suitable for people |
| **Trace** | Particle and vapor residues left by the handling of explosives | A finding leads to secondary screening |

- The best protection against high-explosive bombs is to **prevent them from being placed**.
- References cited by the book: description of technologies (Yinon, 1999); survey of commercial equipment (Theisen et al., 2004); survey of standoff technologies (National Academy of Sciences, 2004).

##### Bulk Explosives Detection

- It measures characteristics of bulk materials:
  - X-ray absorption coefficient.
  - X-ray backscatter coefficient.
  - Dielectric constant.
  - Interaction with gamma rays or with neutrons.
  - Microwave or infrared emissions.
- From these are calculated: mass; density; nitrogen, carbon, and oxygen content; and effective atomic number (effective Z).
- None is exclusive to explosives, but they indicate a high probability that one is present.
- Few everyday objects share those characteristics.
- Automated detection is effective if the equipment is:
  - **Sensitive** enough: the minimum detectable quantity is less than the threat mass.
  - **Specific** enough: low rate of nuisance alarms.
- Advantages of automating: lower labor cost and less dependence on a human interpreting the image.
- X-rays range from portable package equipment to systems that scan a truck with its cargo.
- Dual purpose: the same scan looks for weapons or contraband and, at the same time, explosives.
- Single energy is not enough for explosives: more information must be extracted.

| Technology | How it works | Data, advantages, and disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dual-energy X-ray | Measures the mass absorption coefficient and approximates the effective Z | Colors the low-Z areas (such as explosives) to draw the operator's attention |
| Backscatter X-ray | Low-Z materials send a lot of energy back toward the source | Low-Z areas look bright; at low dose it is suitable for people |
| Computed tomography (CT) | Source and detectors mounted on a rotating gantry; the computer reconstructs the contents in 3D | Automated; detects small threat masses; **the only X-ray technique that calculates mass, density, and mass absorption coefficient**. Drawbacks: much more expensive to buy and maintain (heavy gantry); nuisance alarms of up to 20% against traces, mainly from foods and some polymers |
| High-energy X-rays (vehicles and containers) | Highly penetrating illumination | Gives a reasonable image through the engine or the full trailer of a truck; large, fixed equipment, sometimes with its own building; it does not matter how the high energy is produced |
| Gamma rays with radioactive source | Radioactive source instead of an X-ray tube | Same use; backscatter can be combined with high energy to detect low-Z |
| Thermal neutron activation (TNA) | A slow neutron is absorbed by nitrogen-14, which becomes excited nitrogen-15 and emits a gamma ray of specific wavelength | Measures **nitrogen**; many explosives are rich in nitrogen; automatic detection; very penetrating neutrons and gamma rays, suitable for large, dense objects |
| Pulsed fast neutron absorption (PFNA) | “Fast” = high energy, several MeV | Measures **carbon and oxygen**; together with TNA, also nitrogen; carbon + nitrogen + oxygen identify better and reject nuisance materials rich in nitrogen |
| Quadrupole resonance (QR) | Low-energy radio pulses | Detects materials rich in nitrogen; very sensitive for some explosives; thin metal shielding blocks it, but it detects the shielding and gives notice; compact, about USD 100,000, no ionizing radiation; handheld versions under development for screening people |
| Raman analysis | Spectroscopy: laser interrogation and analysis of the spectrum of the scattered light | Portable, lightweight equipment for hazardous materials, including explosives; the laser passes through some containers (glass) or goes directly to the surface; requires small but visible quantities; useful for bottles or plastic bags, **not** for packages |

**TNA and PFNA: disadvantages**

- Cost: for vehicles, from USD 500,000 (TNA is cheaper).
- Size.
- Throughput.
- Use of radioactive material in the source or in the neutron generator tube.
- Small TNA systems exist for packages: less than 100 lb (45 kg).

**Food irradiation**

- International law prohibits irradiating food with more than 10 MeV, because of the risk of making it radioactive.
- There is a risk if a system of more than 10 MeV screens food shipments.

> **Exam note:** the book writes “milli-electron volts (MeV).” MeV means mega-electron volts, that is, millions of electron volts (outside the book).

**Low-dose backscatter for people**

- It gives an image of the body under the clothing to find hidden objects.
- The person is scanned **twice**: front and back, to ensure that no explosives are hidden.
- Dose per person: about **10 microrem**.
- It meets the limit of the NRC (Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the U.S. nuclear regulatory body): maximum **100 millirem/year** (10 CFR Part 20, Section 20.1301(a)(1), 1991).
- Exposure must always be as low as reasonably achievable (ALARA, as low as reasonably achievable) (10 CFR Part 20, Section 20.1301(d)(3), 1991).

**Standoff detection**

- In high demand: it makes it possible to detect devices from a safe distance.
- A lot of research and few commercial products; it is a difficult challenge.
- Options:
  - Infrared cameras: images of people to see hidden objects that could be explosives.
  - Passive and active millimeter-wave, of about 100 GHz (sometimes called terahertz, THz): like infrared, but in another part of the spectrum.
  - Laser methods that look for characteristic fluorescence or atomic emission (under development).
- Verify what the vendor promises against the defined threat and in the real environment.

**Combining technologies**

- All bulk technologies have strengths and weaknesses.
- A good system combines two or more: with enough information it is possible to truly determine whether an explosive is present.

##### Trace Explosives Detection

- It became common at checkpoints in the last decade.
- It looks for vapors and microscopic particles associated with explosives and their handling.
- Technologies:
  1. Ion mobility spectrometry (IMS).
  2. Colorimetric.
  3. Chemiluminescence.
  4. Mass spectrometry.
  5. Fluorescence.
  6. Canine olfaction.
- Key metrics:
  - **Limit of detection:** the smallest detectable quantity.
  - **Selectivity:** ability to distinguish one material from another.
- Many detect **less than a nanogram**. Even so, verify what the vendor promises before buying.
- A real trace detection leads to a search for the materials in the threat definition.

**Sampling**: the residue must be collected and taken to the detector.

| Type | How it is done | Best for | Invasiveness |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Swipe sampling | A cloth swab is rubbed over the person, package, or vehicle; it is heated and the vapor passes to the detector | Particles on hard surfaces; it is the most efficient method and collects the most mass | More invasive |
| Vapor sampling | The air next to the object is drawn in, often by agitating it | Inside of containers and soft surfaces | Less invasive: it does not touch the object |

**The challenge of detecting vapor**

- Several common high explosives have very low vapor concentrations: parts per billion (ppb) or per trillion (ppt).
- They are even lower if the explosive is in a gel or oily solvent (example: RDX in the plastic explosive C-4).
- The molecules stick (adsorb) to almost everything at room temperature and decompose with moderate heat or a lot of energy.
- Result: transporting and collecting the vapor involves a large loss of sample.

**Trace technologies**

| Technology | Principle | Figures | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IMS (ion mobility spectrometer) | Negative ionization with a radioactive nickel-63 (Ni-63) source and chloride dopant; a shutter lets ions into the drift cell; they advance through an electric gradient against an inert gas flow; light ones arrive first; they strike a Faraday plate that records voltage against drift time | Shutter every \~20 ms; \~5 cm cell with 200 V/cm; drift times of 5 to 15 ms; nanogram sensitivity to dynamite, military TNT, and plastics; USD 40,000 bench-top and USD 25,000 handheld | Sensitivity, easy to operate, robust, little maintenance; sufficient specificity; few nuisance alarms in the field, except for fragrances of lotions and perfumes | Handhelds are cheaper, but require more maintenance and alternating current (AC) to operate for more than a few hours |
| Colorimetric | Kit with spray, reactive paper, or an ampoule that is consumed; a chemical reaction changes the color; often several solutions in sequence to find out which explosive it is | — | Low cost and portability | Many nuisance alarms, consumable chemicals must be disposed of, odor |
| Chemiluminescence | Photochemical detection: a fast gas chromatograph separates the sample; heat breaks nitrogen compounds down into nitric oxide (NO); NO + ozone gives excited nitrogen dioxide (NO2), which emits a photon read by a phototube | Identifies several explosives in a sample in less than 1 minute; **picogram** sensitivity, even RDX and PETN (very low vapor pressure) | Excellent sensitivity; without the chromatography one would only know that nitrogen is present, with it the nitrated explosive is identified | The most expensive of the commercial ones, the longest analysis, and more maintenance |
| GC/ECD (gas chromatograph / electron capture detector) | The ECD exploits the high electron affinity of nitro compounds; alone it does not identify the explosive; coupled to the GC, it does | Used more in the laboratory than at checkpoints | Low cost, good specificity, low detection limits | Long analyses (typically minutes) and frequent maintenance of the GC column |
| Mass spectrometry | Magnetic and electric fields determine the mass/charge ratio of ions | “Gold standard” of the analytical chemistry laboratory | Specificity and low detection limits; easily reprogrammed for new substances, useful against changing threats | High cost and maintenance and expert operators; newer equipment is better suited to checkpoints |
| Amplifying fluorescent polymers | Their fluorescence is quenched by a molecule such as TNT; one molecule quenches thousands of monomers and amplifies the effect; polymers in capillary tubes next to a photomultiplier tube; alarm if the change exceeds a threshold | From low picograms to **femtograms** | Small, cheap, and highly sensitive | Not all explosives respond; coatings for more explosives are being researched |
| Canine olfaction | The handler detects by observing the dog's behavior | Widely used by police and military for explosives and drugs | Mobility: searching buildings and quick relocation; cheap to acquire | Constant retraining for synthetic compounds; depends on the vigilance and skill of the handler and on the dog's health and willingness; frequent rest breaks, which may require several teams; the handler's labor is a recurring cost; for this reason it is less common at fixed checkpoints |

**Mass spectrometry variants**

- **Quadrupole mass spectrometer:** negative ionization by electrical discharge, acceleration in an electric field, and focusing on the detector with the quadrupole's magnetic field.
  - It monitors characteristic explosive masses separately or scans a continuous range.
  - It determines the parent ion and the fragments or daughter ions.
  - It alarms when the current exceeds a threshold for a mass or combination of masses.
- **Quadrupole ion trap time-of-flight:** the ions orbit in the trap; it is emptied periodically and their time to the detector is measured.
  - Time of flight depends on the square root of the ion's mass.
- **IMS versus time of flight:** they are similar, but IMS operates at atmospheric pressure and time of flight in a vacuum.

```latex
t_{\text{flight}} \propto \sqrt{m}
```

**IMS: positive and negative ions**

- Common high explosives form negative ions.
- Some emerging threats, such as TATP (triacetonetriperoxide), also form positive ions.
- IMS units with positive and negative ion analysis are already on sale.

**Trace explosives detection portals**

- Developed in the last decade; deployed at many airports.
- Short bursts of air agitate the clothing and release residues; the air around the person is filtered.
- The filter collects vapors and particles for several seconds and is then heated to release (desorb) them into an IMS or a mass spectrometer.
- Screening time: **10 to 25 seconds**.
- Advantages: automated detection, high sensitivity (nanograms), and noninvasive screening of the whole person.
- Disadvantages: size, cost (about USD 150,000), and maintenance.
- Comparison: swabbing a person is possible, but would be considered invasive and would take more than a minute.

**How to choose a commercial trace detector**

- Select it according to the needs of each facility.
- Always verify what the vendor promises with tests in the real operating environment.
- Factors to consider:
  - Sensitivity.
  - Resistance to nuisance alarms.
  - Response time.
  - Operating and maintenance costs.
  - List of explosives in the threat definition.

#### 11.2.4 Chemical and Biological Agent Detection

- It is normally done with **point sensors** that look for signs of attack at the **perimeter** of the site.

| Aspect | Chemical agent | Biological agent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lethality | Sudden attack with large, rapidly lethal concentrations | Most are not immediately lethal |
| Response time | Critical | Less critical |
| System goal | Early warning to interrupt and neutralize the adversary | Identify the agent to treat exposed personnel |
| Method | Air sampling at several points on the perimeter; some use optical methods to detect from a distance (standoff) | Filter air for several hours and then analyze the filter for several more hours |
| Limitation | May not be suitable for checkpoint screening | Because of the delay, it is hard to detect in time to avoid exposure |

**Chemical detectors**

- Military and environmental trace chemical detectors exist, developed over the last century.
- They may need modifications if they must operate continuously for long periods.
- Take great care with the nuisance alarm rate: the response to a chemical attack must be quick and complete.
- Those who must respond tolerate nuisance alarms or drills poorly.

**Biological detectors**

- Because of their limitations, biological detection is today a very active area of research.

**Outside the scope of contraband detection**

- It is also possible to monitor what crosses the perimeter by water (rain, streams, piped drinking water) and air (pollen, pollutants).
- That is considered **environmental monitoring**, not contraband detection.

### 11.3 Physical Intrusion Detection Systems

**Role of the sensor**

- Sensors are the **basic building blocks** of an intrusion detection system (IDS).
- They start the detection function: they indicate an intrusion attempt or a tamper event.
- Everything that follows the initial alarm depends on the sensor technology: logical discrimination, transmission, processing, display, and recording.
- That technology can be optical, electronic, electromechanical, or mechanical.
- If the sensor is not appropriate for the environment or the threat, or is not properly installed, operated, maintained, and tested, the whole system is severely limited.
- In that case the system becomes more of a burden than a benefit.
- The Electronic Security Association (formerly the National Burglar and Fire Alarm Association) seeks to reduce nuisance alarms to **one per year per system**.
- In reality, it is very unlikely that an effective sensor has such a low rate: choosing the device well is paramount.
- Key: match the sensor to the threat and the environment, and integrate it into the physical protection system (PPS).

**Components of an IDS (they work in combination)**

- Exterior and interior intrusion sensors.
- Video alarm assessment.
- Access control.
- Alarm communication systems.

**Definition and scope**

- **Intrusion detection:** the process of detecting a person or vehicle attempting to enter an area without authorization.
- When a sensor is integrated, it must satisfy three things: probability of detection, adequate maintenance, and false alarm frequency.
- The detection boundary is thought of as a **sphere** around the protected asset.
- This is how every intrusion is detected: by surface, by air, underwater, or underground.
- Exterior technology focuses on detecting on the ground or slightly above it; this section mainly addresses ground-level intrusion.

#### 11.3.1 Performance Characteristics

Three main characteristics, valid for exterior and interior sensors:

1. Probability of detection (PD).
2. Nuisance alarm rate (NAR).
3. Vulnerability to defeat.

##### Probability of Detection

- A perfect PD would be 1; in reality it is always less than 1.
- After thousands of tests, a sensor's PD only approaches 1.
- For a specific sensor and scenario, effectiveness is described with two values: **PD and confidence level (CL)**.
- Manufacturers usually give the PD without the CL. In that case they probably imply a CL of at least 90%.

> **Exam note:** the book writes “a sensor's partial discharge (PD).” This is an erratum: here PD means probability of detection.

**Factors the PD depends on**

- Target to be detected (for example, an intruder who walks, runs, or crawls, or who digs a tunnel), plus the direction and speed with which they cross the sensor pattern.
- Design of the sensor hardware.
- Installation conditions.
- Sensitivity setting.
- Weather conditions.
- Condition of the equipment.

**PD is conditional**

- Those conditions vary: a fixed PD cannot be assigned to a component or piece of equipment.
- Any assigned PD is **conditional**: it is based on assumptions about the conditions.
- A sensor can have one PD against a low threat (a vandal) and another, lower one against a sophisticated threat.
- The **design basis threat (DBT)** guides the design of the system:
  - DBT of three criminals with a lot of knowledge and skill: use a higher-PD sensor.
  - Less sophisticated threats: a lower PD is tolerated.
- Sensor selection must fit the application and the environment.

**Detection criteria**

The designer must specify them. They must state:

- What will be detected.
- What actions are expected.
- Other conditions, such as weight or speed of movement.
- What PD is required.

| Example | Measurable conditions |
| --- | --- |
| Criterion for a perimeter system | Detect a person of **35 kg or more** who crosses the zone walking, crawling, jumping, running, or rolling, at **0.15–5 m/s**, or who climbs the fence at any point in the zone; PD of **90% with 95% confidence** |
| UL 681 standard | Detect after walking **three steps**, at one step per second, with steps of **30 inches**, in **three of four** walk test patterns, from any point in the coverage area |

- Both are clear, measurable conditions, not vague phrases such as “it should detect most of the time.”
- If a high PD is required at all times and in any weather: use **multiple sensors**.
- Contingency plans and procedures with compensatory measures are needed if one or all of the sensors are lost.

##### Nuisance Alarm Rate (NAR)

- **Nuisance alarm:** any alarm **not caused by an intrusion**.
- **NAR:** number of nuisance alarms in a given period. Ideally it would be zero.
- Every sensor interacts with its environment and does not always distinguish an intrusion from other events.
- That is why **alarm assessment** is needed: it is not effective to send the security force to every alarm.
- Assessment determines the cause and decides whether a response is needed. **Without assessment, detection is incomplete.**

| Type of alarm | Origin | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nuisance from natural cause | Natural environment | Vegetation (trees, brush); fauna (animals, birds); weather (wind, rain, snow, fog, lightning) |
| Nuisance from industrial cause | Industrial environment | Ground vibration; trash blown by the wind; electromagnetic interference |
| **False alarm** | The equipment itself | Poor design, inadequate maintenance, or failure of a component |

- A false alarm is a **type** of nuisance alarm.
- Each type of sensor, interior or exterior, is more or less vulnerable to each source.

**Specifying the false alarm rate (FAR)**

- Example: the FAR of the entire perimeter must not average more than **one false alarm per week per zone**, while maintaining a **PD of 0.9**.
- It is much more useful than saying “higher FAR and NAR are tolerated if they do not degrade the system,” which is hard to measure.
- With concrete values it is easier to decide when to report a sensor for maintenance.

##### Vulnerability to Defeat

- The ideal sensor could not be defeated, but **all existing sensors can be defeated**.
- Each type and model has different vulnerabilities.
- Goal of the PPS designer: that the system be very hard to defeat.

| Way to defeat it | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Bypass | Every detection zone is finite: its volume or pattern is gone around; some accessible sensors can be masked |
| Adversary path exploitation | The direction and speed of movement may not activate the sensor; example: advancing head-on toward a passive infrared (PIR) sensor, which detects cross-walk better |
| Spoof | Any technique for passing through the normal detection zone without generating an alarm |

##### Alarm Initiation Conditions

| Condition | Type of sensor |
| --- | --- |
| Possible intrusion event | Intrusion sensors |
| Change in a monitored security or process condition, for example a rise in temperature or the presence of smoke | State sensors |
| Loss of electrical power | Fault event sensors |
| Opening, short-circuiting, or grounding of the circuit; tampering with the sensors; impedance changes in a cabinet or in distributed control panels (transponders) | Tamper sensors |
| Failure of the sensor itself | Another fault event that must be detected |

##### Operating Conditions

| Location | Temperature range |
| --- | --- |
| Interior | 32 to 120 °F (0 to 49 °C) |
| Exterior or unheated structures | −30 to 150 °F (−34 to 66 °C) |
| All equipment | Must operate at 90 °F (32 °C) with 95% relative humidity |

#### 11.3.2 Standards

- They help judge the quality and suitability of sensors from different manufacturers.
- Many standards, including those in the U.S., are often revised because of technological advances and best practices.
- In each design and installation project: review the most recent version of the standards for that geographic area.

##### UL Standards (Underwriters Laboratories)

- The best-known body; headquartered in Northbrook, Illinois.
- It prepares safety standards mainly as a guide for manufacturers, and certifies whether the equipment submitted to it meets them.
- The standards arise from broad feedback: the public, insurers, government, academia, inspection authorities, consumer organizations, and end users.
- The standards are public, but UL asks that its **periodic directories** be used as a guide: lists of equipment submitted, tested, and certified by UL for a specific standard.
- Directory of security equipment: *Automotive, Burglary Protection and Mechanical Equipment Directory*.
- The asset protection professional must know the standard in detail to understand the real scope of a UL approval.
- UL approval is often required in system specifications and in municipal building and fire codes. List of standards: www.ul.com.
- Many UL fire and security standards deal mainly with the engineering and manufacture of alarms and their controllers.
- **Key:** they are **safety standards (safety), not protection against adversaries (security)**.
- They guide correct installation to reduce the risk of fire or other safety events.
- They **do not** address equipment vulnerabilities or its ability to detect intrusions by malicious adversaries.

**UL security system standards**

| UL number | Name |
| --- | --- |
| 365 | Police Station Connected Burglar Alarm Units and Systems |
| 606 | Linings and Screens for Use with Burglar Alarm Systems |
| 609 | Local Burglar Alarm Units and Systems |
| 611 | Central-Station Burglar Alarm Systems |
| 634 | Connectors and Switches for Use with Burglar Alarm Systems |
| 636 | Hold-Up Alarm Units and Systems |
| 639 | Intrusion Detection Units |
| 681 | Installation and Classification of Burglar and Holdup Alarm Systems |
| 827 | Central-Station Alarm Services |
| 827A | Central-Station Alarm Hosted Services |
| 1037 | Anti-Theft Alarms and Devices |
| 1076 | Proprietary Burglar Alarm Units and Systems USA |
| 1610 | Central Station Burglar Alarm Units |
| 1635 | Digital Burglar Alarm Communicator Units |
| 1641 | Installation and Classification of Residential Burglar Alarm Systems USA |
| 2050 | National Industrial Security Systems for the Protection of Classified Materials |

- All of these standards are also designated as a national standard by ANSI (American National Standards Institute).

> **Pending:** the book says the standards in bold are the most relevant, because they set how to install or operate in the U.S. In the PDF they all have the same format, so it is not possible to know which they are.

**UL-listed equipment versus UL-certificated system**

- There is an important difference between the UL standards for **equipment** and a **UL-certificated burglar alarm system**.
- The same applies to the alarm service company (ASC) of a certificated system, such as those under UL 611, 827, and 2050. It covers:
  - Response options of an agent sent to the site (runner response), with and without keys.
  - Optional categories of UL response requirements.
  - Annual maintenance duties of the ASC.
  - Defects found: minor, major, and maintenance-related.
- Insurers often require these systems for higher-risk commercial IDS.
- High-security classified systems: **UL 2050** is normally required.
- It is required by government security standards such as the NISPOM (National Industrial Security Program Operating Manual) or ICD-705 (Intelligence Community Directive) for IDS in classified spaces or SCIFs.
- SCIF: sensitive compartmented information facility, a facility for compartmented classified information (outside the book).
- UL 611 and UL 2050 require UL-listed equipment (such as UL 634 and 639) and **UL 681 for the installation** of the IDS.

##### ASTM Standards

- ASTM (American Society for Testing and Materials), headquartered in Philadelphia.
- It has **Committee F-12, Security Systems and Equipment**.
- It develops and standardizes for security systems and equipment: nomenclature, definitions, test methods (prepared in design or installation), specifications, classifications, and recommended practices.
- It promotes knowledge about property protection and life safety; it works with other committees, organizations, and individuals.
- **ASTM does not yet have standards for alarm systems or for sensors.**
- It published *Building Security* (Stroik, 1981): a reference base for evaluating the performance of building security systems, components, and equipment.
- Especially relevant section: “Design Considerations for High Security Interior Intrusion Detection Systems.”

##### Other Standards and Specifications (mainly North America)

| Body | Document | Key facts |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GSA (General Services Administration, U.S.) | Specification for alarm system components | First edition in **1969** as Interim Specification WA-0045A; revised twice; final version W-A-45OC/GEN (as it appears in the book) |
| Sandia Laboratories (Albuquerque, New Mexico) | *Intrusion Detection System Handbook* (**1976**) | Based on evaluations by government laboratories and information from commercial vendors; several revisions; it is **not** intended to be an equipment specification or standard |
| NFPA (National Fire Protection Association) | **NFPA 72**, National Fire Alarm Code | In **1993** it consolidated NFPA 71, 72A, 72B, 72C, 72D, 72E, 72F, 72G, 72H, and 74; covers municipal, central station, proprietary, and local fire alarms |
| NFPA | National Fire Alarm and Signaling Code Handbook | Explanatory text for interpreting and applying the formal language of the code |
| NFPA | **NFPA 731**, Standard for Installation of Electronic Premises Security Systems | Installation of electronic premises security systems |
| NFPA | **NFPA 70**, National Electrical Code | Two articles regulate all low- and high-voltage wiring of IDS and electronic systems such as access control |

- NFPA updates and publishes its standards regularly.
- Technicians must comply with NFPA 70 in addition to the manufacturer's documentation, especially in listed electronic systems.

**Current volumes of the Sandia handbook**

1. Intrusion Detection System Concepts.
2. Considerations for Sensor Selection and Subsystem Design.
3. Exterior Intrusion Sensors.
4. Interior Intrusion Sensors.
5. Alarm Assessment Systems.
6. Alarm Reporting Systems.
7. Intrusion Detection System Integration.

##### European Alarm Standards

Four grades of intruder alarm system, according to the expected level of attack (intruder's knowledge and tools):

| Grade | Expected intruder | Risk it is suited for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Little knowledge and limited tools | Low |
| 2 | Limited knowledge and some tools | Low to medium |
| 3 | Knowledge and a full range of tools | Medium to high |
| 4 | Sophisticated knowledge and tools | High |

- Each piece of equipment is marked with the grade it is suitable for.
- Equipment of different grades can be mixed: the official grade of the system is that of the **lowest-grade equipment**.
- There is little grade 4 equipment, so installers tend to use grade 3.
- Grade 4 direct-wire signaling does exist: it must be installed at every medium- to high-risk premises, such as jewelers.

**Who decides the grade (United Kingdom)**

- The ABI (Association of British Insurers) stopped deciding the grade as a group of insurers.
- Reason: the OFT (Office of Fair Trading) considered it anticompetitive.
- Now alarm companies decide the grade according to the risk; some recommend lower grades to win customers.
- Jewelers: insurers usually require a **grade 3 system with grade 4 signaling**.
- Whatever the grade of the system, grade 4 signaling is required.

**Transition from British to European standards**

- On **October 1, 2005**, British standards in conflict with European ones were withdrawn.
- BS 4737, 6799, and 7042 were replaced by the European series **BS EN 50131**.
- The European standards are **not retroactive**: systems installed to a British standard continue to be maintained and updated under it.

**Grading of detection and control systems**

- In addition to more event memory in the panel and more recommended detection, the key difference among grades 2, 3, and 4 is this:
  - **Grade 3:** motion sensors must detect **masking**, that is, something placed over the lens.
  - **Grade 4:** they must detect **range reduction**, that is, something that blocks part of the field of view.

**Grading of signaling systems**

Each grade has options with acceptable combinations: local siren or link to an alarm receiving center (ARC). The likely ones in the United Kingdom:

| Grade and option | Configuration |
| --- | --- |
| Grade 2 – Option X | Siren on site |
| Grade 2 – Option B | Siren + one link to the ARC |
| Grade 2 – Option C | Two links to the ARC |
| Grade 2 – Option D | One link to the ARC |
| Grade 3 – Option B | Siren + one link to the ARC |
| Grade 3 – Option C | Two links to the ARC |
| Grade 3 – Option D | One link to the ARC |
| Grade 4 – Options B, C, and D | Same as those for grade 3 |

**Notice to the ARC of signaling failure (example with option B)**

| Grade | Maximum time to report the failure |
| --- | --- |
| 2 | 25 hours |
| 3 | 5 hours |
| 4 | 3 minutes |

**Grading of maintenance**

| Grade and option | Minimum maintenance |
| --- | --- |
| Grade 2 – Option X | One site visit per year |
| Grades 2 and 3 – Options B, C, and D | Two visits per year, or one visit and one remote check |
| Grade 4 – Options B, C, and D | Two site visits per year |

**Confirmed alarms**

- Systems that generate confirmed alarm conditions are also governed by the British Standards Institute document **DD 243**.
- These documents set minimums for alarms in commercial or domestic buildings.
- They do not cover everything insurers require for commercial premises: when buying a new system, additional requirements must be given to the installer.

> **Exam note:** the book says “these two documents” without naming the second. From context it appears to be the BS EN 50131 series.

#### 11.3.3 Exterior Sensors

##### Classification

One way to classify exterior sensors:

1. Passive or active.
2. Covert or visible.
3. Line-of-sight or terrain-following.
4. Volumetric or line detection.
5. Application.

| Criterion | Option A | Option B |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Energy | **Passive:** only receives. It detects the energy the target emits (for example, the mechanical energy of someone walking on the ground or climbing a fence) or the change it causes in a natural field (for example, the local magnetic field altered by a metal). It can detect vibration, heat, sound, or capacitance | **Active:** transmits energy and detects changes in what it receives, caused by the presence or movement of the target. It usually has a transmitter and a receiver. Examples: microwave, infrared, and other radio frequency (RF) |
| Advantage | Passive: does not emit, so it is hard to find; safer in environments with vapors or explosive materials | Active: fewer nuisance alarms, because of its stronger signal |
| Visibility | **Covert:** hidden, for example underground; hard for the intruder to detect; does not alter the appearance of the surroundings | **Visible:** for example on a fence or structure; can deter; usually easier to install, repair, and maintain |
| Terrain | **Line-of-sight (LOS):** needs a clear line of sight between transmitter and receiver; on uneven terrain it requires a lot of site preparation | **Terrain-following:** detects equally on flat or uneven terrain; the transducers and the radiated field follow the terrain and give uniform detection across the whole zone |
| Zone | **Volumetric:** alarms when the intruder enters the detection volume; it is hard to know that the space is being monitored (video motion, magnetic field, microwave) | **Line:** detects movement along a line, for example when the fence fabric where the sensor is mounted is moved; its zone is usually easy for the intruder to identify |

**Groups by application**

- Buried line.
- Fence-associated.
- Freestanding.

**References cited by the book**

- Reviews of sensor technologies: Barnard, 1988; Cumming, 1992; Fennelly, 1996 and 2013; Williams, 1988.
- More detail on the technologies: Garcia (2006 and 2008).

##### Technologies (most common exterior technologies)

**Classification summary of the technologies in this part**

| Technology | Passive / active | Covert / visible | LOS / terrain-following | Application |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ported coax | Active | Covert | Terrain-following | Buried |
| Fence disturbance sensors | Passive | Visible | Terrain-following | On fence |
| Sensor fences (such as taut-wire) | Passive | Visible | Terrain-following | Form the fence |
| Electric field / capacitance | Active | Visible | Terrain-following | On fence or on their own posts |

**Ported Coaxial Cables**

- Also called *leaky coax* or *radiating cable sensors*.
- Active, covert, and terrain-following; they are **buried**.
- They respond to the movement of material with **high dielectric constant or high conductivity**: human bodies and metal vehicles.
- The outer conductor does not fully shield the center one: part of the signal leaks out through its ports.
- Detection volume: about **1.5–3.0 ft (0.46–0.91 m) above the surface** and about **3–6 ft (0.91–1.83 m) wider** than the separation between cables.
- **More sensitive in frozen soil than in thawed soil**: conductive soil absorbs part of the energy, and frozen soil conducts less.
- Major sources of nuisance alarms: **moving metal objects and moving water**.
- To avoid distorting the field, exclude nearby metal objects and utility lines from the detection volume.
- Its PD depends on:
  - Processor settings.
  - Orientation of the intruder.
  - Soil characteristics.
  - Presence of metal, including large amounts of salt or metals in the soil.

**Fence Disturbance Sensors**

- Passive, visible, and terrain-following; they are installed mainly on **chain link fences**.
- “Terrain-following” because the fabric itself follows the terrain.
- They detect movement or impact; they mainly look for the intruder who **climbs or cuts** the fabric.
- Transducers used:
  - Switches.
  - Electromechanical transducers.
  - Strain sensitive cable.
  - Piezoelectric crystals.
  - Geophones.
  - Fiber optic cable.
  - Electric cable.
- Nuisance alarms: wind, wind-blown trash, rain with wind, hail, and seismic activity from nearby traffic and machinery.
- They are reduced with rigid posts and taut fabric:

| Element | Test | Maximum allowed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Post | Pull of 50 lb (22.7 kg) at 5 ft (1.5 m) above the ground | Move 0.5 in (12.7 mm) |
| Fabric | Pull of 30 lb (13.6 kg) at the center between posts | Deflect 2.5 in (6.4 cm) |

- In a **two-fence** system, put the sensors on the **inner** one: the outer one stops trash and small animals.
- Do not hang movable objects on the fence (signs, loose ties): they generate nuisance alarms.
- How they are defeated: **tunneling under** or **crossing over without touching it**.
- Against digging: concrete under the fence, and even the bottom edge of the fabric embedded in the concrete.
- PD depends on the installation (fabric tension, processor settings, fence rigidity, noise coupled to the fence) and on how the adversary attacks.
- If the adversary uses a ladder and never touches the fence, the PD is very low or zero.

**Sensor Fences**

- Passive, visible, and terrain-following: the fence **is made of the transducers themselves**.
- Designed mainly to detect climbing or cutting of the fence; there are several configurations.
- **Taut-wire fence:**
  - Many parallel horizontal wires, of high tensile strength, connected under tension to transducers.
  - It detects deflections of the wire: cutting it, climbing it, or spreading it apart to pass through.
  - The wire is usually barbed; the transducers are mechanical switches, strain gauges, or piezoelectric elements.
  - It is mounted on the posts of an existing fence or on its own row of posts.
- Fewer nuisance alarms than disturbance sensors: they are not sensitive to vibration and require about **25 lb (11.3 kg)** of force on the wire to alarm.
- But they are defeated with the same methods as fence disturbance sensors.
- Nuisance alarms in taut-wire: mainly large animals hitting the fence, poor installation or maintenance, and ice storms.
- Its PD depends on: wire tension, wire friction, and spacing between wires.
- If the spacing lets a person through undetected, the PD drops sharply. Keep it at **4 in (10 cm) or less** (Greer, VTW-250 and VTW-300, 1990).

**Fiber Fences**

- Light transmitted through a fiber optic cable attached to an existing fence.
- The light pulses are monitored continuously: a change in pattern or power indicates tampering or cutting, possible intrusion.
- They detect minimal changes in the received light.
- Advantages: immune to electromagnetic interference; safe in any environment because they do not conduct electricity; exceptional range.
- The cable uses microphonic technology to detect attempts to climb or cut.
- It resists environmental nuisance alarms; it is not affected by heat, ultraviolet (UV) light, light, or humidity.
- A flexible microphonic cable (stealth-flex) is attached with UV-resistant ties.
- The control analyzer goes in a cabinet with a NEMA (National Electrical Manufacturers Association) rating, on a pole or inside a metal security box.

**Electric Field or Capacitance**

- Active, visible, and terrain-following.
- They detect a change in the capacitive coupling among several wires attached to a fence but electrically isolated from it.
- Sensitivity adjustable up to **3.3 ft (1 m)** beyond the wire or plane of wires; more sensitivity usually gives more nuisance alarms.
- Nuisance alarms: lightning, rain, humidity, fence movement, and small animals.
- Ice storms can damage wires and insulators.
- Nuisance alarms are reduced by:
  - Good grounding of the sensors and of the metal objects in the field.
  - Dual-technology sensors, which require two different sensors.
- **Harder to defeat** than other fence sensors by digging or bridging: their detection volume extends beyond the plane of the fence (Follis, 1990).
- They can be mounted on their own posts, with no fence:
  - Without the fabric, the detection volume is wider and there are fewer nuisance alarms.
  - In freestanding use, some processors add wires in the horizontal plane to reduce the effect of distant lightning and small animals.

**Classification summary (second part of technologies)**

| Technology | Passive / active | Covert / visible | LOS / terrain-following / volumetric | Application |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Multibeam infrared (IR) | Active | Visible | Line-of-sight | Freestanding |
| Bistatic microwave | Active | Visible | Line-of-sight | Freestanding |
| Ground-based radar (GBR) | Active (reflected RF energy) | — | Volumetric | Large open areas |
| Exterior video motion detector (VMD) | Passive | Covert | Line-of-sight | On top of the video camera |

**Freestanding Infrared Sensors**

- Active, visible, line-of-sight, and freestanding.
- Photoelectric beam systems:
  - An infrared LED emits the beam through a collimating lens.
  - At the other end, a collecting lens focuses the energy on a photodiode.
  - If an opaque object blocks the beam, the sensor detects the drop in received energy.
- Wavelength: about **0.9 microns**, invisible to the human eye.
- In high security, **multiple-beam** sensors are used: a single beam is too easy to defeat.
  - They usually have two vertical columns of transmitter and receiver modules; number and configuration depend on the manufacturer.
  - They form a “fence” of infrared beams.
  - They often include electronics that detect attempts to spoof the beams with another infrared source.
- Nuisance alarms:
  - Atmospheric conditions that block the beam: fog, snow, dust storms.
  - Grass, other vegetation, and animals; keep the area between posts clear, because even mowed grass moves in the wind.
  - Ground heave, optical misalignment, and deep snow.
- Section of the detection volume of a multibeam: about **2 in (5 cm) wide and 6 ft (1.8 m) high**. Like fence sensors, it is a narrow plane.
- The beam goes in a straight line: it needs **flat ground**.
  - Convex ground: blocks the beam.
  - Concave ground: allows passing underneath without detection.
- One can dig under the lower beam, unless there is a concrete sill or paved surface.
- Very high PD in multibeam, but it is defeated by bridging, pole vaulting, or passing or sliding between beams.

**Bistatic Microwave Sensors**

- Active, visible, line-of-sight, and freestanding.
- Two identical antennas at opposite ends of the zone (Figure 11.1, Bistatic Microwave Sensors):
  - One connected to a transmitter of about **10 GHz or 24 GHz**.
  - The other to a receiver that measures the received energy.
- Received energy = vector sum of the direct beam and the signals reflected from the ground and other objects.
- They respond to changes in that vector sum caused by moving objects. The sum can rise or fall, depending on whether the reflection arrives in phase or out of phase.
- They are usually installed to detect someone who **crawls or rolls** with the body parallel to the beam (the smallest target). Consequences:
  - **Flat ground** between transmitter and receiver, so that nothing is in the shadow of the beam: specification **+0, −6 in (15.2 cm)**.
  - Even so, it may not detect someone crawling if the antennas are much farther apart than **120 yd (109.7 m)**.
  - There is a **zone with no detection** in the first few meters in front of the antennas: the **offset distance**.

**Design of a continuous microwave perimeter**

| Parameter | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Antenna arrangement | Overlapped, not adjacent |
| Offset distance assumed | 10 yd (9.1 m) |
| Overlap between adjacent sectors | Twice the offset: 20 yd (18.3 m) |
| Antenna height | 18–24 in (46–60 cm) above the sensor bed |
| Maximum slope of the plane | 1 in (2.5 cm) of drop in 10 ft (3 m) from any point |
| Sensor bed | 4 in (10.2 cm) of river gravel of maximum 1.5 in (3.8 cm) diameter, neutral in color for assessment |
| Alternative | Crushed stone that passes through a 1 in (2.5 cm) mesh |
| Largest detection section | Halfway between antennas: about 4 yd (3.7 m) wide and 3 yd (2.7 m) high |
| Vegetation | Maximum 1–2 in (2.5–5.0 cm); better none |

- Main cause of nuisance alarms: **standing water**.
- Larger gravel: rain still causes nuisance alarms. Smaller stones: they fill with dirt and do not drain.
- The plane for detecting someone who crawls must have a **cross slope** to drain, and gravel to prevent puddles.
- The detection volume varies, but it is large compared with almost all other sensors.
- They tolerate many environmental conditions without nuisance alarms. Problems:
  - A parallel chain link fence nearby, with loose fabric that flexes in the wind.
  - Heavy wind-blown snow: nuisance alarms.
  - Accumulated snow: lowers the PD, especially for someone who crawls.
  - Antenna completely buried in snow: constant alarm.
- Bridging or digging underneath is difficult because of the size of the detection volume.
- Sophisticated adversaries could use secondary transmitters to defeat it.
- It is advisable to fence the zone to reduce nuisance alarms and protect the prepared area.
- They are not practical where hills, trees, or other natural features obstruct the beam.

**Ground Based Radar (GBR)**

- Radar: Radio Detection and Ranging.
- It uses radio frequency energy like line-of-sight microwave, but is considered a **volumetric** sensor.
- Intended for large, unobstructed open exterior areas; a good option at night and where rain and fog are a concern.
- It determines **distance, altitude, direction, and speed** of objects by means of reflected energy.
- The area always has elements that reflect energy; in steady state that is the **clutter** (background echo).
- A reflection that stands out from the background in a sweep may be of interest; it is considered an intrusion if it appears in several consecutive sweeps.
- Hardest to detect: objects moving **tangentially** (at constant distance from the radar) or slow objects.
- Integrating analytics improves effectiveness.

> **Exam note:** the book calls the background echo first “clutter” and then “chatter.” The technical term is clutter (outside the book).

**GBR: deployment keys**

- Avoid obstacles that create blind spots.
- Clear line of sight.
- Locate it to detect **radial** movement (toward or away from the radar).
- Reduce clutter.
- Avoid reflective objects, such as metal and glass.

| GBR data | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Horizontal field of view (azimuth) | 100–120° |
| Vertical field of view (elevation) | 20–30°; more degrees give margin to detect downward from the unit |
| Potential coverage | Hundreds of acres |
| Advertised range: vehicles | Up to 13 km (the greatest) |
| Advertised range: people | Up to 800 m |
| Advertised range: drones | Up to 500 m |

**LiDAR (Light Imaging, Detection, and Ranging)**

- It uses pulsed laser light to measure distances, in a way similar to radar.
- It measures how long the light takes to reach an object or surface, calculates the distance, and forms data points.
- Millions of points (**point clouds**) are processed in seconds as a 3D visualization, with no need for a video system.
- According to the book, unlike radar or sound, it does not dissipate when returning to its scanner.

| Aspect | LiDAR | Radar (GBR) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wavelength | Very short: less than one millionth of a meter | Up to about 5 cm |
| Weather | Easily affected by fog, rain, or dust: droplets absorb the light | Passes through fog, rain, or snow droplets or even sand |
| Image | Color gradients that indicate elevation and great 3D detail; useful for recognition | Monochrome: objects as “blobs” of similar tones |
| Assessment | Very effective for detecting people in open areas or crossings, even where they are looking or moving | Also requires a video camera to confirm the type of object |

- Analytics and artificial intelligence (AI) are integrated to learn practically any object.
- They give 3D geolocation to identify and recognize targets faster.

> **Exam note:** the book's text says “all without and VSS”; it appears to be an erratum for “without any VSS (video surveillance system).”

**Exterior Video Motion Detectors (VMDs)**

- Passive, covert, and line-of-sight: they process the video signal from the video surveillance system.
- The same cameras can serve at once for detection, surveillance, and alarm assessment.
- To operate 24 hours, lighting is required.
- They detect a change in the level of the video signal in a defined part of the scene:
  - A large rectangle.
  - A set of discrete points.
  - A rectangular grid.
- They detect human movement well, except in fog, snow, heavy rain, or darkness. That limitation is overcome with thermal cameras.
- If the resolution does not let the operator quickly know what caused the alarm, the VMD performs poorly.
- Nuisance alarms outdoors:
  - Apparent movement of the scene due to unstable camera mounts.
  - Lighting changes: cloud shadows, bright floodlights, vehicle headlights.
  - Moving objects: birds, other animals, wind-blown trash, and precipitation.
- Tactics to defeat it: take advantage of poor visibility and blend in with the background (Ringler & Hoover, 1994; Matter, 1990).
- It helps with assessment: it delivers an image to personnel and usually highlights the area with movement.
- One camera protects a large area, limited only by the lens's field of view and the resolution.
- Or it protects only selected regions by means of **masking**: the parts the VMD monitors are selected and the rest is ignored.
- Masking makes it possible to create several zones in a single camera view.
- Sensitivity can reach **a single pixel**, but more sensitivity gives more nuisance alarms.

**Analog versus digital VMD**

| Aspect | Analog (older) | Digital |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Status | Still manufactured; can be very effective depending on its sensitivity setting | Much more common |
| Cost | Lower | Somewhat more expensive |
| Analysis | Little ability to analyze the image and discard false alarms (leaves on a tree, waves in a pool) | A/D (analog to digital) converters sample the signal and convert it to digital values |
| Resolution | — | The higher the resolution, the greater the precision: longer zones, fewer cameras, and slower and smaller targets at greater distance |
| Adjustments | — | It somewhat compensates for gradual light changes and some camera vibration; it distinguishes in a limited way wind, rain, snow, leaves, small animals, or birds |
| Best use | Low end: detecting any movement in the scene | High end, well adjusted: good false alarm rejection, pixel-level detection, and estimation of the direction of movement; effective with low contrast, low light, and slow movement |

**Adjustable VMD variables**

- Masking: variable number of detection areas and their dimensions.
- Target size and sensitivity, according to the application.
- Tracking functions to help assess the video.

**Recommended use and video analytics**

- The VMD works best **together with other sensors**; its algorithms are continually improving.
- Indoors it has always been effective; with digital ones it is gaining popularity outdoors.
- **Video analytics:** rule-based algorithms on the digital image to provide situational awareness.
- Examples of preprogrammed alarms given by the book:
  - People gathering.
  - People moving fast.
  - Abandoned packages.
  - Removal of packages.
  - Loitering.
- It makes it possible to search for exceptions in investigations, assess threats quickly, and detect volumetrically.
- **The VMD is more suitable for indoors**: good detection and low NAR.
- Outdoors, development is lacking to reduce its excessive NAR before using it at high-security sites.
- If it is combined with other exterior sensors, its sensitivity can be lowered: fewer nuisance alarms and the operator retains some visual assessment.

##### Other Exterior Sensor Concepts

**Technology Maturity**

- Risk: unknowingly accepting a significant risk by deploying an immature technology too early.
- Solution: use a **maturity model** for security technologies.

| Level | Name | What it means |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Research | Research | Scientific basis established; the security application is not necessarily identified yet. Example: the ferroelectric properties of lithium niobate, a material used to sense infrared energy in IR detectors |
| I | Concept feasibility | Demonstrated in the laboratory |
| II | Research prototype | Handmade in the laboratory; breaks often; does not withstand an operational environment |
| III | Engineering prototype | Close to 90% of functionality; reliability improves |
| IV | Field prototype | Fully functional in an operational environment; reliable, repeatable results; driven and accepted by the user; ready for production at scale |
| V | COTS (commercial off-the-shelf) | Production units available, with spare parts infrastructure and technical support |
| VI | Performance testing | Sets metrics: PD, NAR, vulnerability to defeat, and performance. Outdoors they last **about 12 months** to observe all climates |
| VII | Onsite testing | Daily; real performance in the operational environment (foliage, weather, and terrain), including integration with the site's monitoring station |
| VIII | Nontechnical maturity factors | The site's concept of operations: how the response force uses the information, how it should respond, and whether legal or policy issues prevent using the technology |

- Novelty at the perimeter: **thermal imaging radar**.
  - Cameras that rotate continuously with high-definition thermal surveillance.
  - Full 360° thermal images, with onboard geospatial and computer analysis.
  - They send alerts through integrated Wi-Fi, cellular, or satellite modem.

**Continuous Line of Detection**

- Perimeter: closed loop around the area being protected.
- Design goal: uniform detection along its entire length.
- The zone of each sector must **overlap with those of the two adjacent sectors**.
- Where the primary sensor cannot be deployed well, the gap is covered with another type of sensor.

**Protection-in-Depth**

- Here, protection or security-in-depth = **multiple lines of detection**.
- In high security: **at least two continuous lines** of detection.
- Some systems have three lines and a few, four.
- Example of three lines: buried sensor that creates a magnetic field to detect vehicles + fence-associated sensor + freestanding sensor.
- Advantages: more detection, more reliability, and **fail safe**, because it still gives some protection.
- A sensor can fail without compromising the security of the facility.
- Eliminating single-point or component failures ensures balanced protection even in adverse conditions.

**Complementary Sensors**

- The perimeter is improved with multiple lines and also with **complementary types**, for example microwave and active infrared.
- It takes advantage of the fact that each technology has a different PD, NAR, and vulnerabilities. Results:
  - It detects a wider range of intruders.
  - It keeps operating under different environmental disturbances.
  - It is harder for the intruder to defeat the system.
- They are an alternative to dual-technology sensors: each sensor performs at its maximum, with no compromises from locating them together and filtering.
- They are expensive, but give more protection: that is why they are preferred in high security.
- **Detection patterns must overlap** to be complementary.

| Combination | Complementary? | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Microwave + infrared | Yes | Patterns can be overlapped and the technologies differ |
| Microwave + ported coax | Yes | Patterns can be overlapped and the technologies differ |
| Ported coax + infrared | Yes | Patterns can be overlapped and the technologies differ |
| Microwave + fence sensor | **No** | Their patterns cannot be overlapped without serious nuisance alarm problems |
| Bistatic + monostatic microwave | **No** | Both are vulnerable to the same attack methods and the same sources of nuisance alarms |

**Priority Schemes**

- Disadvantage of multiple lines: more nuisance alarms may appear.
- If the operator becomes saturated, the effectiveness of the system drops.
- PD drops as the time to assess alarms increases.
- The assessment subsystem must help the operator. One aid: a computer that orders the assessment of simultaneous alarms.
- Priority is based on the probability that the alarm is a real intrusion.
- Alarms are displayed in decreasing order of priority until all are assessed.

> **Exam note:** in this section the book inserts a sentence unrelated to the topic: that occasional access points are best for passive sonic and infrasonic sensors. It appears to be misplaced text.

**Combination of Sensors**

- Ideal: high PD for all expected intrusions and low NAR for all expected environmental conditions.
- **No current exterior sensor meets both perfectly**: all have limited detection and high NAR under certain conditions.
- Two main techniques: **OR** combination and **AND** combination.

> **Exam note:** the book writes “OR combinations and combinations”; the word AND is missing.

| Aspect | OR combination (OR gate) | AND combination (AND gate) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| When it alarms | When **any** sensor is activated | When two or more sensors are activated within a selected period |
| When it is suitable | The sensors compensate for each other's deficiencies: each detects different types of intrusion. Those that detect intrusion above ground, overhead, and by tunnel are combined by OR | The nuisance alarms of the sensors are not correlated. Example: seismic + electric field, which respond to different things; if they alarm almost at the same time, it is probably an intrusion |
| NAR | **Sum** of the NARs of each sensor: it rises, so it is useful mainly with sensors of low individual NAR | **Lower** than that of each sensor |
| PD | The book does not quantify it | **Lower** than that of each sensor; with independent detection and redundant coverage, it is the **product** of the individual PDs; each sensor needs a high PD |
| Weakness | More nuisance alarms | The intruder only has to defeat one sensor to avoid the alarm |

- In AND, a long interval helps detect slow intruders; if it is too long, the NAR does not drop enough.

```latex
NAR_{OR} = \sum_{i=1}^{n} NAR_{i}
```

```latex
PD_{AND} = \prod_{i=1}^{n} PD_{i}
```

- Example (outside the book): two sensors with a PD of 0.9 in AND give 0.9 × 0.9 = 0.81.

**Clear Zone**

- The perimeter performs best in an isolated clear zone (clear zone or isolation zone), usual in high security.
- Benefits:
  - It increases the PD.
  - It reduces nuisance alarms.
  - It prevents the system from being defeated.
  - It makes visual assessment of the causes of alarms easier.
- Normally it is defined by **two parallel fences** along the entire perimeter.
- The fences keep people, animals, and vehicles out of the zone.
- Between them, aboveground structures, including overhead utility lines, and plants are removed.
- **Do not put sensors on the outer fence**: it would have nuisance alarms from wind-blown trash and small animals.
- Assessing with video outside the fence is difficult: the camera cannot see through the fabric.
- Clear zones with several complementary sensors are reserved for high security: nuclear plants, prisons, military bases, and other government facilities.

**Sensor Configuration**

- Overlapping the detection volumes of different sensors in each sector creates a larger total volume.
- Defeating the pair is less likely: one must go around a larger volume or defeat two technologies.
- A third sensor improves things more if it forms a **separate line of detection**, rather than overlapping with the first two.
- Physically separate lines help prioritize simultaneous alarms.
- The order of alarms in a sector or in adjacent sectors can follow the logical sequence of an intrusion.

**Site-Specific System**

- A program designed for one site **cannot be transferred to another**: each site combines a unique configuration and physical environment.
- The physical environment defines which types of sensors to use.
- The natural and industrial environments define the nuisance alarm rates.
- Topography defines the shape and size of the detection space: width of the clear zone and flat or uneven terrain.
- Recommendation: set up a **demonstration sector** at the site with the candidate sensors before committing to the full system.

**Tamper Protection**

- Hardware and design must prevent the system from being defeated by tampering.
- The system must be **tamper-resistant** and **tamper-indicating**.
- Measures:
  - Tamper switches on the sensor electronics and in the mounting or wiring junction boxes.
  - Power and signal cables aboveground inside metal conduit.
  - **Line supervision** on the alarm communication lines: it detects cuts, disconnections, short circuits, or bridging.
  - A relatively vulnerable sensor is placed, if possible, where the intruder must be in or pass through the detection volume to get close to the receiver.

**Self-Test**

- Detection capability must be tested regularly.
- Manual testing is recommended, but it consumes a lot of personnel time.
- Remote test: the central control system sends trigger signals (closing or opening a switch) at random times.
- The system verifies that the alarm occurs within a given period and resets within another.
- If not, there may be a hardware failure or tampering, and an alarm must be generated.
- Computer self-test must keep a history of tests and results.
- Irregularities are checked physically and repaired or adjusted as soon as possible.

**Pattern Recognition**

- Cheap, powerful computers analyze the sensors' signal patterns looking for those typical of an intruder.
- With neural networks or artificial intelligence, they learn the patterns of intruders and thus avoid nuisance alarms.
- It applies to any sensor or combination with a signal that is not just on/off; example: smart infrared and fence sensors.

**Effects of Physical and Environmental Conditions**

Six conditions, different at each site:

| Condition | Effect | Measures |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Topography | Ravines, slopes, lakes, rivers, and swamps must be studied during design | Level hills and slopes; drainage to reduce water running through ravines and ditches and avoid seismic disturbances; avoid lakes, rivers, and swamps, because few commercial sensors work in water |
| Vegetation | Underground: wind moves trees and roots and triggers seismic sensors. Aboveground: large plants and trees give cover to the intruder and attract small animals | Prune, remove, sterilize the soil, or pave |
| Fauna | Large animals hit the equipment and damage it; burrowing ones gnaw the cable insulation; birds and insects cause alarms that are hard to assess | Double mesh fence and chemical controls; poisons and repellents may be regulated locally |
| Background noise | Wind, traffic, electromagnetic interference, and seismic sources | Identify them with a site survey and information from the utility companies and plant engineering |
| Climate | Hail, thunderstorms, rain, and wind | Obtain climate data: frequency, speed, accumulation, and duration of each phenomenon; average minimum and maximum temperatures; other environmental conditions |
| Soil and pavement | Seismic and electrical conductivity of the medium | See detail below |

**Detail of noise sources**

- Wind: passes energy to the ground through trees, light and power poles, and fences. Sensors on fences: alarms from strong winds and trash.
- Traffic from highways, railways, and airports: alarms in seismic sensors.
  - Keep roads smooth and speed limits low.
  - Seismic sensors are not practical near heavy air or rail traffic, which causes disturbances even at great distances.
- Electromagnetic interference: lightning, radio transmitters, welding, and electrical transients.
  - It is reduced by shielding the signal cable in conduit or shielding the sensors, with a proper common ground.

**Soil and pavement**

- In buried seismic sensors, what is decisive is the **seismic conductivity of the medium**: enough for them to work, but not so much that it causes nuisance alarms.
- Wet soil conducts well: it can give alarms from distant seismic sources.
- Seismic-magnetic and seismic sensors can be embedded in, or under, concrete or asphalt pavement.
  - Embedded and well coupled to the medium: gains sensitivity.
  - Poorly coupled: can be less sensitive than in soil or under the pavement.
- In ported coax, very conductive soil **reduces the detection volume**.

**Lightning Protection**

- Lightning can disable, damage, or destroy the electronics of exterior sensors.
- Three main precautions:
  1. **Shield the signal cables**, by their internal construction or with metal conduit.
  2. **Good grounding system**: eliminate ground loops and ground at a single point.
  3. **Passive transient suppressors** at the ends of the cables.
- Fiber optic is not affected by lightning: it is the preferred method for transmitting data over long distances outside a building.

**Integration with Video Assessment System**

- The designs of the systems and subsystems must be compatible.
- Video linked to sensor activation greatly reduces the time to know what caused the alarm.
- That takes advantage of the remaining delay and increases the probability of interrupting the adversary.
- Assessment can be by video or in person.
- Advantage of video: remote assessment, without dispatching officers to every alarm, who might arrive too late to assess well.
- Locate the sensors so that, when they alarm, the camera for that zone sees **the entire zone**.

| Design decision | Value or criterion |
| --- | --- |
| Width of the clear zone | Sensor engineers prefer it wide (fewer nuisance alarms); video engineers, narrow (better resolution). Compromise: **10–15 yd (9.1–13.7 m)** |
| Camera tower | Must see the entire area; the sensors must be far enough away to avoid nuisance alarms and distortion of the detection volume |
| Typical tower position | **1–2 yd (0.9–1.8 m) inside the outer fence** of the clear zone, so the adversary cannot use it to bridge |

- Megapixel cameras and digital zoom are covered in section 11.4, Video Surveillance.

**Integration with Barrier Delay System**

- A balanced PPS includes barriers or access denial systems.
- They provide delay time to assess the alarm by video and for the response force to arrive.
- The barrier must not degrade the performance of the sensors or block part of the camera's view.
- Perimeter barriers are usually in, or near, the **inner fence** of the clear zone.
- That way the intruder must first cross the detection zone before being able to tamper with or defeat the barrier.
- Result: the response begins **before** the delay begins.

**Procedures**

- An effective system integrates **people, procedures, and equipment**.
- Establish installation, maintenance, testing, and operating procedures **before** training the operators.
- Current and new personnel need training in them, to learn the basics and stay up to date.

**Manufacturer's instructions**

- They are a starting point. Many manufacturers serve high- and low-security markets, so it is advisable to go beyond them to optimize.
- Example: several microwave manufacturers say they operate with up to **300 yd (274 m)** of separation; at that distance they do not detect someone crawling.
- Example: many fence sensor manufacturers suggest using them around a corner; that zone is not practical to assess.

**Maintenance**

- All sensors and components need periodic maintenance:
  - Calibration.
  - Sensitivity checks.
  - Alignment.
  - Visual inspection.
- Poor maintenance affects the PD and NAR and can make the system more vulnerable.

**Operational tests**

- Regular, day and night, to verify performance against the design basis threat (DBT).
- Test with adversaries at various speeds: walking or running (slow and fast) and crawling.
- Also test how slowly an object can move and still be detected.
- Ideally, standardized tests; each type of sensor needs its method:
  - Microwave: an **aluminum sphere** simulates a crawling intruder; it gives more accurate and repeatable results than actually crawling.
  - Infrared: measure the **attenuation factors**.

**Contingency plans**

- For when a sensor or other equipment is lost.
- They must say when they apply; example: if two of the three perimeter sensors are lost.
- If they include compensatory measures (portable sensors, security officers), they must specify them.
- Procedures defined in advance and always at hand for the operators.

**Key documentation that site personnel must gather, keep, and maintain**

- Required, recommended, and troubleshooting procedures.
- Maintenance logs for each sensor.
- Training records for all employees.
- Results of one-of-a-kind cases, such as the causes of any false alarm.

#### 11.3.4 Interior Sensors

- They are very effective against the **insider threat** if combined with administrative procedures, access control, and material monitoring.
- Properly located, installed, maintained, and tested, they alarm on unauthorized acts or unauthorized presence by insiders and outsiders.

##### Performance criteria

- As with exterior sensors, clear, measurable specifications are needed.
- Example specification: a volumetric sensor must detect a person moving at **1 ft/s (0.3 m/s) or more** throughout its field of view.
- Example from the **UL 681** standard: detect after **3 steps**, at **1 step per second**, with steps of **30 in**, in **3 of 4** walk test patterns, from any point in the covered area.
- **Exam note:** later (volumetric detection) the book cites the same UL 681 with **4 steps** and detection 3 out of 4 times. It contradicts itself: 3 steps versus 4 steps.

##### Nuisance / false alarms

| Concept | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Nuisance alarm | Alarm not caused by an intruder. Sources indoors: electromagnetic, acoustic, thermal, meteorological, seismic, optical, and fauna (birds, insects, and other animals). |
| False alarm | Type of nuisance alarm generated by the equipment itself: poor design, inadequate maintenance, or component failure. |

- Each type of sensor has a different sensitivity to these sources.

##### Vulnerability to insiders

- The interior system is vulnerable to attacks by outsiders and insiders.
- The insider has authorized access: many exterior perimeter sensors are not on their route. Interior ones can detect their theft or sabotage.
- During working hours the sensors are usually in **access mode**: their alarms are temporarily masked and do not appear at the monitoring station. This makes insider tampering easier.
- **Maintenance** personnel probably have the greatest opportunity and technical ability to compromise sensors or the entire system. Other employees may have them too.
- What an insider with technical knowledge can do:
  - lower the sensor's sensitivity;
  - shift its coverage area;
  - change the characteristics of a zone.
- They do not disable the sensor entirely, but open a **gap in detection**.

##### Classification

The book classifies interior sensors in four ways:

1. active or passive;
2. covert or visible;
3. volumetric or line detection;
4. application.

| Criterion | Type | How it works | Advantages and notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Active or passive | Active | A transmitter emits a signal and a receiver detects its changes or reflections. It generates an energy field. | Bistatic: transmitter and receiver separate. Monostatic: together. A very sophisticated adversary can detect the field before entering the zone. |
| Active or passive | Passive | It does not emit; it only receives nearby energy: vibration (person walking, truck), infrared (human, hot object), sound of a destructive break-in, or mechanical change in the sensor (simple electromechanical ones). | Harder to locate: puts the intruder at a disadvantage. Safer with vapors or explosive materials: it emits no energy that could detonate them. |
| Covert or visible | Covert | Hidden: inside walls or under the floor. | Harder to detect and locate, therefore more effective. It does not alter the aesthetics of the place. |
| Covert or visible | Visible | In plain view: on a door or other supporting structure. | Can deter. Easier to install and repair. |
