| Volumetric or line | Volumetric | Protects all or part of the volume of a room or building. Examples: ultrasonic, microwave, passive infrared. | Detects the intruder in the zone regardless of how they entered, if the application and mounting are correct. |
| Volumetric or line | Line | Detects only at a point or very narrow strip: forced entry through doors, windows, or walls. | Only detects if the intruder violates that specific entry point. |

- **Exam note:** the book says that volumetric sensors have a “higher partial discharge.” It appears to be an erratum; from context it should say higher probability of detection.

###### Classes by application

| Class | What it detects |
| --- | --- |
| Boundary-penetration sensors | The penetration of the boundary of an interior area. |
| Interior motion sensors | The movement of the intruder within a confined interior area. |
| Proximity sensors | The intruder next to an object in an interior area, or when they touch it. |

- Some sensors can be applied in several ways.

###### Four types of interior motion detection according to UL 681

| Type | What it covers |
| --- | --- |
| Trap detection | Narrow or wide detection in high-traffic zones or the intruder's expected routes to get from A to B. |
| Spot detection | A high-value object or zone: safes, vaults, warehouses, money rooms. |
| Channel detection | Narrow detection where the intruder is expected to cross. |
| Volumetric detection | Wide detection in a defined area. It is adjusted to detect a person at 1 step per second, in 4 steps, with detection 3 out of 4 times. |

##### Technologies

- The book groups the technologies by application.
- Sources it cites for more technical detail: Barnard (1988); Cumming (1992); Fennelly (2013); Garcia (2006 and 2008); Patterson (2013); Rodriguez, Dry, and Matter (1991); Zwirn (2014).

##### Boundary-Penetration Sensors

- The most common: **vibration** and **electromechanical**.
- They best protect roofs, floors, walls, and doors.

###### Vibration sensors

- Passive and line; visible or covert.
- They detect the movement of the surface they are attached to.
- A blow makes the surface vibrate at a frequency of its own construction. The tool used also influences the frequency.
- They give **early warning** of a forced entry.

| Type | Operation | Typical frequencies |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Jiggle switch | The simplest; the book does not detail its operation. | The book does not give it |
| Inertial switch | Metal ball on metal contacts. When the sensor body moves, inertia separates the ball for an instant and it alarms. | **2–5 kHz** |
| Piezoelectric sensor | The element moves relative to the sensor's mass. When it flexes it generates a voltage that is analyzed. | **5–50 kHz** |

**Glass-break detectors**

| Type | How it works | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Glass-mounted (shock) | Vibration or shock sensor, piezoelectric or inertial, on the glass or its mounting surface. It alarms on the frequencies of broken glass. | Must be suitable for the type of glazing: some do not generate the right frequency. Consider aftermarket additions: plastic anti-theft laminates (security film) or solar film. |
| Active glass-break | It introduces a vibration into the glass; another transducer listens for it at another point. When the glass breaks, the signal changes and it alarms. | More expensive, but with fewer nuisance alarms. |
| Acoustic (audio glass break), simple or dual | Microphone on a wall or ceiling that listens for the frequency of broken glass (Figure 11.2). It needs a general direct line of sight to the fixed or movable glass. | Typical range **25–40 ft**, but it should never be so far from the protected glass. |
| Dual-technology acoustic | Requires two signals: an ultra-low frequency (like striking the glass) and an ultra-high one (like breaking it). | It is the dual version of the acoustic. |

Key points of the acoustic:

- Choose only **UL-listed** equipment; there are unlisted products that look the same.
- Curtains, covers, and blinds dampen sound: the detector does not hear it.
- Furniture in front of the glass creates an acoustic barrier.
- Respect the manufacturer's glass types: double insulated, plate, laminated, tempered, or with security film.
- To reduce false alarms: **discrimination algorithms**.

**Fiber-optic intrusion sensors (vibration)**

- Passive and line; visible or covert.
- They detect **micro-bending** of the cable from surface vibration.
- The processing unit sends light through the cable and receives it at the other end. Micro-bending changes the received light.
- Typical settings: low- and high-frequency filter, amplitude filter, and pulse duration and count. They seek to reduce sensitivity to nuisance sources.
- Before choosing it, consider vibrations from nearby machinery, vehicles, trains, and air traffic.
- Filtering those frequencies is possible, but may reduce sensitivity to intrusions.

**When to use vibration sensors**

| Situation of the structure | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Exposed to external vibrations | Risk of nuisance alarms |
| Severe vibrations | Do not use vibration sensors |
| Only occasional impacts | Use with a **pulse accumulator / count circuit**: it tolerates a limited number of impacts before alarming |

###### Electromechanical sensors

- Passive, visible, and line.
- The most common: magnetic switch or contact on doors and windows.

| Sensor | Operation | Strengths and weaknesses |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Simple magnetic switch | Switch unit with a **reed switch** on the fixed part. Magnet unit with a permanent magnet on the moving part. Closed: the field keeps the switch closed (secure). When it opens the field drops and it goes to open (alarm). | Easily defeated by bringing a strong magnet near the switch, which forces it to the secure position. |
| **BMS** (balanced magnetic switch) | It has an adjustable **bias magnet** in the switch unit. It detects changes in the strength of the magnetic field. | Prevents spoofing with an external magnet. Protects more than magnetic or mechanical contacts and tilt switches. |
| **Hall effect switch** | Fully electronic, with no mechanical reeds; **requires power**. It measures and monitors the field strength of the magnet unit. It alarms if it detects enough field change. | More security than the BMS. Harder to tamper with and defeat. |

- Anti-tamper alternatives to the BMS:
  - several reed switches and several magnets;
  - fuses and voltage breakdown sensing devices;
  - shielded case.
- Some come with internal electromagnets for self-test. Their complexity increases that of the equipment and reduces its vulnerability.
- Hall effect: a current-carrying conductor exposed to an external magnetic field accumulates charges on one side. The amount and polarity of that separation are proportional to the strength and polarity of the field. It is measured between the sides of a metal strip.
- Scale of difficulty for the intruder: simple contact → BMS → Hall effect. Each step requires more knowledge.
- BMS and Hall protect better than the simple contact against internal tampering and spoofing.
- Limit: the protection is only as good as the penetration resistance of the door or window. They only alarm if it is opened. Cutting through the leaf avoids the BMS.
- Example design criterion for a BMS: alarm if the door separates **1 in (2.5 cm) or more** from the jamb. **Do not** alarm at **0.5 in (1.3 cm) or less**.

**UL 634, 9th edition (2007): high security switches**

| Level | What it is | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| HSS1 (Level One high security switch) | What used to be called a BMS | May remain in an existing SCIF. If modified or upgraded, it must be changed to HSS2 to comply. |
| HSS2 (Level Two high security switch) | Higher level, much harder to defeat and with additional requirements | Mandatory in new high-security IDS (intrusion detection system) applications, such as a SCIF. |

- SCIF = Sensitive Compartmented Information Facility, a facility for compartmented classified information (outside the book).

**Continuity / break wire sensor**

- Fine conductive wires and electronics: it alarms when the conductor breaks.
- It is attached to or embedded in walls, ceilings, or floors to detect penetration.
- The wires can form any pattern, even for irregular areas. They also exist on printed circuit.
- Break wire grids and screens for forced penetrations in: ventilation grilles, floors, walls, ceilings, closed cabinets, vaults, and skylights.

| Advantages | Weaknesses |
| --- | --- |
| Few nuisance alarms: the wire has to break | Defeated with a jumper around the cut |
| Adapts to any shape | Defeated by moving the wire aside to pass |
| With electrical supervision tampering is reduced (recommended) | Only detects if there is a break or cut |

- Fiber-optic version: same principle. The fiber must break or be damaged until the light is cut or greatly reduced.
- It is different from, and much simpler than, vibration fiber optic.

##### Interior Motion Sensors

- By far the most common: **monostatic microwave** and **passive infrared (PIR)**.

###### Microwave sensors

- Active, visible, and volumetric.
- They create an energy field, normally on the order of **10.5 GHz**.
- Indoors they are almost always **monostatic**: a single antenna transmits and receives.
- They detect through the **Doppler shift** between the emitted and received signal, caused by a moving object.
- To alarm, the Doppler change needs sufficient amplitude and duration.
- If a higher or lower frequency comes back, the target is approaching or receding.
- According to the book, most operate in the **K band** and trigger radar detectors of that band.
- **Exam note:** 10.5 GHz is X band; the K band is around 24 GHz (outside the book). For the exam, stay with what the book says.
- Optimal detection: target that **approaches or recedes** from the sensor, not one that crosses the zone. Orient it to force that movement.
- Mount it **near the ceiling** of the protected area.

**Detection zone (Figure 11.3)**

- **Elongated balloon** shape, defined by the antenna: microwave horn, flat printed-circuit, or phased array.
- The manufacturer's pattern may not be the real one. If the target falls in the concave part of the real pattern, it can be defeated.
- Some are defeated by crawling slowly and close to the floor. This happens if the pattern was raised so that foliage or rodents do not alarm.
- It passes through most glass, plaster, gypsum, plywood, and other common wall materials.
  - Advantage: it detects through partitions within the protected volume.
  - Disadvantage: it detects movement outside the area or building, with nuisance alarms.
- Metal objects (large bookcases, desks, monitors, fences): create shadow zones and incomplete coverage, or nuisance alarms from reflection.
- Because it reflects, metal can also improve detection in shadow zones. Take care that it does not reflect energy toward zones that must be avoided.

| Advantages | Disadvantages (in addition to penetration and metals) |
| --- | --- |
| Invisible and inaudible pattern | Requires fully rigid mounting |
| Reliable and low maintenance | Susceptible to pattern drift |
| Low cost per area covered | Tends to reflect off metal objects |
| High probability of detection | Requires special care in light construction (glass, gypsum, wood) |
| Immune to high air turbulence and to changes in temperature and humidity |  |
| Variety of detection patterns |  |

**Use and installation**

- As a spot sensor: limited coverage of a point where other sensors cover poorly or are vulnerable to tampering.
- Typical use of the monostatic: automatic door openers in supermarkets and airports.
- Mounting: near the ceiling, aimed at the desired coverage and away from reflecting metals.
- Several in the same area: **different frequencies**, or they interfere and alarm nonstop.
- Sources of nuisance alarms:
  - movement of nonhuman objects inside and outside the zone;
  - small animals or birds;
  - vibration from poor installation or mounting;
  - **fluorescent lamps**: their ionized gas reflects microwaves at the rate of **60 Hz**. They must not be in the zone; some models filter that Doppler.
- Vulnerabilities:
  - slow targets;
  - absorption or reflection of the energy;
  - blocking of the field of view (stacked boxes, moved furniture);
  - movement along the circumference of the pattern.
- More sensitive to approaching and receding movement; less to crosswalk. Choose the mounting with that in mind.

###### Passive infrared sensors, PIR

- Passive (they do not transmit), visible, and volumetric.
- They respond to changes in the energy an intruder emits in their field of view. It is roughly equivalent to the heat of a **50 W bulb**.
- Under certain conditions they detect changes in the background thermal energy if someone hides in it. Relying on that is not recommended: their optics and electronics limit detection to moving sources.
- They only detect what they see in their coverage pattern.
- Most effective with movement that **crosses** the pattern (crosswalk): the target must enter and leave several segments. Less sensitive to approaching and receding.
- **Frequent walk tests** are recommended.

Four characteristics of infrared radiation:

1. All objects emit it in proportion to their temperature.
2. It is transmitted without physical contact between the emitting and receiving surface.
3. It heats the receiving surface; any device sensitive to temperature changes detects it.
4. It is invisible to the human eye.

- PIR response band according to the book: **8 to 14 nanometers (nm)**.
- **Exam note:** erratum. The thermal infrared of the human body is at 8–14 **micrometers (µm)**, not nanometers (outside the book).

**How it works**

- **Thermopile** or **pyroelectric** detector: it receives the intruder's radiation and converts it into an electrical signal.
- The signal is amplified and passes through logic circuits that usually require the source to move in the field of view.
- If the signal is strong and the required movement is present, it alarms.
- It detects by the temperature difference between intruder and background: **minimum resolvable temperature (MRT)**. Some manufacturers indicate an MRT of only **1 °C (1.8 °F)**.
- A high ambient temperature affects detection, depending on the mounting and the intruder's speed and direction.
- Pyroelectric: certain dielectric materials of low crystal symmetry exhibit spontaneous dielectric polarization.
- The energy reaches the detector through **segmented parabolic mirrors** or **Fresnel lenses**.
  - A single long cone (single-segment): protects corridors.
  - Several segments (multisegment): protects large open areas.
- As with microwave, the pattern does not have a perfect shape: take care when locating it.

**Nuisance alarms and installation**

- Birds and flying insects: they block the background energy; if their movement meets the alarm criterion, it alarms. Also an insect walking on the lens.
- Infrared does not pass through most materials, nor glass. What is outside the building does not usually cause alarms.
- But sun coming in through windows heats interior surfaces, which radiate in that band.
- Install it away from heat sources: radiators, heaters, hot pipes, or other heating elements. They create thermal gradients in front of the lens that alter the background.
- An unshaded incandescent bulb at **3–5 yards (2.7–4.7 m)** can alarm if it burns out or goes off in a power outage. Exam note: 5 yards is about 4.6 m, the book gives 4.7 m (outside the book).

| Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- |
| Fully passive technology | Moderate sensitivity to vibration |
| Well-defined detection zones | Sensitivity varies with ambient temperature |
| No interaction among several units | Operates by line of sight; field is easy to block |
| Low to moderate cost | Possible nuisance alarms from rapid temperature changes |
| Relatively few nuisance alarms |  |

**Microwave versus PIR**

| Aspect | Microwave | PIR |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Classification | Active, visible, volumetric | Passive, visible, volumetric |
| Movement it detects best | Approaching or receding from the sensor | Crossing the pattern (crosswalk) |
| Passes through glass and light walls | Yes (advantage and source of alarms) | No |
| Several units in the same area | Need different frequencies | They do not interact |
| Typical alarm source | Fluorescents, metals, moving objects | Heat sources, sun, birds and insects |

###### Photoelectric beam

- Also called **active infrared sensor**.
- Active: it transmits infrared light to a photocell and alarms when the beam is interrupted.
- Depending on the application, it works as a perimeter or space sensor.
- Ideal in long, straight, defined areas, for example across corridors.
- Also as backup on several overhead doors or skylights.
- It exists in dual-beam so that birds do not cause nuisance alarms.

##### Dual-Technology Sensors

- Active and passive at once, visible, and volumetric.
- They seek **absolute alarm confirmation** without losing a high probability of detection (PD).
- Ideal: two technologies, each with a high PD and **no shared susceptibilities** to nuisance alarms.
- The usual today: **microwave + PIR** combined in **AND-gate** logic. It only alarms if both alarm almost at the same time.
- Well applied, and if each has a low nuisance alarm rate (NAR), they usually have a lower NAR than a single-technology sensor.
- Drawback: with AND, the combined PD is **lower** than that of each sensor. It is the product of both.

```latex
P_D = P_{D,microwave} \times P_{D,PIR} = 0.95 \times 0.95 \approx 0.90
```

- The book rounds 0.9025 to **0.90**.
- In addition, microwave detects approach better and PIR detects crossing better. In a single housing the PD is lower than with two sensors mounted **perpendicular** to each other, with overlapping patterns.
- To optimize the PD: sensors **mounted separately but combined logically**.
- In high security: **never** a single dual sensor in place of two separate sensors.
- If duals are used, install several units that cover each other.
- Many government standards **do not allow** dual sensors in high security, such as a SCIF or other classified spaces.

##### Video Motion Detection

- **VMD (video motion detector)**: **passive** sensor that processes the signal from a video surveillance camera.
- A single camera serves for detection, assessment, and surveillance.

| Type | Operation | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Analog | Monitors the signal and detects changes in brightness. The detection area (percentage of the camera's field) varies greatly. It generates an external alarm if brightness or other conditions (for example, time) are met. It highlights on the monitor the section where it detected. | Cheaper than digital. |
| Digital | Digitizes the signal. Divides the scene into zones, elements, or cells and processes each one. It monitors: change in brightness or contrast; logical movement between neighboring cells; speed between cells; size of objects in the cells; global changes in almost all the cells. | More sophisticated and with fewer nuisance alarms. Better distinguishes intruder from nuisance source, which broadens its use. |

- Digital performs well indoors if the nuisance sources are analyzed and there is good lighting, good cameras, and good transmission equipment.
- Indoor nuisance sources: insects on or near the lens, flickering lights, pets, birds, and rats.
- The camera matters: low-contrast output reduces detection; a lot of noise causes nuisance alarms. It needs sufficient, uniform light.
- Any change in brightness can alarm. Flickering lights and camera movements raise the nuisance alarm rate.
- **Very slow** movement defeats most VMDs.
- Indoors they work well because there is no snow, fog, traffic, or clouds.
- Tests before installing:
  - **low-profile** target, such as a person crawling;
  - fast high-profile targets, such as people walking or running;
  - always in the **lowest-contrast** lighting condition expected.
- Factors for selecting a VMD:
  - constant, controlled lighting (no flicker);
  - camera vibration;
  - objects that create blind zones;
  - moving objects, such as fans, curtains, and small animals;
  - changing sun or shadows coming in through windows or doors.

##### Proximity Sensors

Two types: pressure mats and capacitance sensors.

| Sensor | Operation | Status and use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pressure mat | Under the carpet next to doors, on steps, or at other strategic points. It alarmed with a weight of **5–20 lb/ft² (24.4–97.6 kg/m²)** on the surface. | Practically obsolete; replaced mainly by PIR. New installations are rare; some remain. |
| Weight transducer (variant of the mat) | With other detectors, it controls the number of people or the gross weight allowed in a portal. At a card or keypad access it prevents anyone exceeding a programmed weight from passing. | Still in use. With distributed databases and a unique PIN or card, it stores the holder's actual weight ± a margin. When the PIN is entered or the card is swiped, the software compares the measured weight. |
| Capacitance sensor | Large electrical capacitor that radiates energy and detects changes in the capacitive coupling between an antenna and ground. The wire is connected to the protected object (safe, file cabinet). Anyone who touches it absorbs energy, alters the circuit, and it alarms. | Many replaced by PIR, which detect the intruder much earlier. Still the preferred option if detection must be limited to **only the protected object**. |

##### Wireless Sensors

- The most common: **radio frequency (RF)**.
- In the U.S. they usually operate in the **300 MHz or 900 MHz** bands. Some use **spread-spectrum**.
- Components: sensor/transmitter units and a receiver.
  - Sensor/transmitter: sensor and transmitter in a single package, with a battery.
  - Advertised battery life: **2 to 5 years**, depending on the number of alarms and transmissions.
  - Each unit carries a **unique identification code**.
  - Each system has its range and its maximum number of sensors per receiver.
  - Receiver outputs: RS-232, logic levels, or relay contact.
- To save battery, the transmitter stays at rest (sleep mode) until an event requires transmitting.

| Event | When it is transmitted | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Alarm | When it occurs | Alarm notice |
| Tamper | When it occurs | Tamper notice |
| State-of-health | At fixed intervals | Battery, alarm, and tamper status; confirms that the sensor is still present and operating |

- If the receiver does not get a state-of-health message on time, it indicates a **fault**.
- **Universal transmitter**: in wireless and hybrid systems it allows connecting a wired device that does not require power, such as an alarm contact.
- Wiring between the universal transmitter and the wired circuit: **maximum 3 ft**.

| RF problem | What happens |
| --- | --- |
| Collision | Several signals arrive at once (for example, state-of-health) and the receiver reads none of them. |
| Signal fade | Excessive distance or a path blocked by material that shields RF: large metal objects or metal cladding on the building. |
| Interference | Other RF sources in the same band exceed the sensor's signal. |

- Mitigation: spread-spectrum transmission and random dithering of the timing of state-of-health messages.
- Before installing: verify a good transmission path, away from sources of interference.

##### Other Interior Sensor Concepts

###### Environmental Conditions

- They generate noise in the same energy spectrum the sensor is looking for. They degrade its performance and can cause alarms with no intruder.
- They are often the cause of vulnerabilities: the technology must be chosen and operated well.

| Condition | Sources | Effect and mitigation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Electromagnetic energy | Lightning; electrical distribution lines and equipment; radio transmissions; telephone lines and equipment; lighting; computer and data processing equipment; electric vehicles (forklifts, elevators); TV equipment; car ignition; electrical machinery; intercom and paging; aircraft. | Wood and concrete do not shield: there may be a lot of external background energy. Mitigate with electromagnetic shielding of all components (including data links) and a common, proper electrical ground. |
| Nuclear radiation | Environments with radiation. | Damages components such as semiconductors. Total invulnerability is not achieved; design, components, and shielding reduce the risk. Neutrons degrade semiconductors and integrated circuits according to the total dose. |
| Acoustic energy | Inside or outside the area: meteorological phenomena; HVAC (heating, ventilation, and air conditioning); air compressors; TV equipment; electronic telephone equipment; aircraft; vehicles; trains. | Affects the performance of interior sensors. |
| Thermal environment | Climate; HVAC; machinery that produces heat; interior lighting; chemical and radioactive reactions; sun variation through windows and skylights. | Uneven temperature, air movement, and expansion and contraction of the building. |
| Optical phenomena | Sunlight; interior lighting; highly reflective surfaces; infrared and ultraviolet energy from other equipment. | Light energy that affects the sensor. |
| Seismic phenomena | Earth tremors; machines or equipment; vehicular traffic; trains; thunder; strong winds. | They produce vibrations. |
| Meteorological phenomena | Lightning; thunder; rain; hail; temperature; wind; earth tremors; high relative humidity; sunlight. | They adversely affect interior sensors. |

###### Sensor Selection

- Before choosing, consider the interaction among **equipment, environment, and potential intruders**.
- Two key physical conditions:
  - the **construction** of the building or room;
  - the **equipment and objects** that occupy the space.
- Interiors are usually controlled, predictable, and measurable: there are almost always sensors that perform well there.
- Consider the sources of nuisance alarms present, especially if motion detectors are used.
- With the right combination of sensors and technologies, optimal performance is achieved.
- Useful summary of operational topics: **Adams (1996)**.

###### Procedures

- They increase the effectiveness of the system:
  - two-person rule;
  - sensor effectiveness tests;
  - good maintenance and documentation practices.
- When buying: choose the sensors closest to the performance goals and protection requirements, and compatible with future systems.
- **Two-person rule**: two knowledgeable people take part in an activity. It prevents a single insider from compromising security.
  - It applies to granting access within the site and to handling critical assets, information, or equipment.
  - Each person must be able to detect the other's tampering.
  - It fails if it is relaxed because of friendship or a long relationship between them.

###### Testing Mechanisms

- It is advisable for the sensor to have an audible or visible alarm indicator, recognizable at **10–35 ft (3–10 m)**. It must be deactivated during normal operation.
- **Walk tests**: daily at first; later they are spaced out if they go well.
- Test the performance of **all** sensors after any maintenance.
- Sensitivity analysis or effectiveness test: confirms performance, verifies coverage, and looks for blind zones from changes in the room layout.
- **Self-test**, as separate equipment or integrated: it makes it possible to test the sensor and alarm communication often.
- The self-test must be activated **randomly** (Graham and Workhoven, 1987).

###### Installation and Maintenance

- At a minimum, according to the manufacturer's specifications. It can be optimized beyond them.
- Periodic inspection: check the required configuration and specifications, and look for alterations or modifications.
- Acceptance tests, operational tests, and maintenance logs per piece of equipment indicate how many and which spares to keep.
- Spares: inspect them carefully before installing them and store them securely to deter tampering.

###### Inspection

- Inspect the sensors after any maintenance.
- After maintenance of the data-collection control panel: walk test of all its sensors.
- Require **prior approval by security personnel** for plant modification plans, such as:
  - relocating the detectors;
  - adding objects that cause nuisance alarms;
  - moving large objects within the protected area.
- After a remodel, the sensitivity of the detectors may need to be readjusted.

###### Documentation

- Have available:
  - theory of operation of the equipment;
  - functional block diagrams;
  - wiring diagrams;
  - schematics;
  - parts lists with the manufacturer's number and its commercial equivalent.
- With the maintenance logs, reliability is monitored and problem components or areas are detected.

##### System Integration

- Definition: the process of combining **technological elements, procedures, and personnel** into a single facility security system.
- It requires balance among hardware, personnel, and operating procedures.
- Interior sensors, like exterior ones, are integrated with:
  - the display and control subsystem;
  - the access control subsystem;
  - the delay mechanisms.
- Integration must include:
  - protection-in-depth;
  - balance on all routes into the facility;
  - backup systems and contingency plans.

##### Line Supervision

- Definition: a way to **monitor the communication link** between the sensor and the alarm control center.
- Supervised lines and continuous monitoring of tamper switches help against the insider threat.
- Better still if each tamper is on its own **24-hour zone**: it gives notice of tampering even if the system is disarmed.
- Supervision techniques the designer must know:
  - reverse polarity;
  - sound monitoring;
  - radio class C;
  - steady direct current class B;
  - tone;
  - digital classes A and AB.

| Off-site communication path | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| Digital dialer | The most common method in IDS monitored off site. It uses the existing telephone line. Cellular or radio backup is recommended: most intruders cut the line. |
| IP network transmitter | Rapidly growing technology. It requires a network connection. It adds encryption and instantly transmits alarm and trouble signals. |

- Several sensors on a single alarm processor: line supervision is needed between the processor and **each** sensor.
- Recommended method: **end-of-line (EOL)** device, normally a resistor of the value the manufacturer indicates.
- The EOL goes in the circuit **next to the sensor, in the field**, **not at the** IDS control **panel**.
- Liability risk: on some panels, a short in the data or power bus, intentional or accidental, can shut down the entire system depending on where it occurs.
- After that short, the panel may stop communicating off site or stop working properly.

##### Summary of 11.3 (closing of the section in the book)

- Exterior and interior sensors were analyzed along four axes:
  - classification and application;
  - probability of detection;
  - nuisance alarm rate;
  - vulnerability to defeat.

| Subsystem | What the designer must consider when integrating sensors |
| --- | --- |
| Exterior (perimeter) | Specific design goals; effects of physical and environmental conditions; interaction with a balanced, integrated PPS (physical protection system). Desired features: clear zone; good sensor configuration in it; combination and alarm priority schemes; tamper protection; self-test capability. Site-specific design, well integrated with the video and barrier subsystems. |
| Interior | Skill level of the intruder; design goals; effects of environmental conditions; interaction with a balanced, integrated PPS. |

Principles of a good IDS:

1. protection-in-depth;
2. complementary sensors;
3. eliminate single-point failures;
4. minimum consequence on failure of a component;
5. balanced protection;
6. integration of people, equipment, and procedures.

- Follow the manufacturer's requirements and recommendations, and proven standards, listings, and codes such as **UL** and **NFPA 70**. That way the IDS performs well, is more reliable, and complies with regulations.
- NFPA 70 is the U.S. National Electrical Code (outside the book).
- Sensors and subsystems must offer:
  - high probability of detection;
  - low nuisance alarm rate;
  - low vulnerability to the defined threat.
- Other desirable features:
  - fast communication to send and assess alarms;
  - good lighting and good assessment systems;
  - balanced system with adequate protection on all routes to the target's perimeter.

### 11.4 Video Surveillance

- According to the Partner Alliance for Safer Schools (PASS), video surveillance is an integral part of the physical security plan.
- It provides **deterrence**, **detection**, and, in advanced implementations, **enhanced response**.
- It is a **dual-purpose** solution:
  - Primary use: physical security.
  - Secondary use: business applications (growing with hardware and software analytics).
  - The primary security function **always takes priority**.

**Uses of video surveillance**

| Use | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Surveillance | Monitoring video in real time, manually or automated. |
| Assessment | Viewing recorded video to assess something that is happening or has just happened; verifying active threats. |
| Forensics | Using recorded video as a record of what happened; it serves as evidence of illicit activity. It answers who, what, when, and where, after the fact. |
| Risk Mitigation | Using video analytics to proactively warn that an event is occurring. Proactive detection and prevention system. |

**Main elements of a video surveillance system**

| Element | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Field of view | Area visible through the lens. |
| Scene | Place or activity to be observed. |
| Lens | Determines the sharpness and the size of the field of view. |
| Camera | Converts the optical image from the lens into an electronic signal for transmission. It requires a mounting support and, at times, a housing against vandalism or weather (rain, snow). |
| Transmission medium | Carries the signal to the display or recording equipment: coaxial cable, twisted pair, network cable, fiber optic, or radio frequency. |
| Workstation | Client equipment with monitor, computer, and keyboard/mouse or other controls. Fixed or mobile. It displays one or several images; it allows live viewing, playback, alarm management, and control of capture and other devices. |
| Recording equipment | Recorders and equipment for choosing which images are recorded, at what speed, at what resolution, and with what compression format. |

**From CCTV to VSS**

- It used to be called **CCTV** (closed-circuit television).
- Today the correct term is **VSS** (video surveillance system).
- Reason: the circuit is no longer closed and a television is no longer used for viewing.
- The shift from analog to digital changed the basis of design.
- The application theory of video surveillance remains the same, even with more pixels, smaller components, and more reliability.
- The integrated VSS must be **tested before being installed**: verify that it picks up the alarm sensors fast enough to see the source of the intrusion.

**Key design points for the security manager**

- The VSS is above all a **visual tool** for security and is applied as such.
- The primary security use governs the design. Business uses are allowed only if they do not interfere with it.
- **The application dictates the equipment, not the other way around.**
- Conduct periodic reviews and needs assessments to confirm the system still meets the site's needs.
- Always design with future growth or changes in the application in mind.

#### 11.4.1 Functional Requirements

- First the purpose of the system is defined; for example, with the **ASIS General Security Risk Assessment Guideline (2004)**.
- Then a **functional requirement** is written for each component.
- A functional requirement is like a **job description**.

**Questions to define the functional requirements**

- What is the purpose of the system?
- What, specifically, must each camera see?
- What is needed in proactive risk reduction, real-time monitoring, or recorded video?

##### Camera Functional Requirements

Each function requires a different field of view. Three factors are considered:

| Factor | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Target | People (individuals or groups); packages or objects; vehicles (individual); traffic; perimeters; terrain/property. |
| Activity | Assault; vandalism; trespassing; robbery; abandoned package or vehicle. |
| Operational requirement | Identify a person, or show in which direction a suspect left a parking lot. |

**Example: two operational requirements, two designs**

- **Identify a person:** defined focal view that includes the face. The best is a fixed camera.
- **Direction of exit from the parking lot:** wider focal length that takes in the parking lot.
- The final size of the image is determined by the **lens**, the **focal length**, and the **image sensor**.

**Fixed camera vs. PTZ**

| Aspect | Fixed camera | PTZ (pan/tilt/zoom) camera |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Movement | Static; it does not move. | Pan: rotates from side to side. Tilt: moves up and down. |
| Lens | Fixed focal or varifocal. | Motorized varifocal to open or close the field of view. |
| Advantage | Always points at the desired field of view; makes assessment easier. | Covers a much larger area; more flexibility. |
| Disadvantage | — | If it looks elsewhere, it does not capture the event occurring outside its current view. |
| Typical use | Identification at a defined point. | Assessment or video patrol. |

#### 11.4.2 Uses of Video Subsystems in Security

- Video used to be only a surveillance and assessment tool.
- With today's IP (Internet Protocol) cameras it is also a **proactive** tool: detect, deter, notify, respond automatically, and analyze events or threats.

**Concepts**

| Concept | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Visual assessment | Having identifying or descriptive visual information during an incident. |
| Surveillance forensics | Having visual information stored in a format that allows the images to be studied or reviewed. |
| Visual documentation | Includes embedded authenticity points: time/date stamp or character generation. |

**There are only four reasons to have cameras in security**

1. Get visual information about something that **is happening**.
2. Get visual information about something that **already happened**.
3. **Deter** or discourage undesirable activities.
4. Use **video analytics** tools.

**How to choose the camera (reasons 1 and 2)**

- They require the right combination of camera and lens.
- Order of criteria: **1st sensitivity, 2nd resolution, 3rd features**.
- **The camera is chosen before the lens.**

| Criterion | What it means |
| --- | --- |
| Sensitivity | Minimum amount of visible light needed for a quality image; also invisible spectra such as infrared (IR). |
| Resolution | Measured in horizontal and vertical pixels; it defines the quality of the image in detail or reproduction. |
| Features | What gives one camera an advantage over another. |

- Lens + camera + image sensor determine how much image, and of what kind, appears on the monitor.
- The hardware is chosen for its ability to give the desired identification information.

**The three theoretical views of identification**

##### Subject Identification

- Ability to identify something or someone in the scene **beyond a shadow of a doubt**.
- Example of the $100 bill:
  - At **3 ft (0.9 m)**, someone with mediocre eyesight identifies it without doubt.
  - At **30 ft (9 m)**, a person with normal eyesight can no longer identify it.
- Video does not allow touching, smelling, or tasting (and almost never hearing). The visual information must suffice on its own.
- The **viewing angle** matters: someone standing on a desk looking down at people's heads does not identify them well. With video, such a steep angle is even worse.
- It depends **first on the size and detail of the image** and **second on the viewing angle**.
- **10% rule:** the object must occupy at least **10% of the width of the scene**.
  - Average person: **2 ft (0.6 m)** wide.
  - For full body and identification beyond doubt, the scene must be no more than **20 ft (6 m)** wide.
  - Rule based on a minimum of **325 horizontal lines** of resolution (television quality).
- Beware of digital storage or projection: often the on-screen resolution is much lower.
  - An image that is sharp in a 1–2 in (2.5–5.0 cm) frame on screen may look blurry printed at 5 × 7 in (12.7 × 17.8 cm).
  - Match the recording resolution to the purpose of each camera.

##### Action Identification

- It captures **what happened**.
- Example: someone pins a $100 bill to the wall and watches. Another person comes in and the first closes his eyes. The bill disappears. He saw the second person come in, but did not see him take it: there is no proof.
- **Lesson:** the system must be **automated by means of a trigger**.
- Possible triggers: video motion detection, pressure on a floor mat, interruption of a photoelectric beam.
- Possible responses: raise the resolution, raise the recorded images per second, or bring the image to a guard's attention.
- That way the system records the important actions and captures useful evidence.

##### Scene Identification

- Each scene must stand on its own.
- Example: an officer sees on video an employee who has collapsed and calls paramedics. If he does not read the on-screen character generation, he may send them to a place that looks similar but is not the right one.
- A form of identification that is **crucial but often forgotten**.
- It is critical to **name and identify each camera and its view correctly** in the recorder/VSS.

#### 11.4.3 Analog System Components

**Three main components**

| Component | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Camera | Transforms the image of reflected light into an electronic signal. |
| Transmission cable | Carries the video signal from the camera to the monitor. |
| Monitor | Converts the video signal into an image on screen. |

**Other possible parts**

| Part | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Pan or pan/tilt unit | Pan: moves the camera from side to side. Pan/tilt: side to side and up/down. Today it is usually integrated into a single unit inside a dome. In most applications it is **more cost-effective to use several fixed cameras** than one pan/tilt. It used to reduce costs by saving cameras; today several fixed ones cost what a pan/tilt does. It is used mainly with zoom lenses, with an alarm interface, or with pre-positioning. It may require more staff time; do not add it without analyzing the application. |
| Controller | Commands a function of the pan, the pan/tilt, or the automatic lens. The application chooses the equipment, not the other way around. |
| Switcher | Shows several cameras on one or more monitors. It saves money: it allows more cameras than monitors. |
| Lens | If it focuses light onto a chip or tube inside the camera, it is a lens. There are many sizes and fields of view (width and height of the scene). The right lens gives the proper identification within the scene. |
| Video transmitter/receiver | Transmits by cable, telephone line, radio waves, light waves, or other media. Mixing several in a system is common. **Coaxial is the most common, but not always the best.** Example: a long exterior stretch in fiber optic within a system that mostly uses twisted pair (two-wire). |
| Amplifier | Boosts the signal over long runs. **Avoid it when you can**: almost no installer has the tools to balance it. If the coaxial run is so long that it needs one, it is better to switch to fiber optic, microwave, or twisted pair. |
| Video recorder | Stores the video on CD, DVD, hard disk, digital or network recorder, or other medium. The need defines the form of recording. Modes: time-lapse, event-triggered, 24 hours, 72 hours, and more. |

**Types of switcher**

| Type | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| Passive four-position switcher | The user presses a button and the image appears. |
| Sequential switcher | Changes automatically from camera to camera. **Dwell time** = time it stays on each camera before switching. It does not matter if the switcher has more inputs than cameras. |
| Quad splitter | Shows four images on one screen. |
| Multiplexing switcher | Interacts with the recorder to store more information per camera. It plays back streams separately or in quad format. Recommended upgrade for anyone with a sequential or quad. |
| Matrix switcher | Organizes large groups of video inputs and outputs and integrates them with alarms and display options. |

- With the basics of each category, a simple or complex system that works in the field can be designed on paper.
- **Key ideas:** (1) simplified, even the most complex system can be handled by almost anyone; (2) **the application governs the choice of equipment**.

#### 11.4.4 Network Video System Components

- Analog video has been left behind by network video solutions.
- In fully digital environments the term is **digital VSS** (digital video surveillance system).
- Sometimes also: **DIS** (digital imaging system) or **VIS** (visual imaging system).

**Typical parts of a VSS**

- Camera.
- Transmission media: coaxial cable, twisted pair, network cable, fiber optic, or radio frequency.
- **PoE** (Power over Ethernet) network switch; sometimes without PoE, with an auxiliary power supply.
- Recording equipment: recording hardware on a server and viewing platforms (computers and mobile devices).

**Other parts of the digital system**

| Part | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Switcher | Three types: (1) high-speed analog-to-digital converter (**encoder**) that receives several analog signals and delivers a single multiplexed digital signal; (2) high-speed digital switcher, which is essentially a multiplexer; (3) **DVR** (digital video recorder) with built-in multiplexer. Once the camera is connected to a PC, the video is stored on hard disk, CD, DVD, or the Internet; not always multiplexed, it can go as sequences or image files. |
| Lens | One of the few elements that is **not going digital**. Many cameras come with lenses optimized or specific for the application. |
| Video transmitter/receiver | **Ethernet** is the most common medium in digital, but not always the best. A long exterior stretch in fiber can be combined with twisted pair as the main medium. **RG-59/U, RG-6/U, and RG-11/U** coaxials can carry a digital signal with special converters. |
| Amplifier | In digital they are mainly **repeaters**. The signal is binary and is not amplified; what is maintained is the carrier. The signal is received and an exact copy is emitted with the carrier renewed. **Normal maximum digital cable length: 328 ft (100 m)**, except wireless, microwave, fiber, modified coaxial, or other long-distance medium. |
| Video recorder | DVRs arrived strongly at the start of this millennium, but in large applications stacking several DVRs was not a good solution. Fixed inputs: 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, or more cameras. Most are **not true digital recorders**: analog inputs and outputs. In large systems they are replaced by servers and massive digital storage. The **NVR** (network video recorder) ranges from equipment with a fixed number of channels (like a DVR) with a built-in switch to enterprise systems with a VMS (video management system) and servers. |

#### 11.4.5 System Design

**Simple design rules**

| Rule | What it implies |
| --- | --- |
| Keep the system in perspective | It can be as simple as cameras, cables, network equipment, server/recorder, and client/station. |
| Let the application choose the equipment | Vendors may promise that their equipment covers everything without asking. First the needs are defined with a complete risk assessment and the system, security, and operational needs; then the equipment is chosen. |
| Design generically | The best design does not specify brands or models; it stays open according to the requirements of the site or scene. |
| Design for the best option first | Design before setting a budget. Then calculate the cost and, if needed, remove elements. |
| Don't feel the system must be built all at once | Installation can be spread over several budget years. Use solid, proven equipment that will remain available for several years. Design with ample expansion capacity and easy upgrades. |

**Steps before putting the system out to bid**

##### Step 1: Write the Purpose of the Proposed System

- Monitoring the back hallway of a store requires little planning; covering several places in several complexes requires more.
- Over time, parts of the system may serve non-security purposes.
- Consider all possible uses. Example: some segments for security and others for traffic or product flow.
- That way the **budget can be shared** with other departments.
- Involve other stakeholders early: HR (human resources), risk management, operations, quality control, or marketing.
- The essential point: **put the purpose in writing**. It keeps the designer on course and allows logical changes during upgrades.

##### Step 2: Write the Purpose of Each Camera

- The security risk of each area viewed is weighed.
- **High-security area:** it may be advisable to link the video to an alarm: door contact, microwave motion detector, photo beam, or video motion detector.
- **Low-security area:** the alarm may not be needed; recording as reference backup may suffice.
- **Visible or covert?**
  - Visible: deters, to a limited extent, casual or opportunistic crime in shopping centers.
  - Covert: favors security and leaves people comfortable in their surroundings.
  - If a covert camera is used, the manager must **respect privacy rights**.

##### Step 3: Define the Areas to Be Viewed by Each Camera

- The actual view is defined by **height** and **cardinal points**.
- Example: camera No. 1 at 30 ft (9 m) height at the northeast corner of building 5, looking at the west door. Camera No. 2 above the west exit of the north hallway of building 6, looking east.
- Always include a **drawing or plan** with all the camera locations.
- Interior plans and exterior satellite images help confirm location and direction of coverage.

##### Step 4: Choose a Camera Style

- Based on sensitivity, resolution, features, and other design factors.
- Whenever possible, conduct a **live, on-site demonstration** of the proposed cameras for each location or type of location.

**Sensitivity**

- Minimum amount of light the camera needs to produce an image.
- The first thing is to analyze the lighting of the area:
  - Bright or dark? Constant or variable?
  - Are there lights that brighten the scene at night or on cloudy days?
  - Are there large windows? With heavy curtains? Are they closed during the day or opened according to the outside light?
  - Will there be a bright background that leaves people backlit (silhouette)?
  - What luminaires are there: sodium, metal halide, fluorescent, or LED?
- **Indoors:** sensitivity is not usually a concern, except for poorly lit areas at night.
- **Outdoors (several cameras):** a **lighting study** is advisable. It requires some training and a good light meter.
- If there is not enough light at night: **visible** or **infrared (IR)** lighting.
  - IR is not seen by the human eye, but many cameras see it as visible light.
  - Solution if the building's decor or the neighbors prevent putting up visible light.

| Sensitivity | What for | Price |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Full light | Interiors with full, constant (or little varying) light. | The cheapest. |
| Lower light | Light equivalent to dawn or dusk. | Medium to high. |
| Low light | Little or no light. | The most expensive. |

- A lens with a **lower F-stop** lets in more light.
- The supply of equipment for **no-light** conditions is growing.
- The conditions in which the system must operate must be assessed realistically.

**Resolution**

- Critical measure of image quality: number of **horizontal scan lines** (analog) or **pixel arrays** (digital).
- It must suffice to give enough detail to serve as valid visual evidence.
- Lens and camera are chosen for their ability to give the desired identification.
- **Analog:** the level of identification is expressed in horizontal lines.
- **Digital:** it is expressed in **pixels per foot or per meter at the recording point**.
- Thanks to HD (high definition) and megapixel, extreme identification is achieved, impossible with standard analog.
- Analog: 3 levels of identification (subject, action, scene). Digital: the book speaks of **seven theoretical views of identification**.

**Digital identification views (pixels at the recording point)**

| View | What is achieved | px/ft | px/m |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| General | Clothing and colors cannot be distinguished. No detail when enlarging person-sized targets. | 5 | 16 |
| Monitor | General flows of people or vehicles. No serious detail when enlarging. | 7 | 23 |
| Detect | Person-sized targets are detected, but not identified. No significant detail when enlarging. | 11 | 35 |
| Observe | Clothing and colors begin to be distinguished. No good detail when enlarging people. | 18 | 58 |
| Recognize | High accuracy to identify and separate known people. Good detail when enlarging in general. | 35 | 118 |
| Subject identification | Establish identity beyond a shadow of a doubt. Excellent enlargement. | 46 | 150 |
| License plate identification | Plates and similar-size objects with excellent detail when enlarging. | 70 | 231 |
| Facial recognition | Extreme detail. Excellent resolution for enlarging. | 88 | 289 |

- In the analog view, the book says that in subject identification the subject equals **20% of the width of the scene** at the recording point (minimum 325 horizontal lines).
- **Exam note:** the book announces “seven” views, but the list has **eight**. Memorize all eight with their figures.
- **Exam note:** in 11.4.2 the subject identification rule is **10%** of the width (2 ft in 20 ft). Here it says **20%**. Internal contradiction in the book.
- **Exam note:** 35 px/ft is equivalent to about 115 px/m, not 118 (outside the book). Use the book's figures.

**Features (Camera features)**

| Feature | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Digital Pan-Tilt-Zoom (digital PTZ) | A fixed megapixel camera appears to be mounted on a mechanical pan/tilt. It moves across the imager along a predefined path without moving the camera. There are also digital cameras with mechanical pan/tilt inside a dome. |
| Automatic gain control (AGC) | Internal circuit that amplifies the video to keep the signal at a fixed level when the light drops. It was created so that the image would stay stable when panning through shaded areas. **Every exterior camera must have AGC turned on.** Many CCD (charge-coupled device) cameras do not allow turning it off. Disadvantage: it **multiplies the noise by 10** and greatly degrades the image in low light. Advantage: it lets you keep seeing areas somewhat below the minimum light, which would otherwise look black. |
| Electronic shuttering | Manual or automatic. It compensates for changes in light without automatic or manual iris lenses. Like glasses that darken in strong light. It allows working in a wide range of light without auto-iris or taking high-speed images of fast objects. With IP and digital hybrid cameras, **each pixel** is analyzed and filtered: it attenuates only the very bright points without lowering the overall sensitivity, with or without auto-iris. Even so, test the stated specifications in the field. |
| Backlight compensation | Challenge: seeing someone in front of a bright background; typical case, a person in front of a glass door to the outside. Methods in the following table. |
| Autofocus | Camera and lens focus on their own when following an action or changing scenes. It fails if the target is among several closer objects. Dome covers can interfere. In general, digital hybrids and IP cameras with autofocus work well. The best equipment allows **turning it off** and focusing by hand in difficult cases. |
| Privacy blocking / image protection | Hides a specific area of the image (for example, certain windows) with a control or digital effect. See detail below. |

- **Exam note:** AGC sensitivity is not the overall sensitivity. AGC gives a usable but low-quality image. It is sometimes cited to make the camera look, on paper, more sensitive than it is.

**Backlight compensation methods**

| Method | How it works | Limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Auto-iris lens | The most common tool. Every analog auto-iris responds to the **average amplitude** of the raw video signal by means of a sampling circuit. More light on the CCD, more signal; less light, less signal. Properly installed, it keeps the image at an average of **1 volt peak to peak (1 Vpp)** with optimal light. It closes or opens in direct proportion. | It works with normal light. Because it works with averages, when looking at bright areas it compensates for the brightness and leaves the rest dark or backlit. Many designers do not understand it and apply cameras beyond their capability. |
| Masking | Digital interface built into certain cameras and controllers. It divides the image into a grid, sections are programmed to be ignored, and the grid is turned off to view without obstruction. | — |
| Electronic iris | **First real digital enhancement method** that eliminates the need for the auto-iris. It works with a true average of the signal: it **de-amplifies the super-brights and amplifies the sub-blacks**, giving an even 1 Vpp image. The person in front of the glass door is seen in detail, and so is the surroundings. Each manufacturer calls it something different. | — |
| Wide dynamic range (WDR) | Analog method that makes a **double scan** of the CCD: one overexposed image and one underexposed. The processor combines the best of each into a single image. In digital cameras it is called **multi-scanning**. | It does not work in all situations. |

- Few methods are more effective than **not pointing the camera toward direct light**.

**Privacy blocking: detail**

- A single liability case is enough to remember that there are people behind the pan/tilts and the zooms.
- Some cameras stray from their use and become “invasive toys.” Even in public places, small invasions of privacy grow, especially if the images are published.
- The mask is **mathematically anchored to the pixels**: it keeps working when the camera pans, tilts, or zooms.
- It is affordable. Options:
  - Locked position, or pan/tilt/zoom keeping the mask.
  - Flexible drawing: mask an area of any size or shape.
  - Blackout or blur the area.
  - Password protection against unauthorized reprogramming.
  - Temporarily remove the mask or see through it during playback, for valid purposes and under control.
- **Exam note:** the book says that it “cannot be reversed” right after citing the option of removing the mask during playback. It seems contradictory; it is understood that a mask recorded into the image cannot be undone.
- Other methods:
  - Send the VMS **two streams** from the camera, one with the mask and one without, restricted by permission levels.
  - Software that controls who accesses the original video.

**Other Design Factors**

*Environment*

- Does the camera need protection from vandals, customers, or employees? Must the housing blend with the decor?
- Interior housings: ceiling mount (in place of a drop-ceiling tile), hanging bubbles, stainless steel or bronze corner housings.
- A lot of dirt or dust makes the camera heat up and shortens its performance or service life.

| Exterior condition | What is needed |
| --- | --- |
| Winter below **35 °F (1.7 °C)** | **Heater** in the housing. The CCD withstands the cold, but zoom and auto-iris lenses slow down or freeze because of the grease in the iris, focus, and zoom rings. |
| Summer above **80 °F (26.7 °C)** | **Fan** or even air conditioning in the housing. |
| Direct sun | **Sun shield** to avoid excessive heat. |
| East or west orientation | **Sun visor** against the glare of sunrise or sunset and overheating. Often the sun shield doubles as a visor. |

*Mounting*

- If it goes up high, how is it reached for maintenance?
- Will the angle be so steep that only the tops of heads or the backs of necks are seen?
- Will it go under an eave or gutter next to a wall, or over obstacles such as air conditioning units?
- Is there room to open the housing from the top or is one with a bottom hinge preferable?
- Swing arm to access from a window or rooftop on high floors?
- In a drop-ceiling housing, is there enough space above the ceiling panel?
- **Objectives rule:** do not ask a camera for more than **two objectives (one major and one minor)**.
- **Sweep rule:** it must not auto-pan (physically or digitally) more than **45° left or right** of the center of its primary objective.
- Common mistake: expecting too much of a single camera or installing fewer than needed.
- **Analog:** the higher the risk of the area, the fewer objects each camera should watch.
- **Digital megapixel:** one camera covers what two or perhaps three analog cameras do with the same quality.
- **High security, 360°:** at least **four analog cameras**; the budget may force a compromise.
- Today there are **180° and 360°** megapixel cameras with software for individual or panoramic views.

*Power and enclosures*

- The availability of power weighs heavily on the budget.
- The typical: power and video cables separated by conduit up to the camera.
- Some IP cameras receive power over the same cable as the video (PoE).
- Indoors: housings for physical protection or aesthetics. Outdoors: special housings for weather or extreme environments.

*Transport medium*

- The security manager may find it hard to choose; he or she may prefer to leave it to the bidding contractor.
- **Coaxial:** sufficient for analog cameras; **it does not work for IP without a media converter**.
- **Distances of 1,000 ft (304 m) or more** between camera and control point: **fiber optic** is better, whatever the camera.
- Available methods (each with advantages, disadvantages, and costs): coaxial; fiber optic; twisted pair (two-wire); **UTP** (unshielded twisted pair, network cable); microwave and radio frequency; infrared transmission; existing telephone lines, Internet, or intranet.
- A system can use more than one method.
- **Encryption** protects wired and wireless transmissions against hackers and unauthorized onlookers, but it can affect video speed.
- The VSS can be connected to the organization's main network.

**Most common connection cabling**

| Cable | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Coaxial and BNC | The first **10BASE2** Ethernet networks used **RG-59** coaxial, similar to that of analog CCTV but with a different diameter and resistance. |
| Twisted pair and **RJ-45** | Common in Ethernet: **eight wires in four twisted pairs**. Normal maximum length: **328 ft (100 m)**. The categories (Cat) are defined in **ISO/IEC 11801**, *Information technology — Generic cabling for customer premises* (2010). |

- **Exam note:** the book expands BNC as “British Naval Connector.” The technical name is Bayonet Neill–Concelman (outside the book). On the exam, if it appears, accept the book's version.

**Twisted pair categories (Cat)**

| Category | Use according to the book |
| --- | --- |
| Cat-3 | **Should no longer be installed.** |
| Cat-5 | Still in use, even at new sites. |
| Cat-5e | **1000BASE-T** gigabit networks. |
| Cat-6 | Subcategories according to distance and transfer frequency. |
| Cat-7 | Up to **600 MHz**; meets the requirements of **10GBASE-T**. |

**Figure 11.4. Network types and required transmission medium**

| Type | Speed | Medium | Distance |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10BASE-T | 10 Mbit/s | Twisted pair (TP) Cat. 3 | 100 m |
| 100BASE-TX | 100 Mbit/s | Twisted pair (TP) Cat. 5 | 100 m |
| 100BASE-FX | 100 Mbit/s | Multimode fiber (MMF) | 2,000 m |
| 1000BASE-SX | 1,000 Mbit/s | Multimode fiber (MMF) | 500 m |
| 1000BASE-LX | 1,000 Mbit/s | MMF or single-mode fiber (SMF) | 2 m to 5 km |
| 1000BASE-T | 1,000 Mbit/s | Twisted pair (TP) Cat. 5e | 100 m |
| 10GBASE-LX4 | 10,000 Mbit/s | MMF or SMF fiber | 2 m to 10 km |
| 10GBASE-T | 10,000 Mbit/s | Twisted pair (TP) Cat. 6a to 7 | 100 m |

- MMF = multimode fiber. SMF = single-mode fiber.

##### Step 5: Choose the Proper Lens for Each Camera

It is determined by three factors:

| Factor | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Format size | Size of the sensor area onto which the lens focuses the light; it is measured on the **diagonal**. The lens format must **match or exceed** that of the camera. If it is smaller, it does not fill the sensor and the image looks like **tunnel vision**. Example: a 1/4 in camera requires a 1/4 in lens or larger (1/2 in, 2/3 in…). |
| Distance from camera to scene | Defines the **focal length** needed. It is measured from the front of the camera to the main subject, accurately. It is calculated with the Pythagorean theorem (example below). |
| Field of view / focal length | The height or width of the area viewed determines the focal length. It is calculated with simple math, a field-of-view calculator, a quick chart (cheat sheet), or a viewfinder. **The more area viewed, the less detail.** |

**Numerical example of distance (Pythagoras)**

- Camera on a building wall at **40 ft** above the ground.
- Center of the scene **30 ft** from the building.
- The relevant distance **is not 30 ft but 50 ft**.

```latex
d^{2} = A^{2} + B^{2} = (30 \times 30) + (40 \times 40) = 2{,}500 \quad\Rightarrow\quad d = \sqrt{2{,}500} = 50\ \text{ft}
```

- A = horizontal distance from the building to the center of the scene; B = mounting height; d = camera-to-scene distance.
- **Exam note:** the book prints “2,500, which equals 502.” It is an erratum from a lost superscript: it should read 50².
- Following the example: with a distance of 50 ft and a scene 20 ft wide, the lens focal length is calculated.
- **Megapixel:** more area can be seen without losing the required detail, or the same area with **two or three times more detail**. What counts are the **pixels per foot or per meter**, not a fixed number of horizontal lines.
- Tools: **rotating lens calculator**, often a manufacturer giveaway; calculators on manufacturers' websites; comparison images and tutorials with mounting and interconnection suggestions.

**Three main types of lens**

| Type | Field of view | Adjustment |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fixed lens | Only one. | — |
| Varifocal lens | Range of focal lengths and fields of view. | Normally adjusted and **refocused by hand**. |
| Zoom lens | Range of focal lengths and fields of view. | **No need to refocus.** Often motorized and remotely controlled. |

##### Step 6: Determine the Best Method for Transmitting the Video Signal

- It may be hard for the security manager; he or she may prefer to leave it to the bidding contractor.
- **Coaxial:** sufficient for analog; for IP it requires conversion electronics that transmit a digitally encoded signal over coaxial.
- **1,000 ft (305 m) or more** between camera and control point: **fiber optic** is better, whatever the camera.
- Methods: coaxial; fiber optic; twisted pair (two-wire); Cat 5 or Cat 6 network cable; microwave; radio frequency or other wireless technology; infrared; telephone lines, Internet, or intranet.
- More than one method can be combined.
- **Exam note:** the book gives 1,000 ft = 304 m in Step 4 and = 305 m here (1,000 ft = 304.8 m, outside the book). The key figure is **1,000 ft**.

##### Step 7: Lay Out the Control Area and Determine Needed Enhancements

- With the purpose of each camera (Step 2), **triggers and priorities** are assigned and the functions of the control equipment are defined.
- Example:
  - Camera 1: digital video motion detection, extendable pole, and alarm interface.
  - Camera 2: pan/tilt/zoom controller with pre-positioning.
  - Camera 3: alarm activation by door contact.
  - Manager's office in building 5: access to view or control all or part of the system, with a password.
- Triggers and priorities **automate** the system. It is inefficient and ineffective to have people continuously watching dozens or hundreds of scenes.

**Recording or storage system**

- **VHS and SVHS** tapes have been left behind by digital recording.

| Digital option | Compatibility |
| --- | --- |
| Simple DVR | **Analog cameras only.** |
| NVR (network video recorder) | Analog, IP, or hybrid cameras. |
| Server or network storage | Separate space on the organization's own network. |
| External storage via Internet | **Cloud storage**, off site. |

- Keys to digital storage: formats compatible among cameras, compression factors, and space required per image.
- “At the beginning it is all math; at the end, it is all the quality of the recovered image.”

#### 11.4.6 Equipment Selection

- The goal is not to choose a vendor, but to **define the operating parameters** that the application demands.

##### Camera: six main types

Knowing them avoids overspending on unnecessary functions.

| Type | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Standard analog CCD | Black and white or color. **The most common**; it works in every interior and in many exteriors. Analog, with or without digital effects. Resolution: **220 horizontal lines (very low) to 580 (very high)**. Sensitivity: **0.00046 foot-candles (0.005 lux), very low, to 0.929 fc (10 lux), very high**. Color ones are the most limited in low light. Hybrid solutions: IR sensitivity; color by day and black and white at night; or an **intensifier** between lens and CCD that amplifies the light tens of thousands of times. |
| IP cameras | Digital, black and white or color. They need visible light. Three styles: **standard definition, megapixel/high definition, and smart**. They measure their resolution in multiples of **CIF** (Common Intermediate Format). CIF ≈ half the average analog resolution in horizontal lines; **not recommended as a storage standard**. Standard IP cameras range from **1/4 CIF to 4 CIF**. 4 CIF = twice the horizontal pixels and twice the vertical. Power: transformer or **PoE** from the network switch. |
| Megapixel cameras | Sensor of **more than one million pixels**, normally minimum **1280 × 1024**. Great improvement over analog. |
| Infrared (IR) cameras | **They need an IR light source.** They are used where visible light is not an option. |
| Thermal cameras | **They need neither visible nor IR light.** With special filters and lenses they measure the temperature of objects and represent it with colors: **cold in shades of blue, hot in shades of red**. Long-range surveillance, e.g., ships in a harbor at **5 mi (8 km)**. Popular with police and border patrols. |
| Day-night cameras | All cameras give some day-night function, but these work outdoors and in low-light interiors. They almost always use a **removable IR-cut filter** (IR-cut or IR-blocking filter). They monitor sensitivity by themselves: by day, **color** image; at night, they remove the filter, let the IR through, and give a **black and white** image. |

**Smart cameras = edge devices**

- They use powerful processors and software inside the camera.
- Functions: digital motion detection, facial recognition, privacy blocking, digital pan/tilt/zoom, and more.
- They are called **edge devices** because they bring the computing to the outer edge of the system, instead of sending the information to the controller to be processed.

##### Lens

- After the camera, choosing the lens is the **second most important decision** of the project.
- It depends mainly on the **size of the scene** and the **degree of visual identification** required.

**Five types of lens**

| Type | Use | Range |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wide-angle | Very wide scene at short distance. | **0 to 15 ft (4.5 m)** |
| Standard | Medium scene at medium distance. It reproduces what the human eye sees at the same distance, except that the eye has a peripheral vision **about 2.5 times** greater. | **15 to 50 ft (4.5–15.25 m)** |
| Telephoto | Narrow area at long distance. Like a telescope: it brings the distant closer, but narrows the field of view. | **More than 50 ft (15.25 m)** |
| Zoom | Moving optics that give the wide-angle, standard, and telephoto views in a single unit. **Manual:** the installer sets the field and focus, and it stays fixed. **Motorized:** motors in the lens; the operator adjusts remotely with a controller. It has a **tracking mechanism**: a physical link between focus and zoom optics that readjusts the focus when going from tele to wide angle. | Entire range |
| Varifocal | Smaller version of a manual zoom; the view is fine-tuned on site. The installer carries few lenses for many scene ranges. | Short range around a standard lens (e.g., **8 mm to 12.5 mm**) |

**Varifocal vs. zoom: two differences**

- The varifocal lens **does not cover** wide angle to telephoto; only a short range on either side of a standard fixed-focal-length lens.
- The varifocal lens **has no tracking mechanism**: it must be refocused every time the range changes.

**Lens–camera compatibility**

- Cameras are smaller and have changed: light transmission, detail, size, electronic controls (moved from the lens to the camera) and cost.
- Incompatibility causes **physical damage** and image problems. Over time, the industry is eliminating it.

| Question | What it decides |
| --- | --- |
| Is the light fixed, slightly variable or highly variable? | **Fixed iris** (fixed-iris / fixed-aperture): no light control. **Manual iris**: opened or closed at installation. **Auto-iris**: a motor opens or closes according to the video sampling circuit in the lens or the camera. |
| Format 1/4, 1/3, 1/2, 2/3, 1 in or megapixel? | The lens format must **match or exceed** the camera's. The format **does not define the final size** of the image; the **focal length** does. |
| Is it a color camera? | **Color-corrected** lens: different cut and a special coating so that all colors in the visible spectrum focus at the same point. Many old black-and-white cameras use uncorrected lenses: reusing them causes problems. |
| C or CS mount? | See the mount table below. |
| AC/EC (video) or DC/LC lens? | See the table below. |
| Is it megapixel? | See the explanation below. |
| Is there IR illumination? | **IR-corrected** lens: optics and coatings that focus the longer IR waves on the CCD. |

**Auto-iris: cost and when not to use it**

- According to the book, auto-iris is now smaller, lighter and cheaper than fixed or manual iris; it costs “only a fraction.”
- In many applications it is prudent to use auto-iris on all cameras.
- Fixed or manual iris is preferable if the camera has a wide light range, automatic electronic shuttering or electronic iris.
- Risk: auto-iris can conflict with the camera electronics and cause **extreme flutter** (image flutter) or a **total blackout** (blackout).
- **Exam note:** in practice auto-iris usually costs more than a fixed lens (outside the book). For the exam, stick with what the book says.

**C and CS mounts**

| Mount | Sensor–lens distance | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| C (older) | **17.526 mm** | Many C cameras are still in use and C lenses are still sold. |
| CS (since the late 90s) | **12.5 mm** | The CCD moved closer to the mount; the CS lens sits less far into the camera. |

- **C lens on a CS camera without an adapter ring: breaks the CCD.** With an adapter ring, no problem.
- **CS lens on a C camera: focus problems with no solution.**
- Some cameras accept both. A **5 mm spacer** (C/CS adapter ring) converts a C lens for a CS mount.
- **Exam note:** the book says the CCD moved “15 mm” closer. With its own figures the difference is about **5 mm** (17.526 − 12.5), the same as the spacer. Probable erratum.

**AC/EC (video) vs. DC/LC lenses**

| Type | Meaning according to the book | How it works | Compatibility |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AC/EC (video lens) | AC = auto circuit; EC = electronic circuit (metric equivalent). | Original, outdated auto-iris design. It contains a video sampling circuit that controls the iris according to the signal level. | Older cameras accept only video lenses. |
| DC/LC | DC = direct circuit; LC = logic control (metric equivalent). | No iris electronics; the camera supplies the positive or negative motor voltages. Cheaper and more efficient. **It is the current standard.** | Cameras from **1995 to 2002**: accept both. Since **2002**: only DC/LC. |

**Megapixel lens**

- Main difference: the megapixel lens is of **much higher quality**.
- Every lens focuses the image pixel by pixel. A 4 CIF lens focuses on an area the size of a 4 CIF pixel.
- A 16 CIF camera (1.3 megapixels) has twice the horizontal and vertical pixels: **four pixels** where the 4 CIF has one.
- A 4 CIF lens on that camera focuses onto four pixels instead of one: the final image is **equivalent to 4 CIF**.
- Result: **technology, money and effort are wasted**.
- **Exam note:** the book says those pixels are “half” the area. With double the density on each axis, each pixel occupies **one quarter** of the area (outside the book).

**Specification sheets**

- Compatibility is ensured by checking the **specification sheets**.
- The camera's sheet says which lenses it accepts. The lens's sheet says whether it meets what the camera requires.
- You do not need to understand every process, but you do need the basic principles and the nomenclature.

##### Camera Formats and Lenses

- The smaller the CCDs, the harder it is to match lens and camera.
- Example: a site with 1/4 in cameras and 6.5–65 mm zoom lenses wants to add a 1/3 in camera with the same view.
- Key: know the **standard lens** for each format and calculate from there. The standard lens recreates what the human eye sees at the same distance.

| Format | Standard lens |
| --- | --- |
| 1 in | **25 mm** (reference) |
| 1/2 in (half of 1 in) | **12.5 mm** |
| 1/3 in (one third of 1 in) | 8.333 mm, rounded to **8 mm** |

**Format conversion example**

- A 1/2 in camera with an 8 mm lens is changed to the 1/3 in format with the same image.
- Step 1: convert to the 1 in equivalent. The 1 in is double: 8 mm × 2 = **16 mm**.
- Step 2: convert from 1 in to 1/3 in: 16 mm ÷ 3 = **5.33 mm**.

```latex
f_{1/3} = \frac{f_{1/2} \times 2}{3} = \frac{8\ \text{mm} \times 2}{3} = \frac{16\ \text{mm}}{3} \approx 5.33\ \text{mm}
```

- Best option: a **1/3 in varifocal** whose range includes 5.33 mm.
- Alternative: a fixed-focal-length lens close to 5.33 mm, in 1/3 in format or larger.

**Field of view and aspect ratio**

- The field of view is the final size of the area viewed, in width and height.
- **Analog:** images **4 parts wide by 3 high** (4:3). If the image is 12 ft wide, it is 9 ft high.
- **IP/digital:** works the same way, but the image is no longer fixed at 4 × 3.

**CIF and IP resolutions**

- Digital resolution is measured in **CIF** (Common Intermediate Format). All resolutions are multiples or divisions of CIF.

**Figure 11.5. Common IP resolutions**

| Ratio | Label | Horizontal × vertical | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CIF | CIF | 352 × 240 | 84,480 pixels |
| One quarter CIF | QCIF | 176 × 120 | 21,120 pixels |
| 4 times CIF | 4CIF | 704 × 480 | 337,920 pixels |
| 16 times CIF | 16CIF | 1,280 × 1,024 | 1.31 megapixels |
| 25 times CIF | 25CIF | 1,700 × 1,200 | 2.04 megapixels |
