| 36 times CIF | 36CIF | 2,112 × 1,440 | 3.04 megapixels |

- Film equivalents: **4 megapixels = 400 ASA film**; **6 megapixels = 100 ASA film**.

**What determines the final resolution (in order)**

1. The camera.
2. The transmission method.
3. The weakest link in the video system interface.
4. The playback capability of the storage system.

- The higher the resolution, the sharper the image.
- A high-resolution camera yields a low-resolution image if low-resolution monitors or high compression are used. **The monitor is rarely the problem.**

**Resolution losses in analog**

| Source | Loss |
| --- | --- |
| Analog video recorders | Play back an average of **325 horizontal lines**: the image looks good live and bad on playback. |
| Multiplexers | Digitize for functions and switching and convert back to analog: **up to 25%** of the resolution. |
| Coaxial cable | **10% to 15%** more from careless installation, poor connections and cheap cable. |

**Digital resolution and compression**

- Digital resolution is ultimately **unlimited** and very flexible.
- Compression reduces bandwidth and storage.
- Most effective and fastest-growing compression standard according to the book: **H.265**; good compression with little or no effective loss of detail.
- **Once compressed, the image almost never recovers its original quality or detail.**
- Resolution is not a big problem in most interiors and many exteriors.
- If you depend on recorded video: make sure the object is large enough, or that after compression there are **enough pixels per foot (or meter)** to recognize it for the original purpose.

##### Controlling Software

- Most modern systems connect to a **DVR (analog), NVR or server**.
- With a DVR or NVR, all the controls and functions come in the equipment used together.
- In large, complex applications, all the video is brought together on a network and controlled from a central point with software.
- Depending on size, a single PC may suffice or a great deal of computing power and software may be needed.

**Network concepts**

- **Node:** central point where equipment is gathered to enter a network path.
- **Network path:** central connection between all the field nodes and the **head-end** (headend or control center).
- In digital, because of length and access limits, most cameras are wired **to the nearest node**, not “home run” (direct run) to the central point.

**Key design questions**

| Question | What it defines |
| --- | --- |
| How many cameras per node? | Encoding, power and switching at each node, and the bandwidth from each node to the head-end. Depends on the size of the area. |
| Will any camera be viewed at more than one monitoring point? Does each point have its own control? | Cabling, bandwidth per data sent or received, and the requirements of the software and client stations. |
| Will there be integrated alarm triggers? | Door contacts, infrared motion detectors or photoelectric beams that bring a camera to the screen when an intruder or motion is detected. Advantage: the operator attends to other tasks until the alarm. Smart/edge cameras often have **alarm inputs on the camera itself**, which avoids running wire from the contact to the node or head-end. |
| Must the switcher activate other devices on alarm? | Buzzers or lights; also sending an email, text or image to a mobile device. |
| Will there be future expansion? | If a 16-input node has a small power supply in a wall box and six or seven cameras are added later, the supply, switching or box may need to be replaced. Planning can save **hundreds or thousands of dollars**. |
| What goes in a rack? A table or desk? | Secondary decisions about the type of monitoring station. |

- The final layout of nodes, head-end, switching, encoding, network and software **cannot be done until the camera views are fully defined**.

##### Recording Systems

- First decide whether the video will serve to **verify**, **prove** or **support prosecution** (prosecution).
- Video retention for prosecution **varies between states and countries**: consider this when choosing storage and data management.
- That decision defines the type of image, the quality or resolution and the images per second per camera.
- In court, **admissibility** may depend on quality, on how it was obtained and on **proof of originality**.
- Consult the **legal team** before installing.
- Modern storage comes down to: how many images, at what resolution, for how long.

**Numerical storage example**

- 1 camera of **1.3 megapixels**, **H.264** format, **15 images per second**, **2.35 MB per image**.

```latex
\text{Storage per second} = 15\ \tfrac{\text{images}}{\text{s}} \times 2.35\ \tfrac{\text{MB}}{\text{image}} = 35.25\ \tfrac{\text{MB}}{\text{s}}
```

- That is an **unrealistic** amount to store.
- To save space, reduce: the images per second; the resolution (normally with compression); the actual recording time per camera (with triggers such as video motion detection); and more.
- It can be useful to meet with the server or NVR manufacturer to calculate storage according to the final design.
- **The only reason to cut bandwidth and storage is cost.**
- Other factors that weigh heavily: lighting, scene complexity, percentage of motion over time and percentage of motion in the scene.
- There are compression technologies with noise reduction and dynamic adjustment that save bandwidth and storage.

**Basic recorder types**

| Type | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| DVR, digital video recorder (analog) | Captures an analog signal and converts it to digital. Stores on hard disk, CD, DVD or other media. Requires a lot of space, so it compresses with a **codec**: a compression engine or sequence that combines colors, lowers resolution or both. After compression, quality can be poor. **Test before buying**, especially playback and enlargement. |
| NVR, network video recorder (digital) | Accepts a digital or analog signal from the field. **Much better than the DVR**; controllable over the network; very sophisticated. Usually sufficient for systems of **several hundred cameras**. |
| Server/cloud applications (digital) | For sophisticated control software, analytics or complex integrations, and for very large systems. Compresses the signal and stores it in complex tiers on servers. Advanced searches and multiple visual outputs for fixed and mobile equipment. From variable frame rate per camera to full digital zoom and pan/tilt control. |
| Managed video systems | Storage and viewing **“for hire”** (for hire). Video stored and retrieved remotely from many mobile and desktop devices. Priced by number of cameras and clients (viewers), among other criteria already discussed. |

**Remote video management services (interactive services)**

- Video escort for workers after hours (escort surveillance).
- Monitoring of unattended deliveries.
- Alarm verification and streaming to view possible incidents.
- Also used in residential applications.

#### 11.4.7 Additional Design Considerations for Video Assessment

- The video assessment system is designed as a **component of the total intrusion detection system** (IDS, intrusion detection system).
- Interactions among video, sensors and the display system to consider in conceptual design:

| Interaction | What it covers |
| --- | --- |
| Site/sector layout | Sensors arranged so that assessment is possible at reasonable cost. |
| Video automation software | Automates tracking between cameras. |
| Video/sensor interference | That the video does not cause nuisance alarms from the sensors. |
| Command center | Central place to view, record, retrieve or respond to video. |
| Monitor location | Where the monitors go in the display system. |
| Construction | Common construction and installation requirements, techniques and locations. |

##### Site/Sector Layout

- A perimeter assessment system should show as much of the **clear zone** as possible, with both the inner and outer fence.
- Camera and lens must ensure detection and classification of any visible cause of a fence or sensor alarm in the clear zone, at all times.
- Criteria:
  - (a) Separation between inner and outer fence **relatively uniform**.
  - (b) Consider **minimum widths** of the clear zone.
  - (c) **Grade the terrain** or remove vegetation in the clear zone.
  - (d) **Adequate lighting** of the area.
- Departing from these criteria lowers efficiency and raises cost (more cameras and equipment to achieve the same effectiveness).
- **Exterior: one fixed camera per assessment zone.**
- **Interior:** evaluate the effect of using more than one camera per alarm.
  - Small facilities or lower threat: several cameras per alarm may work without duplicating viewing and recording equipment too much.
  - Large systems: **one camera per alarm** is simpler. Switching decisions are simpler and the operator concentrates on little video.

##### Video Automation Software

- “Stitches” camera to camera the logical path of an intruder or an activity of interest.
- The operator focuses on assessing, without having to choose which camera the event passes through.
- Requires prior programming, now much simpler.

##### Video/Sensor Interference

- Outdoors, camera towers are installed near the sensors.
- Tower height and location: so that **pole vibration from wind** does not generate seismic energy that sets off **buried cables**.
- Locate towers so that an adversary **does not use them to cross** the perimeter or the isolation zone.
- Power, video, sync and control cables: placed so that noise is not induced between video and sensor cables.

##### Command Center

- Central place where personnel view, record, retrieve or respond to video from one or more cameras.
- Range: from a closet with one camera watching a store's cash register, only for later investigation, to a facility with hundreds or thousands of cameras integrated with access control and intrusion detection.
- Paradigm shift: the command center can be **virtual**. Staff collaborate and manage everything from anywhere with a good network connection.
- The physical center may still exist, but network connectivity opens new scenarios of **redundancy and emergency recovery**.

##### Monitor Location

- On the control console, where they allow **effective and rapid** assessment without interference from other controls and system outputs.

##### Construction

- It is common to run signal and power cables and to modify buildings.
- **Combining** the sensor and assessment requirements (conduit, junction boxes) lowers costs and improves the design.
- Leave **room for expansion** in these elements.

##### Alarm Assessment by Response Force

- Security officers supplement video assessment with visual checks.
- They must be able to assess if the video is not working (maintenance, bad weather) or is not available (e.g., at some classified facilities).
- Whether by video or by officers, the alarm must be assessed **shortly after it is reported**.
- **Towers with officers:** effective if number, design and location give full visual coverage of the perimeter.
- **Patrols or mobile officers:** effective only if they respond in time (before the intruder or the cause of the false alarm disappears) and if **enough delay** remains in the system.

##### Integration with Safety Systems

- Today it is common to add cameras to detect critical safety events.
- It can lower labor costs, but also the **effectiveness of the security system**.
- **Large or complex facilities:** separate functions with different views or roles in the VMS client. That way safety events do not distract and do not **mask a malicious attack**.
- **Simple, low-threat facilities:** sharing functions may be acceptable, but it can still compromise effectiveness during an attack.

**Intelligent video**

- Also called video intelligence, video content analysis or video analytics. Rapidly developing market.
- Benefits: proactively reduce or prevent threats; shorten response times; reduce the operators needed; extract information for other purposes, such as **people counting**.
- **Sandia National Laboratories (US):** an operator probably **loses important information after only 20 minutes** in front of a monitor.
- Main use: high-risk transportation, government and retail (**Nilson, 2017**).

| Architecture | Where the intelligence is | Advantage | Disadvantage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Centralized | On the recording server. | Server almost always an open platform: adding functions is easy. | The algorithms consume a lot of computing: one server handles only **a few cameras** at a time. |
| Distributed | At the edge: network camera or video encoder. | Local analysis; **fully scalable**; can reduce bandwidth because the camera decides whether to send video based on its content. | — |

##### Legal Issues

- Privacy is a major consideration.
- **Do not place cameras** in locker rooms, restrooms or other places with a **reasonable expectation of privacy**.
- Hidden or covert cameras: legal in many cases, but consult an attorney about justification or legal authority.
- **Dummy cameras = legal liability.** They create an expectation of protection: someone who is attacked believes they are being watched and that help is coming.
- Accepted legal practice: **signage** for a video-surveilled area, often at the entrances, to minimize signs and to notify visitors and staff.
- For video to be **admissible as evidence**, depending on the jurisdiction:
  - Image quality, date/time stamp and percentage of the scene occupied by the subject.
  - An **eyewitness** may be required.
  - In many states, a **unique scene identifier** proving where it was recorded (distinguishing one office or hallway from another).
- **Digital watermarks:** ensure integrity and prevent tampering, with varying legal acceptance.
- Consult an attorney or the law enforcement authority in the jurisdiction.
- **Audio:** modern IP cameras capture it. Ask legal counsel whether it is legal and appropriate; it may vary by country.

##### Procedures

| Order | Criterion | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1st | **Sensitivity** | Needed for a complete video signal under the lighting of the area to be assessed. It must match the lighting design objectives, whatever the sensor. |
| 2nd | Sensor **resolution** | Defines how many cameras are needed along a straight perimeter. **The higher the resolution, the greater the spacing between cameras.** Object resolution is defined before choosing a camera, although it can be adjusted a little if there are few options. |
| 3rd | Camera **format** | Smaller formats = less sensitivity and less resolution. In exchange, lower price; but the camera is only part of the total cost. The format affects the field of view and the range of lenses available by focal length. Carefully evaluate special lenses with a nonstandard focal length. |

- Evaluate cameras under the **actual lighting** expected at the site.
- Experience from other installations helps narrow the options.
- Manufacturer literature **should not be the only criterion**: its specifications or test conditions may be unrealistic for the case.
- Whenever possible, **demonstration in the environment of use**.
- Other criteria: difficulty of maintenance; **MTBF** (mean time between failure); **total cost of ownership**, including power consumption; housing appropriate to the environment; manufacturer maintenance support; documentation.
- The documentation should include: operating, adjustment and maintenance procedures; theory of operation; block diagrams; schematics; manufacturer and commercial parts lists.
- Seriously consider **rejecting any product without that documentation**.

##### Acceptance Testing

**Incoming inspection**

- Every camera purchased is inspected, whether for evaluation or for final installation, with different parameters.
- Evaluation cameras: compared with others purchased for the same purpose.
- Cameras for final installation: conformance to the manufacturer's specifications, compatibility with the design criteria and **uniform performance across cameras**.
- The final inspection at the factory is not consistent; performance can deviate widely and the equipment can be damaged in shipping.
- **Burn-in:** operating the equipment for **a few hundred hours** continuously before installing it reduces maintenance problems at the perimeter site.
- Problems found are referred to the manufacturer **while the warranty lasts**.

**Installation and resolution verification**

- Exterior cameras: install per the manufacturer and **focus at night** with the same lighting as normal operation.
- If possible, evaluate resolution before buying.
- **Target method:**
  - Shapes of **1 ft (0.3 m)**: circle, square and triangle.
  - Painted **black on one side and white on the other**.
  - Placed at the **far end** of the assessment zone; the operator must recognize each shape. This quickly shows whether the resolution is sufficient.
  - Moved to light and dark areas: **black side for dark areas, white side for bright areas**.
  - The 1 ft target simulates the **cross-section of a person crawling**.
  - The size can vary with the threat; resolution charts also work in interior or exterior areas.
- Other aids: a large resolution chart in the area, or test targets such as the **Rotakin**.
- There are no accepted resolution standards for private integrators. The designer or the manager must **define the required resolution and specify it** in contracts and purchases.

**Test bench**

- In the laboratory it measures resolution, focus and sensitivity; it can be more cost-effective than performance tests.
- It is **not enough** to ensure performance in the protection system.
- Some cameras come prefocused, but in an environment different from the operating one.
- Afterward: indoor or outdoor tests and final adjustments of focus, sensitivity and field of view according to the actual light.

**Exterior lighting**

- Survey with quality light meters and a **grid**: e.g., every **3 ft (0.9 m)**, at **1 ft (0.3 m)** above the ground.
- Do it when installing the lighting and **repeat it every year**.
- Prepare a **preventive maintenance** program for lamps: replace all at once or as they fail, depending on size and budget.
- Exterior replacement usually requires a **bucket truck**. If the site owns one, the schedule is more flexible than if it must be rented. It is also useful for exterior cameras.
- Over time, the data make it possible to set a routine replacement cycle.
- **Turning the lighting on:** one photocell for all the lights; one cell per light, per side or per sector; or manual switching.
- The type of light affects the **CRI** (color rendition index). A poor CRI alters colors and harms evidence or prosecution.
- **Exam note:** the usual name is color rendering index (outside the book); the book says “color rendition index.” Same acronym, CRI.

**Interior lighting**

- Evaluate it continuously, with less effort than outdoors.
- There are lighting specifications for tasks such as reading, inspecting or office work.
- Almost always the work lighting is enough for video, but it must be **verified**.
- Watch for furniture or objects that move and create **shadows or blind spots**.

**Subsystem speed and vendor acceptance tests**

- Test that alarm detection and video capture are fast enough to capture the actual intrusion.
- Performance tests: alarms captured and reported **within one second**, camera switching times and recording times.
- It is strongly recommended to require **acceptance tests** from every vendor or integrator. They should cover:
  - Adequate resolution in the actual operating environment.
  - Recording speed.
  - Number of alarms that are acquired and stored for review in one second.
  - Related details, such as the light-to-dark ratio.
- Include the specifications and the acceptance test statement in the **terms and conditions of the contract**.
- **Withholding final payment until the acceptance test is passed** ensures that the project gets finished.

**Maintenance**

- With incoming inspection and burn-in, problems are minimized in the short term.
- **Camera adjustment** consumes most of the maintenance time.
- **Optical focus** has always been a major, slow factor in installation. It is caused mainly by day-night lighting changes and changes in the energy spectrum.
- Focusing **at night**, with the scene lighting and from the final location, is more reliable.
- Sealed environmental housings greatly limit this procedure.
- Many new cameras are refocused remotely through the web interface (GUI, graphical user interface) or the VMS.
- Best person responsible: **competent on-site staff** who understand the concepts of the original design and know how to diagnose electronic systems.
- Periodic maintenance requirements come from the manufacturer, in writing.
- A **nuisance alarm rate specification** is advisable:
  - It allows some nuisance alarms without penalty.
  - Value of occasional nuisance alarms: **they maintain confidence that the system works**.
  - Example: **one nuisance alarm per zone per day**.
  - Low enough to operate under varying conditions, but not so high that it creates a vulnerability: officers being tempted to ignore them.
- Investigate every recurring nuisance or false alarm to improve the system.
- After maintenance done by outside personnel, verify that everything works and **has not been modified**.
- Keep **equipment logs** of replacements and repairs.
- Spares: **10% to 20%**, especially of cameras, depending on budget and site size.
- If cameras are replaced with new or different models, test compatibility and performance and note it in the log.
- **Contingency plans** for loss of video (for different periods or in one or several places): assign a guard to the location until repaired, or deploy portable systems.
- Keep the manufacturer's documentation **on site and in a central file**. The same goes for modifications made on site.
- Log of repairs and adjustments for each camera = history per piece of equipment. It shows trends, recurring failures and underperforming equipment, and greatly reduces repair time.

#### 11.4.8 Evaluation of Video Assessment Systems

**Parameters that determine the effectiveness of the video assessment subsystem**

1. **Minimum time** between the sensor alarm and the display of the video.
2. **Complete coverage** of the sensor's detection zone (called the **assessment zone** when sensors and video are integrated).
3. Ability to **classify a 1 ft (0.3 m) target** at the far edge of the assessment zone.
4. **Vertical field of view** at the far edge of the exterior zone that spans the height of a standard fence (if there is one) and a person climbing over it.
5. **Continuous 24/7 operation.**
6. **Minimum sensitivity to environmental conditions** in all cameras.
7. **Minimum obstruction** of the assessment zone: trees, fences or junction boxes outdoors; furniture blocking the view indoors.
8. Integration of the field of view and the recording system that **shows the operator the source of the alarm**.

- The further the subsystem departs from these requirements, the worse the image quality and the more performance degrades.

**Classification vs. identification**

| Concept | Definition | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Classification | Accurately distinguishing an object in the image by category. | Human, animal, wind-blown trash or other. |
| Identification | Distinguishing between people. Some protection systems require it. | “It's John, not Jim.” |

- Both depend on **image quality**, which is measured by **video resolution**.

**What a VSS evaluation normally verifies**

- That cameras and pan/tilt/zoom controllers work.
- That date/time stamps and other on-screen text are accurate.

**Test targets**

- Simple geometric shapes: **circle, square and triangle of 1 ft (0.3 m)**.
- Their size is based on a horizontal field of view with a required resolution of **six horizontal television lines per foot** (6 HTVL/ft, horizontal television lines).
- That resolution is enough to **classify a crawling intruder** with adequate lighting.
- If the expected threat always presents a larger profile, a lower horizontal resolution is accepted. The targets work in both cases.
- **Black on one side and white on the other**: check resolution in dark and bright areas, respectively.
- A simple way to see whether they can be distinguished at the lighting extremes of the area.
- They work for **black-and-white and color** cameras.

**How they are used**

- They are placed at several points in the assessment zone (or across the field of view, in a surveillance system). The operator must distinguish them.
- **The more targets clearly distinguished, the more confidence** in the image quality.
- Locations are chosen that **do not make** identification easier: dark or bright areas, obstructed view or uneven surface.
- Valid for exterior and interior cameras.
- They test the black and white extremes of the subsystem against the background color of the area.
- Other quality aspects to consider: lighting, camera mounting, transmission system and integration of switchers and controllers to assess alarms.
- **Far-field resolution:** the far field is the most distant point and has **fewer lines per foot**. Verifying it confirms that the horizontal resolution holds throughout the zone.

**Recorded video**

- Live quality is only one aspect.
- Not all alarms can be assessed live: multiple alarms, a distracted operator or an adversary running across the zone.
- That is why a **recording and storage** system is needed.
- Recording and playback must be fast and detailed enough to determine the cause of the alarm.
- If the response will be an after-the-fact review, speed matters less, as long as quality is sufficient.
- The test targets also verify the quality of recorded video.
- Depending on the medium and the configuration, the recorded image **may have less resolution than the live image**.

#### 11.4.9 Maintenance

- If the VSS (cameras, recorders, monitors) does not work well, the organization is left:
  - **Vulnerable.**
  - With **delayed incident response**.
  - Exposed to **legal liability**.
- Camera maintenance is considered **before implementing** the system.
- Recommended:
  - Sufficient spares.
  - Trained staff **or** a service contract with a vendor or integrator.
  - Periodic testing and operation of all video equipment (the book expands on this in later chapters).

#### 11.4.10 Future of Video Surveillance Systems

| Area | Trend according to the book |
| --- | --- |
| Cameras | Improving in light sensitivity and flexibility, lens quality, resolution (especially megapixel sensors) and image processing. |
| Video compression | Will improve and greatly speed up data transfer between video devices and network equipment. |
| Networked audio and video | Rapid improvement with great impact on security. |
| Wired and wireless networks | More performance. |
| Servers and storage | Will hold more for less; the cloud will be even more affordable. |
| Analytics, video management (VMS) and intelligent video | Easier for the user to configure; new integrated functions and software scalability. |
| Controls and services | Remote and local controls and hosted services will continue to evolve. |

### 11.5 Communications and Annunciation Systems

- **AC&D, Alarm Communication and Display:** the part of the electronic security system that carries alarm and assessment information to a central point. There it is presented to a human operator.
- Challenge: presenting the data effectively and reacting in time.
- **Two critical elements of the AC&D:**
  1. Transport or communication of the data.
  2. Presentation (display) of that data to the operator in an understandable way.

#### 11.5.1 AC&D Attributes

| Attribute | What it requires |
| --- | --- |
| Robustness | A measure of performance in all likely environments. E.g., outdoor equipment that withstands large temperature changes without failing. |
| Reliability | Durable components with a long MTBF (mean time between failures). Less maintenance and more operator confidence. Reliable communication and display, with no loss of information. |
| Redundancy | Backup for critical components, because every electronic component eventually fails. |
| Timeliness | The speed of the AC&D must be a small fraction of the total assessment and response time. It must be a negligible factor in those calculations. |
| Security | The AC&D is a main component of the PPS (physical protection system). It must be protected against adversaries. |
| Ease of use | Show only the essentials, without overloading the operator. Efficient interaction and fast operations. Reduces training and retraining. |

- **Reliability:** no system guarantees 100% delivery. Modern equipment approaches it with:
  - Data checking and verification techniques.
  - Local and remote display of events.
  - Storage of the history of events and responses.
- Maximizing robustness + reliability + redundancy = minimum downtime (down).
- **AC&D security in practice:**
  - Procedures that limit who accesses the displays and the configuration.
  - Only authorized persons access information, components and wiring.
  - Protect the alarm communication subsystem against attackers.
- **Most important measure of effectiveness:** how quickly and clearly it communicates sensor alarm data to the operator.
- **On an alarm, the AC&D must tell the operator:**
  - Where it occurred.
  - What or who caused it.
  - When it occurred.
  - What response or action is required.
- The operator must know how to respond: through training and through system prompts.
- **Problem in measuring effectiveness (dual nature):**
  - Electronic communications are quantifiable: network topologies are modeled and times are measured.
  - The response of the human operator is very hard to measure.
  - For the human side, "soft" sciences are needed: ergonomics, human factors engineering and physiology studies.
- **AC&D subsystems (6):**
  1. Communications.
  2. Line supervision and security.
  3. Information handling.
  4. Control and display.
  5. Assessment.
  6. Off-line.

**Exam note:** the book announces that it covers all six in detail. In fact it develops communication and line supervision (11.5.2–11.5.3), control and display (11.5.4) and video assessment (end of 11.5.4). Information handling and off-line are not developed.

#### 11.5.2 Alarm Communication Subsystem

- **Function:** transfer data from one physical location to another. It goes from the point of acquisition (sensors) to the central repository (display).
- If the central repository has several computers or displays, it also moves data within that network.
- **Characteristics that guide the design:**
  - Amount of alarm data.
  - High reliability required.
  - Speed of data delivery (throughput).
- **Assured message delivery:** if a sensor activates, the AC&D computers must receive accurate data about that activation. It requires a reliable communication system.
- The alarm reporting speed is determined by: human factors + interaction between the AC&D and the assessment system.

| Operating model | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Proprietary, in-house | Ownership, operation, management and response with internal resources. |
| Remote central station | Alarms reach an outside operator, who has a list of agreed responses. |
| Hybrid | Internal response supplemented by an outside central station under contract. |

- **Reporting times:**
  - For human factors: no perceptible delay for the operator = a few tenths of a second.
  - AC&D–assessment interaction: only milliseconds, a small fraction of the total assessment time.
  - Communication is only one part of the total reporting time. That is why automation software is used to manage, prioritize and respond to incoming alarms.
- **Other design factors:**
  - Bandwidth of the physical medium sufficient to operate at full capacity.
  - **Protocols:** communication rules that the endpoints of a connection use when exchanging signals.
  - System speed dictates the type of protocol. Protocol overhead must match the type of data.
  - Balance channel bandwidth and protocol overhead to achieve the required speed.
- **Ideal vs. reality:** the ideal would be instantaneous and 100% reliable communication. It is impossible, and high speed + high reliability is expensive.
- Good design = a balance between cost and performance. Protocols are combined to balance speed, reliability and cost.
- Advances: alarm broadcasting by e-mail. Alarms not attended to within a predefined time are escalated to other people for response and notification.
- **Maximum-security or very complex systems:**
  - Redundant hardware against failures.
  - Automatic routing of messages through that redundant hardware.
  - Protocols that detect and correct errors and duplicate messages.

#### 11.5.3 Security Communications

- Task of the assets protection professional: address the relevant concerns when choosing a communications application. The growing variety of technologies complicates the task.
- **Strategies to mitigate damage to communications:**
  - Divergent routing.
  - Two physically separate paths for the cables entering the facility.
  - Hot/cold sites.
  - Replacement agreements.
- **Media of choice:** telephone, cellular, optical fiber, satellite and Internet/network connectivity.
  - Fiber: the cable preferred by terrestrial carriers.
  - Satellite: versatile, useful in developing areas, with explosive growth.
  - Network: is displacing the other methods.
  - All modes must be evaluated to achieve maximum reliability.

**The professional's 4 concerns in any communication:**

| Concern | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Integrity of the communications medium | Availability of the message path. |
| Integrity of the message | Complete, error-free transmission. |
| Timeliness | The data arrive within an adequate time. |
| Message security | Only authorized persons have access. |

##### Wire and Cable Communications

- **Alarms:** pair of direct current (DC) conductors, shielded or unshielded.
- The sensor manufacturer usually specifies connectivity and cable, both power and signal.
- **Cable gauge and resistance:**
  - Resistance varies directly with the length of the line.
  - It varies inversely with the diameter of the cable.
  - Resistance limits the effective length of the line.
- **Audio:** alternating current (AC) shielded twisted pairs. In telephony they are called voice-grade lines.
- Alarm and audio can travel together on the same shielded twisted pair.
- **Over the power line:**
  - Little used commercially: it is hard to pass the signal through transformers and quality is poor.
  - US: power at 60 Hz (cycles per second). Other countries use other frequencies.
  - A device couples the higher-frequency signals onto the AC cable.
  - At the receiver the frequencies above 60 Hz are separated out and displayed or recorded. The normal 60 Hz current is not altered.
  - Risks: a transformer can block the path, and interference can degrade or distort the signal.

**Exam note (outside the book):** strictly, resistance depends on the cross-sectional area, that is, on the square of the diameter. The book simplifies it as "inverse to the diameter."

```latex
R = \rho \frac{L}{A}
```

##### Optical Fiber

- **Definition:** strand of high-purity spun glass, about the thickness of a human hair.
- Transmits enormous volumes at the speed of light. Its capacity far exceeds that of a similar amount of copper.
- **Numerical example:** a system at 565 Mbit/s (565,000,000 bits per second) supports 8,064 telephone conversations over fiber.
- Carries video, data and audio, one at a time or simultaneously, in one or two directions, over a single fiber.
- **How it works:**
  1. A light source (laser or LED) introduces a modulated beam into the fiber.
  2. The beam reaches the other end with practically no attenuation or change.
  3. There it is decoded and the original information is recovered.
- **LED = preferred light source:** lasts longer without maintenance and is less sensitive to fluctuations in power, temperature and humidity.
- Carries voice-grade, video and digital or data signals.

| Differences of fiber compared with metallic cable |
| --- |
| It is not affected by EMI (electromagnetic interference) or RFI (radio frequency interference). |
| It does not conduct current or radiate signals. Very valuable where there are electrical storms and ground strikes. |
| It carries many more multiplexed messages. |
| It is much smaller and lighter. |
| It is flexible and can follow irregular paths. |
| It is not vulnerable to interception by acoustic or inductive coupling. |

##### Video Transmission

- There are products for sending video over DC lines, but they are not suitable for commercial use.
- Media: coaxial, optical fiber, standard telephone line and balanced twisted pairs. Wireless and network are growing.

| Medium | How it works | Distance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Coaxial | No extra processing between camera and monitor if the distance is short. | Typically 1,000 ft (305 m). Farther if amplified along the way. |
| Normal telephone line | Video → digital → audio. At the receiver the audio goes back to video. The operator amplifies continuously and it can pass over cable, microwave or satellite. | No theoretical limit. |
| Optical fiber | Conversion of video to an optical signal at the transmitter and reconversion at the receiver. | 1 mile (1.6 km) or more without amplification. |
| Dedicated twisted pair | No bridging, taps or other connections along the path. The equipment must do conversions, impedance matching and frequency compensation. | Good performance up to 4,000 ft (1.2 km). |

- Fiber + digital telephone signal = real-time transmission, because fiber carries more data and faster than the copper pair.
- Fiber supports color or monochrome video.
- Over the same fiber as the video travel the control data: pan/tilt, zoom and auxiliaries (housing heaters and wipers).
- Three functions can travel simultaneously, in two directions, over a single fiber.

##### Status and Alarm Transmission

- **4 types of installation:** loop, point-to-point, multiplex and wireless.
- They can use proprietary wired networks, leased lines and internal Wi-Fi networks.
- In any configuration, whoever gets to the alarm panel can disable the whole system. That is why the physical security of the panel or console is critical.

| Type | How it works | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Loop | Devices on a pair of wires that runs through an area, building or facility and reaches a control center. A signal indicates an anomaly in the loop. | May be enough for a small space or a single facility. | A short or a cut interrupts all signals beyond the cut. Cutting the loop at the right point can disable all the detectors in an area. |
| Point-to-point | Each sensor has its own pair of wires direct to the control center (hardwired). | A line failure affects only one detector. The attacker would have to identify and disable the cable of each sensor. | More expensive than the loop because it requires more cable. |
| Multiplexing | Several signals at once over the same medium: they are encoded into a composite signal on a single circuit. At the receiver they are separated and go to a control unit for recording and display. Medium: cable, RF (radio frequency), microwave or fiber. | May be more cost-effective over long distances. Reduces leased-line charges and gives more security. Large savings in wiring labor. | Expensive equipment: do not assume it comes out cheaper. If the link is cut, all its signals go down. |
| Wireless | See "Wireless Communications" and "Wireless Alarm Signals" below. | — | — |

- **Loop, recommended improvement:** encode the signal of each detector to know its origin at the central point.
- **McCulloh circuit:** partly corrects the loop problem.
  - Sends current from the control unit down both sides of the circuit to the point of the break.
  - With one break (open), all the devices on both sides keep working.
  - With two breaks, the devices between them are lost: there is no path to them.
- **Multiplexing, when it applies:** as the number of remote sources or sensors grows, the loop or one line per sensor stops being practical.
  - Always do a cost analysis for each system.
  - Because of the savings in wiring labor, it usually has an advantage over hardwiring.
- **Redundant path for multiplexing** (for large installations or high protected value):
  - Alternate multiplexed trunk.
  - Digital communicator over telephone circuits between remote transponders and console.
  - RF link between transponders and console.
  - Its cost must enter the cost-benefit analysis.
- **Figures:** 11.6 Multiplexing on a Pair of Wires; 11.7 Elements of Multiplexing (process sequence); 11.8 Simplified Time Division Multiplex System. **Pending:** the graphic content of the three figures is not in the extracted text.

**Two methods to separate signals that share a path:**

| Method | Principle | Key points |
| --- | --- | --- |
| TDM (time division multiplexing) | Each sensor has a time slot and transmits only in it. Same path, but not simultaneous. | Channels are sampled in regular sequence and then it restarts. No channel is monitored continuously. Sampling must be so fast that the amplitude barely changes between samples. The small time differences are transparent to personnel. |
| FDM (frequency division multiplexing) | Each sensor occupies a different portion of the spectrum on the common line. | They are transmitted at the same time, but their frequency makes them identifiable at the receiver. |

**FDM example from the book:** three transducer outputs modulate three subcarrier frequencies.

| Channel | Center | Peak deviation | Occupied range (own calculation from the book's data) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 400 Hz | ±30 Hz | 370–430 Hz |
| 2 | 560 Hz | ±42 Hz | 518–602 Hz |
| 3 | 730 Hz | ±55 Hz | 675–785 Hz |

```latex
f_{\min} = 400 - 30 = 370\ \text{Hz} \qquad f_{\max} = 730 + 55 = 785\ \text{Hz}
```

- Total common band: 370 to 785 Hz. The channels do not overlap.
- Unused guard bands between channels ensure separation.
- **FDM receiver:** bandpass filters separate the channels. Each goes to its discriminator, which demodulates and recovers the signal. The output ends at a display in the control center.
- **In both types:** the demultiplexer must operate at exactly the same frequency as the multiplexer.
- **TDM:** depends on precise timing. Multiplexers and demultiplexers must be exactly synchronized.

##### Wireless Communications

- **Requirements (4):**
  1. Transmitter that supplies radio frequency energy.
  2. Antenna that radiates that energy into the atmosphere.
  3. Receiver.
  4. Power for transmitter and receiver.
- The transmitter modulates (varies) a carrier wave to represent the characteristics of the source signal.

| Modulation | What varies in the carrier |
| --- | --- |
| AM (amplitude modulation) | The amplitude or range of the signal. |
| FM (frequency modulation) | The frequency. |

- The receiver tunes the signals, amplifies them and demodulates them. The original signal is reproduced on a printer or speaker.
- **Rule:** all wireless communication that is unscrambled or unencrypted must be considered interceptable.

##### Voice Radio

- The transmitting base station is usually in the control center, where the other alarm signals terminate.
- Security personnel use mobile or portable units.
- Units that talk directly to each other must be on the same frequency.
- A base with several frequencies can relay between units that do not share a frequency.
- Trend: communication between different products and protocols, radio to radio. Also integration of digital and cellular into a unified communications package.
- **What determines output power and cost:** transmission distances, physical barriers and interference in the area.
- Repeater stations or remote transmitters may be needed. They receive the signal, amplify it and retransmit it.

##### Wireless Alarm Signals

- **At the protected site:** alarm detection array + RF interface module.
- **The provider supplies:** network of RF receiver sites (base stations), linked by redundant leased lines to its central station.
- Some networks have thousands of base stations and cover almost the whole country.
- **Sequence:**
  1. The sensor activates.
  2. An omnidirectional antenna sends a coded alarm signal to all base stations in range.
  3. The station that receives the strongest signal passes it over a leased line to the central station.
  4. The central station sends an acknowledgment to the site panel over the same channel.
- RF or digital cable can be chosen. **Dual mode:** the alarm travels over both paths at once.
- The central station can poll the system at regular intervals to verify that it works.

##### Cordless Telephones

- Normally not used in security operations. But a security call to an executive's residence might be answered on one.
- Components: base and handset, both transceivers, linked by radio frequency.
- **Nominal range:** 700–1,000 ft (213–305 m). May be greater depending on the equipment or atmospheric conditions.
- US law prohibits intercepting them, but listening to the neighbor's calls is not unusual.
- Historically, a hook switch bypass turned a telephone into a hidden microphone, whether in use or not.
- **More security:** certain frequency ranges, security codes and multiple frequency settings.
- **Basic rule:** do not discuss sensitive information on any cordless telephone call.

##### Cellular Telephones

- In countries without a developed fixed network, several generations were skipped: digital cellular is widespread.
- **Principle:** the mobile user has a radio link, via a computer-controlled switching center, to the fixed network or another mobile.
- **Cellular concept:**
  - The service area is divided into cells grouped in clusters. Cells can have any shape and are not uniform.
  - Each cluster receives a group of frequencies. Adjacent cells use different frequencies.
  - A frequency is not repeated within the same cluster. The same pattern is repeated in the neighboring cluster.
  - The power level keeps the signal from reaching cells of other clusters with the same frequency.
  - Each cell has at least one cell site: low-power transmitters, receivers, control system and antennas.
  - Dedicated voice and data trunks link the cells to a computer. It controls the cells, connects to the fixed network, captures billing and controls the activity of the units.
  - **Handoff:** when changing cells, the call passes to the new cell on another frequency. This lets many users use the frequencies at once.

| Service | US | Other countries |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cellular in general | 800 MHz or 1,900 MHz | 900 MHz or 1,800 MHz |
| Analog: AMPS (advanced mobile phone service) | 800 MHz | Often incompatible with local technology |
| Digital: DCS (digital cellular service), also called PCS (personal communication service) | 1,900 MHz | 900 MHz or 1,800 MHz |

- The professional should not assume that service in one area is compatible with that of another. Multifrequency phones solve many of those problems.

| Interception | Analog (AMPS) | Digital (DCS/PCS) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| What the interceptor gets | Clear signal, usable immediately. | A bit stream that cannot be used immediately. |
| Residual risk | Total. | It can be recorded and converted to analog to reveal the content. |

**Signal compression:** two digital ways to make better use of the spectrum in cellular and PCS. They are incompatible with each other.

| Technique | How it works | Result |
| --- | --- | --- |
| TDMA (time division multiple access) | Divides calls into pieces of data. The receiver identifies them by the assigned time slot. | One cellular channel handles several calls at once. |
| CDMA (code division multiple access) | Spreads call segments over a wide range of frequencies. Each segment carries a code from the originating phone, which the receiver uses to reconstruct the signal. | Capacity 10 to 20 times greater than analog cellular. |

- **Uses of cellular in security (5):**
  - Backup for the wired network in transmitting alarms.
  - Disasters that interrupt the wired network.
  - Hostage situations.
  - Backup for routine security communications.
  - Mobile applications that control AC&D products and software suites.
- **Scrambling in cellular:**
  - Normally a scrambler is needed at the origin and another at the destination.
  - With a telephone privacy service, the customer needs only one.
  - **Procedure:** 1) from its phone with a privacy device, it calls a toll-free number of the provider, which has similar equipment; 2) the privacy network gives it another dial tone; 3) it dials the destination number.
  - The device at the origin scrambles the voice and the one at the privacy network restores it.
  - The voices of both parties travel securely over the cellular network to the privacy service's switch.
  - Two cell phones with the device can call each other directly and securely.
  - Only calls that need privacy go through the service. The others are made normally.
  - Supports secure domestic and international calls. The provider can assign a private toll-free number to receive secure calls.
  - There is a version for an office or home landline, compatible with fax.

**Exam note:** with the privacy service, the book guarantees only the leg up to the provider's switch.

##### Private Fixed Wireless Systems

- **Wireless PABX:** low-power transmitter that communicates with portable phones within a limited range.
  - Main advantage: two-way communication with employees who move around the facility.
  - Risk: as with digital cellular, it is intercepted with a receiver tuned to that frequency.
  - Transmission compression, such as TDMA, adds some security.
- **Exam note:** the book develops PABX as "private automatic board exchange." The usual term is "private automatic branch exchange" (outside the book).
- **Local digital fixed wireless:**
  - The US FCC (Federal Communications Commission) allowed providers to compete in local service.
  - The competitor is expected to lease facilities from the local provider that already has cables.
  - A large provider created a system where low-power transmitters serve several properties in an area. Each property has a device that sends and receives voice and data.
  - It eliminates installing or leasing wiring. Its security is yet to be evaluated.

##### Satellite Communications

- GEO (geostationary earth orbit) satellites are at high altitude and have inherent signal delay. It is a problem for some applications.
- Delay-sensitive communications benefit from LEO (low earth orbit) systems.
- **Footprint:** wide reception area. Any properly tuned receiver within it receives the signal.
- **Rule:** all satellite communication must be considered susceptible to interception.
- **Portable satellite phones:** today they cost about US$500 with the antenna in the cover. In 1993 they cost US$20,000.
- They can be compatible with cellular networks and switch depending on whether there is cellular coverage.

| System | Altitude | Main use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GEO + global network of earth stations | 22,300 mi (36,000 km), fixed position | TV, digital voice, fax, data and e-mail. |
| MEO (medium earth orbit) | 6,500 mi (10,300 km) | Dual-mode portable phones (satellite + cellular), phones on ships and aircraft, fixed phones in developing areas. |
| 28 LEO satellites | 480 mi (770 km) | Data messages for people and industries: monitoring of industrial installations and tracking of truck trailers and barges. |
| 66 LEO satellites | 421 mi (675 km) | Dual-mode portable phones, paging, low-speed data and fax. |
| 48 LEO satellites | 763 mi (1,220 km) | Worldwide mobile and fixed telephony. |
| 840 LEO satellites | 440 mi (700 km) | Characteristics of terrestrial fiber and coaxial networks. No delay: suited to delay-sensitive applications. |

- **840-LEO system:** transmits at high frequency, 30 GHz uplink and 20 GHz downlink. That allows small, low-power terminals and antennas.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** 6,500 mi is equivalent to about 10,460 km, not 10,300 km. For the exam, use the book's figures.
- **Transportation:** companies in the US and in developing areas equip trucks with two-way satellite messaging.
  - The message for the vehicle must be typed.
  - Advantages: the driver concentrates on driving and the message arrives even if the vehicle is unoccupied.
  - With GPS (global positioning system), the base interrogates a transponder and knows the vehicle's location at any time.

##### Wireless Interference

- Good reception depends on the equipment distinguishing the desired signals from the undesired ones and from noise.
- **Interference:** unwanted signals. It can appear in any radio system.
- **Most common causes:** signals from other transmitters, industrial noise and atmospheric noise.
- There are standards on frequency deviation tolerance and maximum authorized power. Interference can still occur because:
  - Another transmitter let its frequency drift.
  - Atmospheric conditions bring in a station of the same frequency from elsewhere in the world.

| Type of noise | Sources and traits |
| --- | --- |
| Man-made | Electrical transients radiated by circuits with electric arcs: switches, motors, ignition systems, industrial precipitators, high-frequency heating and other equipment. It affects AM more than FM: using FM is one way to avoid it. |
| Natural | Shows up as static. Example: an electrical storm. |

- **Noise in video:** momentary interruptions of the image, lines or waves on the screen, or "snow."
- **Effect of construction:** heavy steel and concrete limit the radio and video reception distance. Dead spaces are common.
- **Usual solution:** antennas connected to the receiver, or antennas that radiate from a loop or run laid throughout the building.

##### Microwave Transmissions

- Operates at "super high" frequencies, between 30 and 300 billion Hz (30–300 GHz).
- **Components (4):** microwave generator, power amplifier, means of modulating the carrier and antenna to transmit into the atmosphere.
- Supports one-way or two-way communication. The transmitting antennas can also receive.
- Because of the frequencies and powers used, it often requires an FCC license.
- Passes through rain, fog and snow. It is not affected by man-made noise.
- **Uses:** TV transmission, multiplexed telephone signals, multiplexed alarm circuits and high-speed data transfer.
- Travels in a straight line (line of sight). If it hits an object it is reflected, with possible loss of energy depending on the object's shape, size and reflectivity.
- **How to get around obstacles:**
  - **Passive reflector:** surface where the microwave bounces like a billiard carom. Any flat surface works, such as the side of a building, or a metal plate designed for it.
  - **Repeater:** another microwave station at a third point with a view of both ends. It also serves to extend the range. It uses the same hardware as a terminal.
- **Short distances:** it can be cost-effective in protection systems.
  - The transmitter is connected to an outdoor antenna with a low-voltage power cable and a signal cable. It requires no conduit or elaborate installation.
  - At the receiver: metal reflector or antenna, connected to the receiver with a power cable and a signal cable.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** in the usual classification, 30–300 GHz is EHF (extremely high frequency). SHF (super high frequency) is 3–30 GHz. For the exam, use the book's figure.
- **Exam note:** erratum in the book, it writes "line of site" for "line of sight."

##### Laser Communication

- **LASER = light amplification by stimulated emission of radiation.**
- Laser light is coherent (highly focused in one direction). It can be modulated to carry signals, like radio waves.
- **How it works:**
  1. The beam passes through a crystal. An electric field applied to the crystal modulates the polarization of the monochromatic light.
  2. The audio, video or data signal enters the transmitter circuit and changes that electric field.
  3. The crystal rotates the axis of the beam and varies the transmitted light. The beam flickers at a very high rate that corresponds to the information.
  4. At the receiver, a photodetector follows the changes in intensity and recovers the changes in the electric field.
  5. An electronic circuit converts the electrical signal into conventional audio or video.
- It is practically impossible to intercept the beam without being detected.
- Requires line of sight. Range of up to 4 mi (6.4 km) without repeaters.
- Without line of sight, the beam is reflected off one or several mirrors. Each reflection reduces resolution quality.
- Snow, fog and rain interfere. This is compensated for by expanding the beam.
- Does not require FCC approval.

| Comparison | Microwave | Laser |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Line of sight | Yes | Yes |
| Weather | Passes through rain, fog and snow. | Snow, fog and rain interfere; compensated for by expanding the beam. |
| FCC license | Often yes | No |
| Obstacles | Passive reflector or repeater. | Mirrors, with loss of resolution at each reflection. |
| Interception | The book does not say. | Almost impossible without being detected. |
| Range | The book gives no figure. | Up to 4 mi (6.4 km) without repeaters. |

##### Interconnection

- **FCC rule (US):** private systems may connect to the public telephone network if the equipment is safely compatible with it.
- The equipment must comply with **Part 68** of the FCC rules.
- When connecting, the telephone company must be notified of the FCC registration number and the ringer equivalency rating of the equipment.
- **Bundling:** one provider, such as a cable company, delivers TV, network and voice over a single path. It is very common.
  - For conventional digital alarm communicators to work, the panel must be compatible with the carrier's system.
  - The cable company must provide a modem interface, called VoIP (voice over Internet Protocol).
  - VoIP converts the analog alarm signal to digital. It is converted back to analog on arrival at the monitoring company.
  - This configuration has a higher probability of failure: consider alternate communication paths.
- **Copper:** traditional telephony uses copper pairs because they support DC and AC voltage.
  - Much of that infrastructure fails, is expensive to maintain and does not support new network technology.
  - That is why digital alarm communicators/dialers are quickly becoming obsolete.

##### Communications Security: Line Protection

- The switched telephone network, dedicated telephone lines or proprietary circuits can be used.
- **Physical measures:**
  - Exterior wiring: underground.
  - Interior wiring: in conduits.
  - Ask the telecommunications provider for an underground service entrance.
  - In very vulnerable situations, take the service drop from a farther pole, not the nearest one, to conceal the cable path.
- If a conventional telephone path is used, such as a digital dialer, also use an alternate path: cellular or radio.
- Local and long-distance service is open to competition. The large networks are very reliable, but there have been several large-scale outages.
  - Thoroughly evaluate the reliability of the proposed provider.
  - Splitting the needs between two providers gives some assurance of continuity.

##### Line Supervision

- Every wired system must have line supervision: it checks the circuits automatically and immediately signals line faults.
- **Simplest form:** end-of-line resistor (EOL). It introduces a constant, measurable current.
  - A variation beyond a set threshold generates an alarm.
  - Normally detects three faults: open circuit (broken connection), ground and wire-to-wire short.
  - An attempt to tap or defeat the circuit usually causes a variation and is detected.
  - **Limit:** a tampering that barely changes the circuit value is not detected by basic supervision.
- **High-value assets or information, other methods (4):**
  1. Reduce the variation allowed in the circuit value.
  2. Quasi-random pulses that the control equipment must recognize.
  3. Changes in the transmission frequency.
  4. IP network communicators with encryption. They are becoming the preferred method for communicating and monitoring alarms off-premises.

| Communication | What someone who accesses the line gets |
| --- | --- |
| Analog | Mere audio reproduction of the source signal (voice, instrument or other tone). It sounds intelligible. That is why devices that alter intelligibility were created. |
| Digital | The voice is converted to a binary stream (zeros and ones). Only bits are obtained, not immediately intelligible. It could be recorded and converted to analog, but the content may still be protected by encryption. |

##### Scramblers

- What travels over copper on the telephone network can be intercepted at any point in the circuit.
- **Scrambler:** tool that disguises information so that it is unintelligible to a listener.
- **Selection criterion:** the importance of the information and the skill of whoever might intercept it.
- Voice has two modifiable traits: frequency (pitch) and amplitude (loudness). Scramblers always distort the frequency.

| Type | How it works | Advantages | Weaknesses |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Frequency inverter, Figure 11.9 | The simplest: inverts the frequency content of the voice. | Low cost. Tolerates poor channel conditions. | A trained listener understands the voice. It is a single-code device: anyone with a similar scrambler can break it. |
| Band splitter, Figure 11.10 | Extension of the inverter: divides the voice band into smaller bands, which are inverted and swapped before transmission. | With 5 bands there are in theory 3,080 combinations. | 90% of those combinations are no more secure than an inverter. A trained listener obtains a lot of information. |
| Rolling band splitter | Continuously changes how the bands are swapped, following a predetermined pattern. | Normally cannot be broken by trial and error of combinations. | A trained listener still obtains some information. |
| Frequency or phase modulator | Frequency: inverts the spectrum and shifts it continuously following a pattern. Phase: the same, but changes the phase of the wave. | Similar to the rolling band splitter, but usually more secure: the pattern changes more often. | A trained listener can still extract some intelligence. |
| Masking | Modifies the amplitude by adding another signal in the voice band. It is removed at the receiver. The mask can be a single tone, switched tones or RF noise. | Very effective at destroying or interfering with part of the syllabic content. | Alone, it is not very secure: the voice is understood fairly easily through the mask. |

- **Key combination:** masking + band splitter, or masking + frequency or phase modulator = high degree of security.
- **Pending:** the graphic content of Figures 11.9 (Frequency Inverter) and 11.10 (Band Splitter) is not in the extracted text.

**Rolling codes:**

- Rolling band splitters, frequency and phase modulators and many scramblers with masking change their format periodically and in a predetermined way.
- **Usual rate of change:** between 100 times per second and once every 10 seconds.
- **Keystream:** the electronic control signals that change the format.
  - Ideally it would be totally random, so that no one can duplicate it.
  - But it must be predetermined, because the receiving scrambler has to reproduce an identical one.
  - It has a fixed pattern length and then repeats. Typical length: between 15 bits and several billion bits.
- **Pseudo-random generator:** electronic shift register with well-connected feedback. It makes it cheap and easy to generate long keystreams.
- Keystream length, with N = number of register stages:

```latex
L = 2^{N} - 1
```

- **Book example:** 23-stage register → keystream of 8,388,607 bits. Changing 10 times per second, it repeats every 9.7 days.

```latex
L = 2^{23} - 1 = 8\,388\,607 \text{ bits} \qquad T = \frac{8\,388\,607}{10\ \text{s}^{-1}} \approx 838\,861 \text{ s} \approx 9.7 \text{ days}
```

- **Second trait of the generator:** many connection configurations, each with a completely different maximum-length keystream. The 23-stage register has 364,722 configurations or codes.
- A scrambler cannot decode what is transmitted by another programmed with different codes.
- **Exam note:** the extracted text of the book shows "2N-1". It must be read as 2 to the power N, minus 1: that is what matches 8,388,607.
- **Cost of scramblers:** all of them degrade voice quality.
  - It is worse when the link is already degraded.
  - Those with the highest security demand more link performance than those with lower security.
- **Digitized communication:** can be encrypted with any data encryption method. With a fast computer, the conversation is in real time and the parties do not notice the countermeasures.

#### 11.5.4 Alarm Control and Display

- **Control and display subsystem:** gives information to the security operator and lets the operator enter commands that affect the operation of the AC&D.
- **Goal:** support rapid assessment of alarms.
- The alarm display equipment is the operator's console. It receives the information from the sensors.
- **Concerns in console design (4):**
  1. What information is shown to the operator.
  2. How it is presented.
  3. How the operator communicates with the system.
  4. How the equipment is laid out at the operator's station.
- **Effective subsystem:**
  - Presents the information quickly and clearly.
  - Responds quickly to commands.
  - Does not overwhelm with detail: only what is necessary.
  - Offers only the controls pertinent to the context of the current display.
- **Information that helps zone security (10):**
  - Zone status: access, secure, alarm or tamper (access/secure/alarm/tamper).
  - Location of the zone.
  - Time of the alarm.
  - Hazards or special materials associated with the zone.
  - Instructions for special actions.
  - Phone numbers of the people to call.
  - Maps of the zone.
  - Video, where available.
  - Retrieval of video before and after the alarm (pre- and post-alarm video call up).
  - Status-change icons with color and animation, to make use easier.
- It is necessary to analyze how the operator is alerted that action is needed: type of display equipment, format and visual features of the information, and design of the input equipment.

##### Ergonomics: Human Factors

- The subsystem must be designed to serve the human operator.
- Normal temperature, humidity, noise and comfort promote effectiveness and reduce frustration and fatigue.
- Adjustable lighting to see the video monitors well.
- The console must facilitate the system–operator exchange: alarm reports, status indications and commands.
- The data must show their interrelationships. Human–machine input techniques must limit errors.
- **Workspace design considers (3):**
  - What the operator must see: people, equipment, displays and controls.
  - What the operator must hear: other operators, communication equipment and warning indicators.
  - What the operator must reach and manipulate: hand or foot controls and communication equipment.
- Around the operator there are zones of differing accessibility and visibility. All displays must be nearly perpendicular to the line of sight and visible from the normal position.

| Area (Figure 11.11) | What is placed there | Movement required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Primary (primary interface area) | The most important indications and inputs, already prioritized. | Little eye or head movement: within a 30-degree viewing cone. |
| Secondary | Frequently used operating displays. | Move the eyes, not the head. |
| Beyond secondary | Rarely used support displays, such as backup systems and power indicators. | The book does not specify. |

- **Pending:** the graphic content of Figure 11.11 (Placement of Operator Controls in an AC&D Console) is not in the extracted text.
- **Audible signals:** the operator does not always look at the panel, so they announce significant status changes.
  - Different tones and volumes distinguish classes of alarm: security, safety or maintenance.
  - Computerized voice can also be used.
  - Few types of audible signal, and clearly distinguishable in the complex sound environment of the control room.
- **Console layout:** displays in the center, with easy-to-identify controls above, below or around them.
  - Clarity: orderly labeling, color coding, well-spaced grouping and shape coding.
  - Each control near its display: less searching and less eye movement.
  - Touch panels put the controls on the display and eliminate many other devices.
- **Visual signals:** flashing lights or blinking messages for significant information.

| Traffic-light color | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Red | Alarm / action |
| Yellow | Caution / abnormal |
| Green | Proceed / normal |

- **Support equipment:** located according to its importance and frequency of use.
  - Communication equipment needs adequate space on the console: microphones, telephones, additional video monitors and controls.
  - What is not needed for display and control goes outside the operator's immediate space.
- **Separate room** for computers and automatic control circuits: video switching and communications electronics, except microphones and controls. Advantages:
  - More space for maintenance personnel.
  - Maintenance does not disturb operators.
  - Less distracting noise, such as from fans.
  - Better protection of the equipment against tampering.
  - Environmental conditions suited to the equipment. E.g., cooling and humidity that would be uncomfortable for operators.
- **Several operators at once:** consider the operator–equipment interrelationships.
  - Duplicate the essential equipment for each operator.
  - Share the secondary or infrequently used equipment.

##### Ergonomics: Graphical Displays

- Well-designed GUIs (graphical user interfaces) improve alarm display. Poorly designed ones quickly overwhelm the operator.
- **Good graphical annunciator:** few functions. It limits the ways of showing information and restricts the operations allowed.

| Element | Rules from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Windows | Maximum 3 at a time. One full-screen with the overview of system status. Another smaller one with subordinate information, no larger than half the screen. A third with menus or other controls. The operator should not have to resize or move windows. |
| Menus | At the top of the window; can be nested. Maximum 9 items and 3 levels. Common commands do not go in menus, but as buttons. |
| Buttons | Work as push buttons. Only commands valid in the current context. Sensor or map icons can act as buttons. Grouped in bars. Maximum 9 visible, with a text label indicating their function. |
| Maps | Scanned from paper or electronic graphics. The best are stylized drawings without excessive detail. Small scale of approximately 1:5,000. They must be interactive: show the sensors and allow operating them from the graphic. |
| Sensor icons | Consistent graphics, sizes and colors. Group sensors into a single icon when possible. Maximum 50 sensor or group icons per map. The group icon shows the status of the worst sensor: if one is in alarm, the group shows alarm. |
| Text | Dedicated areas with sensor descriptions. Only what is vital; the details go in subordinate windows. Quick help must be available. |
| Color | Sparingly. Do not rely on color: 10% of the population has some degree of color blindness. Maximum 7 colors. Menus, buttons and backgrounds in consistent tones, often gray. Maps in black and white or with low-saturation colors. Red, yellow and green reserved for sensor status. |

- **Why short menus:** in deeply nested menus the user gets lost. A short menu reduces search time. Complicated ones intimidate novices and annoy experts.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** in cartography, 1:5,000 is considered large scale (a lot of detail). The book calls it "small-scale." For the exam, remember the figure 1:5,000.

**Design philosophy: "operator first."** Rules (7):

1. Minimize the actions per command. One important command = one click or a single key.
2. Only operations valid in context. E.g., not being able to grant access to a sensor that is already in access.
3. Guide the operator through complex operations. Context-based command selection directs without taking control away.
4. Do not interrupt operations in progress. If the operator is assessing an alarm, the new alarm does not replace the screen: a nonintrusive notification arrives and the operator decides whether to abort.
5. Do not be annoying: avoid loud, continuous alarms or bright, flashing displays.
6. Every command must be doable in several ways: menu, button and key. The user chooses.
7. Screens customized per user: nothing is omitted, only the presentation is adapted to each one.

- **Exam note:** the book recommends flashing lights and messages for what is significant, and then asks to avoid bright, flashing displays. It is reconciled this way: flashing to highlight, never continuous or annoying.
- **Central message:** the AC&D exists to improve the security of the site. If it fails at that, it fails as a system.
  - Elaborate graphics do not save an ineffective system.
  - An easy-to-use system is more likely to succeed than an unnecessarily complex one.

##### Video Alarm Assessment

| Concept | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| Assessment | Associates the immediate capture of an image with a sensor alarm to decide the response. |
| Surveillance | Collects video without associated sensors. |

- **Video alarm assessment system:** cameras in the assessment areas, monitors at the local end, and transmission, switching and recording systems.

| Main component (7) | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Camera and lens | Convert the optical image of the scene into an electrical signal. |
| Lighting | Illuminates the alarm location evenly and with sufficient intensity for camera and lens. |
| Transmission | Connects the remote cameras with the local monitors. |
| Video switching | Connects the signals of several cameras to monitors and recorders. |
| Video recording | Produces a record of the event. |
| Monitors | Convert the electrical signal into a visual scene. |
| Video controller | Interface between the alarm sensor system and the assessment system. |

- **The required resolution depends on (4):** the expected threat, the expected tactics, the asset to be protected and the use that will be made of the video.
- Assessment performance must support the objectives of the protection system.
- The assessment subsystem is designed as a component of the intrusion detection system (IDS).
- The interactions among video, intrusion sensors and display must be considered.
- **Book summary on the AC&D:** it is key to a successful and timely response.
  - It controls the flow of information from sensor to operator and shows it quickly and clearly.
  - It collects alarm data, presents the information and lets the operator give commands.
  - Goal of the display: rapid assessment of alarms.
- **Exam note:** the close says that operator loading was covered. In these pages it appears only implicitly: do not overload the operator with data.

### 11.6 Trends and Issues in Electronic Systems Integration

The book lists 12 trends:

| Trend | Key idea |
| --- | --- |
| Big data | One of the strongest technology trends: it affects everything from the economy to the climate and the forecasting of flu outbreaks. In security, it helps process large volumes of video and of alarm inputs and outputs. Analyzing large data sets will be the basis of competition, productivity and innovation. |
| Data storage technologies | Exponential growth. From early magnetic storage to micro-drives, SD cards, Blu-ray discs, xD cards, WMV-HD, HD-TV and cloud backup. |
| Analytics | Ability to distinguish patterns in the data. Requires a lot of computing and depends on algorithms and software. "Perfect storm": big data + storage + optimization and analysis software. |
| Standards | Pressure on manufacturers to meet communication standards and database architecture standards, and thus integrate into a global security control system. Video and building management systems lead, with standards already consolidated. Leading devices will perform better and meet standards. |
| Data aggregation | Managing diverse databases from a single interface, thanks to standards. Makes it possible to take advantage of legacy control systems. Proprietary protocols will cease to thrive. Compatibility with different operating systems is crucial for reliability and performance. |
| NFC (near field communication) | Lets smartphones and other devices communicate by radio. The standard covers protocols and is based on existing RFID (radio frequency identification) standards. |
| Smart cards | More storage allows data to be kept on the credential itself. Miniaturization will bring new applications and perhaps more attacks on the card and its electronics, in hardware and software. The industry will have to adopt broad standards of functionality and security. |
| Smart cards + biometrics | The combination usually yields a trusted credential. Source cited: Smart Card Alliance Physical Access Council (www.smartcardalliance.org). It will probably change the identification landscape. |
| Managed services | Cloud to store and retrieve data and applications, and remote third-party services for a recurring fee. Some organizations are not attracted to it; others value reducing staff or not buying software. |
| Product hybridization | Formerly independent products merge. E.g., smoke detectors with mini-cameras, microphones and inert gas monitoring. Better communications + standardization = more hybrid products. |
| Robotics | First, mechanical arms for repetitive tasks in manufacturing. Later, "humanization" for tasks with human attributes. Advances in health care and services for the elderly will probably lead to robotic security functions. |
| E-learning training tools | New suite of training software under continuous improvement, for users of legacy and new systems, with case-specific training. |

- **NFC, details:**
  - Driven by the proliferation of smartphones and mobile devices.
  - Not all mobile networks are ready to commercialize it, but new phones already come with the hardware.
  - Platforms are emerging to track all kinds of goods.
  - **Uses:** payments, marketing and promotion, transportation and ticketing, education, health care, municipal services, remote work and connectivity, and identification (IDs).
  - There are already smart card integration kits for contactless cards, and more adaptations are coming.
  - Goal for businesses: secure access from mobile phones.
- **Managed services, available applications:**
  - Access control.
  - ID and credentialing.
  - Video viewing.
  - Virtual guard tours.
  - Unattended delivery.
  - Video escort.
  - Remote opening of perimeter checkpoints.
  - In some cases: backup after evacuating the site, or coverage of posts after hours without on-site staff.
- **E-learning, benefits:**
  - Create, publish and manage repeatable enterprise-wide training and site-level awareness programs.
  - Reduces the costs of training operators and administrators.
