  - Improves compliance training in regulated industries.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** WMV-HD and HD-TV are video formats, not storage media. The book mixes them into the list.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** the Smart Card Alliance changed its name to Secure Technology Alliance in 2017. For the exam, use the book's name.

## Capítulo 12 — Security Officers and the Human Element (págs. 417–470)

**Central idea:** Despite the technology boom, the security officer remains an essential piece of integrated asset protection; the key is combining people, processes and technology. The chapter covers when personnel are needed, which force model to use, their functions and roles, uniforms and weapons, selection, training, force management (orders, schedules, supervision, evaluation) and how to make the most of the human element.

- Evolution of the name: *watchmen* → *guards* → today **protection officer** or **security officer** (preferred term).
- The name change reflects the greater responsibility, training and professionalism demanded.

### 12.1 Security Officer Utilization Growth

| Year | Data (US) | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2002 | After Sept. 11, 2001 the security workforce grew **13%**; more than **1 million** jobs | U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS), 2007 |
| 2008 | **1.1 million**; \~**55%** in *contract agencies*; \~**6 of every 7** full-time | BLS |
| 2019 | Fairly stable figures | BLS, 2019 |

- Many of those who were not full-time were *moonlighting law enforcement officers*.
- The growth of private police and private security is similar worldwide.
- **New settings** (sometimes replacing police or soldiers):
  - patrolling urban centers or military installations;
  - guarding heavily trafficked areas: stadiums, shopping centers, large apartment complexes;
  - transporting prisoners or detainees.
- Growth in regions with civil unrest and war: energy exploration companies face armed adversaries and use heavily armed forces.
- **Legal cannabis** (medical and adult-use): expanding global market. Requires officers at:
  - cultivation centers;
  - manufacturing and production plants;
  - dispensaries;
  - secure transport.
- In cannabis, regulation often **requires contract officers**.
- More diverse services: communications, customer service, transportation and other nontraditional functions.
- Consequence: more responsibilities and a need for higher-quality personnel.
- With technology, fewer officers cover more tasks.
- Trend: better-trained and better-educated officers, who give a higher return on investment (ROI).

### 12.2 Contemporary Challenges

- *Security in 2025* (Fennelly, Beaudry and Perry, 2017) points to three growing challenges closely tied to officer services:
  - climate and population insecurity on a global scale;
  - advances in *machine learning* and other artificial intelligence (AI) technologies;
  - privacy concerns.

#### **Growing Regulation**

- States and local jurisdictions increasingly regulate officers and those who employ them.
- A reflection of this: the **IASIR** (International Association of Security and Investigative Regulators).

| IASIR | Data |
| --- | --- |
| Founding | **1993**, with **15** regulators |
| Today | **32** regulatory agencies or boards |
| Scope | **20** US states, **7** Canadian provinces and **France** |
| Associates | **38** associate memberships **without a vote** (they represent the regulated industries) |

- Challenge: keeping up with legal changes on licensing, registration, reporting, training and uniforms.
- To comply: monitor the regulator's communications. Good practice: subscribe to its email alerts, if offered.

#### **Technological Change**

- The officer must handle programs on desktop computers and mobile devices.
- Must understand how to operate electronic systems for:
  - barriers;
  - intrusion detection;
  - alarm assessment;
  - identification (ID);
  - communication;
  - visitor management;
  - video surveillance;
  - access control.
- Evolution of guard tours: **Detex Watchclocks (1902)** → electronic versions with Bluetooth, GPS and WiFi.
- Evolution of reports: paper → cloud reporting software and computer-aided dispatching (CAD).
- Ability and willingness to learn and adapt are required.

#### **Education and Certifications**

- University security programs and the prestige of certifications are growing.

| Organization | Certification | Full name |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ASIS International | CPP | Certified Protection Professional |
| ASIS International | PSP | Physical Security Professional |
| ASIS International | PCI | Professional Certified Investigator |
| ASIS International | APP | Associate Protection Professional |
| IFPO (International Foundation for Protection Officers) | CPO | Certified Protection Officer |
| IFPO | CSSM | Certified in Security Supervision and Management |

- The IFPO's are highly relevant for front-line officers and supervisors.
- The **CPO** is entry level. It serves to acquire knowledge, improve promotion options and professionalize the industry.

#### **Expectations and Cost**

- Services outside security's own scope are requested as **“value add”** to justify the expense.
- Reason: it is hard to demonstrate what return the money invested in officers yields.
- Caution: those extra services must not weaken overall security.
- More expectations require more resources for training. This applies to those who have proprietary officers and to governments or companies that contract.
- Persistent challenge in contract security: overvaluing **low price** when buying. Typical result: poor, compromised quality.

### 12.3 Determining the Need for a Security Force

- Every security plan or integrated asset protection plan starts from the risks it must control.
- Balanced approach: **defense-in-depth** with layered controls.

| Layer | Examples | Name in the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Behavior modification | Policies, procedures, training, awareness programs | *Security management* |
| Electromechanical | Security systems, locks, lighting | *Security technology* |
| Personnel | Personnel in general, including security officers | *Security operations* |

- The officer is **not an isolated resource**: the officer is part of a comprehensive protection plan.
- Personnel are usually the **most expensive component** of the program.
- In exchange, they provide capabilities that technology or other controls cannot viably provide.
- Periodically reevaluate the use of officers: does it meet the objectives? does it make the most of the budget?
- Goal: a balance among **people, process and technology**.

**“Necessary human being” concept**

- Key question: do the characteristics and reactions the position demands require human capabilities?
- Every position combines three types of skill:
  - **cognitive** (knowledge);
  - **psychomotor** (physical);
  - **affective** (attitude).
- A human is needed if the position demands any of these five capabilities:
  1. Distinguishing among events, people, circumstances or objects using complex and variable criteria.
  2. Conversing rationally face to face and deciding the next step based on that conversation.
  3. Using physical force, or the threat of using it, to subdue one or more people.
  4. Using judgment to choose a logical course of mental or physical action in randomly changing circumstances.
  5. Reporting one's activities continuously.
- If a human is not needed, the position may not require personnel.
- Examples of substitution:
  - person-trap with interlocking or revolving doors for entries and exits;
  - fully automated access with video, audio transceivers and card readers; the human responds only to a problem.
- If personal recognition is required and the queue is *low-density queuing*, **a remote operator can control 3 or 4 entrances**.
- Using officers only where human capabilities are needed maximizes spending and effectiveness.
- The **size of the force** is decided within the risk analysis. That analysis considers:
  - the organization's mission;
  - core values;
  - operating environment;
  - security objectives;
  - threats;
  - vulnerabilities;
  - risk acceptance.
- The book leaves this list open (“and other matters”).

### 12.4 Security Force Models

- If it is decided that officers mitigate a risk, there are three models:
  - **proprietary** (or *in-house*): direct employees of the organization;
  - **contract**: employees of a security services company;
  - **hybrid**: both at once.
- Factors in choosing:
  - type of organization to be protected;
  - program goals, based on a needs assessment;
  - location or locations;
  - regulatory requirements;
  - financial and management capabilities;
  - performance expectations;
  - cultural factors (the book leaves the list open with “and others”).
- Weigh the advantages and disadvantages of each model and build **budget models** for each alternative.

| Model | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Proprietary | More direct control of selection, screening, training and supervision. Employee loyalty to the organization: lower turnover, more job knowledge, better performance | Can be **more expensive**. The organization takes on what the contractor would do: recruiting, application processing, screening, training, benefits administration, performance management, discipline and open post coverage |
| Contract | Shifts part of the burden of hiring, training and supervising to the security company. A legal review will say whether the contract shifts part of the legal liability. More flexibility in workforce size | Less control. Loss of the officer's knowledge of the organization's specific needs and other elements of the program |
| Hybrid | More control of operations, less administrative burden and maximum savings. Combines benefits of the other two | **Co-employment** rules must be reviewed before implementing |

**Variants of the hybrid model**

- Proprietary managers administer a program staffed by contract officers.
- Contract officers work side by side with proprietary officers.
- Contract officers only for specific tasks or as supplemental on-call coverage.

**Buying contract security**

- Do not decide **on the lowest bid alone**.
- Companies cut costs in order to sell. The largest cost component is the **officer's wage**.
- Lowering the price usually lowers wages and, with them, the quality of service.
- Client's responsibility: write detailed **bid specifications**.
- They must set requirements, wages, benefits and performance expectations.
- That way the contractor can be held to the quality standard after award.
- The trend toward cutting staff and outsourcing favored the contract model.
- Case: **General Motors** converted its large proprietary force into a contract operation. A common pattern when the budget tightens.
- If the security company subcontracts selection or training, the subcontractor must meet the company's criteria.
- Those criteria must be consistent with legal, regulatory and contractual obligations (ASIS PSO, 2019).

### 12.5 Basic Security Officer Functions

- Whatever the model, the specific tasks the force will perform must be defined.
- The **12 basic functions** according to the book:
  1. Control of access and of pedestrian and vehicle movement.
  2. Patrol of buildings and perimeters.
  3. Inspection of security and fire exposures.
  4. Monitoring of assets from a control center.
  5. Safety and emergency response.
  6. Traffic control.
  7. Dealing with disturbed people.
  8. Escort of material and of people.
  9. Special assignments.
  10. Recordkeeping.
  11. Reporting of incidents and damage.
  12. Enforcing regulations.
- The specific application of these functions at each post is set in the **post orders**.
- Good practices with *post orders*: easy access, central topic of training and consultation while performing the tasks.
- **Exam note:** the list has 12 functions, but the book develops only 10 sections (12.5.1–12.5.10). “Reporting of incidents and damage” and “enforcing regulations” have no section of their own; the latter reappears as a role in 12.6.3.

#### 12.5.1 Access Control

- **Definition:** regulating movement into, out of and within a building or designated area.
- People, means of transport and assets are controlled.
- Typical tasks:
  - verifying identification;
  - ensuring that employees wear their badge at all times inside the facility;
  - regulating access and issuing passes to visitors;
  - identifying and reporting anyone without identification or authorization; some people may be **temporarily detained**;
  - reporting anyone who tries to bring in alcohol or other contraband, or appears to be under the influence of alcohol or controlled substances;
  - identifying and questioning anyone removing goods, to verify that they have a **property removal pass** or other authorization;
  - monitoring materials entering and leaving;
  - monitoring vehicles entering and leaving.
- **Exam note:** the sentence that introduces this list is cut off in the book (editing error); the list itself is complete.

#### 12.5.2 Patrol

| Type | Means |
| --- | --- |
| Foot patrol | The officer on foot |
| Mobile patrol | Cars, trucks, bicycles, golf carts, mopeds, Segways and horses |

- With any method: cover the area **systematically**, retracing steps often and using unexpected routes.
- The officer must always be alert and know the area and its surroundings well:
  - shortcuts and dead ends;
  - ongoing construction;
  - building hours and operations;
  - hazards;
  - legitimate and illegitimate activities;
  - the institution's policies and procedures.
- That knowledge makes it possible to detect what is unusual, suspicious or implies a threat or risk of loss.
- Typical response: report to the appropriate personnel (may include the police) and document according to procedure.
- The vast majority of private officers **are not police**: their key task is to **observe and report**.
- Conditions sought on patrol:
  - unsecured doors, windows or other openings;
  - suspicious people, vehicles, packages or circumstances;
  - disorderly or unusual activity, for example people under the influence of alcohol or other substances;
  - dangerous or unsafe conditions: liquid leaks, fire hazards, failing equipment;
  - equipment running at odd hours;
  - problems with fire equipment;
  - violations of the company's or client's security or *safety* policies;
  - unauthorized entries or trespassers.

#### 12.5.3 Inspection

- Can be done along with other tasks, such as patrol.
- Special inspections of:
  - fire hazards;
  - unlocked doors, safes and windows;
  - other security and *safety* matters, including packages, bags and personal belongings.
- Because of their 24-hour presence, at many sites they are expected to watch mechanical equipment, including fire detection and suppression equipment.
- **Documenting the findings is paramount.**

#### 12.5.4 Monitoring

- More and more officers watch from centralized **security control centers**.
- Typical systems in a control center:
  - security video;
  - access control;
  - fire alarm controls;
  - elevators;
  - building lighting;
  - duress alarms;
  - emergency call stations.
- Scope: a single site or sites across a country or around the world.
- Required profile: very well trained and comfortable with electronic systems, computers and high-stress environments.

#### 12.5.5 Emergency Response

- The officer must be prepared for the emergencies possible at the site.
- Often **the first to arrive**: must direct, coordinate and carry out the responses set by security management.
- Possible responses:
  - first aid;
  - full or partial evacuation;
  - lockdown;
  - shelter-in-place.
- Example of partial evacuation: for a bomb threat on a specific floor, concentrate on **the floors immediately above and below**.
- Emergencies the officer may face:
  - active shooter;
  - fire alarms, real and nuisance;
  - hazardous materials (HAZMAT);
  - medical emergencies;
  - natural disasters, including extreme weather;
  - power outages;
  - demonstrators and civil disturbances;
  - suspicious packages;
  - terrorist acts, including bomb threats and bombings.

#### 12.5.6 Traffic Control

- Tasks:
  - regulating vehicle flow;
  - protecting pedestrians;
  - assisting emergency vehicles;
  - directing those who ask for information.
- Essential equipment (Holm, 2010):
  - clothing suited to changes in weather;
  - reflective vests and armbands;
  - flashlights;
  - whistles;
  - radios;
  - flags;
  - stop and go signs.

#### 12.5.7 Dealing with Persons with Altered Mental Status

- Possible causes the officer will see:
  - mental illness;
  - effects of alcohol or illegal drugs;
  - temporary loss of control from physical or emotional trauma;
  - diabetic emergency;
  - traumatic brain injury;
  - any other irrational behavior that may harm the person or others.
- The officer is **not qualified to determine the cause**. Only a physician can do so.
- The officer's task: secure the person and the scene, call emergency medical services (EMS) and keep the person in custody until handing them over.
- Key rule: it is a **medical emergency, not a police problem**.
- The safety of the responding officer is **paramount**.
- Requires great sensitivity and training.
- Objective: avoid harm and control the person without injury, discomfort or humiliation.
- Once the danger has passed, witnesses will judge the treatment. Poor treatment exposes the officer's conduct to scrutiny.
- Goal in any crisis: safety of the person and of those nearby.
- Other considerations:
  - legal liability of the force and the organization if the disturbed person is injured;
  - legal liability if the person is **not** contained and later injures others;
  - impact of the response on relations with employees, the community, the public and the media.
- Recommendation: periodic training and certification in **crisis intervention**.

#### 12.5.8 Escort

- Escorting someone carrying large sums of money or information or special goods.
- Escort on request, for employee safety:
  - accompanying an employee to the parking lot at night;
  - helping sick or physically disabled people;
  - supporting human resources during a termination.
- Rule: the **fixed-post** officer should not escort visitors or customers, because the officer abandons the protection task.
- Alternatives: have whoever invited the visitor escort them, or a **non-static post** officer.

#### 12.5.9 Special Assignments

- Because the force is on duty 24 hours, it is sometimes the only personnel available.
- Examples: messenger, communications coordinator, driver.
- Risk: diverting officers to miscellaneous services **erodes the protection program**.

#### 12.5.10 Recordkeeping

- The officer documents activities and events in a **post log** or an incident log.
- Some incidents require a more complete **formal report**.
- The organization must teach report writing and define what goes into a formal report and what goes into the log.
- Form and format depend on the site's mission and security objectives; increasingly supported by technology.

| Log | What it is | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Post log | **Chronological and local** record of events at **a single post** | The hourly count of people or vehicles required by the *post orders* |
| Main / control log | **Cumulative and consecutive** history of significant events at **all posts** | Proof that that count was reported to the control center or supervisor and received |

**Data carried by each log entry**

| Element | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| Entry number | Makes it easy to locate or cite |
| Day of the week | Normally abbreviated |
| Time of day | In **military time** (24-hour format) |
| Category tag | One or two words on the nature of the entry (e.g., “Weather”); allows quick scanning of the page |
| Description of the incident or event | Brief; often with symbols or codes |
| Reference | Name, date, number or other identification of another document with more detail (e.g., a detailed complaint) |

**Electronic and paper logs**

- Technology platforms allow custom data fields and required fields in each entry.
- With a sufficient sample of records, most allow reports and trend analysis.
- On paper: write **in ink** and **in consecutive order**, so that nothing is altered or removed.
- Any correction is made **on a separate line, as a new entry**.

**“The eyes and ears of management”**

- Officers are said to be the eyes and ears of management, but their information often does not reach it.
- Typical case: daily logs of “Arrived 0700. All quiet. Relieved 1500.”
- Several days like that suggest one of three things: the post is unnecessary, the officer is not performing, or was not trained to report.
- The *post orders* should require recording concrete facts: key sign-outs, escorts, assistance to visitors or contractors.
- Every event relevant to security or to the continuity of operations deserves an entry.
- Logs and reports have been used in **court proceedings** as an authoritative source to prove:
  - the exact time of an event;
  - the presence of certain people in the facility;
  - the receipt of a telephone call;
  - any action by security personnel.
- That is why the officer must understand the importance of the data being recorded.

### 12.6 Security Officer Roles

- In addition to the basic functions, the officer can fill **five support roles**:

| Role | Key idea | Training required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 12.6.1 Public relations / management representative | Agent of management to visitors, customers, tenants and vendors | Corporate expectations and culture; dealing with the media |
| 12.6.2 Information collection | Detects and reports criminal or adversary activity and loss conditions | Fundamentals of investigation, interviewing and investigative reporting |
| 12.6.3 Enforcement / compliance agent | Enforces management's rules | Rules, enforcement procedure, conflict handling |
| 12.6.4 Legal consultant | Understands legal and quasi-legal matters of the work environment | Civil liability, negligence, contracts, criminal, labor and regulatory law |
| 12.6.5 Physical security or crime prevention specialist | Advises on physical protection and prevention | Technical aspects of equipment; lifelong learning |

#### 12.6.1 Public Relations/Management Representative

- To fill it, the officer must master the company's expectations and culture.
- With that knowledge the officer becomes an **adjunct member of the management team**.
- Is often the **first contact** for visitors, customers, vendors or employees: sets the first impression of the organization.
- Helps create good relations between the security department (proprietary or contract) and the rest.
- If the officer takes part in the security awareness program, he or she encourages employees to report or reduce risks.
- Dealing with people hostile to those who exercise authority: courtesy, restraint and good judgment usually overcome resistance.
- Most training programs **do not cover** this role well.
- Exceptions that do recognize it:
  - *Private Security Officer Selection and Training Guideline* (ASIS, 2019): added **media relations** as a recommended topic;
  - *The Professional Protection Officer: Practical Security Strategies and Emerging Trends*, 8th ed. (Holm, 2010), text of the CPO program.
- New chapters of that CPO text:
  - “Human Relations in a Global Environment”;
  - “Community Relations: Making the Strategy Come Alive”;
  - “Networking and the Liaison Function”.
- **Media:** the officer is often on the front line of media incidents. The organization must give clear guidance.
- Usual guideline: do not make statements on behalf of the organization; put the media in contact with the public relations spokesperson.

#### 12.6.2 Information Collection

- Gathering asset protection information, especially on **criminal or adversary** activity: a role of great value.
- Includes discovering and reporting violations of rules by employees or tenants that may cause losses.
- Patrols look mainly for conditions or situations that would cause losses; they also assess fire and *safety* risks.
- The role grows when there is **organized crime**:
  - narcotics sales;
  - retail theft rings;
  - prostitution;
  - illegal gambling;
  - gang activity.
- Most critical: when the target is suspected **terrorist activity**.
- Training needed: fundamentals of investigation, interviewing techniques and writing investigative reports.
- Must know what to look for, whom to report to, and assess whether to correct on the spot or only report.
- Must be alert to **criminal indicators** and **terrorism indicators**.

#### 12.6.3 Enforcement/Compliance Agent

- Ensures that management's rules are followed.
- Resembles the police function, but within the legal and cultural framework of the protected environment.
- Sometimes enforces laws that are also internal rules, especially if the officer has **commissioned police officer**.
- Needs to know the rules and the procedural side of their enforcement.
- Must achieve compliance efficiently and in line with what management expects.
- Must reduce and resolve conflicts: the **interpersonal side is vital**.
- Has an investigative side: documents and **testifies** about violations. There the intelligence role combines with the compliance role.

#### 12.6.4 Legal Consultant

- Must understand the legal and quasi-legal (disciplinary) matters of the environment.
- Must know: torts, negligence and contracts.
- Practical knowledge of criminal procedure: it may not bind the private officer, but it does bind the police. Knowing the basics improves the police–security relationship.
- Administrative or regulatory laws: great weight in many protected environments.
- Industry standards: they are not law, but their principles and the need to comply are similar.
- Example: understanding and complying with **audits** by government, insurers and professional organizations.
- Labor law: privacy rights, disciplinary processes and union contracts.

#### 12.6.5 Physical Security Specialist

- Also called a **crime prevention specialist**.
- Physical security is the core of the discipline. The role grows because of technological complexity.
- Learning the technical side of new equipment can be a lifelong task.
- Reference publication: *Journal of Physical Security*.

| Author (year) | Idea |
| --- | --- |
| Anderson (2001) | The officer who knows how to advise the client on prevention quickly gains respect and cooperation |
| Maggio (2009) | Security = protection against danger, risk, threats and problems in general; acts as a lubricant that touches the whole organization and makes it work better |
| Palacios and Hertig (2010) | Central function of the officer: to **improve** the policies, procedures and functions of the post, not just follow them; provides perspective to management |

- Conclusion: the role varies greatly by organization, but it is always demanding and dynamic.

### 12.7 Uniforms and Equipment

- Many private security regulators set uniform and equipment requirements.
- Before buying, review state and local laws and regulations.
- **Purpose of that regulation:** so that the public can clearly tell the private officer from the police.
- Case: the **Arizona Department of Public Safety** (state licensing authority) details requirements for badges, shoulder and chest patches, shirts, jackets and vehicle lights.
- It also requires **prior approval** of each garment before issuing the agency's license.

**Figure 12.1 — Uniforms and Equipment (what to consider)**

| Item | Considerations |
| --- | --- |
| Uniforms | Meet the state or local standard, suit the environment and project a professional image appropriate to the facility |
| Communication devices | Complete training on two-way radios and smartphones, if used. Test them regularly to avoid failures in a crisis |
| *Safety* equipment | Each post may require different equipment (flashlight, safety vest; the book leaves the list open) |
| ID badge | Identifies the officer and gives access to the areas patrolled or entered in an emergency. Those with proximity or memory chips require care so they are not damaged or deactivated |
| Keys | Basic tool of almost every post; mechanical or electronic. Training in **key control** policies and procedures |
| Weapons and restraints | Complete training in use, care and storage |
| Vehicles | Safe driving, regular inspections and adherence to procedures |
| Laptops or tablets | Comply with IT and device policies; consider security and encryption; protect them in dusty or dirty environments |

**Issue and uniform policy**

- Most organizations **issue** uniforms and equipment: that way the appearance is standard and the size is right.
- Complete uniforms are usually issued by season, including bad-weather clothing.
- Quantity to issue: according to the type of service and the frequency of laundering or dry cleaning.
- To be recognizable **without appearing authoritarian**: casual or civilian clothing with shoulder or pocket patches.
- The policy should say whether the uniform may be worn off duty, including the commute to work.
- Allowing it only on duty avoids bad publicity if the officer is involved in an incident in his or her free time.

#### 12.7.1 Weapons

- Factors in deciding whether to arm officers:
  - state and local regulation;
  - greater legal liability;
  - higher costs of training, insurance and regulatory compliance.
- Whoever employs or provides officers must have a **use-of-force policy** and integrate it into training.
- Objective of that policy: reduce injuries to officers and third parties, and mitigate liability for inappropriate or excessive force.
- Many jurisdictions require the armed officer to be licensed or registered and to meet minimum qualification and training standards.
- Some require the agency to have a **gun custodian** and may prohibit officers' personal weapons.
- Because of the cost and time of training, many organizations opt for **unarmed** officers.
- In **Canada and Latin America**, according to the book, armed officers are rare: only cash-in-transit or custody of precious metals.
- **Exam note:** answer with the book's information. In practice, in several Latin American countries the armed guard is common (outside the book).

**When to arm (firearms)**

- Issuing lethal weapons is one of an organization's **greatest legal responsibilities**.
- If the regulations allow it, arm only if one or both conditions exist:
  1. Without the weapon there is **greater danger to life**.
  2. It is reasonable to expect that the officer will have to use **lethal force**.
- Case of a lot of cash and risk of robbery: the armed officer deters more and lowers the overall risk to life. Against: a shootout puts third parties at risk.
- Strategic value: the aggressor may desist if he knows there will be armed resistance.
- But the **quality and quantity** of defensive weapons must be a real obstacle.
- Example: one or two officers with pistols may not deter if the target is very attractive and there are no other countermeasures.
- Stronger deterrence: combine armed officers with vehicle control, close-in and boundary security, and hardened locations.
- Shoulder weapons in hardened or defensible positions along the attack route deter more than pistols.
- It can also be justified with a **lone officer** at a post or escort where *felonious attacks* have occurred or are expected.
- Examples: remote access booths and high-crime areas with exterior patrol.
- Alternative: not using officers and replacing them with remote surveillance or intrusion alarms.

**Management's responsibilities if it arms its officers**

- adequate training and maintenance of qualification;
- selection of appropriate weapons and ammunition;
- maintenance of the weapons by a **qualified gunsmith**;
- records of all of the above;
- sufficient liability insurance.
- The officer must master the weapon **before** carrying it.
- Training: handling and storage, live-fire in the field to qualify, and periodic refreshers.
- If officers' personal weapons are permitted, set and enforce standards for type of weapon and ammunition. Alternative: prohibit them.
- Company weapons and ammunition: issued at the start of the shift and collected at the end.

**Policy Considerations**

- Besides the officer's competence, the lethal weapons policy must cover:
  1. conditions under which weapons are issued;
  2. specifically who receives a weapon;
  3. type of weapon and ammunition;
  4. quality and reliability of the weapon when issued;
  5. repair and maintenance by a qualified armorer;
  6. accountability for each weapon and its ammunition;
  7. specific training with that weapon;
  8. safety precautions when issuing and carrying;
  9. return of the weapon when not needed for authorized use;
  10. secure storage when not in use: unloaded, separate from ammunition and locked in an approved container;
  11. accurate records of all of the above.
- Extra controls for weapons in large quantities, in areas prone to theft by terrorists or other adversaries, or highly dangerous (automatic):
  - storage in a hardened building;
  - intrusion detection;
  - armed patrols;
  - dual access.
- The book leaves that list open; everything is part of defense in depth.
- In these cases, government standards and the insurer's requirements must be **exceeded**.

**Less Than Lethal Weapons**

- They demand the **same** thorough reflection as firearms.
- Do a full analysis: do the benefits outweigh the risks?
- Depending on the makeup of the organization, convene a **multidisciplinary team** to evaluate risks and legal liability.
- They can kill: in 2017, **Reuters** documented **more than 1,000 deaths** in US police incidents involving the use of a TASER.
- In most of those deaths other force was also used, such as batons or pepper spray (Reuters, 2017).
- Properly used, they put the officer in a better position to protect self and others from serious injury or death.
- In private security they are **self-defense tools only**, never offensive weapons.
- They are not used to show force, intimidate or apprehend.
- Every tool of the officer has a single purpose: to defend his or her life and that of another person.
- They are never deployed without two elements:
  1. a clear and concise use-of-force policy;
  2. sufficient initial and recurrent training.

| Type | How it works | Risks and rules | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Electroshock: **TASER** (registered trademark) | Compressed gas fires **two barbed darts**. Affects the neurological system; the subject usually loses muscle control | Temporary effect; falls can cause secondary injuries | Carli, 2018 |
| Electroshock: **stun gun** | Requires **direct contact** with the subject | May cause pain without incapacitating | — |
| Electroshock: **stun baton** | Direct contact, but it is longer: the officer does not get as close | May cause pain without incapacitating | — |
| **Batons** | Perhaps the most widely used less than lethal weapon in security. The **expandable / collapsible** one predominates; also the straight-handled nightstick and the side-handled one. Of wood, metal or plastic resin | **Impact** weapon: almost always causes some injury. Only on large muscle masses (thigh, shoulder). **Never** on joints or the head. Misuse: serious injury or death | — |
| **Aerosol irritants** (“tear gas,” “pepper spray”) | CS, CN and OC compounds mixed with propellant in a compact container. Irritate mucous membranes and eyes: tearing, coughing, difficulty breathing, skin irritation | Today they last less and leave fewer symptoms. They require **aftercare and custody**. Greater residual risk for third parties: irresponsible in places such as a crowded emergency room | Mesloh, 2008 |
| **Handcuffs** | Technically not weapons; they help to subdue | Can cause discomfort and injury: require training | Stowell, 2016 |

- Electroshock weapons are also called **CEW** (conducted electrical weapons) or **CED** (conducted energy devices).
- Their effects are temporary and can stop aggression; in general they leave no permanent effects.
- **Exam note:** the book swaps the chemical names. It gives CS = 2-chloroacetophenone and CN = “0-chlorobenzylidene.” The correct ones are CN = 2-chloroacetophenone and CS = o-chlorobenzylidene malononitrile (outside the book).
- OC = oleoresin capsicum, the base of pepper spray.

### 12.8 Security Officer Selection

- The quality and competence of the force directly determine the success of operations.
- That is why **minimum standards** for hiring and training must be set.

#### 12.8.1 ASIS Guideline PSO–2019

- In **2001** ASIS International recognized the need to raise the standards of private officers.
- It developed the *Private Security Officer Selection and Training Guideline* (ASIS GDL PSO–2010), **revised in 2019**.
- **Section 5.3, *Selection of Personnel*,** of the PSO 2019. The organization must:
  1. set minimum criteria of knowledge, skills, experience and competence for each position;
  2. make them consistent with legal, regulatory and contractual obligations;
  3. evaluate each person's competence against those criteria;
  4. screen out anyone who does not meet them, **or** give them the training needed to meet them.
- **Exam note:** the book mixes dates (need in 2001, cited version of 2010, revision of 2019). The first edition of the guideline was published in 2004 (outside the book).

#### 12.8.2 Personal Attributes

- The essential attributes must be **aligned with the core values** of the organization.
- The six common attributes and how the book develops them:

| Attribute in the list | Section of the book |
| --- | --- |
| Good character | *Character* |
| Good customer service skills | *Behavior* |
| Neat appearance | *Appearance* |
| Knowledge of the job | *Knowledge of the Job* |
| Education | *Education* |
| Ethical conduct | *Ethics* |

**Character:** honest, courageous, alert, disciplined and loyal.

| Trait | What it implies |
| --- | --- |
| Honesty | Is custodian of company and employee property. The regulations must require scrupulous honesty **in writing** |
| Courage | There is always danger. Courage in physical incidents and emergencies, and also to report every violation completely and faithfully |
| Alertness | Can make the difference between life and death. Difficult when routine makes the work monotonous |
| Discipline | Obey promptly and carry out orders well. **Never leave the post** until relieved or ordered. Personal opinions must not influence performance |
| Loyalty | To the post and the company; decide according to what is best for the organization. Discretion with confidential information |

- Causes for discipline, including dismissal:
  - failing to prevent damage or theft of property;
  - accepting bribes or gratuities;
  - allowing violation of company rules.
- Because the officer often works **alone and without direct supervision**, strength of character is key.

**Behavior:** the officer is often the first contact and shapes the perception of the company. Three factors:

| Factor | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Courtesy | Expressing consideration for others. Eliminates friction and achieves cooperation. Applies when informing, directing or enforcing rules and traffic |
| Restraint | Must be emphasized in training. Act without haste or excessive emotion; avoid abusive language, arguments and, if possible, force. Calm, dignified conduct; tolerance for other opinions |
| Interest | Without interest in the job, the officer projects a bad attitude and an unprofessional image |

**Appearance**

- Good appearance = good impression of the organization.
- Ripple effect: employees respect officers and the security department more, and the department's morale rises.
- A single officer influences, for better or worse, the opinion of many people.
- In uniform or in civilian clothes: always neat and well groomed.

**Knowledge of the Job**

- Continuous learning: the *post orders* and policies change often.
- Must know the facility thoroughly, also for the public relations side (giving information to employees and outsiders).
- Must know:
  - applicable federal, state and local laws;
  - company rules and regulations;
  - criminal procedures;
  - *safety*;
  - fire protection;
  - emergency plans;
  - first aid.

**Education**

- The educational level has improved greatly since reports that criticized the low education and competence of private officers.
- Academia and professional organizations created degree programs and certificates in response to demand.
- Available in the US, Canada and other countries.

**Ethics: IFPO code of ethics.** The protection officer must:

1. Respond to the professional needs of the employer.
2. Maintain exemplary conduct.
3. Protect confidential information.
4. Maintain a safe and secure workplace.
5. Dress in a way that projects professionalism.
6. Enforce all lawful rules and regulations.
7. Encourage liaison with public officers.
8. Cultivate good relations within the profession.
9. Strive to attain professional standards.
10. Promote high standards of ethics among officers.

### 12.9 Security Officer Training

- After hiring, the organization must train its officers well.
- The content is aligned with the officer's responsibilities and with the officer's role in the organization's mission.
- Reference guideline: ASIS *Private Security Officer Selection and Training Guideline* (GDL PSO-2010, revised in 2019), section 5.4 *Training and Competencies*.

**Factors for setting the training criteria for each position (PSO 5.4):**

- Jurisdictional laws/statutes.
- Policies and procedures of the organization or the client.
- Contractual provisions.
- Site-specific job responsibilities.
- Security-related risks associated with duty assignment.

**Example methods and formats (PSO):**

- Orientation programs.
- Online training.
- Instructor-provided classroom training.
- Scenario-based approaches.
- Lectures and/or professional development courses.

**Other PSO points:**

- Training must cover the position's minimum criteria and the legal, regulatory and contractual obligations.
- Duration and content are set according to each officer's assignment.
- *Proficiency* and *competency* level must be measured.
- Training monitoring is continuous, to detect improvements.
- Ultimate goal: build, promote and sustain a professional culture.

#### 12.9.1 Benefits of Training

- Training costs money; costs and benefits must be evaluated to achieve the highest return on investment (ROI).
- Five main benefits:

| Benefit | What it provides |
| --- | --- |
| Improved job performance | Gives mastery of the tasks. Without training, competence cannot be expected. |
| Ease of supervision | Well-trained personnel are easier to supervise. The manager spends time on other matters, not on teaching the basics. |
| Staff motivation | Raises morale, self-esteem and job satisfaction. |
| Reduced turnover | Reduces stress in the face of new challenges and shows management's interest. Knowing the environment, expectations and problem solving lowers anxiety. |
| Legal protection | Failing to train can lead to union grievances or negligence lawsuits. |

**How to document training (for legal protection):**

- Attendance list and list of activities for each class.
- Tests signed by the instructor and by the person tested.
- Centralized database of all officer training.
- Checklists for on-the-job training.
- Course evaluations completed by employees: they show the instructor's effectiveness and what remains to be clarified or improved.

#### 12.9.2 Identifying Training Requirements

- Before giving any instruction, the requirements must be identified.

**Sources:**

1. **First step: legal requirements.** Review state statute; administrative rules, rulings and forms; contracts with clients; union contracts (if applicable). Insurers may also impose requirements.
2. **Then: standards, guidelines and best practices.** ASIS International, the National Association of Security Companies (NASCO) and the International Association of Chiefs of Police (IACP) have published officer training guidelines.

- **Community standards:** see what training similar facilities in the area provide (hotels, universities). Their schedules reveal your own deficiencies, innovations and useful resources.
- **Performance deficiencies:** they show up in after-action reports and in performance evaluations. Training prepares for routine and emergency functions.
- **The officers themselves:** they can identify and prioritize topics. It is a form of job task analysis.
  - Uncovers previously unknown needs.
  - Validates their self-esteem and encourages them to participate later.

**Other Organizations Promoting Training Standards and Guidelines:**

| Year | Organization | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1988 | International Foundation for Protection Officers (IFPO) | Officer education and certification. Develops and maintains training standards to improve performance. |
| 1989 | Confederation of European Security Services (CoESS) | Umbrella organization of national security services companies in Europe. Represents common interests; gathers and disseminates information on the sector. |
| 1991 | British Security Industry Association (BSIA) | Training materials and standards. Lobbying and work with governments so that regulation reflects the sector and its clients. |
| 1991 | Security Industry Training Organization (SITO), created by the BSIA as an independent body | Training, conferences, consulting, publications and apprenticeship program. |
| 1993 | National (later International) Association of Security and Investigative Regulators (IASIR) | Founded by 15 licensing regulators from several US states. Model laws and regulations, reciprocity between states and expedited background investigations. |
| — | Service Employees International Union (SEIU), labor union | Survey of state requirements with a rating for each state. Position papers and testimony before governing bodies. |
| — | Loss Prevention Foundation | Loss Prevention Qualified (LPQ) for loss prevention personnel in retail. Loss Prevention Certified (LPC): management certification. |
| — | International Association of Healthcare Security and Safety (IAHSS) and IFPO | Have similar designations. |

**Certifications and career path:**

- Certifications matter, among other reasons, because they create a visible career path.
- **Health care:** IAHSS programs basic → advanced → supervisor. Later: Certified Healthcare Protection Administrator (acronym CHPA: outside the book).
- **Any setting (example ladder):**
  1. Certified Protection Officer (CPO), from the IFPO.
  2. Security Supervision and Management Program → Certified in Security Supervision and Management (CSSM).
  3. Associate Protection Professional (APP).
  4. Years later: Certified Protection Professional (CPP).
- APP and CPP are from ASIS International (outside the book; the text does not say so here).

#### 12.9.3 Methods of Training

**Training as a process:**

- Series of structured sessions that support each other. Each one builds on the previous one.
- Ideal: lifelong learning, constant throughout the entire employment relationship.
- To ensure this, it is divided into segments. Each topic has an introduction, a body and a close (summary + test).
- Some redundancy and a sequence are needed. The manager checks that each topic has all three segments.

| Segment | Example from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Introduction | At orientation the topics are presented: patrol functions, fixed posts, executive protection, vehicle safety and fire prevention. |
| Body | On-the-job training (OJT), study of the Certified Protection Officer (CPO) program, online programs or in-house courses. |
| Close | Supervisor sign-off on an OJT form; commercial test (CPO, Critical Information Network — CiNet, American Safety Training); or the in-house course's own test. |

**What conditions the choice of method:** schedules, available technologies, the instructor's comfort and the number of people to be trained.

**Six methods to consider** (more detail in chapter 2 of *POA: Personnel*):

| Method | What it is and when it works | Limits and tips |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lectures | Traditional method. Gives a group a common body of knowledge. Works to introduce a topic and set the direction; then continue with role-playing, video or physical skills. | Not suited to individual instruction. Ineffective unless short and dynamic. After a meal: drink water before sitting, a bit more light and get them to move. Use visual aids: whiteboard, slides, videos. |
| Case studies | Created at Harvard University in the 1880s; used to teach law. Useful where discretionary judgment is required: crisis management, use of force and ethics. Complements the lecture: the facts are given and then discussed; a video shortens the presentation of facts. | Works best in groups with active discussion. Could be used in individual tutoring; new communication technologies make it viable one on one. |
| Job aids | Practical reminders of how to do a task. Detailed (video surveillance system operator's manual) or simple (signs for operating detection equipment). They ensure that what was taught is followed. | They supplement, not replace, training. |
| Distance learning | Learner and teacher in different places, but in communication. From the correspondence course to the online course. Used by certifications (individual study + test) and service providers, to deliver uniform training at many sites. More popular after COVID-19 and with remote work. | Some trainers reject it. Whoever does not use all the techniques, including this one, almost certainly will not train everyone well. It is accepted and necessary. |
| On-the-job training (OJT) | Delivered at the work site. Very useful for ensuring the transfer of learning. | Works if structured, with procedures and a competent coach. Written part: simple checklist of key topics with space for comments. Without structure it can be ineffective. |
| Mentoring | See the next table. | — |

**Mentoring and field training officer (FTO):**

- Many officers today are representatives of management. They must know its philosophy and the corporate culture: they must be thoroughly socialized.
- Mentoring and FTO programs reinforce that socialization.
- In both, the new officer completes pre-assignment training and then works alongside a guide.

| Aspect | Mentoring | FTO program |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Role of the guide | Teaches, but does not evaluate. | Is also a supervisor and evaluates performance. |
| Final decision | Does not decide. | At some point recommends retaining or dismissing the employee. |
| Complexity | Lower. Helps socialize and bridges the gap between the classroom and the reality of the post. | Complex role; requires planning and training of the FTO. |
| Book's verdict | Works best in most security settings. | — |

- Recognize good mentors: a raise or a distinct title ("lead protection officer", "senior security officer").

#### 12.9.4 The Training Process

**Learning:** positive change in a person's knowledge, skill, ability or perspective.

- Each person learns a little differently: there are visual learners, readers and active learners (who need to be involved).
- It also changes with life and career stage:
  - Veteran or supervisor versus novice.
  - Older versus younger.
  - With a graduate degree: better view of management theory. With work experience only: adapts ideas to the practical better.
- Instruction cannot be designed for each individual style. Several media are combined, systematically and continuously throughout the employment relationship.

**Three domains of learning:**

| Domain | What it covers | Key points |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cognitive (knowledge-based) | The intellectual: how much material is learned and its theory. Key to solving problems: grasping the theory and applying it. | Thibodeau (2008): do not shorten it with brief, generic videos without an instructor. It requires full explanation. Video is supported with questions to the viewer, a pre-test, a challenging and valid post-test, and an instructor or supervisor guide. |
| Affective (attitudes and perception) | Changes how the person sees a situation. Examples: understanding other cultures, dealing with people with disabilities, complying with workplace *safety* rules and knowing how adversaries obtain information. Also: understanding loss and its effect, understanding the adversary and the nature of aggressive behavior. | One must grasp the criminal mindset and the speed of an attack; whoever does not understand it becomes a victim. Essential in the face of terrorism, espionage, workplace safety and relations with the various publics: customers, employees, vendors, police, emergency medical personnel and hazardous materials (HAZMAT) teams. It is integrated with the cognitive and the psychomotor. |
| Psychomotor (physical skills) | The physical or practical: from operating equipment to defensive driving and emergency response. | Requires repetition, practice and refresher courses. Examples: defensive tactics and firearms regularly, fire extinguishers every year, first aid recertification. Research the recognized refresher frequency and include it in the schedule. |

**Retention and transfer:**

- **Retention:** how much learning remains over time. Requires repetition.
- The highest retention is achieved when the learner does the task while listening to the instructor.
- Continuous learning helps retention and transfer.
- **Transfer:** relevance of what is learned to the job; that what was seen in the classroom is used at the post.

**Socialization:**

- Process by which the employee learns and adopts the values of the workplace.
- **Formal:** training (occupational or professional socialization) and supervision. Management must send a single coherent message.
- **Informal:** usually influences more than the formal. Beware of negative socialization through peer group influence.

**Education, Training, and Development:**

```latex
\text{Education} + \text{Training} + \text{Guided Experience} = \text{Development}
```

- Supervisors and managers guide the experience so that it is formative. That is why it is key that they know how to teach, counsel and coach.
- They are three distinct concepts, related and mutually reinforcing. All produce learning: a positive change in job behavior.

| Concept | What it is | Traits |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Education | Acquiring knowledge and information. Broadens perspective and appreciation (affective learning). | Concerned with the why, not the how; may not apply directly to the post. The more education, the easier it is to learn and accept change. They read faster, write better and solve problems better, the officer's greatest challenge. |
| Training | Acquiring knowledge, skills and abilities directly tied to performance. It is the formal process that facilitates learning. | Defined purpose: e.g., operating detection equipment, conducting interviews or monitoring alarms. Uses practice and repetition; changes skills and behavior. Must be continuous and repetitive. |
| Development | Culmination of education, training and experience, plus a strong dose of professional commitment. | "Experience is the best teacher" is not necessarily true. Experience must be guided; if it is not understood, wrong lessons are learned and repeated. |

**What development achieves (7 effects):**

1. Adds new skills or competencies.
2. Sharpens or improves existing competencies.
3. Encourages applying knowledge or skills to the post.
4. Increases performance: the employee is more efficient and works smarter.
5. Prepares the employee for a future promotion.
6. Prepares the subordinate for new challenges.
7. Helps the officer or the investigator solve problems.

#### 12.9.5 Training Strategies

- Objective: make training more cost-effective.
- **12 strategies used successfully:**
  1. Off-duty training.
  2. Tuition reimbursement.
  3. Recognition programs.
  4. Integrated training.
  5. Videos.
  6. Selling security services.
  7. Supervisory training.
  8. Internships.
  9. Previous experience.
  10. Officer selection.
  11. Job rotation.
  12. Professional memberships.
- The book develops only 7 (below). Videos, previous experience, selection, rotation and memberships are only mentioned.

| Strategy | How it works | Benefits and cautions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Off-duty training | Voluntary, outside working hours. E.g., Saturday seminars on executive protection, counterterrorism and defense against multiple attackers. Provide food and a certificate. | Motivates more than mandatory training (e.g., recertifying first aid or cardiopulmonary resuscitation, *CPR*), which can seem repetitive. The most motivated stand out and positive peer pressure is generated. Does not generate overtime. |
| Tuition reimbursement | Pay for emergency medical technician, state firearms qualification or the CPO program from that budget. | Does not come out of the department's training budget. Low cost compared with the large growth. These milestones can be a criterion for promotion to supervisor. |
| Recognition programs | Rewards that reinforce the officer's role (details below). | Must be designed to project the desired image: customer service, enforcement or emergency response. |
| Integrated training | Including officers in the parent company's or client's training: customer service, communication, writing and other corporate courses. | Gives cheap access to computer-based instruction from corporations and government. Socializes and broadens perspectives; keeps the focus on service. Builds trust and a high profile for the department. Employees trust officers they know: it is community policing that helps investigate and obtain intelligence. |
| Selling security services | Equipment is expensive: costs are shared. Departments sell their training to other entities and fund their internal training. Some contractor providers create corporate institutes for clients and their own staff. | Watch liability in certain types of training. The contractor must not end up training its competition. |
| Supervisory training | Teaching supervision topics to officers. | Reinforces their role as representatives of management. They are not supervisors, but they lead in routine and emergencies: they are trained as adjunct members of the management team. |
| Internships | Students see line and management functions. They research to create instruction and help recruit their peers. | Recruiting tool: a college-student officer already trained and who knows the environment. They also attract older people changing careers. Postsecondary level: management focus. They are mentoring and education of novices. |

**Recognition programs, examples:**

- Uniform patch for completing advanced first aid.
- CPO pin for whoever earns the designation.
- Plaques with photo and achievements (CPO, IAHSS Basic, CSSM) in visible areas; also online certificates or plaques.
- Company newsletter and dinners or lunches for training milestones.
- Lateral promotions or "promotion-in-place": same level, but with a raise, change of rank or title, or change of duties.
  - Extra benefit: creates specialties. E.g., workplace *safety* specialists, investigators, crime prevention coordinators and FTOs.
- Bonuses: one-time payment that controls salary cost and reinforces behaviors. E.g., advanced first aid, CPO, certification to carry weapons, the IFPO's Security Supervision and Management Program or the IAHSS Supervisor Program (health care).
  - They create another level of professional expertise within the force.

**Off-duty, how to lower instructor costs:** partner with US high school protective services programs. In exchange: a guest speaker from the company, job shadowing for students or visits to the facility.

**Internships versus externships:**

|  | Internships | Externships |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Origin | Postsecondary level (university). | Protective services programs of secondary schools. |
| Duration | Longer. | Shorter: the student accompanies a professional for a day, more or less. |
| Use | Management focus; recruiting. | Recruiting entry-level officers. |

#### 12.9.6 Obstacles to Providing Training

- The obstacles must be known before launching the program and how to face them planned.
- Launching it blindly usually derails it, and the officers are left without the training they need.
- **The 5 most common obstacles:** budget limits, scheduling, lack of management expertise in training, stereotypes and ego.

**1. Budgetary limitations:** the great enemy of training.

- Realistically identify all the costs:
  - Salary of those being trained.
  - Overtime of replacement officers.
  - Instructor fees.
  - Training aids and equipment.
  - Purchase of materials.
  - Administrative development.
- With a firm figure, decide how to proceed.

| Way to stretch the budget | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| Buying off-the-shelf programs | Much cheaper than custom development. They must be relevant, not perfect, especially if the alternative is not to train or to take a long time. They can be supplemented with in-house material. |
| Subcontracting to a community college or technical institute | Fixed courses at an outside institution. Can be a condition of employment or promotion. |
| Seeking grant money | State and federal programs may fund job training. |
| Joining professional organizations | ASIS International, IFPO and the International Law Enforcement Educators & Trainers Association (ILEETA) give discounts to members. Access to instructors and materials that are hard and expensive to replicate. |
| Sharing instructional materials | With other branches or neighboring facilities with similar needs; also training alliances. |
| Using distance learning | Reduces classroom and instructor time and costs. |
| Using free training from government agencies | What is offered in the community. |

**2. Scheduling:**

- In 24/7 operations it is hard to find time; classes must be repeated many times to cover everyone.
- Most useful: some type of distance learning or self-study.

| Option (5 in the book) | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Online learning programs | Synchronous: instructor and learner at the same time, in real time. Asynchronous: feedback is not immediate. There are packages by type and level of officer and webinars with a test and documentation. More stimulating and interactive than media that are simply passed from hand to hand. |
| ↳ within option 1: in-house e-learning | For organizations with a lot of personnel, especially providers with a centralized training department. In proprietary settings there is economy of scale if it serves more people: managers, emergency squads or all employees. A learning needs assessment of the organization says whether it is viable. |
| 2. Written procedures | Handed out and then the content is tested. Cheap, specific to the facility or organization and easy to administer. There must be some human interaction to ensure they are interpreted correctly. |
| 3. Supplemental media | Procedures, instructions or articles that pass from one recipient to another: by mail, website or a document with a routing slip. Pass-along logs are cheap, keep learning alive and remind of what is important. |
| 4. Distance learning | Online programs from ASIS International or universities. Used mainly for supervisors and managers; they also serve investigators. |
| 5. Certifications | Physical Security Professional (PSP), from ASIS International. Certified Protection Officer (CPO), from the IFPO. Certified Institutional Protection Specialist (CIPS), from the International Foundation for Cultural Property Protection. Specialties: IAHSS and the American Hotel and Lodging Association. Usually distance or at home. |

- Benefit of certifications for the employer: recognized educational standard, benchmark and promotional tool for the security area. They also bring international reach in a global economy.
- **Exam note:** the book lists "online learning programs" and "distance learning" as separate options. The second refers to programs for supervisors and managers.

**3. Lack of management expertise in training:**

- Almost every manager can teach some topics. Without training in design and delivery, it will be hard to do so.
- Often outside the manager's expertise: diagnosing needs, writing instructional objectives, creating materials and designing tests.
- Two ways out: learn the whole process or hire someone expert.
- Creating materials takes time; even knowing how, the manager may not have the time. It is crucial that the manager assess strengths and limits.
- Main instructor certification in the sector: **Certified Protection Officer Instructor (CPOI)**, from the IFPO. Those with teaching experience who are already CPOs may apply.

**4. Stereotypes:**

- Society's view of the officer is usually not very positive.
- 2020 survey of UK residents: the security officer ranked 9th out of 10 in a list of essential services.
- In popular culture the officer is portrayed as older or uneducated, sitting in a booth or making rounds.
- Managers may also believe it. The slightest hint of prejudice in instruction can derail the program.
- The manager must examine his or her own feelings toward subordinates. A willing and creative one always finds a way to train.

**5. Ego:**

- Some managers believe they have the expertise and the time to write every lesson plan and deliver every course.
- **"Frog Syndrome" (Hertig, 1993):** the manager "jumps in" to train everyone in person and "jumps out" on seeing the load. Result: training stops and organizational development stagnates.
- **McGlaughlin case (2003):** a security director saw that he needed help. He redesigned a sergeant-level vacancy so that the new hire would evolve into training operations manager. It shows that this is a rational way out that adds to organizational development.
- A position dedicated to training lets the department train other areas:
  - Hospital: certified training for nursing in nonviolent crisis intervention or management of aggressive behavior.
  - Hotel: Training for Intervention Procedures by Servers of Alcohol (TIPS) certification for restaurant staff.

### 12.10 Managing the Security Officer Force

- Despite the efficiency technology provides, security officers remain:
  - The largest segment of employees in the security sector.
  - The largest cost item in most security operating budgets.
- For that reason, their deployment is guided by rational, objective business criteria, with a complete assessment of needs.
- Once the need is established, the next step is to schedule and assign them cost-effectively.

#### 12.10.1 Personnel Requirements

- The manager determines how many posts and officers are needed to protect the facility's assets.
- Post orders are based on standard operating procedures (SOP), which must be site-specific.
- **SOP:** a compendium of the procedures the security group follows day to day. It may include emergency response procedures.
- By setting what the organization expects, SOPs help determine the requirements of each post.

**Definition of a security officer post:** any location or combination of activities that requires a trained human being.

| Post type | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Stationary or fixed post | Stationary location. |
| Patrol post | Officer on a roving patrol. |

- The location may have desirable or undesirable activities that require the officer's presence.
- Or it may be a predetermined point: access control for employees, visitors, or contractors, or a central control facility.

#### 12.10.2 General, Post, and Special Orders

| Type | What it is | Scope |
| --- | --- | --- |
| General orders | Canons or body of principles for the officer. They have been used by military police, municipal police, state police, and contract security firms. | Apply to the entire force. |
| Post orders | The most important written instructions of the security force. | Specific to each post and site. |
| Special orders | Address temporary events. | Only during a defined period. |

**Common general orders (9):**

1. Familiarity, fraternization, and unnecessary conversation are prohibited while on duty.
2. Maintain professional or military bearing.
3. Smoking and eating are prohibited while on duty.
4. Any statement to the media is made by the designated spokesperson or the public information officer (PIO).
5. Enforce the rules with diplomacy.
6. Do not use the employer's uniform or equipment without authorization.
7. Immediately inform the employer of any criminal conviction.
8. Do not leave the post without proper relief; never leave a post open for any reason.
9. When leaving, fully brief the relief and pass on every order issued.

**Functions of post orders (4):**

- They express the policies of the protected company.
- They summarize the tasks the officer must perform.
- They avoid the problems of word-of-mouth instructions.
- They are the basis of site-specific training.

**Why they matter:**

- In serious incidents, the officer's integrity, competence, or ability can be questioned. Clear orders carry weight there.
- Not everything can be foreseen; innovation, flexibility, and improvisation are important qualities in an officer.
- But if an officer decides poorly in a foreseeable incident without clear instructions, it is a sign of poor management.

**Criteria for writing post orders (7):**

| Criterion | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| 1. One subject per order | It is found quickly when consulted. It makes it easier to revise or cancel if circumstances change. |
| 2. As brief as possible | It is an action document: what to do and when. The reasons are explained elsewhere in the training. |
| 3. Simple, clear language | No jargon, nuances, or ambiguity. |
| 4. Detailed index | A complete, cross-referenced index; each subject listed in several ways. E.g., "Snow removal, emergency procedures" and "Emergency procedures, snow removal". |
| 5. Available at every post | Up to date and accessible: they are the link between what the client requires and the officer's ability to deliver it. Review them periodically so they are current and complete. |
| 6. Coordinated effort | There must be no contradictory orders. This happens with complex systems; electronic reporting or many posts make it worse. Always maintain a single coherent set. |
| 7. Special orders for temporary events | E.g., temporary closure of another gate that affects a post, or a special event. They must clearly state the period in which they are in effect. |

**Where to keep post orders:**

| Post type | Where |
| --- | --- |
| Fixed | In a designated place at the post. |
| Vehicle patrol | Inside the vehicle. |
| Foot patrol | Electronic devices (smartphone or other mobile device) that give access to the orders and allow reporting and sharing information. On paper: a photographically reduced, pocket-size version, only for that post. |

- **Exam note:** typo in the book on special orders: it says "clearly specific the time period"; it should read *specify*.

#### 12.10.3 Scheduling

**Scheduling software:** now common in private security. It is usually modular.

| Module | Function |
| --- | --- |
| Basic | Assign personnel to posts. |
| Additional | Time and attendance recording; payroll administration. |
| For contract companies | Client billing; job cost control. |

- They generate reports, charts, and graphs; the data can usually be exported to other programs.
- Security: access authorization levels, from read-only to permission to change data.
- To build the best schedule, they cross-match work requirements against the pool of available personnel.
- Some automatically report the officer's arrival and departure. The officer logs into the system with a site number, officer number, and personal password.

**Work site requirements:** detailed analysis of posts and schedules, whether manual or with software. It includes:

1. Each post by type (fixed, vehicle, or foot) and by post number.
2. Start and end times of each shift, by day.
3. Number of officers per shift and per day.
4. Special staffing requirements per shift, if applicable.
5. Names of personnel who are not assigned to the post.

**Personnel database:** a profile of each employee with:

1. Name, address, and phone numbers.
2. Employee number.
3. Shift and overtime preferences.
4. Maximum hours the employee can work.
5. Availability (key for part-time officers).
6. Special training, certifications, and qualifications (skills inventory).
7. Vacation days entitled to and days requested.
8. Scheduled training days.
9. Posts the employee is excluded from.
10. Other considerations, such as physical limitations.

**Schedules:**

- By combining site requirements and personal data, the program optimizes the use of personnel and avoids overtime.
- If an additional officer is needed, it considers: travel time from the officer's home to the site, scheduled hours, and the overtime policy.

**Proprietary force versus contract force:**

| Aspect | Proprietary force | Contract force |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Basic schedule | Besides post hours and shifts, other factors must be included, such as each officer's paid holidays. | Client and agency agree on the basic schedule: total officer-hours per week. The agency places the officers at the posts. |
| Holidays | Some collective bargaining agreements give the option to refuse to work a holiday: review their wording. | — |
| Illness | Average sick days of the force over 2 years as a basis for estimating future years. Look at their distribution: absences are higher in winter. | — |
| Other absences | Jury duty, illness of family members, or emergencies. | Covered by the agency. |
| How they are covered | With overtime. Expensive in small forces. Other costs: fatigue, damaged morale, and reduced alertness. They can cancel out the apparent savings of a tight basic schedule. | In theory, the agency has a broad base and brings in officers from other clients. In practice, the replacement may never have been at the site. |
| Risk | — | The post is not learned in 1 or 2 hours; an inexperienced officer can do more harm than good. The problem shifts from the client to the agency, but it is no easier. |
| Solution | — | If relief must come from the site's regular staff: overtime. In large accounts: occasionally assign personnel from other clients for relief, so they gain experience at the site. |

**Maximizing the straight time schedule:**

- Planned overlapping of shifts makes it possible to have more personnel at peak hours without paying overtime.
- A 30-minute or 1-hour meal break within an 8-hour shift extends the officer's presence by that same amount of time.

| Shift | Scheduled hours | With 1-hour meal break |
| --- | --- | --- |
| First shift (day) | 0700–1500 | 0700–1600 |
| Second shift (afternoon) | 1500–2300 | — |
| Overlap | — | 1500–1600: both shifts fully on duty |

- Useful for peaks: plant shift changes or office breaks.
- But during the meal break the officer leaves his or her duties. It may be unpaid, depending on the policy or the collective bargaining agreement.
- Difficult with small forces or forces with only fixed posts. Solutions:
  - Creative use of patrol officers.
  - Temporary closure of fixed posts.
  - A supervisor covers posts during meals.
- With a collective bargaining agreement, or if a contract prohibits it, the supervisor cannot do the officer's work on a regular basis. It can cause a formal grievance.
- Officers must log their breaks like any activity. In the event of an incident, the log makes it possible to verify whether the officer was on break or should have been at the post.

#### 12.10.4 Supervision

**Organization models:**

| Model | Rank structure |
| --- | --- |
| Military or police (historical origin) | Security chief (highest rank) → captain → lieutenant → sergeant → officer. |
| Business (current trend) | Entry level: security officer → supervisor → conventional managerial titles, e.g., assistant manager, manager, and director. |

- The trend is to move away from the military or paramilitary model and organize with business supervision lines.
- The number and levels of supervision depend on factors such as facility size, schedules, and geographic location of the posts.

**Special training for everyone who supervises:**

- Management and leadership.
- Human relations and interpersonal communication skills.
- Labor and criminal law.
- Emergency response.
- Other topics.
- Ideal: complete it before promotion to supervisor and maintain it as a continuing requirement in the position.

**Role of the shift supervisor:**

- Is on the front line: is a mentor, provides on-the-job training (OJT), and fosters employee satisfaction.
- Must understand the site's requirements, management's needs, and his or her own role.
- **The quality of asset protection is a direct function of local supervision.**
- The experienced supervisor is a local trainer:
  - Observes performance and gives feedback when something was not done correctly or the officer hesitated.
  - Introduces new material to the officer or to the whole shift.
- **Formation at shift change:** at the start or end of the shift. In those minutes a topic is explained, e.g., the essentials of a new post order. Then the officers are asked to read it during the shift.

**Orientation of new officers to the site:**

- If there is an opportunity and budget, pair the new officer with the supervisor or with an experienced patrol officer.
- Keep log sheets and training records for all officers.
- Training log sheets break each responsibility of the site down into a series of tasks.
- Trainer and trainee initial each task to verify it was completed.

**Verifying that a new order was read (during the post visit):**

1. Ask one or more specific questions about the order.
2. Observe the officer in a real situation where it applies.
3. Pose a hypothetical situation that requires showing practical knowledge of the order.

- It takes a few minutes and should be routine on every supervisory visit.

**Post visits:**

- The supervisor must visit every post. If he or she stays at the command post or another fixed post, performance at the others drops and problems requiring his or her intervention increase.
- Radios, tablets, public address systems, and smartphones eliminate the need to stay at a fixed post.
- Regular but unscheduled visits to all posts, with immediate communication with the entire force.
- Observes the post, officer, surroundings, and level of activity. By observing the route between posts, he or she performs a supplementary patrol.

**Evaluations:**

- Constant problem: rating officers' performance.
- Without an incident that shows very good or very bad performance, the shift may go unevaluated. That causes problems in the salary review.
- Partial solution: the supervisor evaluates and records performance after each post visit.
- The evaluation must include, at a minimum (5):
  1. Personal appearance and condition of the officer.
  2. Physical condition of the post.
  3. Availability and condition of the required personnel and equipment, including post orders.
  4. Quality of the response to questions or training situations.
  5. Quality of the response to real situations that occurred during the visit.
- Record everything and pass it to a security force management program. It serves to detect trends and training needs.
