#### 12.10.5 Effectiveness Assessment

- Applies both to those who provide contract security and to those with in-house officers.
- They must create methods to ensure the service meets the security and organizational objectives.
- **Objective:** to test, inspect, or identify problems, normally at the service or contract site, in order to:
  - Correct them.
  - Maintain and improve the service.
  - Prevent problems before they occur.
- It is essential for maintaining quality and continuously improving performance and operations.

#### 12.10.6 Inspections

- On-site supervisors are essential for the force to perform as expected.
- Some organizations, especially contract security companies, add inspectors outside the force's direct chain of command.
- They conduct quality control inspections.

**Advantages of "independent" inspectors (3):**

| Advantage | Why |
| --- | --- |
| 1. They are not known | The inspector can observe or interact without the officer knowing he or she is being evaluated. |
| 2. More objectivity | The immediate supervisor may be influenced by personal biases. |
| 3. Better reaction tests | When testing the officer's reaction, e.g., bringing a suspicious package into the facility, it is best done by someone the officer does not know. |

**Objectives of inspections:**

- Identify poor performance.
- Identify topics requiring supplementary training.
- Identify procedures that need clarification.
- Identify and reward exceptional performance.
- Communicate the quality of performance to clients or to management.

**Procedures that must be established:**

1. Frequency of inspections.
2. Method for capturing and recording the data.
3. How and to whom the data are communicated.

- An effective quality control plan is the basis of continuous improvement of the contract. It is fundamental to the success of security operations.

#### 12.10.7 Management Use of Data

- Management must regularly review and analyze sources that show the force's strengths and weaknesses.
- **Sources cited by the book (6):**
  1. Post logs.
  2. Main or control logs.
  3. Supervisor reports.
  4. Independent inspector reports.
  5. Disciplinary actions against employees.
  6. Complaints or commendations.
- Automated web-based data management systems make capture more efficient and generate reports quickly.
  - They provide a bird's eye view or isolate one element for in-depth analysis.
- Making use of all the data is a continuing challenge.
- Even if collected because of legal or contractual requirements, they are useful for detecting trends.
- **Regular trend analysis:**
  - Basis of continuous improvement and fine-tuning of operations.
  - It can justify the existence of the operation, by detailing incidents and officer responses.

**Reading the reports (source of officer dissatisfaction):**

- The officer is bothered when management appears not to read reports or logs.
- The manager must read them promptly, recognize what is important, and order correction if needed.
- **Example:** an officer reports a burned-out emergency exit light and it stays that way for weeks. He thinks no one cares and stops reporting that problem and others.
- Lesson: be proactive when the data show something needs correcting.
- **Exam note:** typo in the book: "when a data sources clearly reveals"; it should read *a data source*.

#### 12.10.8 Enhancing Job Performance

**Why the job is demanding:**

- Tasks range from routine to highly complex.
- The officer often works alone with little direct supervision.
- He or she is expected to take personal risks that other employees avoid and to deal with the full spectrum of human behavior.
- Makes decisions that affect the well-being of others and millions of dollars in physical assets.
- Because of the importance of first impressions, an inefficient or indifferent officer affects visitors, other security employees, and the entire workforce.

**Implications:**

- From both the fiscal and the human standpoint, efficient officer performance is critical to the asset protection program.
- The officer is an expensive and valuable resource; management must ensure optimal performance.
- The consequences of poor personnel development are quickly noticed when a serious incident occurs.

**Factors of performance excellence:**

| Type | Factors |
| --- | --- |
| Traditional | Pay, supervision, and deployment. |
| Additional | Job analysis, training techniques, work environment (including corporate and departmental cultures), and ergonomics. |

- Managers and supervisors must detect deficiencies and decide on and apply the appropriate corrective actions.
- Attending to these factors has a direct impact on job satisfaction, the turnover rate, and ultimately the success of security operations.

### 12.11 Leveraging the Human Element

- The human element of security goes beyond contract, proprietary, and hybrid security officers. It also includes non-security personnel.

**Examples of non-security personnel with security functions:**

| Person | Main focus | Security functions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Office building concierge | Customer service. | Access control, observation, and reporting. |
| Manager, often in human resources | The book does not detail it. | Workplace violence and preemployment screening. May be a liaison with law enforcement and with community support resources. |

**Who receives security tasks (officially or unofficially) depends on:**

- The organization's needs assessment.
- Resources.
- Location and schedules.
- Each person's capabilities, interests, and current workload.
- Caution: these tasks often require specialized knowledge. Seek training and education from organizations such as ASIS International.

**Security awareness for the whole organization:**

- According to Chapter 2, a comprehensive strategy includes awareness training for personnel in all departments.
- It applies with proprietary, contract, hybrid security, or none at all.
- According to section 12.6 (*Security Officer Roles*), officers perform tasks beyond the traditional; at times they are a liaison with management, clients, and outside agencies.
- Their visibility and daily contact with people put them in a unique position to expand awareness.
- **Cost-effective and practical ideas:**
  - Include it in new employee orientation.
  - Special lunches.
  - Regular work meetings.
  - Free online courses: Nationwide SAR (Suspicious Activity Reporting) Initiative; courses from the U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS) and the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) in the Independent Study Program (ISP) of the Emergency Management Institute.
- Underlying principle: awareness is a powerful security strategy, especially with broad training that reaches beyond security personnel.

**Business Watch programs:**

- They expand awareness and reduce burglary, robberies, shoplifting, fraud, and vandalism.
- Similar to the very successful Neighborhood Watch programs. They take advantage of the interest of businesses and community groups united by a common cause.
- Strategy: link designated police officers with business and community leaders. Together they help local owners, operators, and employees report and prevent crime.
- They usually include security, safety, and awareness training, and programs for marking valuables, such as Operation Identification.

**Case: Prince William County (Virginia) Police Crime Prevention Unit (Prince William County Police, 2020):**

- Its Business Watch allows a participating organization to become a Certified Crime Prevention Business.
- **Requirements for certification (4):**
  1. Have written policies and procedures that address crime.
  2. Train its employees in crime prevention.
  3. Accept an on-site security assessment by Crime Prevention Unit personnel who know CPTED (Crime Prevention Through Environmental Design; Chapter 10).
  4. Designate a point of contact as liaison with the police department.
- What it teaches: involving the community recognizes the enormous power of non-security personnel in crime prevention.
- It is another important aspect of the human element in an integrated physical security strategy.

### Key terms

security officer / protection officer · necessary human being · defense-in-depth · proprietary security force · contract security force · hybrid model · co-employment · bid specifications · post orders · general orders · special orders · property removal pass · foot patrol · mobile patrol · security control center · duress alarm · lockdown · shelter-in-place · altered mental status · emergency medical services (EMS) · crisis intervention · post log · main/control log · military time · management representative · enforcement/compliance agent · torts · use-of-force policy · gun custodian · qualified armorer · less than lethal weapons · conducted electrical weapon (CEW) / conducted energy device (CED) · TASER · stun gun · oleoresin capsicum (OC) · Private Security Officer Selection and Training Guideline (PSO) · job task analysis · after-action reports · IASIR · IFPO code of ethics · CoESS · BSIA · SITO · CPO · CSSM · CPOI · APP · CPP · PSP · CIPS · LPQ / LPC · computer-aided dispatching (CAD) · lectures · case study · job aids · distance learning · synchronous / asynchronous delivery · on-the-job training (OJT) · mentoring · field training officer (FTO) · cognitive / affective / psychomotor learning · retention · transfer · socialization · Education + Training + Guided Experience = Development · tuition reimbursement · promotion-in-place · internships / externships · off-the-shelf programs · pass-along logs · Frog Syndrome · TIPS · SOP · fixed post / patrol post · PIO · personnel database / skills inventory · straight time schedule · training log sheets · effectiveness assessment · independent inspectors · quality control inspections · trend analysis · Business Watch · Operation Identification · Certified Crime Prevention Business · Nationwide SAR Initiative

## Capítulo 13 — Principles of Project Management (págs. 471–478)

**Central idea:** A project is a temporary effort, with a beginning and an end, limited by scope, schedule, budget, and quality. The security project manager must balance those constraints and base every project on a risk analysis. The chapter gives the typical process of an integrated physical protection systems project, from concept to the 12-month warranty.

### Project management and projects

- **Project management:** planning, organizing, and controlling a project's resources.
- **Project vs. operations:** a project is temporary, with a defined start and end; operations are ongoing.
- A project pursues specific goals and objectives that benefit the organization.
- Two types of security project:
  - Inherent to the security mission. Example: improving visitor security.
  - The security part of a larger project. Example: the security (capital and operating) of a new building on a campus.
- Limits: start and end date, and an allocated budget.
- Exceeding them has consequences for the organization, stakeholders, the team, and above all the project manager.
- Challenge: achieving all the goals within time and budget, with minimal budget and operational impact.
- Well managed, it leaves lasting benefits; poorly managed, it leaves lasting negative effects.

### Goals and objectives of security projects

- Improve the experience of employees and others in how security is managed.
- Ensure the effectiveness of the security organization so that:
  - Only authorized persons enter semipublic, controlled, or restricted spaces.
  - Responders are reliably alerted to any unauthorized entry.
  - Incidents receive an effective response.
  - Usable evidence is gathered from incidents.
  - Usable metrics are gathered on everything security-related and used to manage better and support the mission.
- Examples of projects:
  - Design and installation of **physical protection systems (PPS)** in a new or renovated building.
  - Helping the architect plan the space with **CPTED (crime prevention through environmental design)** or **security landscaping**.
  - Human resources planning to optimize facility security.

### Project constraints

| Constraint | What it limits |
| --- | --- |
| Project scope | What the project includes |
| Project schedule | Dates and deadlines |
| Project budget | Money allocated |
| Project quality | Level of quality required |

- A well-conceived project → the project manager can balance all four and not exceed them.
- Exceeding them is usually due to poor conception or poor management.
- A poorly conceived project will surely fail to meet its goals.

### Project management process elements (Project Management Institute, 2013)

In addition to scope, schedule, and budget, the process includes these areas and their subprocesses:

| Area | Subprocesses |
| --- | --- |
| Project integration management | Project plan development · project plan execution · integrated change control |
| Project quality management | Quality planning · quality assurance · quality control |
| Project human resource management | Organizational planning · staff acquisition · team development |
| Project communications management | Communications planning · information distribution · performance reporting · administrative closure |
| Project risk management | Risk management planning · risk identification · qualitative risk analysis · quantitative risk analysis · risk response planning · risk monitoring and control |
| Project procurement management | Procurement planning · solicitation planning · solicitation · source selection · contract administration · contract closeout |

- **Exam note:** the book cites PMI 2013, but these subprocess names are those of old editions of the PMBOK (Project Management Body of Knowledge). Recent editions have other areas, such as stakeholder management (outside the book). For the exam, use the book's list.

### Roles of the security project manager

| Role | Who or what it does |
| --- | --- |
| Creator of the design concept | Creates the concept to gain senior management support |
| Principal decision maker | For example, CEO or CFO; influences scope, budget, and schedule |
| Funding controller | Manages or influences the budget |
| Project influencer | Security manager, chief legal counsel, or consultant; influences scope and budget |
| Project stakeholder | Security manager when others control scope and funds |
| Project contractor | Manages labor and materials according to a schedule |

### Project phases

1. Project conception.
2. Project planning.
3. Project design management.
4. Project bid process management, or participation.
5. Project construction, or construction review.

### Integrating security elements

The project integrates physical, electronic, and operational elements into a cohesive solution for five functions:

| Function | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- |
| Deter | Signage and fences |
| Detect (detect and assess) | Perimeter detection and video cameras |
| Delay | Barriers and doors |
| Respond | PPS, console personnel, communications equipment, patrol vehicles, and response officers |
| Recover | No specific examples in the book |

- The system must also accumulate evidence for discipline or criminal prosecution.
- That evidence also serves procedures, training, and program improvement.
- Design and integration bring the technological and the physical into the overall asset protection program.
- Result: an integrated program that combines the architectural, the technological, and the operational in a flexible, responsive system.
- Key design factors: facility environment, unique needs, threats, risks, vulnerabilities, and constraints.

### Security risk assessment

- Every security project must be based on a **security risk assessment**.
- Without it, the scope is decided without knowing threats, likelihoods, vulnerabilities, or consequences.
- The organization is left unprepared for unknown risks; decisions are made only on assumptions.
- The risk assessment: identifies assets to protect, determines the impact of losses and the perils to the assets.
- Typical phrase after a catastrophe: “I had no idea that could happen”.
- Knowing the risk in advance can prevent worst-case events, often at minimal or no cost.
- **Exam note:** the book says that a project without a risk assessment “will not necessarily be flawed”. From context, it means the opposite: it will necessarily be flawed. Probable typo.

### Basic tasks of PPS implementation

1. Planning and assessment: determine the security requirements.
2. Develop conceptual solutions that address the vulnerabilities.
3. Prepare the PPS design and construction documentation.
4. Solicit bids, set prices, and negotiate with vendors.
5. Install, test, and commission the PPS.

### Project manager’s tools

- **Training:** project management is not a natural skill; it is learned.
- **Project management software:** scheduling software and office software.
- **Determination:** firmly fight the necessary battles to control scope, schedule, and budget.

### Project management process for an integrated PPS project

1. **Project concept:**
   - Understand who the decision makers, influencers, and stakeholders are.
   - Define the scope: architectural aspects; security needs; monitoring, response, and management requirements; performance (functions and quality); capacity and flexibility; compliance with codes and regulation; operational considerations; visual aesthetics; ease of operation; cultural aspects.
   - Define the preliminary budget.
   - Define the schedule.
   - Do a reality check of scope, schedule, and budget.
2. **Designing the project:**
   - Gather the stakeholders’ operational needs.
   - Apply design concepts to the vulnerabilities and operational needs.
   - Coordinate with other disciplines: electrical, doors, elevators, architecture, landscaping, and lighting (the book leaves the list open).
   - Adjust the design to applicable codes and regulations.
   - Adjust it to the organization’s standards: product, aesthetic, signage, and operational (open list in the book).
   - Create drawings, specifications, and perhaps a bill of quantities.
   - Revise the preliminary budget.
3. **Managing the bid process:**
   - Assemble the bid package: manufacturer contractual info, drawings, specifications, hardware schedules, instructions and requirements for bidders, licenses, experience and qualifications, terms and conditions, and sensitive information requirements.
   - Look for qualified bidders: a questionnaire to find credible and competent vendors, or publish the package as a **request for proposal (RFP)**.
   - Issue the bid package.
   - Control sensitive information.
   - If applicable, schedule a bidder’s conference or site visit **one week after** issuing the RFP.
   - Receive the bids.
   - Evaluate them: conformance with the requirements and financial evaluation.
   - Select the bidder.
4. **Managing the implementation process:**
   - Contract signing and initial kick-off meeting: sets the course for scope, schedule, and budget.
   - Manage progress: weekly reports on status, challenges and solutions, and alerts about anything that may affect schedule or scope.
   - Substantial completion.
   - Initial testing: system programming, acceptance testing, and management of corrective work or the punch list.
   - System monitoring training takes place **30 days before** acceptance testing.
5. **Acceptance of the work.**
6. **Warranty:** start and manage the **12-month** warranty period within scope and schedule; evaluate whether the warranty work conforms.

### PMP and PMBOK

- The **Project Management Institute (PMI)** offers the **Project Management Professional (PMP)** certification.
- The PMP is based on the PMI’s **Project Management Body of Knowledge (PMBOK)**.
- The book’s recommendation: anyone serious about project management should pursue the PMP.
- Minimum expected: know the PMBOK very well.

### Key terms

project management · project vs. operations · project scope · project schedule · project budget · project quality · project integration management · integrated change control · quality assurance · quality control · project communications management · administrative closure · project risk management · qualitative risk analysis · quantitative risk analysis · project procurement management · solicitation · source selection · contract administration · contract closeout · decision maker · funding controller · project influencer · stakeholder · physical protection system (PPS) · CPTED · security landscaping · deter, detect, delay, respond, recover · security risk assessment · commissioning · bill of quantities · bid package · request for proposal (RFP) · bidder’s conference · site visit · kick-off meeting · substantial completion · acceptance testing · punch list · warranty period · Project Management Institute (PMI) · Project Management Professional (PMP) · PMBOK

## Capítulo 14 — Project Management: Systems Design (págs. 479–534)

**Central idea:** Designing a security system is a serial process: risk analysis, requirements, basis of design, and conceptual design; then design and documentation (specifications, drawings, schedules), cost estimating, contracting, and the role of the consultant. The chapter then covers video systems (from analog to digital, megapixel, and analytics), the integration of legacy systems (access control, video, intercom, and networks), and procurement (forms and process).

### 14.1 System Design Principles

- **System (in security):** a combination of equipment, personnel, and procedures, coordinated and designed to optimally achieve its objectives.
- A system is more than hardware.
- It is evaluated by performance and cost-effectiveness in countering threats, reducing vulnerabilities, and lowering risk exposure.
- The professional examines all activities and considers assets, exposure, and losses.
- The process applies to technology and also to other security elements (design, purchase, and deployment).

**Planning and assessment, first phase:**

1. Task 1: identify critical assets, potential threats, vulnerabilities, probable risks, and functional requirements.
   - Example: against burglary, harden the target (target hardening) to reduce opportunity.
   - Information on needs, objectives, and constraints is gathered to control risk **before** the event.
   - **Proactivity vs. reactivity:** a key aspect of effective risk management.
2. Task 2: analyze the requirements and formulate countermeasure solutions or concepts that reduce or eliminate vulnerabilities and mitigate risks.
3. Once the concepts are validated (operationally and budget-wise), design begins.

- **Output of the design phase:** identification and location of hardware and software, integration, and performance documentation.
- That documentation must be so clear and complete that vendors and installers interpret it the same way.

**Design is a serial process:**

- Each phase and task is completed before the next; the output of one is the input of the other.
- An important concept to explain to senior management.
- It is only shortened a little with a **design/build** relationship with a contractor, as opposed to design, procurement and construction with an architect or the owner.
- Increasing staff or budget generally does **not** shorten it substantially.
- If the PPS is part of a larger construction project, it follows the schedules and documentation of the architectural design.
- That project is normally overseen by a certified architect (project overseer).
- Those responsible for security design work with the architect and put the requirements into the contract documents.

**Typical tasks of a technical security project:**

1. Risk analysis.
2. Conceptual, or schematic.
3. Design development.
4. Construction documents.
5. Bidding.
6. Construction.

**Figure 14.1. System Design Process:**

| Stage | Elements in the figure |
| --- | --- |
| Study and report | Asset definition · threat · vulnerability and risk · requirements assessment · site survey |
| Output of the study | Basis of design · design concept |
| Preliminary and final design | Hardware and software engineering · personnel requirements · operational requirements · support requirements · installation requirements |
| Bid and negotiate | No elements of its own in the figure |
| Construction | Prepare facilities · acquire hardware/software · construction administration · establish support system · install system · select and train personnel · test system |
| Operation | System operation and support · establish self-sufficiency |

- **Note:** the text of the figure comes in an encoded font. I reconstructed it by position; verify the grouping against the original figure.

### 14.2 Initial Phases

- **Planning and assessment** is the first phase of every security design project.
- It consists of gathering all the pre-design information about the assets and analyzing it against requirements and constraints.
- It always culminates in a **security basis of design**: the first and most important output of the process.
- The basis of design focuses on specific requirements and on a conceptual solution based on them.
- These phases: define threats (identify, gather, and organize their information), identify assets, analyze vulnerabilities, and assess risk.
- Security planning, assessments, and operational audits are formal processes to identify and analyze asset protection problems.
- They also develop requirements, objectives, criteria, concepts, and methods that the detailed design will use.
- **A survey is effective if** its recommendations are acted upon and the results are measured against acceptable standards.
- It requires teamwork among operations, facilities, engineering, and architecture, to present the solution to management.

**Three key ingredients for planning success:**

1. A multidisciplinary, committed approach, by one person or a team.
2. Devoting the necessary time and effort: more accurate design, fewer risks, lower cost of losses, and more durable and effective systems.
3. Deciding with solid, relevant information on risk and on the assets’ environment.

**Outputs of the planning phase:**

- A set of security requirements or objectives: the basis of the design, also called the **design basis**.
- **Assessment:** inspecting and analyzing assets and protection, normally with an initial site survey and vulnerability assessment.
- A **conceptual solution** that ranks vulnerabilities by criticality and chooses the preferred and most cost-effective scheme.
- In designing the solution, prevention, control, and recovery are considered.
- The initial solution depends entirely on how the designer interprets the functional requirements.
- Without requirements there is no meaningful design, and capital can be wasted on expensive construction.
- **Business case** for the new or improved system: business impact, investments, quantifiable savings, and other metrics for deciding.
- Systems are evaluated for quality and reliability, but also for cost.
- Main feature of the business case: economic metrics such as return on investment (ROI), payback, and net present value (NPV) of the cash flow.
- A formal presentation is often mandatory before spending capital: needs, business case, costs and benefits, alternatives, and operational impact.

**Equivalent in architecture and engineering:**

- The planning phase is called the **programming and schematic design phase**.
- It leads to the design basis (requirements analysis) and the conceptual design.
- The **requirements analysis** relies on the analysis of threats, assets, and risk.
- Reference in the book: *A Guide to the Project Management Body of Knowledge (PMBOK Guide)*, Project Management Institute, 2017.

**Defining design requirements:**

- This means developing functional design guidance that leads to the security strategies.
- First, the mission or general objectives of the PPS are formulated.
- They must support the corporate mission, or management will not fund or support them.
- They are validated as each planning task is completed; the mission may be revised.
- In the end, the requirements must accurately reflect the protection objectives.
- **Level-of-confidence factor:** add a level of confidence to each requirement; that is why it is better to say **detect** and **delay**, not **prevent**.
- A PPS with absolute objectives (deny all entry, 100% confidence) is impossible or impractically expensive.
- The real danger is not unauthorized entry, but its **consequences**.
- The requirements should focus on preventing, delaying, or modifying those consequences.
- One solution can serve several vulnerabilities. Example: detecting and delaying access also helps against theft of trade secrets.
- **Group of assets:** its protection must cover the group’s **most critical** asset.
- Alternative: separate the critical asset and protect it apart, instead of raising protection for the whole group.

**Requirements analysis and definition serve to:**

1. Ensure that the solutions mitigate real, specific vulnerabilities.
2. Justify each solution with cost/benefit.
3. Identify all the elements (technology, personnel, and procedures) and resources of each solution.
4. Provide a basis for a precise and complete specification for purchase and implementation.

- **Exam note:** in this part the book writes “PSS” once; it is a typo for PPS.

**Figure 14.2. Requirements Analysis (example of a requirements analysis):**

- Case: ground-floor building entrance, with a real estate office and a bank on either side.
- There are two entrances (north and south) and one exit door to the east.
- The real estate office and the bank receive staff and visitors: facilities must be demarcated upon entering.
- Security is essential at that level: the elevator lobby and the day care are vulnerable to outside visitors.

| Attribute | Door 1 / First Floor | Door 2 / First Floor |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Use | Access to the terrace from the office | Secondary door to the day care |
| Assets | Personnel files of the human resources (HR) office | Day care equipment · children’s records · the children |
| Criticality | Medium | Medium |
| Threats | Break-in · arson · theft of records · theft of equipment · door left open | Break-in · theft of records · theft of equipment · door left open · child abduction |
| Vulnerabilities | Fire enveloping the entire floor · compromised HR information | Day care security and care are compromised |
| Risk | Injury to staff · loss of records · interruption of work processes · psychological harm | Injury to staff and children · loss of records · work interruption · psychological harm |

- **Note:** table reconstructed from an encoded font and by position; verify against the figure. Some of the book’s “threats” (door left open) are strictly vulnerabilities.

### 14.3 Design Phases

#### 14.3.1 Development of Design Criteria

- **Design criteria:** basic rules and guidelines for the design; additional requirements that the design considers alongside the risks.
- All stakeholders give operational review in this phase: security manager, procurement, IT, and facilities.
- This brings in the human factor: decision making, common sense, and awareness.
- Categories: expected performance; operational and financial considerations; style, design, codes, and standards.
- Not all measures are possible or practical; other criteria set constraints on design, implementation, and operation.
- The detail goes in the design specifications and in the construction or contract documents.

| Criterion | Key idea |
| --- | --- |
| Codes and Standards | Comply with building, fire, and life safety codes, laws, and internal standards |
| Quality | Balance quality and cost, with uniform quality |
| Capacity | Estimate capacity early and leave 10–15% in reserve |
| Performance | Overall performance parameters in the basis of design |
| Features | Prefer catalog (off the shelf) features |
| Cost | Design fees and construction cost; budget as an initial criterion |
| Operations | Minimum impact on operations and natural use of the system |
| Culture and Image | Reflect corporate culture and image |
| Monitoring and Response | Security operations center and response personnel |

**Codes and Standards:**

- National and local building, fire, and life safety codes must be followed. They are identified and applied from the initial design.
- Laws may apply, such as those on licensing and training of security officers.
- The organization’s own standards: work rules, insurance coverage, acceptable color schemes, and competitive bidding rules.
- Some organizations have security standards or guidelines for design and construction.
- Certain life safety codes greatly affect the selection, configuration, and operation of door control components.
- Failing to comply can lead to rejection of the design in the construction phase and to costly changes.
- Typical case: controls at points along the building’s established egress route that violate the code and must be eliminated.
- Another case: locks such as electric strike, electromagnetic, and vertical pin locks, their use on certain door types, and the required hardware configuration.

**Quality:**

- Know the quality and performance differences between components.
- Good design = a balance between component quality and total cost.
- Quality must be **uniform**. Typical errors:
  - A high-quality lock on a hollow wooden door.
  - A metal door surrounded by a plain drywall wall.
  - An alarm with sensors and a control unit, but no alarm communication and display system.
- The alarm must have **tamper protection**: an alarm signal if the system is compromised.
- The designer shows options so that management understands the cost drivers and relative performance.
- The trade-offs between cost and quality must be documented.

**Capacity:**

- Capacity, size, and space largely determine the solution.
- Examples: number of cardholders, monitored alarm zones, and doors with access control.
- It can change during design, but an early estimate reduces iterations.
- Nothing complicates a design more than revising capacity midway through the process.
- Expansion is planned from the start: typically **10 to 15% reserve capacity**.

**Performance:**

- The performance of each component goes in the performance or project specification.
- Overall parameters also go as criteria in the basis of design, especially if interacting with existing systems.
- Examples from the book:
  - Entry control must connect to the existing local area network.
  - It must handle verification errors and personnel flow at shift changes.
  - Turnstiles with reader: minimum **500 cardholders per hour** and building evacuation in **10 minutes**.
  - Reliability and maintainability: turnstile with a **mean time between failures (MTBF) of 2,000 hours**.

**Features:**

- They are defined in summary in the basis of design and in detail in the performance specification.
- Example: optical turnstiles in the lobby of a tower, based on flow and evacuation.
- Throughput dictates the number of lanes, and lobbies allow few lanes.
- Uncommon features: less competition in the purchase and rising costs.
- Custom features: complicate interfaces, lengthen purchase and implementation, and make maintenance difficult.
- Know what is available off the shelf and do a reality check with several manufacturers before closing the list.

**Cost:**

- Two major cost drivers: **design fees** and **projected construction cost**.
- Some owners ask installer/integrators to design, to save fees.
- Others hire a knowledgeable consulting engineer.
- Strategically, it is best for an expert person to lead the integrated design; it reduces construction, personnel, and procedure costs.
- Reasonable security, especially integrated (entry control, intrusion detection, and video), usually surprises with its cost.
- A well-documented, **quantitative** risk analysis makes it easier to justify more funds.
- The budget is often a mandatory design goal: it goes among the initial criteria.

**Operations:** two main criteria.

1. **Minimum negative impact** on productivity and operations.
   - Restricted access in high-traffic production areas can affect operations.
   - Consult operations managers early to find alternatives, such as a new production layout.
2. **Natural use** of the systems by security operations.
   - The system must adapt to shift changes and normal patrol operations.
   - It must allow scheduling of activations and concentrate alarms, video surveillance, assessment, and communications in one central place.

**Culture and Image:**

- Corporate culture distinguishes each organization and determines how security is defined and implemented.
- Procedures and training must maximize acceptance of change.
- **Image:** the perception of the organization from outside.
- It is shaped by customer service, promotional activities, and exterior and interior design.
- To support corporate goals, the program must reflect the corporate image.
- Whether high or low profile, it takes care of the aesthetics of what is visible: uniforms and security equipment.
- What is not covered by corporate standards is listed here.

**Monitoring and Response:**

- Essential component: a central **security operations center (SOC)** and personnel assigned to monitor and respond.
- It gains importance with **business continuity**: after an event, the center assesses the damage, the next step in continuity.
- That helps management set critical functions and complete a **business impact analysis** (human, financial, and image costs).
- With global integrated systems, effective monitoring is even more important.
- If the budget limits the SOC or personnel: minimize monitoring or staffing requirements, or provide for outside monitoring.
- In enterprise systems, third-party monitoring calls the enterprise investment itself into question.
- Remote or single sites: alarms to a **commercial central alarm station** or to a proprietary on-site system.
- Enterprise video is monitored remotely, assesses local alarms, and retrieves archived scenes over the network.

| Type of organization | Preferred monitoring and response |
| --- | --- |
| Rudimentary systems, small or medium facilities | Central station |
| Complex systems, large organizations | Proprietary on-site personnel, in a well-designed and equipped SOC (preferred method) |
| Mixed | On-site personnel during working hours and remote monitoring after hours |

- On site, personnel training must match the complexity of the systems.
- **High risk:** use a **secondary** alarm communication route and method, in case the primary one fails or is compromised.

#### 14.3.2 Basis of Design

- Once the requirements definition is complete, the designer prepares the basis of design and hands it to the project team.
- **Contents of the basis of design:**
  1. Initial designation of critical assets.
  2. General objectives of the asset protection program.
  3. Results of the risk analysis.
  4. Functional requirements the design must meet.
  5. Narrative description of the operation of the proposed systems, personnel, and procedures that make up the PPS or program.
- It serves to achieve team **consensus** on goals, what the project includes, and how it will protect the assets.
- Every member of the design team must contribute.
- **It is not the time for:** engineering details, budgets, or debating specific countermeasures.
- **It is the time for:** conceiving the project, deriving requirements from a rigorous risk assessment, and functionally describing the subsystems.
- The initial site surveys can also be done: existing conditions and measures, and improvement needs.

#### 14.3.3 Conceptual Design

- Also called **design concept** or **schematic phase**.
- It is the **last task** of planning and assessment; it is born as a product of the risk assessment.
- The designer formulates a complete solution: protection strategies grouped into a new program or reinforcing an existing one.
- It includes security systems complemented with procedures and personnel.
- At this stage it is expressed in narrative and general terms, with an **initial budget estimate** for design and construction.
- It incorporates the basis of design and documents findings, conclusions, and recommendations from the initial surveys.
- It is the first opportunity to document the design. In architecture: initial conceptual design or schematic phase.
- The designer collaborates with the owner on an integrated, holistic approach.
- **Concentric rings** around the assets: it becomes harder and harder to reach the target and escape undetected.
- **Protection-in-depth** or redundant schemes place barriers and delays on the intruder’s route so there is time to respond.

**Level of detail of the concept:**

- There is debate; normally it carries the elements above plus some initial detail.
- Never more than a high-level description of PPS elements, subsystems, and support systems.
- Subsystems and the interaction between them: described in narrative form.
- Representative details, for example: an entry door with access control or an emergency exit.
- General block diagrams: systems, subsystems, and connectivity at the element level, with construction cost estimates.
- For architecture: mark floor plans with devices, control points, and connectivity, to size interfaces (power).
- Architects demand a great deal of detail early.
- **Approval:** the conceptual level is the ideal time to ask for management approval. There is already consensus on scope and detail for an initial budget.

**Selection of countermeasures:**

- They are chosen by the asset owners, guided by security professionals, according to their **cost-effectiveness**.
- Cost-effectiveness criteria from the book:
  - Operational constraints.
  - Susceptibility to nuisance alarms.
  - Installation cost.
  - Maintenance costs.
  - Probability of detection.
  - MTBF.
  - Contribution to reducing manpower.
  - Contribution to the asset’s vulnerability.
  - Risk reduction, normally expressed as the monetary consequence of loss or destruction.

| Approach | Main strategies |
| --- | --- |
| Security designers (4) | Prevention · detection · control · intervention |
| Homeland security (5) | Preparation · prevention · detection · response · recovery |

**Figure 14.3. Countermeasures Development Table (floor: basement):**

| Door | Use | Assets | Criticality | Countermeasures |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Loading dock | Incoming materials | Medium | Surveillance · access control |
| 2 | Shipping/receiving/storage | Stock and products | Medium | Access control |
| 3 & 4 | Door to the corridor | General equipment | Low | Intercom · access control |
| 5 & 6 | Shipping/receiving office | General equipment · stock | Medium | Duress · door release · monitor |

- **Note:** the figure spells “Survillance” (typo for surveillance).

#### 14.3.4 Design and Documentation

- In the building design industry it can be divided into two phases: **design development (DD)** and **construction documents (CD)**.
- Or a single phase called CDs, and the end of design development is called 30, 35, or 50%.
- The percentages indicate the level of progress of the final construction documents.

| Phase | Percent complete | What it includes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Conceptual or schematic | No percentage in the book | Precedes design development |
| Design development: preliminary design | 30–35% | Follows the conceptual design |
| Design development: close | 50–60% | Ends the DD |
| Construction documents: start | 60% | CDs begin |
| Construction documents: interim submission | 90% | 90% submission |
| Construction documents: final set | 100% | Complete set of CDs |

- **With an architect:** the submissions coincide with those in the architect–owner contract.
- **Without an architect:** they are adapted to the project; there is almost always a conceptual, preliminary, and construction submission, with a design review at each.
- **Objective of the phase:** to finish the design and document it to the level of detail that the chosen purchasing method requires.
- Deliverables: specifications, evaluation criteria, and implementation schedule.
- More detail → better responses from bidders and lower project cost.

**Contract (or bid) documents: three sections**

1. Contractual details.
2. Construction specifications.
3. Construction drawings.

- In the purchase of **services** (for example, security officers) the third section is not needed.
- In **construction** purchases (access control, video), specifications and drawings are called **construction documents**.
- In small projects it is common to put all the written specifications on the drawings themselves.

**Design development (DD):**

- It begins when the schematic design is approved.
- It carries the principles into drawings with the location and type of each device on site, in buildings, and on floors.
- It goes with an **outline specification**: from an index of sections, parts, and headings, to common template text.
- The detail of the outline specification is completed in the CD phase.
- **Typical DD drawings:** title sheet; list of schedules (spreadsheets) per system (power, heat load, cable types); floor plans with device types and locations; list of physical details and single-line (block) diagrams for the CD package.
- **Title sheet:** list of drawings, list of symbols, list of abbreviations, general notes, and other schedules common to all the drawings.
- At DD a **realistic budget** is already possible, because exact quantities and types of devices are fixed.
- DD drawings, outline specifications, and DD budgets go to the project team for approval.

**Construction documents (CD):**

- After the DD package is approved; the result is practically ready to bid or build.
- **Typical CD package:**
  - CD drawings.
  - Specifications.
  - Bill of quantities.
  - Refined system budgets.
  - Contract terms and conditions (normally from the owner’s purchasing department).
- **CD vs. DD:** the level of detail changes.
- A good CD package gives the contractor everything needed to quote accurately and answers every installation question.
- It includes coordination with other trades. Example: who supplies locks and their power supplies (security or hardware), how they are mounted, and how they are integrated into access control.
- The same with each trade that touches security: elevators, electrical hardware, lighting, landscaping, signage, and IT (the book leaves the list open).

* **Exam note:** the book calls the end of DD “30, 35, or 50%” and then says DD concludes at 50–60%. And 60% is both the close of DD and the start of the CDs. Memorize the sequence 30–35 → 50–60 → 60 → 90 → 100%.

#### 14.3.5 Specifications

Specifications reflect and complement the design with enough detail to achieve two things:

1. **That the final implementation reflects what was intended.**
   - The specification contains the instructions and performance criteria for building the systems.
   - It must include functional tests and ongoing scheduled periodic tests, to maintain integrity over time.
   - Without specifications, drawings and sketches are nearly useless and open to interpretation.
   - **Drawings = WHAT is built; specification = the owner’s intent and HOW it is built.**
2. **That all bidders understand the same thing.**
   - Incomplete or erroneous specifications produce very different offers that cannot be compared.

**Characteristics and format:**

- They tend to be lengthy and highly technical; they require a lot of experience in PPS design, procurement, construction, and operation.
- Standard format in **three parts: general, products, and execution**. Each part is divided into subsections and sub-subsections.
- With poor specifications, vendors choose quality and performance for the owner, without the owner knowing until operation.
- There are **boilerplate specifications** as a starting point; experienced designers have their **master specifications**.
- They must incorporate lessons learned. Example: a contractor misread a phrase and reduced functions or raised costs.
- Sections are numbered by trade so they can be issued separately.
- Sources of standard specifications: **American Institute of Architects (AIA)** and **Construction Specifications Institute (CSI)**.
- According to the book, CSI publishes the **MasterFormat** and **MasterSpec** standards; their divisions document an entire project.
- **Electronic Safety and Security = Division 28** of the Facility Services Subgroup.
- **Note (outside the book):** MasterSpec is actually published by AIA (via Deltek); CSI publishes MasterFormat. For the exam, remember Division 28.
- With integration and purchase from a single contractor, it is common to put all systems into **a single custom section**.
- Most architects and project managers prefer all security in a single specification.
- Not all titles apply; the designer adapts the format.
- The standard format ensures: (1) specifications that are complete in every detail; (2) that contractors easily find every detail when quoting or implementing.

**A PPS specification must include, at a minimum:**

1. Instructions to bidders, with the list of all contract documents.
2. List of project references.
3. Functional description of the entire system: intended operation in a concept of operations, maintenance and warranty requirements, quality assurance, and installation schedule.
4. List of design drawings.
5. List and description of the contract’s products and services.
6. Products and services included in other contracts. Example: electrical hardware supplied under the hardware contract but connected to the PPS by the security contractor.
7. List of applicable codes and standards.
8. Support services: submittals of drawings, samples, and documentation, commissioning, testing, training, warranty, maintenance, and spare parts.
9. Technical description of subsystems and components: capacity, functional capability, expandability, performance and operating parameters, environmental parameters, installation and integration details, appearance and finish, and acceptable makes and models.
10. General site conditions, installation standards, exact particulars, materials, dimensions, and quality control standards.

#### 14.3.6 Drawings

- Together with the specifications, the drawings are the basis of every implementation.
- A drawing of the design intent is less likely to be misinterpreted.
- **Key rule:** in case of discrepancies, **the specifications take precedence over the drawings**.
- Most are made with **computer-aided design drafting (CADD)**.
- **Advantages of CADD vs. manual drafting:** clearer files; changes are faster and cheaper; they are easier to share.
- If the designer draws directly in CADD: there are no transcription errors and no need to train another person in the requirements.
- **Types of security drawings:** plans, elevations, details, risers, and hardware schedules.
- Each drawing is a site plan or a floor plan with the devices by type and location (Figure 14.4, Typical Floor Plan, Tim Sutton/Guidepost Solutions).

| Type | What it shows | Key data |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Plans | Top view, map-like, of a site, a floor, or part of one | Several sheets to cover all areas |
| Elevations | Vertical surfaces: heights and locations of wall devices (cameras, readers, motion sensors) | Background from the architect or the organization’s files (Figure 14.6, Security Door Elevation) |
| Details | Elements in greater detail: special mounts, dimensions of custom parts, cable terminations | Plans and elevations are at small scale, e.g., 1/8 in = 1 ft |
| Risers | Complete subsystems (video, access control) with devices and interconnecting cables | Master drawing; contractors count devices here (Figure 14.7) |

**Plans:**

- Background: fence lines, walls, partitions, doors, furniture, door and room numbers (called **targets or tags**), room names, floor materials, stairs, and fixed equipment.
- The architect usually provides the backgrounds in new construction; in an upgrade, the company may have them.
- Manual drafting: backgrounds on transparent sheets (**reproducible**) such as **paper vellum** or **Mylar**, on which symbols and connections are drawn.
- The background must be sufficient without being cluttered, so the security team does not get lost.
- In CADD the background has **layers**: walls, doors, furniture, lighting; they are turned on or off.
- Security symbols go on their own layer, as predefined blocks.
- If the architect changes the background, the old security layer is overlaid. It is only changed if it affects security (e.g., new or relocated alarmed doors).

**Security symbols:**

- Many players have their own sets: companies, magazines, architects, engineers, consultants, and standards bodies.
- The most common for manual drafting: **ASTM International, *Standard Practice for Security Engineering Symbols***.
- **1995:** the International Association of Professional Security Consultants (IAPSC) and the Security Industry Association (SIA) created ***Architectural Graphics Standard–CAD Symbols for Security System Layout***.
- Those symbols were incorporated into the ASTM standard.
- The specification must require the **same set of symbols** on the drawings the contractor delivers.
- Figure 14.5 (Typical Drawing Device Symbology): device symbology and numbering scheme to reference them in the schedules.
- **Pending:** the symbols in Figure 14.5 are not legible in the extracted text.

**Details and risers, what to know:**

- Details are usually made for each project, but can be reused from previous projects.
- Risers: on a campus, each building is a block; in a tower, each floor in vertical elevation-style format.
- In small projects, all risers fit on a single sheet.
- Since contractors quote from the riser counts, it must be **exact and complete**.
- **Note:** the book numbers a figure as “Figure 14-5” (with a hyphen), a minor typo.

#### 14.3.7 Hardware Schedules

- **Hardware schedules:** tables of related security devices.
- They give detailed information that is hard to show on drawings or in the specification text.
- They are used for repetitive devices: door hardware, control devices, intrusion sensors, cameras, monitors, and termination panels.
- They are usually on the security drawings, but can be attached to the specification.

**Figure 14.8. Sample Door Schedule (example, all in the basement):**

| # | Room/Location | Door | Access | Electric lock | Other hardware |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B01 | Loading dock | — | — | — | Intercom |
| B02 | Shipping/receiving/storage | DBL (double) | Card reader | Strike | 2 door contacts (recessed) · request to exit |
| B03 | Shipping/receiving office | SGL (single) | Card reader | Strike | 2 door contacts (recessed) · request to exit |
| B04 | Shipping/receiving office | — | — | — | Intercom |
| B05 | Shipping/receiving office | — | — | — | Monitor |
| B06 | Shipping/receiving office | — | — | — | Telephone |
| B07 | Shipping/receiving office | — | — | — | Duress |

**Figure 14.9. Sample Camera Schedule:**

| Camera | Location/View | Type | Lens | Housing | Mount | Height | Alarm call-up |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| B1 | Loading dock and area toward shipping/receiving | PTZ (pan/tilt/zoom) | 10:01 | EnviroDOME | Wall | 12 ft | No |
| C01 | Ground-floor lobby; includes the corridor to the east exit | PTZ | 3.2 mm–**Pending** mm varifocal | DOME | Ceiling | Ceiling | Yes |
| C02 | Third-floor lobby | Fixed dome | 3.2 mm–**Pending** mm varifocal | DOME | Ceiling | Ceiling | Yes |

- **Note:** Figures 14.8 and 14.9 come in an encoded font; I reconstructed them by position. The upper limit of the varifocal lens is not legible.

#### 14.3.8 Design Coordination

- Many disciplines affect the security design.
- Coordination prevents **missing elements** or **buying twice**.
- Part of the system is described and purchased in sections of other disciplines.
- Example: in new construction, all electrical power (including security power) goes in a single specification and is installed by an electrical contractor.

| Discipline | What is coordinated |
| --- | --- |
| Architect | Space (console, locker rooms, riser closets, equipment rooms); the earlier, the more likely to obtain it. Specifies the door hardware, looks after appearance and finishes, and ensures door cutouts for hardware are made at the factory |
| Electrical engineer | Power at every point with security equipment; amperage of dedicated circuits; electrical backup (generator or UPS, uninterruptible power supply). Back boxes, junction boxes, and conduit are usually in its section and installed by the electrical contractor. Fire alarm interface to cut power to **fail-safe** locks, as the code requires |
| Mechanical engineer | HVAC (heating, ventilation and air conditioning) of security spaces: heat loads and duration of occupancy (e.g., control room 24 h). If there is conduit above the ceiling not designed by the electrical engineer, coordinate it with the ducts |
| Vertical transportation designer | Equipment in elevators, inside or outside the car. Location and mounting of cameras in the car, critical and dependent on car design. Traveling cable, power on the car roof, interfaces in the machine room. Also escalators |

- The vertical transportation designer may be on the staff of the architect, of the mechanical or electrical engineer, or a specialist consultant.

### 14.4 Contracting

- Section of the contract documents that describes the form of contract when selecting a vendor.
- **Covers:** insurance and bonding requirements; site rules; labor rules (union or non-union, wage rates); delivery and payment; how to measure progress for partial payments; owner’s remedies in case of default; termination conditions; unit prices for additions and deletions; instructions to bidders (the book leaves the list open).
- **Large project:** drafted by the architect or the owner’s construction manager, for all trades.
- **Small project:** may be drafted by the company’s purchasing department.
- It is included in the contract documents and adjusted as the project advances.

#### 14.4.1 Initial Budget

- Another product of the planning phase: a **capital** budget and a **recurring costs** budget.
- Without detailed design or firm quantities, it is at most a **conceptual order-of-magnitude** estimate.
- Experienced designers can come out **within 10%** of the final bid price.
- Most need vendors, manufacturers, or contractors to obtain the **MSRP (manufacturer’s suggested retail price)** of equipment, installation, software, and support.
- An independent security consultant may be needed.
- Danger of a bad initial budget: repeating a long, difficult approval process.
- If one vendor will supply almost everything, its experience helps the initial estimate.
- Even if conceptual, its accuracy matters: in general, **within 15 to 20%** of the final bid price.
- **Too low:** when the real cost becomes known, the project may be canceled or run out of funds for a complete PPS.
- **Too high:** the budget may not be justified and may not be approved.

#### 14.4.2 Estimation Considerations

**Capital projects:**

- All equipment and support systems and their installation, primary and backup systems: software, components, mounting hardware, sensors, termination panels, control panels, back boxes, junction boxes, conduit, cable, backup batteries, UPS, and main power circuits.
- Freight and taxes (the book leaves the list open).
- Project management and supervision labor.
- Shop drawing submittals.
- Testing.
- Commissioning.
- Training of operators and users.
- **As-built** (record) drawings at project close.
- Warranty.
- Design fees.

**Service projects and recurring costs:**

- Payroll of security personnel: supervision, benefits, holidays, vacations, and sick leave.
- Uniforms and equipment.
- Training.
- Maintenance, repair, and replacement of equipment.
- Consumables: paper, ink cartridges and toner, backup media.
- Replacement of access cards and badges, and review of procedures.
- Central alarm station monitoring and response.
- Early phases: a single lump sum per subsystem and in total, often narrative.
- Later estimates: more detailed (Figure 14.10).

#### 14.4.3 Types of Cost Estimates

| Type | When | Contingency | Prepared with |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Budgetary estimate | Initial planning; getting the system into the budget cycle, sometimes **up to 5 years** ahead | **±10 to 20%** | Costs from companies that installed recently, vendor estimates, **RSMeans** data |
| Preliminary design estimate | If part of a larger construction project; at the **50%** design review | **±10%** | Draft specifications, drawings, and schedules; vendor estimates |
| Final design estimate | At **100%** design | Minimal, **±5%** | Finished documents, drawings, and schedules |

- The budgetary estimate is difficult without doing a good part of the design.
- **RSMeans:** a company that provides construction cost information.
- **Exam note:** do not confuse the 15–20% accuracy of the initial budget (14.4.1) with the ±10–20% contingency of the budgetary estimate (14.4.3). And 10% is what experienced designers achieve.

**Figure 14.10. Sample Cost Estimate Format (“Facility Security System Cost Estimate”):**

- **Columns:** manufacturer · model · unit cost · quantity · base price · installation time (hours) · labor · labor cost · installed cost.
- **Head end:** PC workstations (computer/monitor/keyboard) · application software–network · alarm/access printer · network hub.
- **Access control:** access control panel · alarm input/output board · signal multiplexer · enclosure · panel power supply · lock power supply · proximity cards–thin · card reader–proximity (surface mount) · network connection · modem/telephone connection · terminal server.
- **Photo ID equipment:** photo identification system PC, software, camera, frame grabber, and printer/laminator.
- **Closing lines:** hourly labor rate · total parts and labor · estimated sales tax · estimated freight · estimated permits · total estimated cost.
- **Pending:** the numerical values and makes in the example come in an encoded font and are not legible.

#### 14.4.4 Life-Cycle Cost

- The real cost of a security system is its **life-cycle cost**.

| Component | What it includes |
| --- | --- |
| Engineering and design costs | Planning and design: choosing products for the specified functions; location drawings, subsystem connections, and wiring details |
| Hardware | Original equipment plus startup spare parts |
| Software | Operating system and application software to run the system |
| Installation costs | Installation labor; inspection, testing, and commissioning; equipment rental; permits; bonds; supervision; overhead. **It is the largest percentage of the system** |
| Operating costs | Personnel, energy consumption, and consumables (paper, cartridges) |
| Maintenance costs | Labor and parts for preventive and corrective maintenance |
| Other costs | State and local taxes · **profit 10%** · **performance bonding 3–5%** · **contingency 5–10%** |
| Adjustments | RSMeans data are national averages; adjust for each locality |

#### 14.4.5 Sample Estimate

**Sample system (book text):** 2 perimeter revolving doors · 10 single-leaf interior doors · 12 fixed cameras · 1 pan/tilt/zoom camera in the ceiling · 2 video monitors · 1 digital video recorder (DVR) · 1 computer monitor for access control.

**Figure 14.11. Sample Estimate:**

| Qty | Description | Unit cost | Extension | Labor |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Revolving door | $30,000 | $30,000 | $15,000 |
| 2 | 8 card reader control panel | $1,000 | $2,000 | $1,000 |
| 1 | Access control software | $1,000 | $1,000 | $500 |
| 11 | Proximity card reader | $100 | $1,100 | $1,100 |
| 3 | 12V batteries | $20 | $60 | — |
| 10 | Request to exit motion detectors | $75 | $750 | $1,000 |
| 1 | 24V power supply | $87 | $87 | $100 |
| 10 | 24 VDC electric strikes | $150 | $1,500 | $2,000 |
| 1 | Operator training | — | — | $500 |
| 1 | PTZ CCTV camera, lens, and enclosure | $2,500 | $2,500 | $2,500 |
| 12 | Fixed CCTV camera, varifocal lens, and enclosure | $700 | $8,400 | $8,400 |
|  | **Subtotals** |  | **$47,397** | **$32,100** |

| Summary of costs | Amount |
| --- | --- |
| Components | $47,397 |
| Tax & shipping (12%) | $5,688 |
| Installation labor ($100 per hour) | $32,100 |
| Subtotal | $85,185 |
| Profit (10%) | $8,518 |
| Subtotal | $93,703 |
| Performance bond (4%) | $3,748 |
| Total cost estimate | $97,451 |

```latex
\text{Total} = \left[ C(1 + 0.12) + L \right] \times 1.10 \times 1.04
```

```latex
\text{Total} = \left[ 47{,}397 \times 1.12 + 32{,}100 \right] \times 1.10 \times 1.04 \approx 97{,}451
```

- C = cost of components; L = installation labor. The 12% tax and shipping applies only to components.
- Profit is calculated on the subtotal; the bond, on the subtotal with profit.
- $32,100 at $100 per hour equals 321 installation hours.
- **Note:** the total comes in an encoded font; $97,451 is partially legible and matches the sum.
- **Exam note:** the text says 2 revolving doors, but the figure prices 1. It also does not price the 2 monitors, the DVR, or the access control monitor.
- **Note:** the example uses a 4% bond and 10% profit, within the ranges in 14.4.4, but does not include contingency.

### 14.5 The Role of Consultants in Systems Design

- Key: knowing **whether and when** it makes sense to use a qualified security consultant.
- Many projects are done solely between owner and contractor, as **turnkey projects**.
- Some owners believe the consultant is a waste of time and money.
- There is good evidence that a good consultant saves a lot of money over the life of the system.
- The consultant builds the long-term strategic needs into the concept: capacity growth and flexibility in the face of operational changes.

| Aspect | Contractor | Consultant |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Role | Deliver products or services on time and cost-efficiently, with what is in its distribution chain | Advise in the client’s best interest, with no other influence |
| Focus | The current project and the needs the owner describes | The long-term vision |
| Final product | System installed and commissioned according to what the client described | Biddable documents, the basis of the contractor’s work |
| Who understands the environment | The organization itself must describe it; with the contractor it is addressed only superficially | Designs for the unique environment: security, operational, and physical |
| Strategic planning | Rarely participates; strategic documentation is scarce or nonexistent | Considers it from the concept |
| Risk analysis | Very rarely is the turnkey project based on one done with the contractor; it is extremely rare for its staff to be able to do it | Usually has training and certifications in risk analysis |
| Independence | Tied to its products | No interests, influences, or biases toward manufacturers, contractors, or vendors |

**What a consultant’s design covers:**

- Insists that every design (and the entire security program) be based on an **updated risk analysis**.
- Considers: cultural aspects; security, physical, operational, IT, and architectural environment; and budget constraints.
- The construction documents show only what bidders need to quote and build.
- But they are assembled within the much broader picture that the consultant handles.
- A good consultant optimizes capital and operations; can save **many times their fees**, sometimes already during construction.

**Case (Fortune 500 company):**

- The contractor proposed **85 doors with readers** in four corridors next to the main lobby, so visitors would not enter employee areas on their own.
- The building hardly received visitors; the traffic was mostly employees.
- The consultant changed the scope: automatic, aesthetic electronic doors in each corridor, where previously there were no doors.
- Each door with its readers, which recorded each person’s card on entering or leaving.
- **Total savings: more than $100,000.**
- **Lesson:** the contractor solved within the existing conditions; the consultant **modified the environment** to solve the problem.

### 14.6 Video Systems

- Security video is living through **two evolutions**: from analog to digital and from standard resolution to megapixel.
- Both continue to affect legacy or hybrid installations, and how video is recorded, stored, and used.

#### 14.6.1 The First Evolution: Analog to Digital

- It began in the early 2000s and is still ongoing.

| Aspect | Analog | Digital |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cost | Cheap cameras and infrastructure | Cameras and recorders can cost more |
| Implementation | Simple; requires no networking knowledge | Requires a network |
| Hacking | Difficult to hack | — |
| Architecture | Rigid | Very flexible; covers almost any configuration |

**Recording options that digital allows:**

- Local, on the camera itself.
- Local per group of cameras (e.g., all those on the third floor).
- Centralized.
- A combination of centralized and local.
- Local or remote fail-over (backup): the camera temporarily stores data if the network goes down.

**Phased migration (when requirements grow: backup, multi-building campus):**

1. Remove the multiplexer and the VCR (video cassette recorder) and put an NVR (network video recorder) on a digital network.
2. Convert the analog signal to digital with an encoder (or codec) connected to the network.
3. Gradually replace analog cameras with digital ones.
4. Reuse the coaxial cable with **IP over coax** converters (IP = Internet Protocol): the coax works as a network cable.
5. In new installations, run network cable.

- Advantage: the transition follows the organization’s own calendar, **without losing the original capital investment**.
- It can be done at the pace at which components fail, as a natural renewal of obsolete equipment.

**Why the analog image was poor quality:**

- Tube cameras: 100% analog circuits.
- With solid-state imagers, the image was born digital and the camera converted it to analog output.
- Chain with multiplexers: digital (sensor) → analog (camera) → analog coax → digital (multiplexer) → analog tape → analog monitor.
- So many conversions degraded the image. The natural evolution was a fully digital camera, processing, and display.
- Analog protocols according to each country’s television: **PAL, SECAM, or NTSC**. Quality almost equal among them and low compared with today’s digital.

**Four changes needed to go from analog to digital:**

1. Convert the analog video technology to digital.
2. Transmit over a TCP/IP (Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol) network instead of coax.
3. Compress the files so they fit in the network bandwidth.
4. Compress the files so they fit in the limited storage available.

**Compression:**

- The first digital cameras used the same sensors as analog ones: low-quality images.
- Without compression, each frame would be a huge raw file.
- That would saturate the networks of the time, often **100 Mbit/s for the entire network**, and storage budgets.
- The industry adopted two algorithms: **MPEG-4 and H.264**.

| Codec | How it works | Advantage | Disadvantage or use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| MPEG-4 | Based on the JPEG standard. Stores a complete JPEG frame and, for a fixed number of frames, only the differences. When there are enough changes, a new complete frame | Subsequent frames are very small | A rendering engine (software or hardware) must reassemble the images to view them |
| H.264 | Same approach, even more efficient | More efficient in transmission and storage | Less efficient when rendering: requires more processing power |
| H.265 | Successor used when moving to megapixel | Absorbs the greater data flow and bandwidth | — |

- **Exam note:** the book says the industry adopted "two" algorithms (MPEG-4 and H.264), but then adds H.265 for megapixel. Remember all three.
- (outside the book) The complete frame is called an I-frame; the difference frames, P-frames or B-frames.

#### 14.6.2 The Second Evolution: Standard Resolution to Megapixel

- A natural step after digital video: from consumer standard resolution (sub-megapixel) to megapixel or HD (high definition).
- Megapixel cameras give much more resolution than standard ones.

| New megapixel capabilities | Infrastructure requirements (network and storage) |
| --- | --- |
| Digital zoom to see detail that does not exist in a standard image | Megapixel-quality lenses: a standard lens on a megapixel camera is useless |
| Show several portions of the same image on different monitors | Greater network capacity: **1 Gbps or more** |
| PTZ (pan/tilt/zoom) on recorded video, **"PTZ into history"**: see detail from minutes, hours, days, weeks, or months ago | Much more storage capacity |

- They are usually **only a small part** of the camera system.
- They are installed where their special capabilities are most needed.

#### 14.6.3 Processed Video, Video Analytics, and Intelligent Video

- Functions that digital video makes possible and that were hardly feasible in analog.

| Concept | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| **Processed video** | Any technology that processes the image to improve its clarity or to assess it in some way |
| **Video analytics** | Software or hardware technology that evaluates the video stream to detect undesired, inappropriate, or concerning behavior |

**Image improvement processes (video image enhancement):**

- Anti-glare.
- Anti-backlight.
- Contrast modification, of the whole image or a part.
- Anti-mist or anti-fog.
- Other clarity improvements.

**Two types of video analytics:**

| Type | How it works | Examples or features |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Preprogrammed** | Defined algorithm that looks for very specific behaviors | Walking the wrong way (entering through the exit of an airport checkpoint, crossing onto the subway tracks). Leaving a package. Loitering on a platform. Floating face down in a pool. Increasingly cheap and capable |
| **Learning algorithm** | Observes the scene for a while and learns what is normal (**baseline**). Then alerts on what is not normal | Very powerful: looks for anything out of the ordinary, not a predetermined behavior |

**What the baseline can include:**

- Traffic patterns.
- Weather patterns.
- Day and night patterns.
- Normal types of behavior.
- Normal pedestrian speed.
- Direction of traffic.
- Normal movement of trees, water, fog, and the like.
- Normally occupied areas (pedestrians who reach the railing of a bridge, but do not climb it).
- Normal image patterns (briefcases yes, AK-47 rifles no; luggage on the belt, children no).
- Example: someone jumps a fence with a backpack, leaves it next to a trash can, and flees → **alarm**.
- A pedestrian who enters through a vehicle exit → might generate only an **alert**.
- **Exam note:** the title includes "intelligent video", but the book does not define it separately.

**Origin of the VMS (video management system):**

- Some consultants expected alarm and access control manufacturers to add video management.
- It was the logical extension of their PLC (programmable logic controller) architecture. It did not happen: they did not see the potential or the market.
- Video manufacturers did see it. They created software with a GUI (graphical user interface) that took advantage of the CPU of their equipment.
- That wave of GUIs came to be called VMS.
- Later, VMSs added partial or full integration with large alarm and access control systems, and then with security intercoms.

#### 14.6.4 Video Systems Integration

- Integrating video with other systems increases productivity and monitoring effectiveness.

##### ONVIF

- (outside the book) ONVIF = Open Network Video Interface Forum.
- Founded in **late 2008** by **Axis Communications, Bosch Security Systems, and Sony Corporation**.
- Goal: open, standardized interfaces for the interoperability of IP-based physical security products.
- More than **18,000 products** conformant with profiles. This outnumbers proprietary equipment, which works only with other equipment from the same manufacturer.
- To be conformant, a product must meet **at least one profile**:

| Profile | Covers |
| --- | --- |
| A | Broad access control configuration |
| C | Physical access control and event management |
| G | Video recording and storage |
| Q | Quick installation and "out of the box" operation |
| S | Basic video streaming |
| T | Advanced video streaming |
| D (draft) | Access control peripherals: locks, credential readers and biometrics, PIN keypads, license plate recognition cameras, door intercoms, sensors, and displays |
| M (draft) | Metadata and analytics events for intelligent applications. Makes it easier to integrate IP cameras or analytics with VMS, and with IoT (Internet of Things) |

- When the book was written, D and M were candidates under review. For the exam, answer them as drafts.

##### PSIM (Physical Security Information Management)

- Software platform that integrates applications and systems.
- Collects and correlates events from separate devices and systems.
- Goal: for personnel to identify and resolve situations proactively.
- Typical in a GSOC (global security operations center).
- Provides situational awareness, monitoring, and reporting from almost any input.
- Integrates security and enterprise-wide subsystems: doors, intercoms, cameras, building management, lighting control, telephones, network devices, HVAC (heating, ventilation, and air conditioning), tracking, fire, intrusion, mass notification, social media and deep web monitoring, and license plate and facial recognition databases.

##### Stages of video integration

| Stage | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| **Basic** | Integrates by make and model, or by ONVIF conformance. Typical: show one or several cameras upon an alarm event |
| **Intermediate** = **unified solution** | The VMS is the single GUI to view and control related systems: access control (open doors and gates), intercom (receive and initiate calls), lighting, and delaying devices |
| **Advanced** | VMS and PSIM do all of the above, plus advanced image processing: image enhancement and disruptive risk detection |

##### How the integration is wired

| Integration | Direction of signal | Steps |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Alarms → video** | From the alarm system to the video system | The video switcher (analog switcher or digital software) is connected to the alarm activation signal. Simplest: a relay from the alarm system to a video alarm input. In matrix switchers there are alarm input modules. In digital: input modules or serial interface, if both have it. It is always necessary to program which cameras appear for each alarm |
| **Access control portals** | The reverse: from video to access control | An icon of the portal is created in the video GUI. A video output relay module is connected to an access control alarm input. That input is programmed as a request-to-exit sensor that unlocks the door or opens the gate. The icon triggers the function when selected |

- Advanced integration varies by device type, make, and model.

##### Advanced video functions

- A conventional VMS or PSIM performs much better in surveillance and incident response if the way it is programmed is changed.
- Two methods to make it more useful day to day:
