**1. Geographically related video layouts (camera screens grouped by zone)**

- Instead of showing a single camera per alarm, each camera is part of a layout of neighboring cameras.
- Example: 5th-floor elevator lobby = 6 cameras on the floor + 3 in the cars.
- A duress alarm at the 5th-floor reception shows them all at once.
- An icon per layout on the GUI map enables the **video guard tour**, floor by floor and area by area.
- Each layout is a "stop" on the tour; the operator covers the entire campus quickly.
- They are the step prior to video pursuit.

**2. Video pursuit**

- A VMS or PSIM function for following a subject as if the operator were walking behind him or her.
- It combines the geographic layouts with a flat database of all the cameras and a function that relates them.
- How it works:
  1. An alarm opens a tour stop: the primary camera in the center and the egress route cameras around it. It requires one layout per camera callable from an alarm.
  2. The subject passes into the field of an egress camera. The operator puts the mouse over it and presses the middle button.
  3. The system looks that camera up in the database and opens its layout, with it in the center and its exits around it.
  4. By repeating the click, the operator follows the subject from camera to camera.
- Each layout is a database record; the focus camera is its primary field.
- A queued intercom can be linked to each focus camera for push-to-talk.
- Middle button over the focus camera = talk; over another camera = next camera in the pursuit.
- Effect: a voice that follows the subject through the building tends to disconcert him or her.

### 14.7 Merging Legacy Systems

- Reviews the history of PPS (physical protection system) technologies and how to integrate them into modern digital systems.
- Two types of project: **new construction** or **existing building** (its use changes or the security program is expanded).
- In existing buildings always, and in new construction often, there are prior systems to extend.
- **Legacy systems:** old or obsolete systems. They have all the data points of modern ones, but rarely easy interfaces for integration.
- The PPS designer must integrate them so the overall system works well.
- Typical scenario: several access control, video, and communications systems. They report to a chaotic monitoring center, or each building operates in isolation. Some are up to date and others have been obsolete for years.

**Principles of integration:**

- Improves the accuracy of reporting and incident management.
- Reduces the time the console operator takes to **detect, assess, and coordinate** the response.
- It does so by automating the tasks with which the operator cross-references incoming information.
- Creates an evidence trail for criminal proceedings, employment discipline, or training.

| Non-integrated system | Integrated system |
| --- | --- |
| Announces the alarm "in a vacuum" | Instantly shows the right cameras, and even the **pre-alarm video** |
| The operator must find out which cameras cover the area and select them | The operator assesses on the spot: real or nuisance alarm, severity, and type of response |
| While the video arrives, the aggressor may have left | The best queue the nearby intercom: just press a button to talk |
| — | Automates functions: lighting the route from the parking lot to the office of an executive who arrives after hours; turning on heating or air conditioning when he or she uses a card |

- These integrations are usually feasible with modern equipment, **not with legacy systems**.

#### 14.7.1 Access Control Systems

- Alarm and access control systems are classified into **five generations**.
- The **1st, 4th, and 5th** are still in use.

| Generation | Alarms | Access control | Key features |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **1st** | Separate from access, with their own hardware. **20 mA current loop** monitored with an ammeter: open circuit = alarm. Up to **300 ammeters** in a row. At first without sound: guards scanned the meters. Later, interposing relays sounded a warning | Stand-alone at a single door | Its descendants are still on hotel doors all over the world |
| **2nd** | Separate from access. Colored lamps: **green** = circuit active, **red** = alarm, **yellow** = bypassed | Several card technologies (e.g., barium ferrite), today almost all obsolete. It brought a few doors (typically **8 or 16**) to a central control | Very cryptic to use; the manual had to be at hand |
| **3rd** | Alarm and access **in a single system**. They reported to a minicomputer the size of a four-drawer filing cabinet and to a line printer | More doors (typically **no more than 64**), controlled from a dedicated terminal. **All access decisions, in the central minicomputer** | Also cryptic; a lot of training |
| **4th** | — | Since the **late 1970s**, with 8-bit microprocessors: Motorola 6800, Intel 8080, MOS Technology 6502, and Zilog Z80. **Distributed**, compact panels. Programming in **EPROM** (erasable programmable read-only memory) firmware | Architecture still present in many products. Limited flexibility: it only does what was factory-programmed; almost no field programming |
| **5th** | — | Since the **early 2000s**. Almost no EPROM: each door’s programming is downloaded to the panel as a toolkit. **Every entry and exit point is programmable** | Acts like a PLC, the concept on which all access control is based. Very powerful and highly integrable. Still has little impact on the industry |

**Key points of the 4th versus the 5th:**

- The 4th uses **four hardware points per reader**: reader, door position switch, request-to-exit sensor, and lock.
- It may need extra hardware for unique functions, such as a local door alarm.
- On doors with a reader in and a reader out, the leftover points are wasted.
- In the 5th, those leftover points are available as inputs and outputs.
- The 5th only performs at its best if the qualified installer has access to all of its programming.
- The industry still depends mostly on expensive **PSIMs** for functions that a 5th generation would make easy and cheap.

##### Access Cards

- Many old technologies have disappeared along with their market.
- Cards are the **core of systems integration**.
- Single pass authorization cards give entry to specific areas.
- They are usually **the first thing integrated**: a single technology is chosen and all systems are upgraded to that single standard.
- Modern cards combine several technologies in one: magnetic stripe, invisible barcode, and contact or contactless proximity. That way they work with all of the organization’s systems.

**Card technologies still common:**

- ABA magnetic stripe.
- Visible or invisible barcode.
- Complex barcode.
- **125 kHz** proximity card.
- **13.56 MHz** smart card, contact or contactless.
- **FIPS 201**-compliant card (outside the book: Federal Information Processing Standard 201, the U.S. federal credential).
- **Exam note:** the book expands ABA as "American Banking Association". The real name is American Bankers Association (outside the book).
- The effectiveness of each technology must be weighed: some can be defeated or lack encryption.
- Besides cards: keypad code or biometric credential (fingerprint, hand geometry, handwriting, iris, voice).
- In high security it is common to require **two or more**, e.g., card + fingerprint.

##### Access Control Systems from Different Manufacturers

- Even if a single card works for several brands, the organization may want to monitor everything from **a single center**.
- Fully integrating alarms and access databases may require replacing entire systems and panels.
- With a limited budget, the work can be done **in phases**.

##### Alarm Monitoring

- Usual first step: bring alarm monitoring to **a single platform** at the center, whatever the brand in the field.
- Reason: timely assessment of the data is critical.
- Method on a campus with several brands: one panel from each system at the monitoring center, one per system.
- Example: 2 systems of brand A + 1 of B, 1 of C, and 1 of D = **5 panels** at the center, one per building.
- The panels communicate with their systems by modem or network.
- Each panel is programmed to report any alarm to an output device, software or hardware.

##### Hardware Alarm Reporting Interface

- The output is a dry contact relay.
- The center’s panel handles relay cards: **one relay for each alarm** of the remote system.
- Each relay is wired to an alarm input of the center’s system. That way, each remote alarm is represented at the center.
- **Advantage:** it is immune to software version updates of either system, which are known to put them out of sync.
- It may be mandatory on obsolete systems with proprietary databases, especially if panels are no longer available.
- If there is no spare hardware left, **it is advisable to replace the system**: a failure would take it out of service and create an operational emergency.

##### Software Alarm Reporting

- There are at least **three common methods**:

| Method | How it works | What it requires |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **1. Database link** | The databases of the monitoring system and the monitored system are linked. The monitor watches and reports the other’s alarms | Qualified SQL (Structured Query Language) programmer and license agreements on both systems |
| **2. Alarm printer RS-232 port** | The monitored system sends all its alarms to the RS-232 port of its panel at the center. An RS-232 port on the monitor captures them and an SQL database converts each line into an understandable alarm message | SQL programmer to write the code. Configure the RS-232 interfaces of both systems. Configure the monitor to receive the messages from the SQL database |
| **3. PSIM** | Detects data from several systems and translates them into a common alarm reporting regime. Combines hardware and software creatively | Almost always integrates well with legacy systems |

- Variant of method 2: if the monitor does not adapt well, an **interpreter SQL computer** is installed. It receives the RS-232 messages and harmonizes its database with the monitor’s. It may require virtual device licenses.
- **Exam note:** the book says the SQL database converts the message into one "understood by the monitored system". Logically it should be the **monitoring** system; it appears to be a typo.
- If the monitored system **does not use an SQL database**, the **hardware** method is preferred.
- On campuses or companies with a mix of systems, a **combination** of hardware and software interfaces is often needed.

##### Access Control System Interfacing

- Integrating alarm reporting is not enough to manage access in common. For that there are two methods:
  1. **Convert all systems to a common one.** Almost always requires the same software version too.
  2. **Use a PSIM** that integrates several manufacturers. Its success depends on the manufacturer: some do not cooperate with external interfaces.
- Both give a common interface for programming cards and managing everything centrally.
- Benefit: security management applies standardized practices uniformly across the whole organization.

#### 14.7.2 Legacy Video Systems

- In the 1970s, video was high technology: few cameras, each with its own monitor.
- As the number of cameras grew, rooms filled with monitors: too much heat and too many images to watch.
- The industry responded with this sequence of technologies:

| Technology | Key features |
| --- | --- |
| **Sequential switchers** | First solution. **8–16 inputs** for cameras and almost always **2 monitor outputs**: one cycles through the cameras in sequence and the other shows a fixed camera |
| **Quad display units** | **4 cameras** on a single monitor |
| **Early recording** | **Sony U-Matic**: the size of a suitcase; records **1 camera for 2 hours** on a cassette the size of a large book. Then **VHS**: 1 analog camera up to **6 hours** at the slowest speed |
| **Multiplexers + time-lapse VCRs** | Normal analog video shows **30 frames per second**. The multiplexer takes one frame from each of **16 cameras**, digitizes it, and records it on a slow VHS. Result: about 4 frames/s per camera and **24 hours of 16 cameras on a single tape**. Shows 1–16 cameras live or recorded at **2–4 frames/s**, on 2 monitors like the sequential switcher |
| **Networked video switchers** | Larger switchers, with many inputs and outputs and a keyboard with PTZ controls (pan/tilt and motorized zoom already existed). Functions separated into boxes: camera input bays, monitor output bays, central processing unit, and **cross-point switcher**. Joined by ribbon cables forming a **video buss**. The boxes could be in different buildings: a single system for the whole campus. The bus between bays and matrix runs over **fiber optic**, because of the losses of long coax |
| **DVR** (digital video recorder) | Joins the multiplexer (which digitizes) with a real **hard disk** recorder. Eliminates analog recording. As disks grew and fell in cost, it completely replaced multiplexers and time-lapse. It is a drop-in replacement for multiplexer + VCR. Historically for analog cameras; it increasingly accepts digital cameras |
| **NVR** (network video recorder) | For digital cameras no analog inputs are needed. It connects to the same network as the cameras and is located wherever convenient: near the cameras, the monitoring center, or anywhere else |

- **Exam note:** 30 frames/s divided among 16 cameras gives ≈ 1.9 frames/s per camera. The book says "about 4" and then "2–4". Remember the book’s figures.

**Two versions of NVR:**

| Type | What it is like | Advantages | Disadvantages / when to use |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Hardware-based NVR** | Recording circuitry and software + internal or external disk array. Records video and sometimes audio | Plug-and-play, almost no configuring. Easier to install and perhaps cheaper | Small campus networks. Historically, poor handling of backup and fail-over, although that is changing |
| **Software-based NVR** | Conventional servers with VMS acting as NVR | Well suited to large systems, campuses, or multiple campuses. Especially with centralized recording and, even more, if remote backup or fail-over is required | — |

**Storage of a software-based NVR:**

- Internal.
- DAS (direct-attached storage): disks connected directly to the server.
- NAS (network attached storage): disks on the same network as the server.
- **SAN (storage area network)**: dedicated high-performance device on its own separate network.
  - Made up of one or more video servers, a **SAN switch** (like a normal switch, but with much more throughput), and one or more recording units with disk arrays.
  - Scales from a few terabytes to systems that record many times the digital volume of the U.S. Library of Congress.

#### 14.7.3 Legacy Intercom Systems

- There are many types; the oldest are hard to integrate.
- Difficult example: hardwired intercom, with field stations that report to a dedicated master station.

**Solution when there is a single master station at the console:**

- Replace the master with a single intercom station.
- Use the output relays of the alarm/access system to switch it to each station, with icons on the GUI.
- It can queue the intercom when an alarm is activated: video and intercom respond at the same time.
- The operator just presses push-to-talk to speak with the alarm’s field station.
- It requires a **release call button**: it prevents jumping from an active call to the alarm’s call in mid-conversation.
- A **time-delay relay** cuts calls on its own in case someone forgets to release them.

**DRDP (direct ring-down phones):**

- Include elevator intercoms and emergency call stations.
- Phones programmed to dial **a single number** when picked up or when a button is pressed.
- They integrate with **PABX** (private automatic branch exchange) telephone switches, which receive and route the calls.

**Easy to integrate:**

- Intercoms with an **intercom matrix switch** at their core.
- **All** **VOIP** (voice over Internet protocol) intercoms.
- They have digital interfaces for development with an **SDK** (software development kit) or **API** (application program interface).
- Almost always, the large access control, VMS, and PSIM manufacturers already have those interfaces written.

**Requirements of an integrated intercom:**

- It is operated from the security system’s GUI.
- Intercoms linked to an alarm are queued automatically.
- An alarm **does not cut off** a call in progress; the operator is notified to choose which call to take.
- Going forward, the easiest approach is to use VOIP intercoms with interfaces already written in the security GUI or the PSIM.

#### 14.7.4 Security Networks and Legacy Integration

**Capabilities that the move from proprietary wiring to network wiring gave:**

- Real enterprise systems, crossing campus, community, state, and country boundaries.
- Greatly expanding one part of the system without running many new cables to the monitoring center.
- Deep automation with the right software. Especially the PSIM: integration, automation, and intelligent incident response.
- Much higher video quality than analog, especially with cameras far from the center and with megapixel cameras.
- Growing from a small system to practically any size.

| Class of network | Reach |
| --- | --- |
| **Local** (LAN, local area network) | Normally a single building |
| **Campus** | Several buildings on a single network |
| **Enterprise** | Several campuses and buildings |

**Network issues when integrating security systems:**

| Issue | What the book says |
| --- | --- |
| **Dedicated vs. shared network** with the business | Dedicated: much easier, because there is no concern about the effect of security data on business data (or vice versa). But more expensive: it does not share the capital cost with other services |
| **Segmentation** (security data private and separate) | Best ways: **VLAN** (virtual local area network) and **VPN** (virtual private network, encrypts all its data). On a shared network, encrypt everything **in transit and at rest** |
| **Bandwidth for video** | Follow the manufacturer’s recommendation based on each camera’s resolution and frame rate, summed per branch. Use **no more than 45%** of the bandwidth on any port, switch, or segment. That way there are no lost frames, artifacts, or impact on other data |
| **Multicast** | Some systems and configurations require the multicast protocol for video and audio |
| **Multigigabit architecture** | Before, a gigabit network seemed excessive; today multigigabit is normal. A competent network architect ensures capacity and data security |
| **Tree vs. ring** | Small networks: tree. Large networks: ring, or ring and tree |
| **Programming equipment to work together** | Three ways: (1) wiring with relay contacts and input points; (2) software integration with API and SDK; (3) integration via PSIM |
| **Security of data at rest** | Encrypt all security data on disk with a complex algorithm and key |

- With old systems, much of what seems impossible is achieved **with relays**.
- Case: a consultant created an intercom and video matrix switch for a campus. It queued calls and video on alarms, using only wired relays.
- He did it **almost 10 years** before intercom or video matrix switches existed. Lesson: relay wiring remains a valid way to integrate legacy systems.

### 14.8 Procurement

#### 14.8.1 Procurement Forms

- Three main forms: **sole source**, **RFP** (request for proposal), and **IFB** (invitation for bid).
- They vary depending on whether the buyer is a government agency or a commercial company.
- The form is chosen **before or at the start of the design phase**: it determines the level of detail of the construction documents.
- Large organizations that install, commission, test, and maintain their own systems: extensive design, but simple purchasing.
- In that case it is enough to issue hardware purchase orders, at prenegotiated prices, to prequalified vendors.

| Aspect | Sole source | RFP | IFB |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **When to use it** | A vendor has already been chosen. Small projects | The vendor is chosen by competition on several factors: cost, schedule, technical capability | Government and organizations that require bidding and award to the lowest qualified, responsive bidder |
| **Design detail** | Simple documents; they complement what the vendor already knows | Detailed design documents based on functional requirements. Generic and performance specifications; makes and models with the phrase **"or approved equal"** | **Extremely explicit**, for a firm, fixed price for materials and installation. Choosing makes and models, and the accuracy of the design, rests **solely with the design team** |
| **What the vendor delivers** | Negotiates equipment, installation, and service cost with the owner, and co-designs the system | Transmittal letter, **cost proposal**, and **technical proposal**. **Alternates** (alternative solutions) are allowed | Only the cost offer (bid), with unit prices and the requested breakdown. Perhaps its qualifications. **No** technical proposal or alternatives |
| **Award criterion** | Direct negotiation | **Best value**, not necessarily the cheapest | **Lowest price**, normally without negotiation |
| **Timing** | Saves time | Preparing the proposal: typical minimum of **3–4 weeks** on medium and large projects | More design time and cost, but only **1–2 weeks** of purchasing process |
| **What the owner gets** | — | A security **business partner**, not a one-time installer | — |

##### Sole Source Procurement

- The organization prequalifies a reputable contractor, designs the system with it, and negotiates equipment, installation, and service.

| Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- |
| Simple construction documents | Tends to focus only on hardware and technology; leaves to others the equally important subsystems of **personnel, procedures, and facilities** |
| Lower design cost for the owner | The owner may skip security planning and rely on a contractor with an interest in selling equipment |
| Saves time | Without competitive bidding, there is no way to compare prices |

- **Recommended only** if the owner can do its own security needs analysis and knows systems and prices well.

##### Request for Proposal (RFP)

- It is the **most common** method.
- If specific models are required for compatibility or uniformity with existing equipment, compliance with those items (responsiveness) may be the most important thing.
- It can be open to any contractor or limited to a prequalified list.
- To compare costs meaningfully: everyone responds first to the specified design; afterward, if they wish, they offer alternates.
- It is common to require that an alternate improve performance in a measurable way and cost **the same or less** than the base bid.
- The owner takes advantage of the contractor’s experience without losing control of the design.
- It can choose one or several proposers for the final negotiation.
- It does not obligate acceptance of the lowest offer: it seeks the **best value**.
- The organization defines value, but it must include **price, quality, experience, and schedule**.
- If price will not be the deciding factor, the RFP must say so.
- Preparing the response takes longer than in other forms, because there is a technical and a cost proposal.

##### Invitation for Bid (IFB)

- Since the IFB is decided on price, the design must leave no **loopholes**. If it does, bidders substitute inferior or inadequate equipment to win.
- It is awarded, normally without negotiation, to the lowest qualified bidder that followed the instructions.
- Typical procedure:
  1. **Sealed** bids, delivered at a specific time and place.
  2. Bid opening, often **public**.
  3. The **apparent winner** is announced.
  4. The contract is signed after checking that its proposal is complete, accurate, and meets the qualifications.

#### 14.8.2 Procurement Process

##### Pre-bid conference

- One representative from each contractor is invited.
- The owner or its consulting engineer thoroughly reviews the documents and leads a walk-through of the affected buildings and areas.
- If applicable, it is held at the installation site so they can see field conditions.
- Timing: **approximately one week after** issuing the documents for bidding. It gives time to review them and to incorporate information into the proposal.
- A representative of the design team records all questions and answers in the minutes.
- After the conference, questions are asked **in writing** and the answers are sent to **all** contractors.
- It is advisable to close questions **one week before** the proposal due date.
- **A single point of contact** must be named for all questions.

##### Evaluation of proposals

- Check that each proposal is complete and accurate.
- The contract may say that any inaccurate or incomplete proposal is rejected. But most commercial companies reject only on signs of incompetence or serious incompleteness.
- **Evaluation matrix:** columns = contractors; rows = main functions and components of the system. It verifies that each technical proposal covers everything requested.
- Another similar matrix compares prices and alternates.

**Life-cycle cost:**

- First, identify the system’s objectives and goals and its expected life.
- Simple form: **capital cost + maintenance cost over the useful life**.
- Typically, maintenance and warranty = **11% of the total capital construction cost** of the system.
- **Exam note:** the book does not clarify whether that 11% is annual or total.
- This calculation reveals whether the cheapest bidder cut its margin in order to recover it with high maintenance charges.

**If one proposal is much lower than the others, check:**

- Math errors.
- Quality of the proposed equipment.
- The contractor’s experience on projects of that size and complexity.
- The contractor’s understanding of the project.
- The contractor’s financial stability.

**Before awarding:**

- Verify the references of **all** contractors.
- Interview the front-runners, with their project manager and their site supervisor present (they may be the same person).
- In the interview, the designer should find out:
  - Is there good chemistry with the contractor’s representatives?
  - Do they have the experience and personality to work well with the other trades on the job?
  - How did they resolve problems on other projects? It helps to know what other clients think of those solutions.

**Negotiation and closing:**

- If procurement rules allow it, the final price is negotiated with the short list, **based on value**.
- If the contractor’s margin is too low, quality and responsiveness suffer.
- A good contractor with a realistic profit makes the extra effort to resolve implementation problems.
- Success requires a well-defined and well-executed procurement contract.
- The implementation cycle of a typical system lasts **18–24 months**.
- Final goal: a **business partnership** between owner and contractor, not a one-time sale.

### Key terms

system · planning and assessment · serial process · design/build · risk analysis · conceptual (schematic) design · design development (DD) · construction documents (CD) · basis of design (design basis) · requirements analysis · business case · return on investment (ROI) · payback · net present value (NPV) · design criteria · spare capacity · performance specification · mean time between failures (MTBF) · throughput · protection-in-depth · countermeasures · cost-effectiveness · outline specification · bill of quantities · contract (bid) documents · general / products / execution · MasterFormat · Division 28 Electronic Safety and Security · CADD · plans · elevations · details · risers · hardware schedules · ASTM Standard Practice for Security Engineering Symbols · order-of-magnitude estimate · MSRP · budgetary estimate · preliminary design estimate · final design estimate · contingency · RSMeans · life-cycle cost · performance bond · shop drawings · as-built (record) drawings · turnkey project · consultant vs. contractor · encoder/codec · MPEG-4 · H.264 · H.265 · megapixel · PTZ into history · video analytics · learning algorithm · baseline · VMS · ONVIF profiles A/C/G/Q/S/T/D/M · PSIM · GSOC · unified solution · video guard tour · video pursuit · legacy systems · 20 mA current loop · EPROM · PLC · FIPS 201 · dry contact · RS-232 · SQL · multiplexer · DVR · NVR · DAS / NAS / SAN · DRDP · PABX · VOIP · SDK · API · VLAN · VPN · multicast · sole source · RFP · IFB · or approved equal · alternates · base bid · best value · lowest qualified responsive bidder · pre-bid conference · evaluation matrix

## Capítulo 15 — System Implementation (págs. 535–560)

**Central idea:** The implementation of a physical protection system (PPS) begins right after the contractor is selected. It has five steps, from preparing the site to training personnel. The client controls each phase with reports, tests, and written permissions before accepting the system.

| # | Step (as named in the book) | Step (plain wording) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Site preparation | Site preparation |
| 2 | Contractor coordination | Contractor coordination |
| 3 | Installation | Installation |
| 4 | Testing and warranty issues | Testing and warranty |
| 5 | Training | Training |

- Each step requires the attention of three actors: the contractor, the security system designer, and the owner’s representative.

### 15.1 Site Preparation

- **It is the most important step** when installing PPS elements: site preparation and planning.
- Everything must already be in the design package and located on drawings: door hardware, card readers, sensors, panels, cameras, monitors, console equipment, gates, signage, and lighting.

| Step | Who | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Verify locations | Installation contractor | Reviews the locations on drawings and notes the necessary changes |
| 2. Resolve differences | Project manager + contractor | Examine the installation requirements and resolve every difference before moving forward |
| 3. Site visit | Contractor | Verifies that actual conditions match the design package |
| 4. Written report of changes | Contractor | Documents every change to the site or conditions that affects system performance |
| 5. Inventory of what exists | Contractor | Inspects, tests, and documents the existing protection equipment and signal lines that will be integrated into the new system |

**Rules for the contractor in this phase:**

- Takes no corrective action without written permission from the client.
- Items that do not work: delivers specification sheets or written functional requirements that support the finding, plus the estimated cost of correcting them.
- In the report notes the scheduled date of connection to existing equipment.
- Does not disconnect signal lines or equipment, or cause downtime, without prior written approval from the client.
- If a device or signal or control line fails after the contractor began working on it, the contractor diagnoses and corrects it.
- Pays for repairs caused by negligence or abuse of the client’s equipment.

**Exam note:** the book calls site preparation "the most important step" of the installation.

### 15.2 Contractor Coordination

- The contractor continuously coordinates three things: **resources** (people and material), **environment** (that the site is ready for installation), and the **project schedule**.
- The quality of coordination with other building disciplines can decide the success or failure of the entire installation.

**Security interfaces with other building systems (20 in the book):**

| # | System | Plain wording |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Elevators | Elevators |
| 2 | Escalators | Escalators |
| 3 | Doors and door hardware | Doors and hardware |
| 4 | Windows | Windows |
| 5 | Electrical | Electrical system |
| 6 | Lighting | Lighting |
| 7 | Life safety systems (fire alarm) | Life safety (fire alarm) |
| 8 | Egress locking systems release | Release of locks at exits |
| 9 | HVAC or building management system | Climate control (HVAC, Heating, Ventilation and Air Conditioning, outside the book) or building management system |
| 10 | Timekeeping | Time and attendance control |
| 11 | Parking | Parking |
| 12 | Landscaping | Gardening and landscaping |
| 13 | Fences and gates | Fences and gates |
| 14 | Enterprise resource planning (ERP) | Enterprise resource planning |
| 15 | Human resource management | Human resource management |
| 16 | Signage | Signage |
| 17 | Concrete/asphalt driveways | Concrete or asphalt vehicle access ways |
| 18 | Information technology systems | Information technology (IT) systems |
| 19 | Camera sightlines | Camera sightlines |
| 20 | Directory services (Active Directory, LDAP) | Directory services (LDAP, Lightweight Directory Access Protocol, outside the book) |

**Typical problem: the security contractor is usually the last to arrive on the project.**

| Situation | What the security contractor must do |
| --- | --- |
| The final date is fixed, but the precursor work by others is not ready on time | Immediately notify the owner’s representative. Push for the precursor work to be finished now. If necessary, request a schedule extension or a waiver of late-delay penalties |
| The environment is not suitable for the specified equipment | Notify the owner’s representative. Choose another location or a specialized enclosure |

- Example of an unsuitable environment: sensitive alarm or access control electronics in a boiler room. High temperature and humidity cause failures and shorten the panel’s life.

### 15.3 Installation

#### 15.3.1 Installation and Operation

- The project manager instructs the contractor to install all components, including customer-furnished equipment.
- In many cases an integrator can also do the installation.
- The contractor or integrator installs each subsystem according to the manufacturer’s instructions and applicable installation standards.
- The contractor provides all connectors, terminators, interconnections, services, and adjustments for a complete and operable system.
- After installing, the project manager tunes the system to the specific operation of the facility.

#### 15.3.2 Component Installation

- Reference standard: **NFPA 731**, *Standard for the Installation of Electronic Premises Security Systems* (2020), of the National Fire Protection Association (NFPA).

**Card readers**

- Mounting as required: surface, semi-flush, pedestal, or weatherproof.
- Installed according to local codes, the authority having jurisdiction (AHJ), and applicable local, state, or federal standards.

**Electric locks: comparison of requirements**

| Requirement | Electric door strike or bolt | Electromagnetic lock |
| --- | --- | --- |
| On power failure | Releases (fail safe) or stays locked (fail secure), depending on the application | Always releases automatically |
| Principle | Actuating solenoid energized with direct current (DC) | No moving parts; electromagnetism only |
| Holding force | The book does not state it | At least 1,200 lb (544 kg) |
| Voltage | 12 or 24 V DC | 12 or 24 V DC (actuating armature) |
| Maximum dissipation | 12 W | 12 W |
| Inrush current | Maximum 1 A | The book does not state it |
| Holding current | Maximum 500 mA | Maximum 500 mA |
| State change time | Maximum 500 ms, from fully secure to fully open | Maximum 300 ms, in both directions (secure to open and open to secure) |
| Line supervision | End-of-line resistor | End-of-line resistor |
| Tamper resistance | Mechanism inside hardened guard barriers against forced entry | Same |
| Mounting method | Single and double doors; mortise or rim hardware; right or left hand | Same |
| Size and weight | Compatible with standard door frame preparations | The book does not state it |
| Signaling to the system | Signal switches: notify if the bolt is not engaged or the strike is unlocked. They report forced entry | Integrated bond sensor or integrated request to exit (RTE), on some models |
| Other | — | Internal circuit that eliminates residual magnetism and inductive kickback. Connects to local processors without altering them externally, internally, or functionally |

- **Integrated bond sensor:** monitors like a door position switch (or "door contact").
- **Integrated RTE:** allows unrestricted egress and gives the access control more information. That way it reduces nuisance alarms from door activity.
- **Exam note:** strike = maximum 500 ms; electromagnet = maximum 300 ms. The 1 A inrush current is given only for the strike.

**Intrusion devices: where and how to install them**

| Device | Location and installation rules |
| --- | --- |
| Bell or alarm box | On the front of the building or in plain view of neighbors and passersby: it deters many burglars. High enough to be out of easy reach |
| Control panels | Ideally inside, in a secure place. Unreachable without a ladder. Near the main electrical power. Never over combustible material. With a lock and 24/7 alarm supervision via tamper switches |
| Passive infrared (PIR) motion detector | Do not expose it to infrared light. Avoid windows, fire, filament lamps, and heat sources such as radiators and heaters: they cause nuisance alarms |
| Dual-technology detector (PIR + microwave) | Locate it where both technologies are used, not just one. Microwaves detect through thin walls: do not use it where that happens. Also respect the PIR limits. Used to reduce nuisance alarms from a single sensor |
| Door and window contacts | Normally on exterior doors and windows; also on any that are vulnerable. They detect opening. Use the correct type: on a steel door and frame, an industrial metal surface-mount or recessed contact, not the aluminum or wood type. High-security and fire-rated types exist. Surface-mount types are prone to damage and tampering |
| Shock sensors | In areas susceptible to forced entry, such as door or window frames. Contacts detect opening, but not necessarily breakage. If the intruder could kick in a panel or break a glass, supplement with shock sensors or PIR |

**Interconnection of console video equipment**

| Case | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| Digital video over TCP/IP network | No individual connection longer than 328 ft (100 m) |
| From the patch panel to the PoE (Power over Ethernet) switch | Less than 10 ft (3.048 m) |
| Video cabling for interior and exterior cameras, and between PoE switches and patch panels | Augmented Category 6 (CAT 6A) with unshielded twisted pair (UTP) and RJ-45 connectors. Test each cable against the manufacturer’s performance standards |
| Analog video, path of 25 ft (7.6 m) or less | RG-59/U coaxial |
| Analog video, path longer than 25 ft | RG-11/U coaxial or fiber optic |

- Cables as short as possible, without straining the connectors.
- Rack-mounted equipment with sliding rails: cable long enough to fully extend the rails.
- NFPA 731 expands on how to connect equipment with "category" network cable.

**Surveillance cameras**

- The camera needs a lens with the correct focal length to view the protected area.
- Chapter 7 of NFPA 731 details how to choose camera locations and lenses.

Contractor steps:

1. Connect power and signal. With PoE, power travels over the same digital connection.
2. Fixed iris lens: adjust the f-stop for the best image.
3. Aim the camera to cover the protected area of interest.
4. Exterior fixed camera facing the rising or setting sun: aim it far enough below the horizon so it does not see the sun directly.
5. Focus the lens for a sharp image across the entire field of view.
6. Synchronize all cameras so the image does not "roll" when switching cameras. Not required on digital cameras.

**Exterior mounts (install according to the manufacturer, and also):**

| Exterior fixed mount | Exterior pan/tilt mount |
| --- | --- |
| Hardware sized to hold the mount, camera, and housing under the site’s maximum wind and ice load | Install the pan/tilt, the receiver/driver, and their appurtenances according to the manufacturer |
| Foundation for each camera pole, per specification | Hardware sized to hold the pan/tilt, camera, and housing under the maximum wind and ice load |
| Ground rod per pole, connected to the pole | Install the pan/tilt control wiring |
| Electrical and signal transmission wiring to the mount | Connect the pan/tilt to the control wiring and to alternating current (AC) |
| Connect signal lines and alternating current (AC) to the mount interfaces | — |
| Connect a pole wiring harness to the camera | — |

**Video recording and switching equipment** — according to the manufacturer, and also:

1. Connect all subassemblies.
2. Connect video signal inputs and outputs.
3. Terminate the video inputs as required.
4. Connect alarm signal inputs and outputs.
5. Connect control signal inputs and outputs for auxiliary equipment or secondary control or monitoring sites.
6. Load all site software: databases, operating parameters, and system, command, and application programs.
7. Program the video annotation of each camera.
8. Program screen layouts that group geographically nearby cameras on one screen.
9. Configure a video guard tour so the console officer can tour the facility with those screens.
10. Configure graphical user interface (GUI) icons on maps and plans: on clicking, they show the cameras in that area.

#### 15.3.3 Other Features and Considerations

**Conduit**

- All interior wiring goes in rigid galvanized steel conduit. This includes low-voltage wiring outside the monitoring console, racks, cabinets, boxes, and similar enclosures.
- It must comply with applicable standards: Underwriters Laboratories (UL) in the U.S. or the Canadian Electrical Code in Canada.
- Exception: wiring between components in the same rack or cabinet does not need conduit.
- Minimum conduit size: **1/2 in (1.3 cm)**.
- Tight-tapered and threaded joints. Threadless fittings or couplings are prohibited.
- Conduit boxes: cast metal or malleable iron with threaded hubs or bodies.
- Normally not permitted, unless required and approved by the client: electric metallic tubing (EMT), armored cable, nonmetallic-sheathed cable, and flexible conduit.
- Data transmission media do not share conduit, raceway, compartment, outlet box, or junction box with non-security building wiring.
- Do not power components with flexible cords or cord connections, unless required and approved by the client.

**Grounding**

- Per **NFPA 70, National Electrical Code** (2011), articles **250 and 800** (U.S.), or another applicable national standard. Plus the manufacturer’s requirements.
- All other circuits must test free of grounds.
- Objective: prevent ground loops, noise, and surges from affecting operation.

**Enclosure penetrations**

- Always from below, unless the design requires another direction.
- Seal with an approved waterproof sealant: penetrations of interior enclosures with transition of conduit from inside to outside, and all penetrations of exterior enclosures.
- The conduit riser ends at a hot-dipped galvanized metal cable terminator.
- The terminator is filled with the sealant recommended by the cable manufacturer, without damaging the cable.

**Cold galvanizing**

- Any field weld or brazing on factory-galvanized boxes, enclosures, and conduit is covered with cold galvanizing paint.
- The paint must have **at least 95% zinc by weight**.

**System startup: do not energize until these 5 points are met**

| # | Requirement before energizing |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | All equipment configured according to the manufacturer’s instructions |
| 2 | Visual inspection: no defective equipment installed and no loose connections |
| 3 | Wiring tested and verified as properly connected |
| 4 | Grounding and transient protection verified as properly installed and connected |
| 5 | Power supplies verified for voltage, phasing, and frequency |

**Data deliverables before testing**

| Deliverable | What it includes | Deadline |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Configuration data | The contractor loads into the database everything needed to operate. Delivers it to the client in suitable formats, with its field surveys and other pertinent information | Completed forms to the client, for review and approval, **at least 30 days before the database test** |
| Graphics | When required, the contractor creates and installs them with contract and field data. Enough detail for the operator to assess the alarm. Color printed samples of each type of graphic, of **at least 8 x 10 in (20 x 25 cm)** | Samples to the client, for review and approval, **at least 30 days before acceptance tests** |

**Signal line and data transmission system (DTS) supervision**

- The system supervises all signal lines and DTS: it watches for changes or disturbances in the signal.
- It also monitors the conditions of **UL 1076**, *Standard for Proprietary Burglar Alarm Units and Systems* (1995), for line security equipment.
- It generates an alarm on a **current change of 10% or more**.
- It generates an alarm on opening, closing, short circuit, or grounding of signal lines and DTS.

**Housing by location**

| Use | Enclosure requirement |
| --- | --- |
| Indoor sensors | Protection against dust, falling dirt, and dripping noncorrosive liquids |
| Outdoor sensors | Protection against windblown dust, rain and splashing, hose-directed water, and ice formation |
| Indoor electronics | **NEMA 250-2014**, *Enclosures for Electrical Equipment (1000 Volts Maximum)*, **Type 12** |
| Outdoor electronics | NEMA 250-2014, **Type 4X** |
| Corrosive environment (per NEMA 250-2014) | **Metal** enclosure, NEMA 250-2014, **Type 4X** |
| Hazardous environment | Enclosure that meets the manufacturer’s requirements for that specific hazardous environment |

- NEMA = National Electrical Manufacturers Association.

**Nameplates**

- Consider laminated plastic nameplates or asset tags on the main components.
- Each plate identifies the device and its location in the system. It is attached **inside** the component’s enclosure.
- In addition, each main component carries a corrosion-resistant plate with: manufacturer’s name and address, type or style, model or serial number, and catalog number.

**Tamper switches**

- They are fitted to enclosures, cabinets, housings, boxes, and accessories with a hinged door or removable cover that contain circuits, connections, or power supplies.
- They are cover-operated, resist corrosion, and trigger an alarm when the door or cover is moved.
- Enclosure and switch work together: there must be no direct line of sight to the internal components before the switch activates.

| # | The tamper switch must… |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Be inaccessible until it is activated |
| 2 | Have concealed hardware, so its location cannot be seen from outside |
| 3 | Be connected to supervised circuits in a 24-hour zone, annunciated and supervised separately, regardless of protection mode |
| 4 | Be spring-loaded and held closed by the door or cover |
| 5 | Open the circuit when the door or cover is disturbed |

**Maintenance locks**

- Locks on the system enclosures, for maintenance.
- Type: UL-listed round key with three double pins, one mushroom, or three plain. Alternative: conventional five-pin lock with five-point, three-position side bar.
- Keys stamped "DO NOT DUPLICATE". The key can be removed only in the locked position.
- Maintenance locks are keyed alike.
- Risk: manufacturers often deliver panels with the same key for all customers. It is convenient for integrators, but other installations receive the same key.
- Solution: change all locks to ones keyed alike specifically for the building or facility. It gives more control and security.
- Keys are managed with a key control plan: it is the best defense of locking devices because it accounts for all keys on a regular basis.
- **Exam note:** "keyed alike" is recommended, but site-specific, never the factory one.

**Wire and cable**

- The contractor provides all cable that is not client equipment.
- Complies with national standards (NFPA 70 in the U.S.), unless the end user has stricter standards.
- Installation per security standards and each piece of equipment’s manual:
  - **NFPA 70** — National Electrical Code.
  - **UL 681** — installation and classification of burglar and holdup alarm systems.
  - **UL 1037** — antitheft alarms and devices.
  - **UL 1076** — proprietary burglar alarm units and systems.
- Service points that allow locating a fault in **less than 20 minutes**.
- Minimum conduit: 1/2 in (1.3 cm).

**Local area network (LAN) cabling**

- According to the Telecommunications Industry Association (TIA): **TIA-568 A or B, category 5** (2012), or the country’s national standard.
- **Exam note:** here the book asks for category 5 for LAN, but for video it requires augmented CAT 6 (15.3.2). They are not the same.

**Quality assurance**

| Codes the work must comply with in the U.S. (7) | Organizations whose standards the materials must meet in the U.S. (3) |
| --- | --- |
| Current National Electrical Code (NEC) | NEMA (National Electrical Manufacturers Association) |
| Applicable federal, state, and local codes | American National Standards Institute (ANSI) |
| Current uniform building code | Underwriters Laboratories, Inc. (UL) |
| Local electrical code, if applicable | — |
| Occupational Safety and Health Act (OSHA) standards | — |
| Any other code in force at the job site | — |
| Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) | — |

- General rule: all work complies with regional and national codes.

#### 15.3.4 Tuning the Alarm System

- After installation, the system **must** be tuned to the facility’s operation. Otherwise it generates too many nuisance alarms and confuses personnel instead of helping them.
- Tuning it requires knowing how the facility operates, which employees come and go, and what activities take place.

| Measure | What to do |
| --- | --- |
| Time periods for alarms | The security manager runs periodic reports and reviews the alarm history: nuisance alarms, location, frequency, and time. If patterns appear because of daily operations, he or she adjusts the alarm operating hours so as not to distract personnel |
| Authorized personnel | If they are trusted and can enter at any time, their alarms are shunted so they are not generated |
| Nuisance alarms | Causes: employee errors (opening the wrong door, leaving doors open, forgetting to disarm subsystems) and door hardware that fails. Remedies: signs such as "keep doors closed"; adjust patrol schedules to surprise whoever leaves doors open; have patrols check that all doors are closed; review the maintenance program, with frequent door inspections and prompt replacement of defective parts |
| Improper application | Poorly chosen security or fire alarm components. Examples: standard motion detector in a harsh environment; microwave sensor in a room with a hallway outside. Those devices must be changed |
| Reducing alarms | See the three cases below |

**Cases for reducing alarms:**

- **Loading dock door** that arrives nonstop at the monitoring center during working hours: move the monitoring of that point to the area’s personnel.
- **Mechanical or utility rooms** with maintenance access: procedure to notify the monitoring center which area and for how long. That way the operator temporarily ignores those points.
- **Secured doors with controlled movement of material:** mandatory procedure, such as a call to the central station. An officer may be needed to control access.

### 15.4 Testing and Warranty Issues

- Implementation team tests can cover equipment, personnel, procedures, or any combination.
- **Ideal acceptance test:** takes the system to the established limits of the site-specific threats. It simulates real threat conditions and gives conclusive evidence of effectiveness.

| Type of test | What it determines | Precautions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Equipment performance testing | Whether the equipment works, has adequate sensitivity, and meets design and performance objectives | Always coordinate with facility personnel. May require special test equipment |
| Personnel performance tests | Whether the procedures work, whether personnel know and follow them, and whether personnel and equipment interact well | Some are done without personnel knowing: coordinate them well and take care with safety |

- **Exam note:** it is not enough for a component to meet the manufacturer’s standard if it proves ineffective in the test.

**Four types of test described in the section:**

1. Predelivery or factory acceptance tests (FAT, outside the book).
2. Site acceptance tests (SAT, outside the book).
3. Reliability or availability tests.
4. Post-implementation tests.

**Seven factors for deciding which tests to do:**

1. Prioritize the site-specific threats.
2. Identify the worst-case scenarios: lowest probability of detection, least delay, and the different routes into the facility.
3. Identify the system functions most critical to protecting the assets: detection, assessment, or delay.
4. Determine the assumed probability of detection of each subsystem and its vulnerability to being defeated.
5. Determine the incident assessment time: immediate or deferred.
6. Identify the last points where the adversary must be detected so the protection force can respond in time.
7. Compare the vulnerabilities with the findings and resolution of past inspections and incidents.

**General rules for tests:**

- At the close of each phase (factory, site, reliability), the originals of all data go to the client before the test is approved.
- The client gives the vendor or integrator documentation with the tests required during installation and commissioning.
- **Commissioning:** testing every alarm point and every automatic function of the new system.
- That documentation details the personnel, equipment, instruments, and supplies for the acceptance test. It also states who witnesses the performance verification and the reliability verification.
- The contractor needs written permission from the client to move to the next phase.

#### 15.4.1 Predelivery or Factory Acceptance Testing

- Depending on size and complexity, the client may ask the contractor to assemble a test system with some or all of the components.
- Objective: demonstrate that performance meets what was specified, with approved factory test procedures.
- The client designs the tests, or the contractor does if the client requires it.
- It is scheduled **before any installation**. The client attends and observes.
- The model numbers tested must be identical to those to be delivered on site.
- At the end, the originals of all data and results go to the client to approve the test.
- The report correlates commands, stimuli, and responses to allow logical interpretation.

| Factory test setup (7 items) | Equipment for testing video (9 items) |
| --- | --- |
| All the monitoring equipment of the security control center | At least 4 cameras and each specified lens type |
| At least one link of each type of data transmission, with its equipment, to represent an integrated system | 3 video monitors |
| As many local processors (field panels) as the site design calls for | Video recorder, if the system requires it |
| At least one sensor of each type used | Video switcher with input and output modules and control and application software, if required |
| Enough sensor simulators (manual or software) to match the number of sensors in the design | Alarm input panel, if required |
| At least one terminal device of each type used | Pan/tilt mount and controller, if there are pan/tilt cameras |
| At least one access configuration (portal) of each type, with all its interface devices | Auxiliary camera circuit equipment: equalizing amplifiers, video loss or presence detectors, terminators, ground loop correctors, surge suppressors, and other in-line devices |
| — | Cabling for all components |

- The client requires a report with all tests and results.
- Every deficiency is resolved to the client’s satisfaction **before** installation and the acceptance test.

#### 15.4.2 Site Acceptance Testing

- The client requires the contractor, integrator, or installer to provide a plan to: calibrate and test all components, verify the data transmission system, and install, commission, and test the system.
- **Before** the on-site test, the contractor delivers a report with the results of functional tests, diagnostics, and calibrations.
- That report includes written certification that the entire system is calibrated, tested, and ready. It arrives **at least 2 weeks before** starting, with a copy of the approved procedures.
- With those procedures, the contractor demonstrates that the system meets all the physical and functional requirements of the contract.
- With performance tests it demonstrates reliability and operability at the specified throughput of each access point.
- It also demonstrates the specified **Type I and Type II** error rates. Outside the book: Type I = false rejection of an authorized person; Type II = false acceptance of an unauthorized person.
- It calculates the nuisance and false alarm rates: they must be below the specified maximums, at the specified probability of detection of each subsystem.
- It starts only with the client’s written approval. The client can end it as soon as the system fails.
- If approved, the contractor delivers reports and documentation before starting further tests.

**Acceptance test checklist (14 items):**

1. Data and video transmission system, and signal or control wiring: installed, tested, and approved.
2. Remote control or monitoring stations, or remote switching panels: work, communicate with the monitoring center, and perform their functions.
3. Video switcher fully functional and its software programmed for the site.
4. All system software functions work well.
5. Operate all electrical and mechanical controls and verify that they do what was designed.
6. All video sources and outputs give a full-bandwidth signal.
7. All input signals properly terminated.
8. All cameras properly aimed and focused.
9. Cameras facing the rising or setting sun aimed below the horizon, not seeing the sun directly.
10. If vehicles travel near the assessment areas: verify nighttime assessment. Check whether headlights cause blooming (loss of detail from a spot that is too bright) or degrade the image.
11. Cameras synchronized: the image does not roll when switching.
12. Interface with the intrusion detection subsystem functional, with automatic camera call-up at all designated alarm points.
13. With pan/tilt: limit stops properly adjusted and pan/tilt or zoom controls operational.
14. With pre-position controls: home positions properly set and tested, including the auto home function.

- At the end, the contractor delivers another report of functional tests, diagnostics, and calibrations. It includes written certification that the system is ready for the **reliability test**, with a copy of the approved acceptance procedures.

#### 15.4.3 Reliability or Availability Testing

- It is advisable to do it in alternating phases of testing and evaluation, to validate the tests and the corrections.
- It does not begin until the client notifies in writing of three things: acceptance completed, training finished (if specified), and outstanding deficiencies corrected.
- The contractor provides **a representative available 24 hours**, including weekends and holidays if needed.
- The client ends the test when the system does not perform as specified.

| Phase | What happens | Key rules |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Phase I Testing | **24 hours a day, 15 consecutive calendar days**, holidays included. The system must operate as specified | No repairs except with the client’s written authorization. No failures: goes straight to Phase II with written permission |
| Phase I Assessment | The contractor identifies, explains, and repairs all failures. Delivers a written report: nature of each failure, corrective action, results, and when to resume | About **7 days** later, the client convenes a review meeting on site. The contractor demonstrates the corrections with parts of the acceptance test. The client sets a restart date or requires Phase I to be repeated |
| Phase II Testing | **24 hours a day, 15 consecutive calendar days**, holidays included | No repairs except with the client’s written authorization |
| Phase II Assessment | Same failure report as in Phase I | Meeting about 7 days later. Parts of the site acceptance test are repeated. The client sets a restart or requires Phase II to be repeated. Retesting does not occur without written notice from the client. After each retest, the Phase II assessment is repeated |

- If the Phase I retest comes out with no failures, it moves to Phase II with written permission. If not, the cycle of testing and assessment continues until completed.
- Minimum duration: 30 days of continuous testing (15 + 15).
- **Exam note:** the book says "about a week" for the review meeting; it is not an exact deadline.

#### 15.4.4 Post-Implementation Testing

| Test | What it is | Example or detail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Operational tests | Periodic; they test that the system operates well. They do **not** verify equipment specifications | Yes: alarm when a protected door opens, sensor that activates when walking, tamper or duress button that work. No: the sensor’s detection pattern or the exact opening distance before alarming |
| Performance tests | Verify that the equipment meets its specifications; they thus demonstrate effectiveness | Measure parameters such as the probability of detection. May require meters, calibrated instruments, or special methods |
| Post-maintenance tests | Operational tests after preventive or corrective maintenance | Confirm that the system works according to specifications |
| Subsystem tests | Confirm that large parts of the system work together as designed | Detection with normal response; detection with delays |
| Limited scope tests | Divide a complex system into subsystems or segments that are tested separately | Useful when testing everything at once is difficult and slow |
| Evaluation tests | Periodic and **independent**; they validate the vulnerability analysis and that overall effectiveness is maintained | **At least once a year** |

- **Exam note:** the operational test proves that it "works"; the performance test measures "how well it meets the specification". The post-maintenance test is an operational test.

#### 15.4.5 Warranty Issues

| Requirement | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Coverage | Repair, correct, or replace any defect |
| Duration | **12 months** from issuance of the certificate of practical completion; that is where the owner’s acceptance begins |
| Usual arrival time | On site within **4 hours** of the problem report |
| Spare parts | Enough inventory to repair or replace quickly. Waiting for the manufacturer is **not acceptable** |
| Maintenance | The warranty includes full maintenance according to the manufacturer’s recommendations |
| Contact | The contractor gives the client the details for reporting problems and defects |

**Service visit log:** the contractor records them in a database and gives the client report forms with 5 fields:

1. Date and time of the failure report.
2. Nature of the reported failure.
3. Date and time of the visit.
4. Actual failure found.
5. Corrective work performed.

**Three questions about the warranty:**

1. Is the service provided by the system vendor or by a third party?
2. Are its service levels consistent with those of system maintenance?
3. If something fails under warranty, what happens to the maintenance services provided by other parties?

### 15.5 Training

- No technological or procedural precaution works if it is not executed well.
- With good training, personnel prevent incidents, respond well to those that occur, and help more in recovery.
- Without training, personnel tend to create security risks unintentionally.

#### 15.5.1 General Training Requirements

- The client asks the contractor or integrator for a proposal of operation and maintenance courses for designated personnel.
- It must cover all installed systems, system administration, incident response, and the system overview.
- If there is video along with other systems, its training takes place at the same time and within that of the others.
- Manuals and aids for each participant, plus several extra copies for filing at the project site.
- Each manual includes: agenda, objectives defined per lesson, and a detailed description of the content of each lesson.
- The contractor provides audiovisual equipment, materials, and supplies. Delivers a copy of the audiovisual material in the same medium used in class.
- The contractor recommends how many course days and how many hours per day.
- The client approves content and schedule **at least 30 days before** the training.
- Instructors: certified by the manufacturer of the applicable hardware and software, with experience at other installations and approved by the client.

#### 15.5.2 Training Topics

| Course | Who it is for / focus | Content | When |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| System Administration | Administrators. Define and apply operating parameters and make adjustments | All functions: design and issuance of ID badges, enrollment of cardholders and access levels, door programming, alarm configuration, data and reports, database backups. With video: architecture and configuration, hardware specifications, diagnosis and correction of faults | Class 1 completed about **30 days before** the factory test (if there is one) or the site test. Class 2, **1 week before** the acceptance test. They take part in the acceptance and reliability tests |
| System Monitoring | Operators. Daily operation | Monitor alarm events, personnel access, and door status; assess, respond to, and clear alarms and messages; run routine reports. Topics: see list of 7 below | **Before** the acceptance test. They take part in the acceptance and reliability tests |
| Alarm Assessment and Dispatch | Operators. Assess the cause of each alarm condition and handle it properly | Based on the types of alarm the subsystems can give | Prerequisite: client and contractor already have alarm assessment and response procedures |
| Incident Response | Security response force | Based on the scenarios it may encounter when responding to an alarm | Prerequisite: incident response procedures already developed |
| System Troubleshooting and Maintenance | Usually given by the vendor or integrator; the client can also create or request it. Internal workings so trainees can repair most problems | Communications and network diagnostics; device configuration and programming; controller installation, wiring, and diagnostics; software fault resolution. Topics: see list of 5 below | On site, about **2 weeks before** the reliability test. The trainees take part in it |
| IT Functions | IT personnel. How security works on the local or wide area network (LAN/WAN) | Topologies and communications of each subsystem; bandwidth impact of functions such as digital video storage; maintaining data security | The book does not state it |
| System Overview | The book does not state the audience | How the system supports security goals; how it was adapted to operational requirements; how to communicate security awareness to all employees | The book does not state it |

**System Monitoring: 7 topics**

1. General configuration of the system hardware.
2. Functional operation of the system.
3. Operator commands.
4. Database entry.
5. Report generation.
6. Alarm assessment.
7. Simple diagnostics.

- The course includes the specific configuration of the installed subsystems and how to operate them.
- Outcome 1: with documentation, the operator performs elementary operations with guidance and describes the **general** hardware architecture.
- Outcome 2: the trainee starts up the system, operates it, recovers it after a failure, and describes the **specific** hardware architecture.
- **Exam note:** the book gives two different outcomes for the same course (general with guidance vs. specific with recovery after failure).

**System Troubleshooting and Maintenance: 5 topics**

1. Physical layout of each piece of equipment.
2. Diagnostic and fault resolution procedures.
3. Repair instructions.
4. Preventive maintenance procedures and schedules.
5. Calibration procedures.

**Timeline of training vs. tests**

| Moment | Milestone |
| --- | --- |
| ≥ 30 days before the course | Client approves content and schedule |
| \~30 days before the factory or site test | Administration class 1 ends |
| Before the acceptance test | Monitoring course |
| 1 week before the acceptance test | Administration class 2 |
| \~2 weeks before the reliability test | On-site maintenance course |
| Before the reliability test | Specified training completed (requirement of 15.4.3) |

### Key terms

site preparation · design package · owner's representative · precursor work · customer-furnished equipment · integrator · NFPA 731 · authority having jurisdiction (AHJ) · fail safe · fail secure · electric strike · electromagnetic lock · end-of-line resistor · inrush current · holding current · inductive kickback · integrated bond sensor · request to exit (RTE) · passive infrared (PIR) · dual-technology detector · shock sensor · Power over Ethernet (PoE) · Augmented Category 6 · RG-59/U · RG-11/U · video guard tour · rigid galvanized steel conduit · electric metallic tubing (EMT) · NFPA 70 · cold galvanizing · system startup · line supervision · UL 1076 · NEMA 250 Type 12 · NEMA 250 Type 4X · tamper switch · keyed alike · key control plan · TIA-568 · nuisance alarm · shunt · equipment performance test · personnel performance test · commissioning · factory acceptance test · site acceptance test · Type I / Type II error · throughput · blooming · reliability or availability test · operational test · performance test · post-maintenance test · subsystem test · limited scope test · evaluation test · certificate of practical completion · system administration · system monitoring · alarm assessment and dispatch · incident response

## Capítulo 16 — Follow-On and Support Activities (págs. 561–572)

**Central idea:** Many security systems fail simply because of poor maintenance. Maintenance is planned from the design stage and budgeted from the purchase. A successful program has three elements: maintenance, evaluation, and replacement.

**Starting points (chapter introduction)**

- Typical case: sophisticated systems (critical infrastructure, religious destinations, shopping centers, or government centers) that degrade without maintenance after being installed.
- Cause: the installer came from far away and has already left; local resources are insufficient or are not even used.
- It also happens in developed countries: maintenance is ignored at purchase and afterward.
- Relying on on-call repairs leads to the premature death of the system through deferred maintenance.
- Every system needs regular maintenance; planning for it is an essential part of design.
- Support priority: the equipment whose failure has the greatest impact on the overall system.
- Choose systems according to the support the local market can provide after installation.
- If no local vendor can: allocate a maintenance budget, include spare parts in the original program, and hire a dedicated maintenance technician.

### 16.1 Maintenance

- Greater dependence on PPS (physical protection systems), with more scale and complexity, requires careful maintenance planning.
- Software is never free of errors. Hardware is not immune to electrical or mechanical failures.
- The security investment must include maintenance services and a plan to reduce the probability and impact of failures.
- Effective program: the facility's own technicians (facility technicians), supported by contractor representatives, perform all testing, maintenance, calibration, and repairs.
- Signs of a poor maintenance program:
  - frequent system failures
  - superficial testing procedures (cursory testing)
  - an excessive number of components awaiting repair
- When negotiating maintenance contracts, seek legal advice when needed.

**Two types of PPS maintenance**

| Type | What it is | What it includes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Remedial maintenance or service contracts | Corrects failures and returns the system to operation when a hardware or software component fails | 1) A maintenance function that receives and logs user requests when there is a problem; 2) investigate the problem; 3) resolve it directly or arrange for the fix if a third party is required; 4) restore the system or return its use to the customer; 5) update the documentation on the problem and its solution |
| Preventive maintenance | Scheduled maintenance to keep hardware and software in good condition | 1) Keep electromechanical equipment in good condition (fans, filters, backup batteries, door hardware; the book leaves the list open); 2) replace hardware components to maintain current specifications (e.g., engineering changes); 3) update system and application software (bug fixes, new releases); 4) test and analyze system reports (error logs, self-tests, parameters, performance measures); 5) maintain the system documentation |

- A system maintenance agreement normally includes **both** types.
- All components must work together. The failure of one may not matter or may bring down the whole system.
- Therefore the agreement must address noncritical problems as well as those that cause major business disruption.

**Separate contracts vs. a single contractor**

| Approach | What happens |
| --- | --- |
| Former practice: contract separately for hardware, software, networks, and services | With complex, integrated systems it is harder to identify the failure. There is finger-pointing and time is lost |
| Often the best solution: a single contractor/integrator as the single point of contact | Diagnoses the cause and manages the solution. May involve third parties: component vendors, telecommunications, or application software providers |

#### 16.1.1 Remedial Maintenance

**Maintenance Plan**

- It can be carried out by: the manufacturer, a systems integrator, a maintenance contractor, the users, or any combination.
- Who is responsible for each task must be set in writing:
  1. fault identification
  2. diagnose and verify the problem
  3. correct the fault
  4. test the repair
  5. repair logging
  6. coordinate and follow up on maintenance
- Coordination is the most critical item: each technology may require different skills depending on where the failure occurs.
- It is advisable to train staff in preventive maintenance: this way they understand and operate the system better.
- Ideally, the vendor provides this training, the factory acceptance tests, and the operating procedures during purchase and installation.
- Give technicians time to update their skills by exchanging knowledge with other technicians during installation.
- The plan must provide for periodic tuning of the system to each installation. Objective: eliminate nuisance alarms and false alarms that affect those who monitor and respond.

**When contracting for maintenance, the client and contractor must:**

1. Agree on the basis of the contract document.
2. Document in detail the system components that will be maintained.
3. Establish the service level agreement (SLA): response time.
4. Set the service levels for each component or subsystem.
5. Define the roles and responsibilities of the parties.
6. Agree on prices and payments.
7. Define how the agreement will be managed and administered.

**Service Levels**

- Each failure has a different impact. Example: a camera failing matters less than the communications server for the entire network failing.
- But a workstation may support an essential service and require high priority.
- Client and contractor jointly develop the support plan, service level, and response time for each component.
- Rule: the greater the impact of the failure, the higher the level of support.
- Extreme case: an outstanding engineer on site with all the spare parts at hand.
- Most likely: an immediate callback from a technician and arrival on site within **2 to 4 hours**.
- Levels must be realistic, measurable, and consistent with the business, especially if they involve travel.
- Guaranteeing a response in **under 4 hours** drives costs up quickly (staff hours, travel, and equipment).
- Components can be left out of the full plan. Example: PCs already covered by the computer vendor's contract. Excluding or lowering the level requires careful thought.
- Levels and costs depend on:
  - the distance between the system and the vendor (one in another city greatly lengthens the response)
  - the ability to diagnose and repair remotely
  - whether support outside working hours is required

**Roles and Responsibilities**

- Main goal of the agreement: for the system to operate at optimum capacity with minimum downtime.
- Another goal: minimize the number of parties managing the program.
- The roles of all parties are clearly defined, documented, and agreed with the maintenance provider.
- Sometimes the maintenance provider is the same as the hardware and software vendor, or its agent. The usual arrangement: the integrator assumes maintenance as the prime contractor.
- The plan must: set the responsibilities of everyone, establish the organization's central point of contact, and facilitate agreements among the parties.
- Parties that may be involved:
  1. manufacturer or hardware vendor
  2. systems integrator
  3. provider of system tools and utilities (if not supplied by the hardware vendor)
  4. application software vendor
  5. building owner (power, water, and telephone/data circuits)
  6. air conditioning provider (for the monitoring room equipment)
  7. agent for the UPS (uninterruptible power supply) or emergency generator
  8. LAN and WAN (local area network and wide area network) equipment and service provider
  9. telecommunications provider (telephones, leased data circuits, access to private and public networks)
  10. PC vendor
  11. cabling provider
- Ideally, the main vendor or the integrator manages all of them when resolving failures or performing scheduled maintenance.
- In practice this is not always appropriate or cost-effective. Each client includes or excludes third-party responsibilities.

**Prices and Payments**

- Factors that set the contractor's rate:
  1. complexity of the system
  2. cost of spare parts
  3. estimated number of failures per year
  4. frequency of product use
  5. number of system users
  6. age of the system
  7. geographic location and the possibility of online diagnosis and support
- Standard or high-volume systems: the rate may be a fixed percentage of the purchase price.
- Alternative: bill travel and lodging separately.
- Economies of scale: more units or customers in an area allow savings in travel, spare parts inventory, staff, training, and setup costs.
- A possible payment cycle: a fixed fee for an advance period (month, quarter, or year) plus an allowance or formula for:
  1. discounts for longer-term contracts
  2. credits if target response times are not met
  3. extra costs for travel, lodging, or work not covered
  4. service calls outside the agreed hours (call-outs)
- Over time, most factors increase (inflation, product aging). Some decrease.
