- The usual practice: price review milestones in the contract, with a prior understanding of the size of the increases.
- Typically not covered: misuse, vandalism, lack of training due to staff turnover, and acts of God. The book leaves the list open.

**Administration**

- The environment changes during the contract. The agreement must be monitored and updated.
- The security manager reviews the agreement regularly, measures the vendor, and reviews the scope. The review covers:
  1. vendor performance against the service levels, and system performance, in the prior period
  2. call logging and account management
  3. changes to services or levels requested by the client or recommended by the vendor
  4. changes to the system's equipment or software list
  5. the client's future plans (staffing, new developments, upgrades, special events, changes in priorities)

**Documentation**

- The manufacturer or integrator delivers complete configuration documentation: switch settings, wiring diagrams, spare parts lists, and installation steps.
- All subsystems must have advanced diagnostics that identify defective components so they can be replaced in the field.
- In large, decentralized systems, remote diagnostics help a great deal.
- Subscribing to a hardware and software upgrade service guarantees incorporating the latest engineering change orders and field change orders.
- Those orders can affect the budget and fall outside the scope. The project manager reviews them and approves them if they extend the life of the system.
- All documentation is protected and controlled so as not to disclose the countermeasures installed: the SOW (statement of work), contracts, work orders, diagrams, shop drawings showing sensors, control equipment, power supplies and junction boxes, and cable routes.

**Records**

- Keep accurate records, especially of maintenance and operators. They serve to:
  - know which parts fail or cause problems for operators (weak points and deficiencies)
  - keep track of costs that justify replacing unreliable systems
- Records receive the same security controls as the system documentation.

| Record | Content and use |
| --- | --- |
| Maintenance records | For all components, cross-referenced with subsystems, to see repair patterns. They indicate what to inspect closely during preventive maintenance. Whoever maintains the system records each task and builds cumulative chronological records by major component and for the complete system. A running log per device with calibration, repair, and programming data. Available for on-site inspection, as proof of planned and systematic adjustments and repairs |
| System operator records | Operators' problems with certain subsystems or components. They are analyzed periodically to detect problem subsystems and update the operating procedures |

**Spare Parts**

- Buy spare parts and repair equipment in advance (perhaps with the original purchase) to reduce downtime.
- This is critical in countries with customs delays.
- The right quantity depends on: the time needed to obtain them, the cost of holding inventory, and the probability of replacement.
- Rule of thumb: **about 5% of the equipment capital cost of a site, every year**, to buy spare parts.
- Inventory must follow the vendor's recommendations.
- Standardizing devices reduces inventory and training. Ways: a sole-source relationship with a vendor, if requirements and the market are known, or strict purchasing specifications.
- A centralized budget for unexpected device replacements is recommended.

**Maintenance Manuals**

- The contractor delivers a maintenance manual for all the equipment covering: inspection, periodic preventive maintenance, fault diagnosis, and repair or replacement of defective components.

#### 16.1.2 Preventive Maintenance

- Develop checklists that incorporate the manufacturers' guidelines.
- Applies to almost all of the infrastructure. Examples: replacing batteries and bulbs, cleaning camera lenses and housings.
- The budget and resources must consider security technicians **and** IT (information technology) support.
- To save trips: perform preventive maintenance together with remedial maintenance whenever possible.

**Typical preventive maintenance tasks**

1. Check cabinets: equipment (e.g., power supplies) must have voltage warning labels.
2. Verify that the security system's warning labels, if any, are in place.
3. Check enclosures: damage, unauthorized openings, and corrosion of metal parts. Repair and paint.
4. Check the air passages and remove obstructions.
5. Check, investigate, and resolve unusual odors.
6. Check the lock devices. Repair.
7. When operating and testing the equipment: listen for, investigate, and resolve unusual noises.
8. Check that the equipment mounting is correct.
9. Look for loose cables and components.
10. Check electrical connections for discoloration or corrosion. Repair.
11. Check the electrical insulation for discoloration and degradation. Repair.
12. Check the grounding (conductors and connections). Repair.
13. Clean the equipment: remove debris, dust, and other deposits from non-encapsulated components (e.g., ventilated control panels).
14. Tighten the electrical connections.
15. Give all electrical connections the design torque.
16. Perform periodic operational tests to prove the subsystem works (not necessarily to verify specifications).
17. Open protected doors.
18. Enter protected rooms.
19. Test metal detectors by passing metal through the detection zone.
20. Test fence disturbance sensors by shaking the fence.
21. Visually and operationally check the video system: switchers, peripherals, interface panels, recorders, monitors, electrical and mechanical controls, and the image quality of each camera.
22. Test duress buttons and tamper switches.

**Adjustments**

- Periodic adjustments may be needed for the system to operate effectively.
- Example: detection patterns of motion sensors, according to test results.
- Example: camera varifocal lenses, to view the correct scene.

**Backup Equipment**

- Subsystems require power: there must be an auxiliary source of batteries or generators.
- Switchover must be immediate and automatic if the main source fails.
- With a generator as the only backup, there is almost never an immediate switchover. Batteries are required; the generator takes the load when it reaches full power.
- Regular testing and maintenance program: periodic tests of equipment and circuits (including the backup) and a thorough inspection by qualified personnel.
- A record of each test: date, name of the person who performed it, and results.

**Exam note:** the generator does not provide instant switchover; batteries cover the gap until the generator delivers full power.

### 16.2 Evaluation

- When the system's useful life ends, the replacement process begins.
- Factors to justify the cost of replacement:
  1. maintenance cost
  2. lack of spare parts
  3. hardware and software obsolescence
  4. operating costs
  5. poor reliability
- Also justifying it: new technologies with better security, staff reductions, or other benefits. This often occurs long after the equipment has been fully amortized.
- The security manager forms a team of stakeholders, including IT, to choose a system that meets everyone's needs.
- Address the deficiencies of the old system. Examples: it does not read several card technologies; slow response time.
- Incorporate future uses of the system and of the credentials. Example: debit card function in the cafeteria.
- Provide ample capacity for growth: more sites, panels, and cards.
- Gather information from reputable companies backed by a national network of integrators.
- The software must pass the screening of IT (which will operate it) and Human Resources (a smooth interface between its employee database and the security software).

### 16.3 Replacement

- Optimal: replacement is in the budget, just like that of PCs and laptops. Security equipment also has a useful life.
- Technology advances quickly: equipment only a few years old becomes obsolete at an accelerated rate.
- Getting the most from the investment requires anticipating the trajectory of technology and the needs of the organization.

**Trends cited by the book (2021)**

| Area | From | Toward |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Video | Analog cameras and systems (disappearing) | Digital: moves from the early adopters phase to generally accepted use |
| Alarms / access control | Super-proprietary hardware | IT-centered design with open standards; multiple credentials on a single card (multi-credentialing); more biometrics; easier integration |
| Monitoring (the deepest change) | Proprietary software with visibility into a single system (alarm/access or video) | PSIM (physical security information management) |

**PSIM**

- It connects to many types of sensors, not just security ones.
- It gives the console operator and the incident decision maker real understanding of the situation.
- Situational awareness PSIM: brings together alarms, video, two-way audio, maps, and response resources into a coherent picture of the incident.
- With growing or simultaneous incidents, it helps determine whether they are related (e.g., a moving shooter) or separate (e.g., a small fire and a heart attack in different areas).

**Small systems**

- Digital video usually gives better quality than analog, especially when recorded.
- They may not need to be integrated, but when choosing technology the ability to integrate them as the organization grows must not be ruled out.

### Key terms

remedial maintenance · preventive maintenance · deferred maintenance · on-call repairs · system maintenance agreement · service contract · single point of contact · systems integrator · prime contractor · finger-pointing · service level agreement (SLA) · response time · downtime · factory acceptance test · tuning · nuisance alarm · false alarm · call-out · economies of scale · acts of God · call logging · remote diagnostics · upgrade service · engineering change order · field change order · statement of work (SOW) · shop drawings · maintenance records · system operator records · spare parts · sole-source · maintenance manual · checklist · fence disturbance sensor · duress button · tamper switch · varifocal lens · backup power · switchover · uninterruptible power supply (UPS) · stakeholders · obsolescence · open standards · multi-credentialing · biometrics · physical security information management (PSIM) · situational awareness

## Apéndice A — Key Terms and Definitions (págs. 573–578)

The book warns that many terms mean different things to different people. These are the definitions as the book uses them and, in general, as the professional security community uses them. There are 60 terms, in the order of the book.

| Term | Definition |
| --- | --- |
| access control | Control of people, vehicles, and materials through security measures applied to a protected area. |
| alarm system | A set of sensors, controls, and annunciators arranged to detect and report an intrusion or other emergency. Annunciator: a device that signals with sound, light, or other means. |
| asset | Anything tangible or intangible that has value to a person or organization: people, property, and information. |
| barrier | A natural or man-made obstacle to the movement or direction of people, animals, vehicles, or materials. |
| bollard | Vehicle barrier: a vertical cylinder (almost always concrete-filled steel) anchored in a deep foundation. It stops vehicles and lets pedestrians and bicycles pass. It can take ornamental caps or aluminum, iron, or bronze sleeves over the impact tube (crash tube). |
| building envelope | The separation between the interior and exterior of the building. It is the shell that protects the interior environment and facilitates its climate control. Its design is an architecture and engineering specialty based on building science. |
| camera | A device that captures still or moving images. In security, it is part of a video surveillance system. |
| CCT rating | CCT (Corrected Color Temperature): measures how warm or cool a light is, in kelvin. The book says: sunlight = 5,000 K; above that, warmer (yellowish); below that, cooler (bluish). **Exam note:** this is backwards; the higher the kelvin, the cooler or bluer the light, and the lower, the warmer (outside the book). The usual name is Correlated Color Temperature (outside the book). |
| closed-circuit television (CCTV) | See video surveillance. |
| contract security service | A company that provides security services to another entity in exchange for payment; typically, security officers. |
| crime prevention through environmental design (CPTED) | Pronounced “sep-ted.” Reduces crime and the fear of crime through the good design and use of the built environment. Uses organizational, mechanical, and natural methods to control access, improve natural surveillance, and foster territoriality, supporting legitimate activity. |
| crime | An act or omission that violates a law that prohibits or orders it, and that may be punished with penalties. |
| defense-in-depth (layered security) | A strategy of forming layers of protection for an asset. |
| denial | Frustrating an adversary's attempt to carry out conduct that would be a security incident. |
| design basis threat (DBT) | The threat against which the asset is protected and on which the protection system is designed. It is the baseline type and size of threat that the building must withstand. It includes the aggressors' tactics and their tools, weapons, and explosives. |
| detection | Discovering an attempt, successful or not, to breach a physical or logical secure perimeter. Physical examples: climbing a fence, opening a locked window, entering an area without authorization. |
| duress alarm | A device that lets a person under coercion call for help without raising suspicion. |
| event | A notable occurrence; typically a security incident, an alarm, a medical emergency, or something similar. |
| facility | One or more buildings or structures and parking areas, related by function and location, that form an operating entity. |
| intrusion detection system | A system with one or more sensors that detects an imminent or actual breach (physical or logical). It initiates an alarm, a notification, or a response. |
| lamp | A manufactured light source: filament or arc tube, its glass envelope, and its electrical connectors. |
| light level (intensity, illuminance) | The concentration of light on an area. Measured in lux (lumens per square meter) or foot-candles (lumens per square foot). |
| lighting | The degree of illumination. Also, the interior or exterior equipment to increase it (measured in lux or foot-candles). |
| lock | Equipment that prevents an opening from being opened without authorization (gate, window, building door, vault door; open list) and lets authorized users open it. |
| luminaire (fixture) | The complete lighting unit: lamp, lampholder, and the reflectors and diffusers that distribute and focus the light. |
| metrics | Measures with a reference and at least two points (e.g., quantity over time) that give insight into performance, operation, or quality. |
| perimeter protection | Safeguarding a boundary or border, physical or logical. |
| physical security | The part of security that uses physical measures to safeguard, protect, or prevent unauthorized access to assets. Assets: facilities, equipment, people, intangibles, data, and other property. |
| physical security assessment | See security survey. |
| physical security information management (PSIM) | A software platform that integrates unconnected security applications and devices (video, access control, sensors, analytics, networks, building systems; open list) and controls them from a single comprehensive interface. It collects and correlates events from disparate systems to identify and resolve situations proactively. |
| physical security measure | A tangible device, system, or practice to protect people and prevent harm, loss, or unauthorized access to assets. |
| policy | A general statement of a principle according to which the organization carries out its business functions. |
| private security | Nongovernmental, private-sector practice: protecting people, property, and information, investigating, and safeguarding assets. It is performed by an internal department (proprietary security) or an external provider (contract security). |
| private security officer | A person, uniformed or plainclothes, employed by an organization to protect assets. Formerly often called a “guard.” |
| procedure | Detailed instructions for applying policies (security, organizational, or other) or a business practice of a specific function. |
| progressive collapse | The failure of a main structural element causes adjacent ones to fail, and those cause others to fail. The damage advances until a partial or total collapse of the building. |
| proprietary security organization | Typically, a department of the company that provides security services to the company itself. |
| risk | The probability or potential that a threat exploits vulnerabilities and causes loss or harm to an asset. |
| risk assessment | A systematic process: identify and value assets, list credible threats, document vulnerabilities, describe the impacts of a loss event, and analyze the risks qualitatively or quantitatively. They are reported by priority or severity, with a risk level. |
| risk management | A business discipline that identifies, analyzes, and communicates risk. It then accepts, avoids, transfers, or controls it to an acceptable level, weighing costs and benefits. |
| risk profile | A description or representation of the risks of an asset, system, geographic area, or other entity, considering situational and influencing factors. |
| security incident | An unwanted occurrence or action likely to affect assets. |
| security measure | A practice or device to protect people and prevent harm, loss, or unauthorized access to equipment, facilities, material, and information. |
| security officer | A person, uniformed or plainclothes, employed to protect assets. |
| security survey (physical security assessment) | A critical on-site examination of a building or facility. It seeks to learn its security status, find deficiencies or excesses, set the necessary protection, and recommend improvements. Data: interviews, observation, and document review. |
| sensor | An electronic device that measures a physical quantity (temperature, pressure, or sound intensity) and converts it into an electronic signal. |
| site hardening | Applying reinforcement measures so that a site is harder to penetrate. |
| standoff distance (setback) | The distance between the asset and the threat, typically against an explosive threat. |
| surveillance | Observation of a place, an activity, or a person. |
| tailgating (piggybacking) | Closely following a person or vehicle. In access control: attempting to enter a controlled area right behind someone authorized. |
| threat | Any entity or action that can cause a loss event, adverse impact, or other harm. It includes threat actors (e.g., a terrorist) and threat actions (e.g., a bombing). |
| threat vector | Any path for carrying out a threat action: physical route, sequence of events, or logical path (through cyber infrastructure). |
| throughput | The average rate of flow of people, vehicles, objects, or data through an access point, system, or process. |
| token | An electronically encoded device (smart card, key fob, USB device; open list) with data to authenticate the user's identity. Used in access control to facilities or to IT equipment. |
| uninterruptible power supply (UPS) | A system that provides continuous power to an alternating current (AC) line within tolerances. It protects against overvoltage, loss of main power, and intermittent dips (brownouts). Often used with an emergency generator. |
| vault | A specially constructed room or area that limits access and protects assets (e.g., against forced entry or fire). |
| video analytics | Computer processing of surveillance video for automatic tasks: detecting events instantly, recognizing activities, searching for items (faces, license plates, objects), or extracting data from recorded video. |
| video management system (VMS) | Software that manages video surveillance system functions: network storage, streaming, camera control, alerts, and IP (Internet protocol) interface. |
| video surveillance | A system that captures images and transmits them, typically to monitors, recorders, and control equipment. It includes CCTV and network video systems. |
| vulnerability | A weakness, condition, or organizational practice that makes it easier or possible for a threat (intentional, natural, or inadvertent) to cause harm, or that enlarges a loss event. |

**Exam note:** the book places CPTED before “crime,” out of alphabetical order. “Private security officer” and “security officer” are almost the same definition.

## Apéndice B — Physical Security and Life Safety Considerations in High Rise Buildings (págs. 579–608)

**Central idea:** A high-rise building exceeds the reach of the fire department's equipment, so fire is fought from inside. It concentrates people and property and cannot be evacuated all at once. Protecting it requires balancing life safety, mandated by codes, with physical security, which sometimes pursues the opposite.

### What is a high-rise structure?

- **Definition:** a structure taller than the maximum reach of the available fire-fighting equipment.
- **Absolute figure:** varies between **75 ft (23 m) and 100 ft (30 m)**, about **7 to 10 floors**.
  - It depends on the slab-to-slab height between floors.
- The exact height is set by **local building and fire codes** (Craighead, 2003).
- **Consequence:** above that height, firefighters fight the fire **from inside** the building, not from outside with hoses and ladders.
- **Origin:** the first high-rises in the **United States, in the late 19th century**.
- **Assumption of the appendix:** an **office** building, urban or metropolitan, with direct access from one or more public streets.
  - **Mixed occupancy:** public assembly areas, retail, and conventional offices.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** NFPA 101 (Life Safety Code) and IBC (International Building Code) use **75 ft (23 m)**. It is measured from the lowest level of fire department vehicle access to the highest occupiable floor.

### Life Safety Considerations

- **Risk:** the possibility of loss from a threat, security incident, or event (ASIS, 2004, *General Security Risk Assessment Guideline*).
- **Depends on:** the size of the facility, the number of occupants and, for intentional threats, the value of the target.
- **Why risk is greater in a high-rise:**
  - Very large occupied floors.
  - Difficulty responding to and containing threats.
  - The whole building cannot be evacuated immediately: stairs and elevators have limited capacity.
  - In fires, the elevators on the affected floors are usually not a safe escape route.
- **Mitigation depends on** structural design and technology to:
  - Deter and detect the threat.
  - Communicate its nature and location.
  - Initiate automatic or organizational responses.
- Life safety is planned like a security program: address the assets at risk and follow **deter, detect, delay, respond**.
- Life safety is mandated mainly by building and fire **codes**.
  - They are **minimum** protection standards.
  - Interpreting them usually requires a code expert and clarification from the local authority that enforces them.
- **Key conflict:** life safety requires unimpeded egress. Security does not want a thief with stolen goods to escape freely.
  - Solution: creative access control measures that comply with the codes.

#### Most critical threats to life

- **Fire, explosion, and contamination of life support systems** (air and drinking water).
- They can be accidental or intentional.
- They spread quickly and can reach catastrophic levels.

#### Most significant high-rise life safety factors (4)

1. Early detection and precise location of incipient hazards.
2. Reliable communications throughout the building and with outside agencies.
3. Guaranteed safe escape routes.
4. Rapid application of control measures:
   - Fire extinguishment.
   - Containment or replacement of contaminated air.
   - Shutoff or filtering of drinking water.
   - Containment and removal of explosives.

#### Why fire receives special attention

- Buildings contain a lot of combustible material.
- Constant risk that fire will rise, especially **without an automatic sprinkler system**.
- **In a fire, the first 3 to 4 minutes are critical** (Craighead, 2003).
- Difference from other emergencies (winter storm, civil disturbance): there the initial reaction does not necessarily determine the impact. In a fire, it does.

#### Considering Building Vulnerabilities

- Fire or explosion threaten **structural integrity** and essential services: lighting, communications, elevators, and escalators.
- Threats can spread upward, downward, or horizontally.

| Fire direction | Cause |
| --- | --- |
| Upward | The natural tendency of hot gases to rise |
| Downward | Combustibles that follow a downward path (wall and floor coverings) |
| Horizontal | Within the floor, especially without floor-to-ceiling walls or partitions |
| All three at once | Large conflagrations without containment or early suppression |

- Explosions or sustained conflagrations: partial or total collapse, with multiple injuries and deaths.
- **Airborne contaminants** (smoke, chemical or biological agents):
  - They travel with the air from the point of entry, according to its distribution pattern.
  - With return and recirculation they are redistributed again and again, unless they are filtered or the system is shut off.
  - If an air-handling unit serves several floors, it contaminates all those floors.
  - Depending on the system, they rise, descend, or move horizontally on each floor.
- **Water contaminants:**
  - They move with the water from the point of introduction.
  - Attacking is difficult: any opening in a charged pipe causes a leak.
  - But if a valve is closed, the contaminant is introduced downstream and travels when it is reopened.
  - If that point is near the start of the network, a large part of the system is contaminated.
  - Dissolved gases and solids reach sinks, drinking fountains, and toilets.
  - Heavy contaminants accumulate in traps and pipes, but can be released at the outlets.
  - A small quantity of a highly lethal agent has a wide effect: the water continues to be used before it is detected.
- **Communications:**
  - They rise through common vertical risers and are distributed on each floor from service ports.
  - A complete cut of the cable stops service downstream and can affect upstream by interrupting the direct path.
  - Their interruption mainly affects emergency communications.
  - **Lesson:** distribute alarms and communications so that local points operate on their own if the central panel or processor is lost.

#### Threat of Bombing

- An occupant, visitor, or delivery person can bring in explosives.
- **Screening:** explosives detectors, X-ray machines, and metal detectors.
- **Strict vehicle control:** reduces explosives in garages under the building or underground, and in loading docks.
- **Limit:** in a dense urban area it is difficult to prevent a car bomb parked next to the building.

| Building | Measures | Limit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New | Design to withstand a defined explosive load without progressive failure; maximize the setback distance; window design | The cost must be justified by the probability of the event |
| Existing | Gain distance with heavy planters, bollards, other barriers, and parking restrictions; security film on windows against shards | Structural solutions may be prohibitively costly |

- **4 questions to assess bomb risk:**
  1. Is the building a likely target (e.g., a landmark that attracts attention)?
  2. Is any tenant a particular target of domestic or international terrorists?
  3. Is it an easy or soft target because of poor security?
  4. Is any nearby building a likely target (risk of collateral damage)?
- Although the probability is usually minimal, emergency plans and evacuation preparedness must be **up to date and rehearsed**.
- This weighs more in a high-rise than in a low building:

| Single-story building | High floor of a high-rise |
| --- | --- |
| Quick exit via several horizontal routes | The same horizontal routes **plus** vertical descent by elevators (if available and safe) and stairs |
| No vertical congestion | Congested stairs in a full evacuation |
| Low physical effort | Going down many flights tires even people with full mobility |
| Simple access for people with disabilities | Almost impossible without help from others or mechanical devices (evacuation chairs) |

#### Dealing with the Life Safety Problem

- Many life safety countermeasures also protect assets. Anything that helps respond well adds to total protection.
- **Risk:** an apparent emergency may be a **diversion** to attack assets.
- **Illustrative case:**
  - Stairwells locked from the inside (no reentry to the floor) that unlock with the fire alarm.
  - An intruder activates a manual fire alarm station and enters the chosen floor by the stairwell.
  - The occupants attend to the emergency, not the assets.
- **Lessons:**
  - The system **must** unlock the doors in an emergency.
  - **Life safety takes priority**, unless it is clearly known to be a hoax.
  - Do not rely **only** on locked stairwell doors if they have automatic unlocking; add protection (see Building Stairwells).

#### Local Code Compliance

- **First step** for life safety: comply with local building and fire codes.
- An old building that complied when built may not reflect the best approach today. It may require changes to comply with current requirements.
- Meeting or exceeding the code can offset its cost:
  - Lower commercial insurance premiums.
  - Lower amounts in reserves for retained risks.

#### Fire Detection

- **It must be the first priority.**
- More precisely: detection of **products of combustion** or of the **combustion process**.
- It is the first step of every response: confinement → extinguishment → evacuation and escape.
- It must indicate precisely **where** the fire is and **what stage** it has reached.
- Codes require manual alarm stations (pull stations) on every floor, on the normal exit route. **This is not enough.**
- **Example:** fire on the 4th floor; the person goes down and activates the lobby station. The system says "Fire in Lobby" and provokes a wrong response.
- The manual station lets people give warning while escaping, but does not locate the emergency area well.
- **Solution:** do not eliminate or relocate the manual stations; **complement them** with automatic detection distributed throughout the building.

##### Smoke Detectors

- Automatic devices that detect the presence of smoke.
- **Typical locations** (Craighead, 2003):
  - Open areas.
  - Spaces above suspended ceilings.
  - Spaces under raised floors, especially in computer rooms and data centers.
  - Cafeterias.
  - Air ducts.
  - Lobbies of passenger and service/freight elevators.
  - Elevator shafts.
  - Elevator machine rooms.
  - Enclosed stairwells.
  - Dumbwaiter shafts.
  - Chutes or vertical ducts.
  - Electrical and mechanical equipment rooms.
- Location and spacing: according to local laws, codes, and standards, and engineering issues.
- Even an incipient fire, controlled before the structure burns, can cost a lot in interiors.

##### Heat Detectors

- They detect a given temperature or a **rapid change in temperature**.
- Roberts (2003): very reliable; **the lowest false alarm rate** of all automatic detectors.
- **Best use:**
  - Small, confined spaces where high-heat, rapidly growing fires are expected.
  - Areas whose environmental conditions prevent the use of other detectors.
  - Where very early warning is not required.

##### Sprinkler and Standpipe Alarms

- Wherever there are standpipes and sprinklers: **water-flow** and **valve-position** alarms.
- **Standpipe system:** carries water vertically to upper floors to fight fire by hand with hoses.
- **Sprinkler system** according to Puchovsky (2003):
  - Underground and overhead piping connected to an automatic water supply, installed throughout the building.
  - Piping that is sized or hydraulically designed; inside the building it usually runs overhead.
  - Sprinklers in a systematic pattern.
  - A valve controls each riser, on the riser itself or on the supply pipe.
  - It is normally activated by the heat of the fire and discharges water over the area.
  - A device on the riser triggers an alarm when the system operates.

##### Nature of Fire Alarm Systems

- Computer-based; architecture similar to security alarms.
- Field devices (smoke detectors, air-handling controls) → field panels → main computer.
- They can be in a long series and be **addressable**: each one reports its status under its own name.
- **Some codes prohibit:**
  - The fire alarm performing functions unrelated to fire life safety.
  - Using components not approved for fire life safety.
- **Effect:** depending on the local authority, the fire alarm may not monitor or control security devices, nor vice versa.
- So, releasing electric locks in a fire requires coordinating the design of both systems.
- **Typical solution:** the fire alarm sends no direct signal to the security system. It controls an **approved relay** connected to an **approved power supply** of the lock.

#### Fire Extinguishment

- **Automatic sprinklers** are essential in a high-rise.
- *Fire Protection Handbook* (Cote, 2003): with sprinklers, the probability of dying and the fire loss drop by **one-third to two-thirds**.
- **3 reasons why sprinklers fail:**
  1. Partial, outdated, poorly maintained, or inadequate systems.
  2. Explosions or flash fires that overwhelm the system before it reacts.
  3. Fires very close to people, who die before the system reacts.

#### Other Fixed Systems

- **Standpipe and hose systems** (Shapiro, 2003): typical in tall and large-area buildings.
  - Small hose connections, perhaps with cabinets, for internal use.
  - Larger connections for the fire department.
  - They avoid long hose runs from the truck to the fire.
  - Even with sprinklers, they are **backup and complement**.
- **Water:** the main agent in most high-rises. Other agents:

| Agent | Typical use |
| --- | --- |
| Dry chemical and wet chemical | Hoods, ducts, and cooking appliances in kitchens and cafeterias |
| Halon and halon substitutes | Switchgear rooms and computer and data processing centers |
| Carbon dioxide (CO2), in certain cases | The same spaces as halon |

- **Cost decision** in a building with sprinklers: compare special system + probable damage when activated **against** sprinklers + probable water damage.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** the Montreal Protocol eliminated halon production; today clean agents are used.

#### Smoke Control

- Smoke harms people, the building, and its contents.
- Obvious measure: limit flammable synthetic materials in furnishings; allow only fire-resistant ones.
- The proliferation of personal computers makes it hard to limit combustibles.
- The design and construction of the building influence smoke control.
- Milke (2003): smoke behaves differently depending on height.

| Low buildings | Tall buildings |
| --- | --- |
| Moved by the effects of the fire: heat, convective movement, and fire pressures | Predominant factors: **stack effect**, **external wind**, and **forced air movement** |

- **Stack effect:** natural vertical movement of air in the building due to temperature and density differences between interior and exterior air.
- **Forced air movement:** caused by air-handling equipment.
  - HVAC (heating, ventilating, and air-conditioning).
  - ACV (air-conditioning and ventilating).
- Webb (2003): except for self-contained units, these systems use **ducts**, which can spread fire, gases, and smoke.
- **Keys to containing fire** (Webb, 2003):
  - Location of equipment and of **fresh-air intakes**.
  - Type of air filters and cleaners.
  - Duct and plenum system.
  - Use of **fire and smoke dampers**.
- Systems vary by manufacturer, building, and the code in force when installed.
- **Two basic approaches** (Webb, 2003):
  1. Shut down the HVAC and isolate or compartmentalize the fire area.
  2. Keep the fans running and use the ducts for emergency smoke control.
- In addition, in many high-rises the alarm activates **automatic stairwell pressurization** with fans (Craighead, 2003).

#### Fire Department or Emergency Services Notification

- In the event of a fire life safety system incident, notify the fire department or emergency services **immediately**.
- In addition to notification by building staff: monitoring by an **off-site central station** or by the fire department itself.
- It may be required by the local code or be an additional measure by the owner or manager.

#### Occupant Notification

- Notify all affected occupants and responders promptly (Schifiliti, 2003).
- The alarm indicates to evacuate or relocate to a safe area. It can also notify emergency forces.

| Type of notification | Information conveyed |
| --- | --- |
| Audible signal (bells, sirens, whoopers) + strobe lights | **A single bit:** "fire alarm" |
| Voice announcements or text or graphic annunciators | **Several bits:** alarm, location, and how and where to evacuate or relocate |

- With detailed information, people evacuate **faster and better**.
- Audible and visible devices also indicate system **trouble**.
- Or they serve as a **supervisory signal** on the status of other systems, e.g., sprinklers.
- **Public address (PA) system:** a **one-way** voice system from the fire control panel to the occupants.
- That panel is usually in the **fire command center** or **fire control room**.

#### Emergency Planning

- Essential: a comprehensive **emergency management plan**.
- It describes actions to protect employees, the public, and other assets.
- It covers **2 categories of threats: natural and man-made**.
- Threats are anticipated and initial reactions are designed. This way, in the emergency, the most important things are attended to.
- **Content for a high-rise (6 elements):**
  1. Evacuation signage that clearly marks the exits.
  2. Types of emergency systems and equipment in the building and how they operate.
  3. Organization of emergency personnel, who act until outside agencies arrive:
     - Management and the building fire safety director.
     - Engineering, security, cleaning, and parking staff.
     - Floor staff: floor wardens or fire wardens.
     - Those responsible for assisting people with disabilities, and others as needed.
  4. Contact information for the necessary people and agencies.
  5. Procedures for each anticipated emergency, including evacuation and relocation methods.
  6. Training of occupants, wardens, and staff, including the frequency and type of fire drills.
- **Review the plan with the local fire marshal** or equivalent.
  - Strategy varies according to size, materials, and the fire department equipment available.
  - Depending on circumstances, evacuation can **expose occupants to more risk**.
- **Resource:** the NFPA (National Fire Protection Association) offers *Guidelines for Developing Emergency Management Plans for All-Hazard Emergencies in High Rise Office Buildings* free of charge.

### Security Considerations

- **Security differences between a high-rise and a low building** (Craighead, 2009):
  1. **Several occupied floors stacked** → more occupants and more property to damage or steal.
     - The concentration of property attracts the criminal.
     - More people = more anonymity for the thief, especially if he dresses and acts like the users.
     - Kitteringham (2006): many are in central business districts. Close to mass transit and easily accessible, they attract **professional thieves**.
  2. **More people gathered** → greater chance that one will commit a crime against another.
     - Problem: crime pattern analyses are lacking for high-rises.
     - Crime incidence depends on the neighborhood, the building design, its use and type of tenants, and the security program.
  3. **All have stairs and elevators**; a single-story building does not.
     - Stairwell: a little-used area except in evacuations → possible site for assault (including sexual assault) or theft.
     - Elevator: possible scene of vandalism and crimes against persons.
- Entry and exit are usually channeled through the **core**, where the elevators and escalators are.
- Authenticating and controlling ingress and egress requires special consideration.
- Effective security measures minimize the impact of most threats.

#### The Problem of Exposed Assets

- Assets are vulnerable to natural hazards, theft, and malicious damage.
- In buildings open to the public, the flow of people in hallways, elevators, stairs, and public areas increases vulnerability.
- **Principle:** the need for protection arises from the **nature of the assets**, not from who owns them.
  - It does not matter that the risk is shared by several organizations.
- A highly exposed tenant that does not protect itself threatens others: its assets attract attackers to the building.
- The final scheme must be a **cooperative effort** among owner, manager, and tenants.

#### The Life Safety and Security Dilemma

- The factors that complicate life safety also create security problems.
- The code requires free exit whenever there are occupants → egress with little or no surveillance.
- In a real emergency, security may not be ready to handle the emergency and heightened security at the same time.
- **Two-person attack:**
  - One simulates a fire with a manual station on a floor.
  - The accomplice, many floors away, takes advantage of the doors unlocked by the emergency.
- **Countermeasures:** intrusion alarms and video surveillance with alarm-activated camera deployment and recording.

#### The High-Rise Physical Environment (the model building)

- A notional building that illustrates Figure B-1 (main lobby at street level):

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Height | 40 floors |
| Occupants | About 6,000; about 150 per floor |
| Passenger elevators | 3 banks of 8 cars; each bank serves about one-third of the building |
| Service or freight elevators | 2 cars; they serve all floors, from B-2 to 40 |
| Basements B-1 and B-2 | Mechanical and storage areas; served by the 2 service elevators and the low bank (C) |
| Vehicle access (H) | Ramp to B-1: rolling metal shutter at the street and an open-grille rolling shutter at the bottom |
| Loading dock | 4 trucks; served by the service elevators |

- **Figure B-1 legend:**
  - A = high-rise elevator lobby (8 cars).
  - B = mid-rise elevator lobby (8 cars).
  - C = low-rise elevator lobby (8 cars).
  - D = fire stair shafts and doors.
  - E = service elevators (2 cars, B2 to 40).
  - F = rear doors: one set of double swinging doors and two revolving doors.
  - G = main doors: three revolving doors and two single swinging doors.
  - H = rolling door and ramp down for trucks.

#### Building Operating Modes

| Mode | How it works |
| --- | --- |
| **Open** | Free access at the entrance. Everyone goes directly to their floor; the occupant of the space sets its own control. There may be a concierge or security desk |
| **Closed** | Access to the elevator banks with credential verification. Visitors authenticated before the elevators. Sometimes, a separate mail center |
| **Hybrid** | A major tenant occupies a large part of the building. One or more banks operate closed; the multitenant banks, open |

- **Open building desk:** gives directions, deters undesirable visitors (if they are identifiable), and applies procedures to couriers, deliverers, and contractors.
- **Exam note:** the book announces the concept "open or closed," but describes **three** modes (including hybrid).
- **Modes by schedule** (according to traffic, occupancy, time, and day):
  - **Regular business hours:** most tenants and operations are active.
  - **Intermediate hours:** 1 or 2 hours before and after regular hours; a transition period.
  - **Off-hours or after-hours:** nights, weekends, and holidays, with few or no tenants.

#### Access Control Recommendations (3 classes of interior spaces)

| Class | Includes |
| --- | --- |
| Public access or common areas | Street-level entrance lobbies, main elevator lobbies, access to shops and restaurants, promenades, mezzanines and, increasingly, atriums |
| Rented or assigned occupancies, tenant spaces | Spaces rented or occupied by the owner. Open to the public during hours or restricted to authorized persons, depending on the occupant |
| Maintenance spaces | Mechanical rooms and floors, communications and utility points, elevator machine rooms, janitorial rooms, and very limited-access spaces |

- Access control measures vary for each class.

#### Access Control of Public Areas

- Classify those who enter the lobby according to their **purpose**:
  - **Regular tenants:** credential (e.g., access card) to quickly verify their right to enter.
  - **Visitors** (contractors, deliverers, maintenance providers): additional screening. Authenticate them, check them, more scrutiny and escort.
- **Possible scheme:** stairwells locked against entry from the lobby, with free exit to the lobby, and control points at each passenger elevator bank.
  - To be effective, everyone would show identification, allow inspection of the property they bring in, or both.
  - Acceptable in periods of heightened security.
  - Under normal conditions, some tenants resist it, especially those that receive walk-in clients.
- **Bank:** usually on the ground floor and perhaps in part of the basement (vault and safe deposit boxes). Its lobby is usually open access.
- **Retail only on the ground floor:** give it separate access from the street.
  - It makes use outside the building's hours easier.
  - It reduces outside traffic in the lobby.
- **Owner who occupies some floors and rents others:** plan the assignment or stacking of tenants to control access with minimum cost and inconvenience.
- The building operates as a hybrid. **4 planning points:**
  1. **Identify the owner's sensitive occupancies:** executive offices, data processing, cash or valuables handling, large volumes of sensitive data, and any space the owner declares critical.
  2. **Isolate them:** group them on floors of **a single sector** (low-rise, mid-rise, or high-rise).
  3. **Impose controls in the sector that contains them.** Example with the model building:
     - The owner occupies the floors of bank A (high-rise) and half of bank B (mid-rise); rents the rest.
     - The sensitive occupancies are stacked on the floors of the high-rise bank.
     - The mid-rise lobby is divided: half of the cars serve floors 13 to 19; the other half, 20 to 27.
     - The sensitive half (20 to 27) is controlled the same way and with the same staff as the high-rise bank: card readers and queue or flow devices.
     - If the occupancy proportion changes, those controls are removed without affecting the high-rise sector.
  4. **Ensure the controls cannot be bypassed:**
     - A common or crossover floor between banks must not give free access to a sensitive area.
     - Example: if floor 27 is served by the high-rise bank and the mid-rise bank, put a staffed control point on 27, or readers in the cars of both banks.
     - Without a crossover floor, to change banks you have to go down to the main lobby.

#### Elevator Control

- Occupancy changes over the years and with it the access control required.
- **Great advantage:** design the passenger elevators (or retrofit them, if cost-effective) to control access to **all** floors.
  - This way each car is programmed: how it responds to calls from the car and from the lobbies.
- **Good practice:** card readers in **all** cars, even if they are not always active.
- **Turnstiles:** screen people before they enter the elevator.
- **Going from an open to a closed building:** program the elevators with card access 24/7.
  - Give cards only to authorized persons, for the floors and hours set by each tenant's management.
- **Service or freight elevators:** a special problem, because they usually serve all levels.
  - If they are self-service, they compromise the entire security program, unless:
    1. They are programmed not to reach sensitive floors without special arrangement.
    2. They are locked at the hoistway doors of the sensitive floors.
    3. They arrive at floors with the vestibule or lobby doors closed, where applicable.
  - **Additional risk:** unauthorized movement of property from or between accessible floors.
  - **Mitigation:** assign an operator or provide them only on request to security.
  - Readers in the cars and cameras in cars or lobbies also help.
  - **Avoid** using them for general passenger movement.

#### Building Stairwells

- Emergency exit stairs (fire stairs) are common areas.
- They must be accessible for escape, especially if the elevators are not available or not safe (e.g., in a fire).
- **Two factors define how to secure them:**
  1. **Local code:** with occupants, they are **never** locked on the egress route. Restricting **reentry** to the floor may be permitted.
     - Some jurisdictions: stairwell-to-floor doors locked, provided that **every fourth floor** they are open to exit the shaft.
     - Others: locked if they unlock by themselves (**fail safe**) when the fire life safety system is activated.
  2. **Is movement between floors by the stairs permitted?**
     - In favor (multifloor tenants): saves time, reduces demand on elevators and energy, and is more convenient.
     - Against: reduces security of the stairs themselves.
     - **Better, though costly:** the multifloor tenant's own internal stair.
     - **Ideal:** access to the stairs from the common area of the floor (elevator lobby and corridors), not from inside the tenant space.
     - If the jurisdiction allows: doors with approved access control (readers) and two-way **intercoms** with security.
- Choosing the stairwell locks is critical to comply with the code. Always coordinate with local authorities.
- Craighead (2003): the **stairwell exit door on the ground floor** is where security conflicts most with fire life safety.
  - Immediate, unimpeded exit lets the criminal flee quickly.
- **Delayed-egress locking device:** some authorities allow, outside an emergency, a **15 to 30 second** delay at the exit door to the ground floor.
  - Useful together with:
    1. An alarm that alerts security of the exit attempt.
    2. An intercom to speak with the person.
    3. A camera that views and records the event.
  - The delay may be enough for security to intercept.
  - They must unlock by themselves if the lock's **power fails** or the fire life safety system **is activated**.
- **Exam note (outside the book):** NFPA 101 sets 15 s, extendable to 30 s if approved by the authority having jurisdiction (AHJ).
- **Intrusion alarms on stairwell doors:** indicate entry into the shaft and allow a response.
  - **Doors locked against reentry:** an officer goes to the ground-floor door of that stairwell. Another enters the shaft **above** the alarm point. This way the intruder is intercepted inside.
  - **Doors generally open:** alarms on sensitive floors indicate use of that door.
  - Every intrusion alarm must be **zoned** to indicate exactly which door or area was breached.

#### Access Control of Interior Floors and Spaces

- It can be the only control or be integrated with the general lobby control.
- Programmable elevators give some control by floor.
- Variant, if there is no full control by elevator: readers in the cars for certain floors.
- **Visitors without a card:** special handling by security staff.
  - Floor with few visitors: **escort**.
  - Floor with heavy traffic: free access by elevator and a **reception point on the floor**.
- **Piggybacking or tailgating:** an unauthorized person enters a secure area accompanying a cardholder.
  - Authorized users must help deter it.
  - It is also controlled with turnstiles next to the elevators.
- **Reception in the elevator lobby itself** (building of Figure B-1):
  - One end of the lobby, closed toward the floor (perhaps with remote release or card).
  - The reception, at the opposite end.
- **Larger integrated system:** the central console remotely controls access to floors or areas.
- **Small spaces within the floor:** conventional keys, readers, remote latch release, communication or surveillance, or a receptionist.
- **Doors of elevator lobbies on upper floors:** special care with the lock.
  - **Phase I:** a smoke detector in the elevator lobby, shaft, or machine room is activated. The cars of that bank return by themselves to a designated level.
  - If the alarm was in the lobby of the designated level, they return to an alternate floor.
  - **Phase II:** once recalled, they remain for fire department service with a **special key** (Donoghue, 2003).
  - The key is **for firefighters only**, not for engineering or security. Its use is strictly controlled.
  - Anyone in the lobby when the elevators are recalled must be able to exit to a fire stair.
  - Consult the local jurisdiction on which locks are permitted on those doors.

#### Access Control of Building Maintenance Spaces

- **Critical areas:**
  - Mechanical rooms and floors.
  - Air-conditioning rooms.
  - Fire pump and fire control rooms.
  - Telecommunications and utility installations, including electrical transformer vaults.
  - Elevator pits.
  - Elevator machine rooms.
- Control with selective admittance devices and monitoring with intrusion alarms.
- If a door is opened without an authorized key, automatic device, or remote release → **distinctive** intrusion alarm to the monitoring center.
- Feasible even in large buildings: those who need access are few and easy to identify.
- **Fully automated system:** cards and readers, or remote admission with latch control, video, and two-way communication.
- **Minimum low-cost control** (small building, no large integrated system):
  1. Conventional lock on the door.
  2. Controlled issuance of keys.
  3. Intrusion alarm zoned distinctively.
  4. Contact by telephone or radio from the key holder just before or after entering.
- The contact is eliminated with a **shunt switch** operated with the authorized key.
- **Disadvantage of keys:** they get lost and compromised.
- **Utility personnel** (e.g., telephone installers in distribution rooms and terminal panels):
  - Regularly assigned: key or automatic entry device.
  - Temporary: after verifying their identity, a **one-day** device. Ensure its return before they leave.

#### Access Control of Fresh Air Intakes

- HVAC intakes are usually at or near ground level, covered with louvered grilles.
- Protect them against the introduction of airborne chemical or biological agents.
- **Figure B-2 (Fresh Air Intake Protection Scheme):** grille or baffle at **90 degrees** to the intake, with an **intrusion alarm** on the grille.
  - Limit: the suction could still draw in vapors from a source placed on the grille.
- **Best solution:** locate the intakes high enough above the ground that access is impossible.
- **Figure B-2 legend:**
  - A = exterior building wall.
  - B = grade level.
  - C = open space and intake area.
  - D = louvered intake shutter.
  - E = grille, protected with an intrusion alarm.
  - F = undisturbed earth.

#### Access Control of Telecommunication Services

- They leave the building in one or more trunk cables toward ducts and tunnels in the street.
- Telephone, computing, and alarms use that route: they are vulnerable along the cable.
- **High-security building:** **two physically separate routes** to **different telephone central offices**.
- **Most critical points:** where all services have already joined into one or two trunks that are still inside the building.
  - Old buildings: trunks exposed in basements or service areas.
  - New buildings: usually more concealed.
- Review the route from the risers to the service entrance. Protect every access point.
  - Cable in an enclosed space: intrusion detection on hatch covers and doors.
  - Exposed cable: harden the route with resistant construction.

### Additional Security Measures

- They are part of an integrated physical security strategy for high-rises (8):
  1. Lighting.
  2. Selective site hardening.
  3. Locks and locking devices.
  4. Alarm sensors.
  5. Duress alarms.
  6. Integrated video and security systems.
  7. Security personnel.
  8. Policies and procedures.

#### Lighting

- It creates a **psychological deterrent** against crime, including theft and physical assault.
- **Uses:** perimeter accesses (streets and walkways), perimeter barriers, landscaping, facades, open parking lots, and parking structures.
  - Also all interior areas, including loading docks.
- Relatively inexpensive. Well used, it can reduce the need for security personnel.
- It supports the effective use of video systems.

#### Selective Site Hardening

- A person or company that is already a high-risk target (including for terrorism) is more exposed in a high-rise.
- In addition to access control, hardening of the structure at or within target floors may be required.
- **Ideally from the initial construction:** in a remodel there are fewer options and more cost.

#### Construction Features

- **Main reason:** resistance to **impact and firearms**.
- Bombings add the need to evaluate **blast-resistant** design in new high-profile construction.
- **Resistant materials (9):**
  1. Standard masonry.
  2. Blast-resistant reinforced concrete.
  3. Sheet steel.
  4. Polycarbonates.
  5. Acrylic.
  6. Compressed fiberglass.
  7. Ballistic fabrics.
  8. Bullet-resistive glass.
  9. Security film for windows: holds broken glass together and keeps shards from becoming lethal projectiles.
- **Example (Figure B-1), key personnel floor:**
  - Close both ends of the elevator corridor with resistant material and equivalent controlled doors.
  - Partition of masonry and fiberglass, or of steel between drywall panels.
  - Resistant glass or polycarbonate door.
- **Cost principle:** place barriers and hardened materials as close as possible to the normal points of entry to the floor.
  - Hardening the elevator lobby and all stairwell doors is cheaper than hardening individual offices.
- **Suspended ceilings and raised floors** with plenums or ducts can be used for intrusion. When hardening a floor:
  - Carry the resistant partition up to the slab above or below, or
  - Close the space with heavy-gauge wire mesh, which lets air through.
  - Complement with intrusion devices.

#### Key Reception Area

- If entry control for a target floor is on the floor itself, the reception area must resist attacks.
- Executives often reject barriers in their reception: they say they send a bad message or make a bad impression.
- **Solutions:**
  1. **Reception on another floor** (above or below the protected one).
     - There the visitor is identified and a controller on the sensitive floor is notified.
     - Then the visitor goes up to the sensitive floor, where access is specifically controlled.
  2. **Two control points on the protected floor:**
     - Staffed exterior reception, separated from the interior by protective barriers with observation capability.
     - Someone inside, behind the barriers, unlocks to admit the authorized visitor. They can do so before the visitor reaches the door.
     - Visitors do not notice that the interior doors are locked.
     - With no opening mechanism at reception, no one enters unless someone inside admits them or they have a key.
     - **The receptionist must not have that key or device.**
     - For her safety: a discreet **hold-up button** under the desk.

#### Physical Barriers

- They protect against car bombs and discourage penetration by **accident, force, and stealth**.
- **Types:** standoff distances, perimeter fences, walls, sidewalks, pathways, landscaping, fountains, ponds, sculptures, benches, planters, barricades, and bollards.
- **Functions (3):** **delay** the intruder, **define** an area, and **direct** behavior.
- **Difficulty:** achieving sufficient separation between the barrier and the building. In cities space is scarce and the buildings already exist.
- Even so, well-designed perimeter barriers prevent a vehicle with explosives from reaching the building before detonating.
- **Parking lots and loading docks** (especially under the building): parking controllers and barriers.
  - Gate arms.
  - In high security: **crash-rated hydraulic impact barriers**.
  - Manual or automatic activation, to admit only authorized vehicles.

#### Building Locks and Locking Devices

- The type depends on the door or barrier and on the building or life safety code in force.

| Location | Recommended device |
| --- | --- |
| Main entry and exit doors, and legal exits of the perimeter | Electromagnetic locks with timer |
| Interior side (stair side) of stairwell doors | Hightower-function mortise lock or stair tower lock: a special hybrid electric lock |
| Exterior side of stairwell doors that exit the building | Key-operated pin tumbler locks and electromagnetic locks |
| Elevator lobby or vestibule doors | Electromagnetic hold-open devices |

- **Electromagnetic locks at main entrances** (Craighead, 2003):
  - The timer locks and unlocks them according to the building's schedule.
  - Locked: access from outside with a card reader.
  - Normal exit with panic hardware or automatic unlocking (e.g., motion detectors).
  - Codes require that the power of any lock that restricts egress be **supervised by the fire life safety system**.
  - Power failure or fire alarm → power is cut and they open (**fail-safe**). Occupants get out and firefighters get in.
- **Hightower-function mortise lock** (Geringer, 1991):
  - Allows locking the door on the stair side for security.
  - Always energized and locked. Access with a mechanical key, digital keypad, or reader.
  - Its power is controlled by the life safety system. In an emergency it unlocks instantly, **but remains closed and latched**: it protects the stairwell from smoke and fire.
  - Some jurisdictions allow delayed egress of 15 to 30 s outside an emergency, with automatic unlocking on power failure or system activation.
- **Exterior of stairwell exits** (Craighead, 2003):
  - They can be locked on the outside if they operate freely from the inside.
  - When the exit bar (fire exit hardware) is pushed, they open instantly, except for delayed-egress ones.
- **Electromagnetic hold-open devices in elevator lobbies:**
  - When the life safety system is activated, their power is cut and they release the door.
  - The door closes by itself and protects the lobby from smoke and fire.

#### Alarm Sensors

- Intrusion detection for perimeter areas, stairwells, maintenance, and tenants.

#### Duress Alarms

- **Where:** parking booths, other cash-handling areas, and reception points, including executive reception.
- Train those who activate them and those who respond, with solid policies and procedures.
- **Test them regularly.**

#### Video Surveillance

- Very useful in a high-rise. Its effectiveness depends on **camera location** and on **monitoring and response strategies**.

| Location | What it includes or what it is for |
| --- | --- |
| Critical pedestrian entrances and exits | Stairwell exits to the ground floor, perimeter, critical floor receptions, mechanical floors, and crossover floors. Monitor and identify who exits by the stairwell |
| Remotely operated pedestrian accesses | Remotely controlled doors: so that only authorized people enter, with no tailgating or piggybacking |
| Accesses with automated control (readers) | So that only the person who uses the card enters and there is no tailgating |
| Inside passenger and service cars | Camera in a **visible** place: deters threats to people and property, and elevator vandalism |
| Sensitive interior spaces | Where remote surveillance gives a great advantage: mechanical areas, HVAC air intakes, vaults, storerooms, and elevator lobbies |
| Vehicle entrances and exits | Monitor vehicles and decide on remote access. In garages under the building: record all vehicles, **license plates**, and drivers |
| Covert surveillance | Hidden cameras in sensitive places. **Check local laws** first. Very useful in investigations |

- With remote access control, **two-way voice** is also needed between the entrance and the control point.
- Video without critical analysis or a response resource = **operationally inadequate and economically wasted**.
- **Monitoring requirements (all):**
  1. Each camera's image always visible, unless alarm switched when needed.
  2. Monitors arranged for rapid visual analysis by a trained observer.
  3. A means to bring any camera of interest to a monitor in the operator's direct field of view.
  4. Permanent recording of one or more cameras, identified by **camera location and time**.
- **Systems of 2 or 3 cameras:** one monitor per camera plus a recorder.
- **Systems of 10 or more cameras:** require a more sophisticated design.
- **Design advice:** "design the application first and fit the equipment to it."

#### Security Operations Center

- A central place in the building to monitor and control security systems and equipment.
- It must be used on a **dedicated** basis.
- Because of cost and staffing, it is sometimes installed in the main lobby or next to heavily used entrances.
  - The operator attends to the system and, at the same time, to the people passing by.
  - The theoretical savings of adding other fixed posts to the operator can cause inattention to loss events of much greater magnitude.
  - It also exposes control of the system to compromise or attack.
- With a well-designed system, the monitoring and control center should be **the most secure place in the building**.

#### Security Personnel

- **Main function:** implement the building's life safety and security program.
- **The number depends on:**
  - Type of occupancy and its activities.
  - Objectives of the security and life safety program.
  - Role of security in that program.
- **Offices:** more staff during regular hours than outside hours (Craighead, 2003).
  - During hours there are employees, visitors, vendors, tradespeople, building management, contractors, messengers, deliverers, promoters, and others.
  - Outside hours, pedestrian and vehicle traffic drops sharply, except for special activities.
- **Residential:** it can be just the opposite.
- The staffing level is evaluated, assigned, and adjusted according to the circumstances.

### Summary

- High-rise: taller than the maximum reach of fire department equipment; it has unique life safety and security needs.
- It concentrates people and property. Threats: fires, natural disasters, and crimes (theft of assets, workplace violence, and terrorism).
- In fires and explosions it is necessary to evacuate immediately. But stairs and elevators do not allow everyone to get out at once.
- Therefore: updated, complete, and well-thought-out emergency plans, and occupants trained to react.
- Measures must comply with strict building and life safety codes, and address security needs.
  - Those needs vary according to the type of tenants and the pattern of use.
- When they clash, comply with life safety **without compromising** the security objectives.

## Apéndice C — Standards in Security (págs. 609–612)

**Central idea:** A standard is a set of criteria, guidelines, and best practices that improves the quality and reliability of products, services, or processes. There are voluntary standards, those mandatory by law, and mixed ones.

**What they are and how they are made**

- Thanks to national and international standards, parts, processes, and systems work regardless of who creates them, who uses them, or where.
- They are created to meet a market need or a gap in existing standards.
- They are coordinated by accredited standards developing organizations (SDOs). They bring together public and private stakeholders to identify best practices.
- They are the basis of innovation and competitiveness. They provide a common language for national and international trade.
- Four development principles: consensus, openness, due process, and transparency.
- In security they help nations, communities, societies, organizations, and individuals protect assets and improve resilience to natural and human threats.

**Types of standards**

| Type | Key feature | Examples from the book |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Voluntary standards | They are not mandatory, but are widely adopted. Some are integrated into regulatory frameworks of several countries | ISO and national institutes; in the US, associations such as ASIS International |
| Statutory or regulatory standards | Mandatory by law; enforced by formal authorities | — |
| Mixed standards | The boundary blurs when a voluntary standard is incorporated into laws or regulations (fire, construction, human safety). Or it remains voluntary in theory but mandatory in practice | Insurers that set the availability and cost of casualty insurance according to approved materials or standardized practices; contracts that require standards |

- The adoption of standards is increasingly client driven.

**Standards and organizations cited in the appendix**

| Organization | Code | Title or topic |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ISO (International Organization for Standardization) | — (in general) | Voluntary but widely adopted. Asset protection topics: facilities management; health, environment, and safety; risk management; security and resilience; IT and information security |
| ISO | ISO 9001 | Quality management systems. One of the two most prominent management system standards |
| ISO | ISO 14001 | Environmental management systems. The other most prominent management system standard |
| ASIS International | ASIS ORM.1-2017 | Security and Resilience in Organizations and their Supply Chains – Requirements with Guidance. Basis of Figure C.1 |
| ASIS International | — (library) | Security management standards and guidelines. More information at asisonline.org, in the standards and guidelines section |
| NFPA (National Fire Protection Association) | — | Not all are voluntary: some are incorporated into regulations, such as building codes |
| UL (Underwriters Laboratories) | — | Several standards on security equipment: locks, alarms, and access control systems |
| Illuminating Engineering Society (IES) | — | Lighting standards and practices |
| Electronic Industries Association (EIA) | — | Electronic components and products |

**Exam note:** the appendix gives a code only for ISO 9001, ISO 14001, and ASIS ORM.1-2017. The ASIS standard is published as ANSI/ASIS ORM.1-2017 (outside the book). The EIA has been called the Electronic Industries Alliance since 1997 (outside the book).

**Benefits of Standards (benefits for the security professional)** — a standard can do one or all of these things:

1. Codify best practices and processes, and share lessons learned.
2. Provide tools to assess threats, risks, vulnerabilities, criticalities, and impacts.
3. Define measurement methods.
4. Document equipment performance requirements to ensure effectiveness and safety.
5. Set design requirements so that devices, systems, and infrastructure withstand threats.
6. Define effective methods to identify people.
7. Increase knowledge and competence.
8. Improve information sharing between jurisdictions and interoperability.
9. Provide consistency in services.

**Management System Standards**

- They help improve how the organization delivers services and executes processes. Widely accepted in many fields.
- The most prominent: ISO 9001 (quality) and ISO 14001 (environmental).
- Approach: analyze the requirements of the organization and of stakeholders, and define processes that lead to success.
- They provide the framework for continuous improvement. They raise the probability of achieving strategic, operational, tactical, and reputational objectives, and strengthen resilience.

**Plan-Do-Check-Act (PDCA) cycle**

- It is the operating principle of management system standards: structured problem solving focused on continuous improvement.

| Phase | What is done (appendix text) | What Figure C.1 says |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Plan | Identify and analyze the organization's problems and assets and rank them by importance | Define and analyze a problem and its context |
| Do | Prioritize the next steps, make a detailed action plan, and apply it | Devise a solution, develop a detailed action plan, and apply it systematically |
| Check | Examine whether the solutions give results consistent with the plan | Confirm results against the plan; identify deviations and problems |
| Act | If the solutions do solve the problems: standardize them across the organization, review the list of problems, and define new ones. There the cycle restarts | Standardize the solution; review and define the next problems |

**Figure C.1 — Plan-Do-Check-Act Model (from ASIS ORM.1-2017)**

- Input: stakeholders and interested parties with their requirements and expectations.
- Center: organizational resilience management systems, turning in the PDCA cycle.
- Output: stakeholders and interested parties with managed risk.
